We can gain an understanding of just how vital that food of the word of Christ was to men like Vettori (and others) from the letter that Machiavelli wrote to Guicciardini from Carpi on 19 May 1521: “As for the preacher, I do not expect any honor from this affair because the fellow is on the fence. The Father in charge says that he is pledged to others, so I think I am going to return in disgrace; I am not at all happy about all this because I do not know how I shall show up before Francesco Vettori and Filippo Strozzi, who wrote me privately about this matter, begging me to do my utmost so that during this Lenten season they might dine on some spiritual food that would be to their advantage.” Machiavelli was concerned about his honor and the reactions of his friends, in part because on other, previous occasions he had failed to perform a task of the same kind successfully:

And they will be sure to point out that everything I do for them is cast in the same mold: one Saturday evening last winter I was with them at Giovan Francesco Ridolfi’s house in the country, and they assigned me the task of locating the priest for the next morning’s mass. Sure enough, it turned out that that blessed priest arrived after they had eaten so that the entire household was turned topsy-turvy and I experienced everyone’s ill will because of it. If now in this second commission I set up another disaster, imagine what crazy faces they will make at me. So I am counting on you to write them a couple of lines and to get me out of this mess as best you can.40


In contrast with his illustrious friends, Machiavelli was in no way troubled by the lack of the “spiritual food” that Catholic masses procured, chiefly because the food for which his soul truly hungered was of a different variety, as he revealed to Vettori—and to us—with his letter dated 10 December 1513. When he goes back into his house and steps into his study, he finds himself again: he is reborn and morally redeemed, elevating his mind from abject thoughts and occupations that do not suit him to the great thoughts and occupations of the fatherland—and these are his true life. The same principle applies to him as to religions and states: rebirth comes only by returning to the beginnings and rediscovering—at the cost of great effort—one’s own true nature.41 Machiavelli, who loves his fatherland more than his own soul, seeks, and finds, his redemption in the process of reasoning about great political acts.

The great men of antiquity who welcome him and answer his questions live on, in some place. They were successful in winning eternity, in triumphing over death. Why should it be impossible for Machiavelli to follow in their footsteps? Without any reference to God, or Christ, or religion, Machiavelli knows a way to become similar to God and live eternally. It is surely a different path from that offered by the church, since it does not involve confessions, processions, special prayers, fasting, or penitence. Is it different from the true path that God and Christ showed men?

In order to answer this question, it is crucial to examine the theme of the yearning for immortality and man’s desire to become godlike, in the Florentine religious and philosophical tradition. In particular, Marsilio Ficino (1433?–99) explored these themes; Machiavelli called Ficino the “second father of Platonic philosophy.”42 A lover, wrote Ficino, often “wishes to transfer himself into the person he loves,” and in this act, yearns to “become come God.”43 Love wishes to contemplate and create beauty; it is both death and resurrection.44 It instills in man the love of virtue and through virtue links man again to God: “But when God infused his own light into the soul, He adapted the light above all to this: that it might lead men to bliss, which consists in the possession of Him. To this we are led by four virtues: Prudence, Courage, Justice, and Temperance.”45 God places His spark in the souls of men and some, “thanks to this gift, undergo dangers and death with a brave heart, for the worship of God, for integrity, or for fatherland.”46 “What restores us to heaven is not knowledge of God, but love.”47 Humans can grasp true beauty, and savor celestial viands, only through love. The true lover, Ficino concludes, helps the fatherland to attain a happy and honest life, like Socrates who, “devoted to this [the fatherland] through love,” conversed with young people on an equal footing and defended them from false lovers.48 Love is the desire for immortality (“Amor est immortalitatis desiderium”).49 The best men, such as founders of states and legislators, Diotima of Mantinea explains, do all that they do “because they love immortality.”50 Those who are “pregnant in their souls” turn out of love of immortality to procreation through wisdom, and of all the forms of wisdom, political wisdom is superior to all others. The creations of the soul, like the works that Lycurgus left Sparta and Solon left Athens, engender greater glory than natural procreation.51

Out of love of immortality, Machiavelli sought out the company of men who had become gods and immortals because they were able to achieve great things for the fatherland. He absorbed himself “into them completely” and he fed on the same spiritual food that those great men had consumed. Since he is a “true lover,” he loves his native city more than his own soul, and he writes his political works in order to teach the young to work well on behalf of the fatherland, in a never-ending effort of moral and political generation and regeneration. He cultivates that idea that the love of immortality and beauty is the passion that inspired the great founders of states and great legislators. He would like to be the creator of political orderings himself, and he wishes that there were men, in his own time and in the future, who harbored a profound passion to give origin to political orderings, astonishing in their order and beauty, capable of surviving through the ages. In order to conquer the misery of the human condition and in order to win immortality, Machiavelli has no need of God the creator or even of the God that puts an end to human history, endowing the good with eternal life. Even if the history of the world has neither beginning nor end—an eternal alternation between good and evil—those who are “true lovers” can find salvation by becoming gods.

Nonetheless, in Machiavelli’s intellectual and spiritual horizon, alongside or above the man who becomes a god there is also the God who constitutes the last hope of the derelict and the poverty-stricken. In the last chapter of The Prince, he writes that Italy “prays God to send her someone to redeem her from these barbarous cruelties and insults.”52 In the Florentine Histories, discussing the religious wars that roiled Africa during the reigns of Arcadius and Honorius, he observed: “Living thus, among so many persecutions, men bore the terror of their spirit written in their eyes, because, aside from the infinite evils they endured, for a good part of them the possibility of seeking refuge in God, in whom all the miserable are wont to hope, was lacking. Therefore, as the greater part of them were uncertain as to which God they ought to turn to, they died miserably, deprived of all help and all hope.”53

An image that was “too vivid and colorful to have been true,” as one critic wrote.54 Too colorful, and unnecessary for the purposes of the narrative. The source, in all likelihood Flavio Biondo, mentions religious conflict only in passing.55 Machiavelli, on the other hand, felt the need to write that in times of war and religious conflict, the unfortunate are denied even the last, scanty consolation of asking God for help, because they do not know to which God they should direct their prayers. For that matter, on his deathbed even Machiavelli had confessed his sins to the friar Andrea Alamanni, cousin of Luigi Alamanni, to whom Machiavelli’s Vita di Castruccio Castracani (The Life of Castruccio Castracani) was dedicated; and that friar always afterward “had only a world of good to say” concerning Machiavelli.56

In his private letters, Machiavelli speaks of God saving the innocent from the wickedness of men. On 26 June 1513, a few months after being released from prison, he confided a noteworthy thought to his nephew Giovanni Vernacci:

I have received several letters from you, most recently one from last April in which, among other things, you complain that you have not received any letters from me. My answer is that since your departure I have had so much trouble that it is no wonder I have not written to you. In fact, if anything, it is a miracle that I am alive, because my post was taken from me and I was about to lose my life, which God and my innocence have preserved for me. I have had to endure all sorts of other evils, both prison and other kinds. But, by the grace of God, I am well and I manage to live as I can—and so I shall strive to do, until the heavens show themselves to be more kind.57


Equally revealing are the words that Machiavelli’s son Ludovico wrote to him in his letter dated 22 May 1527: “God help me. I send you my regards as always. God keep you always from ill. Give my regards to Madonna Marietta and tell her to pray God for me; greet the entire family.” Would Ludovico ever have written those words to an atheist father? In his dramatic letter dated 17 May 1526, Machiavelli calls on God three times in order to persuade Guicciardini (and the pope) to wage a straightforward, open war against the horde of lansquenets that have crossed over the Alps: “For the love of God, let us not lose this opportunity”; “For the love of God, see to it now with such measures that His Holiness does not fall back into these same perils”; “Now God has brought things to such a pass that, if this moment is not lost, the pope is in time to take the emperor.” He again invokes God, twice, in his last letter, written to Vettori, and dated 18 April 1527: “And for the love of God, since this treaty cannot be made, if indeed you are unable to make it, break the negotiations off immediately”; “But whoever profits from war, as these soldiers do, would be crazy to extol peace. Yet God will grant them more war than we would like.”58

Perhaps they are only rhetorical statements; or perhaps Machiavelli, in his moments of despair and sadness, at times when he feels a need to confide the contents of his heart, in the midst of the tragedy sweeping over his fatherland, turns to God because he believes in a God who is the last hope of the just. I cannot quite imagine a Machiavelli who utters prayers out of obedience to the church and to tradition. But I can certainly imagine a Machiavelli who invokes God to lend aid to the fatherland, a Machiavelli who thanks God for having succored the fatherland in a time of danger. Who but God could help the fatherland when the efforts of humans prove futile? And if Machiavelli were to fail to thank him, he would be guilty of ingratitude, the sin that he detested above all others.

He speaks of Christ rarely. In letters to his family he sometimes writes “May Christ watch over you,” or “May Christ watch over you all as he should”; “May Christ watch over you all.”59 In the First Decennale he begins with a reference to Christ who came to earth and shed his blood in order to free men from sin: “One thousand and four hundred ninety-four… / Since the time Jesus visited our towns / And with the blood He shed extinguished all / The diabolical, most evil sparks.”60 Cesare Borgia, “as a rebel to Christ,” deserved his grim end.61 He certainly never mistakes Christ for the saints: “Make sure you’re good with Jesus, and thumb your nose at the saints,” he has Nicomaco say in the Clizia.62 In his most important political work, the Discourses on Livy, he acknowledges in an unambiguous manner Christ as the founder of the Christian religion and emphasizes that, had that religion remained faithful to the teachings of its founder, the republics of Christendom would have benefited greatly thereby.63 Christ, then, is not responsible for that religion of lazy idleness that so corrupted the world and led to the downfall of the republics.

Even the earthly glory that makes men immortal, while it may be a human creation, is not isolated from God. Machiavelli credits the “heavens,” or God, for giving men the opportunity to achieve immortality: “Heaven cannot give a man a greater gift than this or point him a more glorious way,” he writes to Giulio de’ Medici, exhorting him to restore a republican government in Florence. He adds that “amid all the happiness that God has given your house and your Holiness personally, this is the greatest: the gift of the power and the occasion to make yourself immortal.”64 And so God loves those men who garner great glory on earth; and immortality on earth, when it springs from true glory, paves the way to immortality in the heavens. The references to a God that comforts the afflicted, redeems the oppressed, brings salvation to the innocent, and encourages and acknowledges earthly glory might very well be mannered phrases or bits of rhetorical artifice. But they might equally well be signs that he believed in a God who was not all that different from the Christian God, even if God was not that important for him.


2. Republican Christianity


The concept of a man whose love of fatherland and glory makes him godlike, and who takes on a certain divine nature, is present in a venerable tradition of political and religious thought. Francesco Petrarca, one of Machiavelli’s favorite poets, emphasizes that nature itself instilled a thirst for glory in humanity. Earthly glory is a goal that men can pursue with a view to eternal salvation, and there was no reason to renounce the former in exchange for the latter.65 Other humanists, such as Coluccio Salutati, chancellor of the Florentine Republic from 1375 until his death in 1406, roundly condemned the love of worldly glory as unworthy of a Christian.66 The sole concern of a good Christian should be the true glory that God alone can provide. God operates through us, and all glory and honor should be attributed to Him.67 True glory springs from loving God and one’s neighbor, from faith translated into worthy works, and from purity of behavior and life. The other form of glory, which springs from power and the praise of other men, is vain, offers no true happiness, and leads directly to eternal damnation.68 A true Christian should pursue Christian glory, leaving pagan glory to the pagans. He should not run the risk that on Judgment Day God might say to him: for your works “you have already received your reward.”69 He should work on behalf of good without seeking or desiring earthly rewards.

The prevailing notion among Italian humanists was, nonetheless, that those who pursue virtue have a right to an earthly reward as well. Giovanni Conversano (or Conversino) of Ravenna (1343–1408), in a lengthy letter to Salutati, points out that the desire for glory moves men to acts of virtue and opens the path of happiness in the heavens. The apostle Paul himself wrote: “Do that which is good, and thou shalt have praise of the same” (“Fac bonum et habebis laudem ex eo,” Rom. 13:3) and the prophet said, “They go from strength to strength” (“ibunt de virtute in virtutem,” Psalm 83: 8). A thirst for glory has made great things possible in human history, and it has driven many men to perform good acts who do not love true virtue. The perfection of virtue is caritas, but the first step toward it can certainly be the love of glory.70 Poggio Bracciolini (1380–1459) too stated that glory is virtue’s reward and that the love of glory drives men to perform good deeds, though it must not endanger eternal salvation.71 While the pagans sought only worldly glory, and glory here on earth, Christians can pursue earthly glory as a preparation for eternal glory.

Far more determined to defend earthly glory was Flavio Biondo, the historian from Forlì who was one of Machiavelli’s sources for his Florentine Histories. Human renown and glory, he wrote, are in no way at odds with the religion of the Holy Fathers.72 A few years later, the Bolognese notary Sabbadino degli Arienti reiterated that the love of glory and the love of God can coexist perfectly well, and that one only helps the other. Every human being, and more than everyone else, princes and eminent citizens, want to win “worthy fame on earth” by “acting in a glorious manner,” and to live in heaven. The one in the dialogue who uttered these words was the Dominican Giambattista Spagnuoli. Even clearer was the conclusion of the “secular participants”: “This company concluded that men should endure every effort and trouble on earth in order to win honor, glory, repute, and renown, through whose excellence and blessed virtue in the end, by becoming worthy of heaven, they will enjoy eternal peace.”73

The Florentine humanists incorporated and reformulated the classical idea of glory as the good repute of good individuals that can be attained through public life.74 Fame, wrote Leon Battista Alberti (1404–72), for instance, in the Libri della famiglia (1432–34), “crowns those who attend to public affairs, not those who enjoy the leisure of private life.”75 The quest for glory, Matteo Palmieri (1406–75) adds, is the product of a natural desire found in all men to remain alive on earth through fame. Those who are concerned about the well-being of their soul, guide it toward heaven and try to leave “a memory of it behind for posterity.” Admiration for the virtues and the glory of noble deeds lives on over time. Let it come as a surprise to no one, then, Palmieri concludes, that those who performed great enterprises also seek glory.76 As these documents indicate, there was a strong Florentine tradition of thought that considered the quest for glory to be worthy of men, acceptable to God, and in keeping with the Christian goal of eternal life.

Another distinctive trait of the religious thinking of humanism was the idea of charity. In the classical Latin world the concept of caritas corresponded to the Greek term agàpe and defined—in contrast with amor—a feeling of affection and benevolence without any erotic connotations.77 The Roman classics offer a broad selection of examples of the distinction between caritas and amor.78 Closely related to the Latin caritas was dilectio, a word derived from the verb diligere, which described a reasoned affection, as distinct from the Latin verb amare. A further and particularly significant example (because it points up the ideal linkage between classical thought and Christian doctrine) is provided by Augustine, who considered dilectio as one of two possible ways to translate the Greek term agàpe—the other being, of course, caritas. He further stated that the Holy Scriptures make no distinction between love and charity (amor and caritas).79 Roman authors use the terms caritas or pittas to indicate love of fatherland, and they emphasize that this bond of affection extends to include parents, relatives, friends, and fellow citizens, and pushes us to perform acts of service (officium) and care (cultus).80

According to the Christian conception, charity is a form of love that does not expect to be returned. It should be extended to everyone, even to one’s (private) enemies; it is obligatory; it has no need of the presence of others; it may even demand the supreme sacrifice; it favors the needy, victims, and the weak. Eros in contrast is a love of enjoyment (a love that is returned or expects to be returned). It is selective, spontaneous, and demands the presence of the other; it can be extinguished through a change in worth or a lack of utility; it appeals to subjects that possess similar qualities. While love remains a passion of selfish desire that never breaks away from cupiditas, or concupiscence, charitable love falls at the far end of that scale, as a disinterested affection.81

For political thinkers of the Middle Ages and humanism—all of them solidly of the Christian faith—the common good was a divine good, and the love of the fatherland, although it was not identical to perfect charity, was nonetheless a form of charitable love. In his commentary on a passage from the Nicomachean Ethics (1094b) in which Aristotle writes that the good of the city is finer and more godlike (“melius vero et divinius”) than the good of the individual, Thomas Aquinas points out that the definition of the common good of the city and of all peoples as a finer and more godlike good is based on the similarity with God, inasmuch as God is a universal cause of all goods.82 His pupil Remigio de’ Girolami (1235–19), who was the prior of the church of Santa Maria Novella, writes in De bono pads that charity does not seek its own good, but rather places the common good before the good of the individual.83 In De bono communi he identifies charity as the foundation of civil life and emphasizes that love of the fatherland is a Christian duty in keeping with the right and natural inclination of man and with the love of God.84 Since the common good, as Aristotle points out in the Nicomachean Ethics, is the finest and most godlike good, whoever loves the common good is that much closer to God.

Another pupil of Thomas Aquinas, Tolomeo da Lucca, in his De regmine principum, reiterates that love of the fatherland is a charitable love of the common good which strengthens the soul, and is entirely in keeping with a love of God, and indeed is a necessary consequence of that love.85 In contrast with Augustine, Tolomeo sees no difference between the caritas patriae of the pagans and the caritas patriae of the Christians. He places charity on the highest level in the hierarchy of virtues and assigns the same rank to the love of the fatherland. In the most influential texts of medieval political thought, the love of the fatherland then takes on the significance of a charitable love of the common good that brings man closer to God and as such is entirely compatible with Christian doctrine.

After setting forth the nature of charity, Tolomeo emphasizes its political and moral effects: charity for the fatherland inspired the Romans to govern well (“ad bene regendum”) and for that reason, they deserved to become masters of the world. He also explained that, of the three virtues that give origin to good government—the love of the fatherland, a zeal for justice, and civil benevolence—charity for the fatherland was a sufficient justification for the conquest of world domination.

He adds a further specification that is deserving of close attention; he states that the love of the fatherland in a certain sense partakes of divine nature (“partecipabant quandam naturam divinam”): just as God is the universal cause of all things, likewise the love of the fatherland addresses the community and the good of the people. To document his view that charity of the fatherland has a divine character because it desires the common good, Tolomeo cites the classical episodes of Marcus Curtius, who leapt on horseback into the fissure; Attilius Regulus, who chose the safety of the fatherland over his own life; and Fabricius, who refused to be corrupted by gold. Those who truly love the fatherland and diligently pursue the common good become vicars of God, and partake of the divine nature (“in zelando rem communem assimilat sibi naturam divinam, in quantum vice Dei diligentem circa multitudinem adhibet curam”); they obey the commandment to love God and their neighbor with all their heart, all their soul, and all their strength. The religion of the Romans and the Christian religion teach that anyone who loves the common good and pursues it with all their might and all their diligence becomes godlike and makes themselves worthy of God’s friendship.

The concept of love of the fatherland as a love that makes man godlike is found again in Dante’s The Banquet (Convivio, IV, V, 13–14). For Dante, charity for the fatherland is a love that God infuses in certain men, making them divine:

If we consider, then, [Rome’s] Adolescence, when it was emancipated from the regal tutorship by Brutus, the first Consul, even to Caesar, its first supreme Prince, we shall find it exalted, not with human, but with Divine citizens, into whom, not human, but Divine love was inspired in loving Rome; and this neither could be nor ought to be, except for an especial end intended by God through such infusion of a heavenly spirit. And who will say that there was no Divine inspiration in Fabricius when he rejected an almost infinite amount of gold because he was unwilling to abandon his country? or in Curius, whom the Samnites attempted to corrupt, who said, when refusing a very large quantity of gold for love of his country, that the Roman citizens did not desire to possess gold, but the possessors of the gold? Who will say there was no Divine inspiration in Mutius burning his own hand because it had failed in the blow wherewith he had thought to deliver Rome? Who will say of Torquatus, who sentenced his own son to death from love for the Public Good, that he could have endured this without a Divine Helper?86


Florentine historians and politicians of the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries continued to speak of the love of fatherland as a charitable love that rendered man godlike and was acceptable to God. Coluccio Salutati emphasized repeatedly that a good Christian had a duty to serve his fatherland with all his power and explained that charity for the fatherland must be placed above and must subsume any and all other affections, bonds, and interests.87 Caritas is the foundation of patriotism and, therefore, is the basis of the ideal Christian citizen. In his younger years, Salutati writes about charity without theological implications or significance; during his years as chancellor of the republic, on the other hand, charity took on a marked religious significance. If we agree that all men have a natural inclination to live in society, it is therefore contrary to human nature for a man to do harm to other men or refuse to lend them aid. It is the duty of Christians to love their neighbor as themselves and even to love their enemies.88 That duty by no means excludes the fact that our strongest ties are to our family and to our compatriots. Christ Himself felt a love of fatherland, and his example teaches us that Christians have a greater obligation to the common good than do other men.

To Salutati, a Christian love of fatherland is even superior to the much celebrated love of the fatherland of the ancients. They were unfamiliar with the true charity (“vera caritas”) that springs from a love of Christ and for Christ and that commands us to love our neighbor as ourselves. Christ does not command the impossible. A society of true friends, which would have been impossible for the ancients, can instead exist among true Christians.89 Christian ethics were, in his view, a perfection of the Ciceronian ideal of civic virtue. Those who live in the world and meet their duties to their families and fatherlands live in a more Christian manner than the anchorites who seek salvation of the soul in solitude.90 The good citizen who serves his fatherland operates as divine Providence and with divine Providence—because it acts for the good of the universe, and desires the good of civil communities. The true Christian who serves the fatherland is therefore an instrument of Providence, and in this manner becomes an imago del: he is transformed from a man to a god.

The Christian religion is the path of virtue, and it does not contrast with, but rather contributes to, the vision of the world as a struggle, where man engages his determination to obtain good. Precisely because he is “religious, with a profound and austere faith, experienced wholeheartedly,” Salutati considered it his duty as a Christian and as a citizen to put first and foremost, in all cases, the struggle for liberty: “What should we not do for liberty? It alone, in our view, makes even war legitimate.”91 The religiosity of Salutati, and of others in early-fifteenth-century Florence, was one in which Cicero, Seneca, and the Gospels all speak the same moral language and the moral and political wisdom of the ancients takes on, in the light of the Christian faith, a new and more powerful splendor.92

When he attempts to become similar to his creator, man is obeying the divine will. Salutati begins with the premise that the purpose of man is not to know God, but rather to obtain eternal beatitude through good deeds performed on earth. A reliable and secure guide to doing good are the laws. Laws encourage use to live in accordance with virtue; they guide us toward earthly happiness and at the same time toward eternal beatitude.93 With good laws, legislators make political happiness (“politica felicitas”) possible here on earth, and that happiness progresses in an unbroken line toward eternal bliss.94 To obey the laws and serve the common good is a duty toward God; and the way in which a man acts in which God has infused in him the sentiment of caritas.95 Founders of states and legislators are not only beloved of God, but through their virtue they win His friendship: “Do we find it written about anyone else, aside from the lawgiver Moses, that they spoke to God face to face, as a man with his friend?”96

Likewise, for Leonardo Bruni, who served as the chancellor of the Florentine Republic between 1410 and 1411 and again from 1427 until his death in 1444, a good Christian should be a good citizen, ready to serve the fatherland. The word of Christ does not clash at all with ancient wisdom or with the love of the fatherland.97 Leon Battista Alberti, in turn, presents in the Libri della famiglia the care and love of the fatherland as a difficult and demanding duty that a good citizen should, nonetheless, accept in order to prevent the arrogant from dominating a republic and enslaving the others. He also explains that serving the public good, even when that good demands stern severity, is a “sacred deed” and acceptable to God.98

The same concepts are also found in that full-fledged compendium of civic humanism, Matteo Palmieri’s Vita civile. The passion that drove the heroes of republican Rome to sacrifice their lives for the liberty of the collective, the author explains, was their “piety of the fatherland” or “civil piety.” To take care of the health of the fatherland, preserve the city, and maintain the unity and concord of the “well assembled multitudes,” is the most meritorious of all human activities.99 Justice is the highest of all virtues, and it is so acceptable to “almighty God” that “throughout the corpus of Holy Scripture no distinction is made by God, calling the blessed just and the just blessed.”100 Not only the Holy Scriptures, but all laws, human and divine, and the common utility, demand a respect for justice. Those governing the republics who defend justice perform works “that are more acceptable to God than anything else that can be done on earth.”101 For that reason, the just rulers must receive in heaven eternal beatitude as a reward for their efforts.102 Men can become divine and eternal by means of a quest for perfection in all the arts and in all their works. In this quest, man attains his own true nature, lives in conformity with the will of God, and wins glory on earth.

The authentic Christian religion demands that each performs his or her task in life without shirking dangers or effort. Giannozzo Manetti, a man of profound faith, explains in his oration Delia dignità e dell’eccellenza dell’uomo (1451–52) that Socrates, Cato, and Cleombrotus “were led to hold life in contempt not by any motives stemming from human misfortunes but rather only by their hope of immortality” He emphasizes that “to flee what is toilsome is not the mark of the brave and magnanimous but rather of the effeminate and weak.” We cannot “leave the watch of life without the order of the commander-in-chief, that is God.” Not wisdom alone, but also faith commands us to take on with virtue the efforts, struggles, and torments that trouble the terrestrial city. Because he is profoundly Christian, Manetti exalts the virtue that allows men to act with strength and magnanimity in civil life, that virtue that is beloved body and soul, and that makes men who practice it godlike: “Love virtue with your whole mind and soul; give your heart to it, embrace it; it is by practicing it without remission or neglect that you will prosper and know peace—nay, you will be made like to the immortal God.”103

All the same, it was Lorenzo Valla (1405–57) who first established a solid philological foundation for the idea of Christianity as a religion of virtue—understood, in the manner of the ancients, as strength. He too began from the basis that the underlying principle of Christianity is charity, which he defined as “love for God and for one’s neighbor” (“amoris in Deum et proximum”). Charity is the mistress of all the virtues (“magistrae omnium virtutum”) and gives men strength.104 Strength, Valla explains, is a kind of affection. Isn’t a person who truly loves especially strong in his resistance against those who try to deprive him of the person he loves? When the apostles received the Holy Spirit, which is the charity of the Father and the Son, they became strong in spreading the word of God. The idea of virtue in the sense of power and strength, not of honesty, can be found—according to Valla—in the Gospels themselves.105 Not only did Valla interpret virtue as strength in the face of danger and suffering, but he also understood this idea of virtue as the true Christian virtue. If charity gives strength, and strength is virtue, it necessarily follows that Christianity cannot be the religion of weakness and humility, but must be the religion of virtue.106 The belief that the true Christianity is Christianity interpreted in terms of virtue, a thesis that Machiavelli sets forth and defends in a fundamental passage of Discourses on Livy, is therefore already present in the work of one of the most authoritative thinkers of Humanism.

Christian religion and political virtue went hand in hand for Bartolomeo Sacchi (or Platina) as well. He was a fervent support of the Christian religion as the moral foundation of republics. He first of all reiterated the classical idea that virtue, in particular, the virtue of those guiding the republic, made men godlike and opened the path to true glory. The people, he explained, love and worship as demigods men who excel in virtue. A good citizen who leads a city must bear a terrible burden. The reward that awaits him is the true and perfect glory (“vera ac integra gloria”) that God provides.107

Similarly, Aurelio Brandolini (1449–1497), a pupil and protégé of Platina at the court of Pope Sixtus IV, developed the theme of charity as a fundamental concept of the Christian religion. The virtue that Christ taught above all others was that of charity, which demands that we forgive those who offend us and that we love our enemies.108 It was out of charity toward humanity that Christ sacrificed Himself, and before drawing His last breath, he said: “Father, forgive them; for they know not what they do.” Charity does not accept being divided, and therefore one cannot love God without loving men. Charity has no restrictions based on difference of languages or customs, and it encourages us to treat other men as if we were all part of the same heaven, the same world, and the same city.109 Like other humanists, Brandolini cites the Romans as well as Christ as examples of charity, and thus allows his readers to understand that there was no contrast between Roman morality and Christian morality.110 Both the former and the latter teach that if we wish to become godlike (“Deo proximi ac simillimi”), we must pursue charity with all our strength.

The theme of charity is a fundamental principle of living as a true Christian, more than any acts of penitence, vigils, or flagellations; we find it as well in sermons delivered in the presence of the pope in solemn masses held in the Vatican. Even Brandolini in his Christiana paradoxa, and in a sermon on the Passion of Christ, delivered on 1 April 1496, insisted on the duty of a Christian to practice toward others the same charity that God gave to all men. Giovanni Antonio Campano, to cite another example, explained in a sermon on the Holy Spirit published in 1495 that when man practices charity, he obeys a divine impulse at work within him.111

The orators summoned to deliver sermons in the Vatican often praised a commitment to the public good and emphasized the affinity of that impulse to Christian charity. Domenico de’ Domenichi, in the Oratio pro pace Italiae that he delivered on Ascension Day in 1468, stated that if men who do good to other men deserve praise and honor, much greater praise is deserved by those who serve the public good, because man was not born for himself alone, but also for the fatherland.112

Marsilio Ficino too stated that religion “is a gift and a virtue belonging to one and all,” and that religion teaches civil virtues and commands a love of the fatherland.113 Marsilio Ficino dedicated Della religione to Bernardo del Nero, “an ardent lover of our fatherland” and a “most diligent observer of civil virtue as philosophy and religion command.” This virtue, he further clarifies, “according to our Plato, consists of four elements, that the citizen should be prudent in discerning in the city, with a view to preserving the common good, both matters present and foreseeing matters future, and that he should employ justice in distributing to each according to their deserts and merits. Strong in winning over fear that prevents one from accomplishing virtuous deeds. And temperate in his appetites.”114 The Christian religion commands that we exercise “virtues ardently, and not toward ends of human ambition or pleasure or rest: but only toward the ends of God and in order to attain the world beyond; he scorns all in this world.”115 An example of the divine idea of virtues was Christ, who “showed us the true justice that owns nothing of its own and renders unto God what belongs to God and to men what belongs to men, the magnanimity that desires nothing on earth as great and holds nothing as difficult, singular temperance and mildness, with which it wards off pride, pestilence of humanity”

Ficino’s ideal of a religion entirely bound up in caritas, which in the name of caritas commands its followers to love and serve the fatherland, was also widespread among the secular confraternities at the end of the fifteenth century. One demonstration of this can be found in the orations of Giovanni Nesi, one of the most typical representatives of a “popular” Platonism, with marked republican aspirations.116 In the Oratio de caritate that he delivered on 25 February 1478, Nesi emphasized that “deus caritas est et caritas est deus” (‘God is charity and charity is God’) and that charity was the reason that “God descended to earth from heaven, that God became a man, from immortal became a mortal, from lord to servant,” just as charity alone “lifted man from earth to heaven, and where he was mortal before endowed him with perpetual life; and where he was a man, made him a god.”117 Charity renders us “similar to our creator,” to the degree that human nature can be approximated to divine nature; it commands us to love our neighbor and it gives us strength with which to obey its commands: “It braves all dangers, it subjects itself to all efforts, it spares no ordeal, it shirks no difficulties.” It is thanks to charity that cities enjoy peace and unity. It deserves, therefore, to be called the “mother of all virtues” and “the form of all virtues,” and as such it deserves to be honored and pursued with all our strength.118

A religion based on charity prevents its adherents from seeking salvation in contemplation and solitude, and demands instead that they serve the public good wisely and courageously. In the words of Nesi, who served on many occasions in the highest magistracies of Florence, religious faith was entirely bound up with the ideals of the civic humanism that Salutati, Bruni, and Palmieri had developed at the turn of the century. That the Christian religion, if rightly interpreted, demands that we serve the fatherland is something that Nesi stated, as well, in his Oratio de humilitate, which he delivered to the “Reverend Fathers and beloved Brothers” of the confraternity of the Nativity on 11 April 1476: “The third contemplation [that virtue inspires in us] is when man considers not merely his own utility, but also that of the fatherland, of his relatives, and of all men that have ever been born, as not only Plato, Aristotle, and Cicero write, but also as the wise Lord commands us on more than one occasion.”119 God not only gave man the possibility of elevating himself with the strength and force of his soul to the level of the divine or to depths of brutality, writes Giovanni Pico della Mirandola in his oration De dignitate hominis (Oration on the Dignity of Man, 1486), the “manifesto of the Renaissance.” It is his duty to elevate himself through virtue until he is imitating his Creator.120

In the years in which Machiavelli was preparing to enter political life, Savonarola was exhorting the Florentines to cultivate a religion of virtue.121 In his third Predica sopra Aggeo (Sermon On Haggai), for instance, he explained that charity “makes man powerful and vigorous to undertake great deeds out of love of God,” and in order to magnify the power of charity he repeated almost to the letter the words of Saint Paul: “Pay no mind to persecutions, nor to dishonors, nor to scourges, fear not death; charity fears nothing; charity is stronger than death; charity separates man from all the things of the world more than does death; death takes the things of the world from you by force; charity takes them away from you through your own will, nor could the entire world triumph over charity”122 Men, he never tired of repeating, must do good works “to honor God and also out of zeal toward the fatherland and for the common good,” and he even went so far as to say that “if you have no charity you are not a true Christian.”123

Love of God is a love of friendship and therefore the citizens of Florence must serve the common good and the fatherland, if they wish to enjoy the friendship of God. But a love of the common good is not a love of concupiscence, or lust and greed: “The love of lust is what deceives self-respect, and when you desire and wish to have what is held in common in particular and you would like to have it for yourself. This self-respect is dislodged when anyone can have what is the common possession of everyone and you are contented that everyone should have their part, and in this way every good citizen is obliged to love the common good.”124 Charity brings together the people and the church, just as mortar “bonds and joins stones together,” and if it is to be true charity it must be free of self-esteem. For that reason, Savonarola emphasized with specific polemical intent, there was no charity among the ancient Romans, because, “although they might have done outstanding things, they did all those things for their own reputation and out of self-esteem.”125

Another important principle of the religion of virtue is the idea that God loves civil government above all others because it is the one most in keeping with liberty and the best suited to encourage Christian living. “But, through the prayers of the good men and women who inhabit the city, the council and civil government,” he writes, “framed [in Florence] not by men but by God himself, have been the instruments of divine power in maintaining its liberty.”126 The love of the common good, along with the fear of God, “natural charity,” and a rigorous respect for justice, makes cities great and multiplies the “spiritual and temporal goods” of the citizens.127 Like the political writers of humanism, Savonarola points to the example of the ancient Romans who “greatly cherished the common welfare of the city.” He also reiterated the fact that magistrates who govern on behalf of the common good of the city will win from God both glory and a great reward in eternal life.128 Last of all, and it is a point that deserves our close attention, Savonarola reiterated that God loves especially those who govern for the common good because He sees them as similar to Him:

Like loves like, and the more it is loved by it, the more it becomes assimilated to it; since, then, creatures are similar to God, all are loved by Him, but because some are more like Him than others, these are also loved by Him more. Insamuch then as the one who governs is much more similar to God than the one who is governed, it logically follows that if he governs justly, he is loved and rewarded by God more than for the personal actions [he performs] when he is not governing, especially since the one who governs is in greater danger and expends greater efforts of mind and body than the one who does not govern; therefore he merits greater reward.129


This republican Christianity began to collapse at the beginning of the 1520s, when the Medici regime was well established in Florence. One piece of evidence to this effect is the Dialogo del reggimento di Firenze (Dialogue on the Government of Florence) that Francesco Guicciardini wrote in 1521, but kept unpublished in his desk drawer for the rest of his life. In contrast with the common view that to serve the fatherland was the one true way of complying with the will of God and winning His friendship, Guicciardini explained that those who truly wish to serve the fatherland must be ready and willing to abandon God and break his commands. After mentioning Gino Capponi’s statement that rulers must love their fatherland more than their soul, a genuine synthesis of Florentine civil religion, Guicciardini has Bernardo del Nero say that it is impossible to live with a Christian conscience and also work for the good of the fatherland:

How can one, according to conscience, wage war from a lust to expand one’s territory, in which one commits so many killings, so many sackings, so many violations of women, so many burnings of houses and churches, and an infinite number of other evils? Nevertheless, if someone stood up in a senate and argued, for this reason and for this reason alone, against undertaking some entirely feasible and useful enterprise, he would be repudiated by everyone. Let’s take this further. How could you, according to conscience, accept a war in order to defend even the lands you already possess?… If one thinks about it carefully, all states are illegitimate; and excepting republics, inside their own city walls, and not beyond them, there is no power whatsoever that is legitimate, even less that of the Emperor, who enjoys such authority that he administers justice to others. Nor do I exempt priests from this rule, for their rule is doubly illegitimate, since to keep us under they use spiritual weapons as well as temporal ones.130


Guicciardini drew from these considerations the conclusion that one had to live either according to the reason of states or else according to the law of God:

You see the position to which someone who wanted to govern states strictly according to conscience would be reduced. Therefore when I talked of murdering or keeping the Pisans imprisoned, I didn’t perhaps talk as a Christian: I talked according to the reason and practice of states. Nor will anyone be more of a Christian who rejects such cruelty but recommends doing everything possible to take Pisa, since this means in effect being the cause of infinite evils to occupy something that doesn’t according to conscience belong to you…. I wanted to say this not to pronounce a verdict on these difficulties, which are immense, since anyone who wants to live totally according to God’s will can ill afford not to remove himself totally from the affairs of this world, and it is difficult to live in the world without offending God.131


Guicciardini’s criticism does not apply to the principle that it is in keeping with a Christian way of life to love and to defend one’s fatherland; it applies rather to unjust domains and the wars unleashed to conquer them, as was the case with the wars that Florence had waged to retake Pisa, a possession that Florence had lost in 1494. The Florentine religion of the fatherland, in any case, justified even unjust wars. The prophet of that religion, Girolamo Savonarola (1452–98), had proclaimed this in unequivocal words: “Pisa is yours and you shall have her back, because God wants Pisa to be yours and for you to hold her, and you have rightful title to her and may take her without troubling your conscience; and if he who holds her will not yield her out of love, he will be made to do so by force and will be obliged to be your friend. Trust to God to do it, He knows well how he will do.”132

While Savonarola preached countless times that God is a friend to the just, Guicciardini pointed out that this faith in divine aid has absolutely no basis, even if it can give us strength.133 Guicciardini calls into doubt not only the idea that God helps the just, but that He involves Himself in any way in the affairs of men with rewards or chastisements. The justice of God exists, but it is not the portion of men to understand divine judgments.134

As the republican experiment was coming to an end, the criticisms of republican Christianity inspired by Savonarola became increasingly harsh, as is shown by the dialogue between Francesco Capponi and Piero Vettori composed by Francesco Guicciardini’s brother Luigi Guicciardini in 1530. In this dialogue, Francesco Capponi reiterated the traditional components of republican Christianity, first and foremost the conviction that in the presence of extreme danger, Florence will not be left without divine aid, because its liberty is “welcome to the one and Almighty LORD GOD.” He then went on to emphasize that in order to defend liberty, it was necessary “to put up happily with all forms of harm, and anyone who believes otherwise, can neither be a good citizen, nor a true Christian.” In response to these words from Francesco Capponi, Piero Vettori shot back that, concerning events placed “within the will of the almighty GOD, we have no way of knowing whether they will occur or not.” Experience shows that hopes of divine aid have frequently been disappointed in the past. True liberty unquestionably deserves to be defended, Vettori added, but the liberty proclaimed by the republican government founded in 1527 was only a simulacrum, designed to conceal a tyrannical power.135

In Machiavelli’s historical context we can discern, then, a long tradition of thinkers who believe in a God that rewards those men who become godlike through charity and virtue and who loves free republics because, better than any other forms of government, they achieve justice and the common good; a tradition that considers the love of true glory entirely in keeping with the teachings of Christ, and sees the love of the fatherland as the duty of a good Christian. Also present in the same intellectual context, however, are thinkers who focus on the contrast between living in a Christian manner and loving one’s native city more than one’s own soul, and who were convinced that it is a vain illusion to believe that God is ready to rescue republics.


3. Machiavelli and the Religion of Virtue


Machiavelli’s God is the God of Florentine republican Christianity. Even if he was unfamiliar with many of the texts that I have cited, Machiavelli accepted the humanistic ideal of man who became godlike through virtue and obtained God’s friendship. Machiavelli’s true hero was Moses.136 God speaks with him as His friend: “And the LORD spake unto Moses face to face, as a man speaketh unto his friend” (Exod. 33:11). With clear reference to this passage from Exodus, Machiavelli placed Moses foremost among the “rare and marvelous” men to whom God was a friend. God gave His friendship to Moses as a grace and a favor, and it was in virtue of that grace received that Moses was able to speak with God: “And although one should not reason about Moses, as he was a mere executor of things that had been ordered for him by God, nonetheless he should be admired if only for that grace which made him deserving of speaking with God.”137

Before God chose him as His friend, Moses had shown his charity and his strength when he “spied an Egyptian smiting an Hebrew, one of his brethren” and killed him, and when he helped the daughters of the priest of Midian when the shepherds came and drove them away. Machiavelli is clearly thinking about these episodes from Exodus when he writes that God will be a friend to anyone who feels compassion for Italy, which is “more enslaved than the Hebrews, more servile than the Persians, more dispersed than the Athenians” and is willing to help to redeem her, just as God was a friend to Moses when he became a redeemer of his people. God, then, gives His friendship to those who are similar to him, because he detests injustice and cruelty, and is capable of loving in accordance with charity.

Machiavelli interpreted the friendship of God in the context of republican Christianity. He explained, first of all, that the friendship of God ensured the success of an undertaking. The Jews reached the promised land where milk and honey gurgled like brooks. From slaves, the Jews became a people. They were obliged to stand on their own two legs, to learn to defend themselves, and to take on full responsibility. God was unwilling to do everything on their behalf, but His aid was decisive in assuring that, under the leadership of Moses, they would attain redemption. Machiavelli insists on the same concept when he talks about the redemption of Italy: the friendship of God allowed the Jews to overcome obstacles that might otherwise have defeated them: “The sea has opened; a cloud has escorted you along the way; the stone has poured forth water; here manna has rained.” The most glorious deed of emancipation, as described in Exodus, was carried out by Moses, the redeemer who—more than any other—had the friendship of God.

In exceptional cases, the Christian God is willing to admit into the ranks of the blessed souls even rulers of pagan states. In his Allocuzione fatta ad un magistrato (Allocution or Address to a Magistrate), Machiavelli narrates the legend according to which God, at the intercession of Saint Gregory, placed the Roman emperor Trajan on the list of “his chosen souls,” even though he was a pagan, in order to reward him for his extraordinary justice and his political virtues.138 In the Exhortation to Penitence (Esortazione alia penitenza), he wrote—this time in reference to David and the apostle Peter—that God “honored them among the highest of those chosen in heaven.” Peter was an apostle, but David, like Trajan, was above all a political hero, “a man very excellent in arms, in learning, in judgment,” who slew the giant Goliath with his weapons, and taught with his example the principle on which Machiavelli insisted for his entire life, that is, that in order to preserve liberty, neither mercenary arms nor auxiliary arms are sufficient, but only one’s own arms.139 Even “fair souls,” which are a good and humble example, can rise “direct to heaven,” Machiavelli assures us in his play Clizia.140

Like the God of the humanists, Machiavelli’s God loves justice and peace and hates pride and arrogance. While he was on a mission to Pisa, Machiavelli wrote on the back of a letter that the Ten sent him, dated 28 May 1512, these verses about San Torpè or Torpezio (Saint Torpes): “Silence. You now hear and shall hear more / of how God views with displeasure all acts of overweening pride; / you will see the heavens destroy all that / Nero accomplished through pride and glory; / aside from this you will see Saint Torpes / go willingly and swiftly to his martyrdom / and, after many miracles and celestial signs, rejoice, / rejoice in bliss in heaven’s kingdom.”141 But Machiavelli knew well that in order to serve the fatherland, to found states, and to redeem peoples it is often necessary to act “contrary to religion,” break one’s word, perpetrate cruelty, lie and dissemble.142 His answer to this problem is that God forgives and gives His friendship even to those founders of states, redeemers of peoples, and rulers who are obliged to be bad in order to achieve their goals. There is consequently no need to choose between God and the world, as Guicciardini had claimed, since God himself wants us to work in the world in order to achieve his plans, even at the cost of committing evil.

Evidence of this truth can be found in the Bible. Moses had God as his “teacher,” he was “deserving of speaking with God,” and he was “a mere executor of things that had been ordered for him by God.” And yet, Machiavelli comments in Discourses on Livy, in order to impose on the people of Israel a respect for the laws that God had entrusted to him and for his political orderings, he was forced “to kill infinite men who, moved by nothing other than envy, were opposed to his plans.”143 Machiavelli was referring to the passage of Exodus (32:27–28) in which we read that Moses punished the Israelites who had set up a golden calf as their God, and issued the terrible commandment: “Thus saith the LORD God of Israel, Put every man his sword by his side, and go in and out from gate to gate throughout the camp, and slay every man his brother, and every man his companion, and every man his neighbour.” The children of Levi, according to the biblical account, “did according to the word of Moses: and there fell of the people that day about three thousand men.”

Machiavelli notes the cruelty of that summary and indiscriminate punishment. Probably he also noted the cunning ploy used by Moses. Moses said: “Thus saith the LORD God of Israel.” But God did not impart that commandment to Moses, or at least there is no trace of such a commandment in the biblical text. Moses’s behavior is a perfect example of a cruelty used well. “Those who observe the first mode [cruelties well used] can have some remedy for their state with God and with men.”144 To have some remedy with God means that God will be able to excuse them and welcome them into the heavenly community, just as He welcomed Moses, who was cruel and who feigned the truth. In order to resolve this conflict between two fundamental principles of republican Christianity—one principle affirming the duty to serve the fatherland, the other principle emphasizing respect for justice—Machiavelli chooses to provide a new definition, with the help of the Bible, of the idea of God.145

Aside from the redeemers of peoples and founders of states whom God chose as his friends, other commanders and princes appear similar to God in the eyes of men because of their remarkable qualities. Hannibal, as a result of his “inhuman cruelty,” managed to hold together and discipline a vast army, “made up of countless nations,” and was “venerable and awe-inspiring” to his soldiers. Likewise, Moses, Cyrus, Theseus, and Romulus, who succeeded in overcoming great dangers with their virtues, were “venerated,” as well. To venerate is to recognize the superiority of a person and at the same time feel admiration for them, be fascinated by them. Victors are venerated, but in particular God is venerated, with His “the greatness, and the power, and the glory, and the victory,” as the Bible states (“magnificentia, et potentia, et gloria, atque victoria,” 1 Chron. 29:11). Another quality proper to God is that of being terrible. Terrible is the quality of striking terror because one can perform vast and unprecedented acts. Moses calls God, “glorious in holiness, fearful in praises, doing wonders” (“terribilis atque laudabilis et faciens mirabilia”—Exod. 15:11). Terrible, or fearful, because he does things that no one has ever seen before: “before all thy people I will do marvels, such as have not been done in all the earth, nor in any nation” (“signa faciam quae nunquam visa sunt super terram, nee in ullius gentibus”—Exod. 34:10). Terrible in his might and grandeur (“Deus magnus et terribilis”; “a mighty God and terribile”—Deut. 7:21).

The more that a prince is able to fascinate and strike terror, the more his power will be like that of God, and the more he himself will appear godlike. Beneath God, and beneath the full-fledged friends of God, Machiavelli placed a group of men who appear to have divine qualities but have not won the friendship of God. Those who obtain the friendship of God and eternal glory are not men like Hannibal and Cesare Borgia, but men like Moses, Scipio, Cyrus, Theseus, and Romulus. It is God who extends His friendship to those men who become similar to Him through the exercise of charity, and it is God who commands us to love the fatherland and to live according to virtue. If men “considered how [our religion] permits us the exaltation and defense of the fatherland, they would see that it wishes us to love and honor it and to prepare ourselves to be such that we can defend it,” he writes in Discourses on Livy (II.2). God cannot wish the impossible. He must therefore be willing to forgive those who are forced to commit evil in order to defend the fatherland, that is, if He does not wish those great men whom He loves above all others to fail in their undertakings and for the weak to be oppressed as a result. As a product of his idea of God, Machiavelli succeeds in resolving the conflict between Christian religion and love of the fatherland, thus keeping alive the religion of virtue that was such an essential part of the Florentine republican experience.

The intellectual bond with Florentine republican Christianity, and the intention to defend and reinforce it, also emerges in his interpretation of glory. Machiavelli explicitly states that a love of glory is the worthiest of all human passions. If they were wise, he writes, men would not allow themselves to be deceived “by a false good and a false glory,” but would instead follow the way that “after death renders them glorious”; they would likewise eschew the way that leads to a “sempiternal infamy.”146 He makes a distinction between fame and glory, and includes goodness as a necessary prerequisite for true glory: “Pompey and Caesar and almost all the Roman generals after the last Carthaginian war gained fame as brave men but not as good ones, while those who lived before them gained fame as brave and good.”147 He praises the pagan religion because it “did not beatify men if they were not full of worldly glory,” as were “captains of armies and princes of republics” and he criticizes the Christian religion because it “has glorified humble and contemplative more than active men.”148

This criticism does not concern the Christian religion at all. The Christian God wants men to pursue the glory that is achieved through great undertakings. God, writes Machiavelli, helps the founders of states and redeemers, but He does not want “to do everything” lest He deprive men of “part of the glory” that is due to them. A part of the glory that men achieve when they carry out great works of redemption, however, is always due to God. It is God who inspires them and aids them, not only because He gives them “opportunities for glory,” but also the “power” to achieve immortality through Glory.149 When men work for true glory, they do something that is more acceptable to the Christian God than anything else.

Machiavelli borrows this topos—quite diffuse, as I have pointed out, in Florentine republican Christianity—in various passages of his writing.150 In the Discursus florentinarum rerum he observes that men who have “reformed republics and kingdoms with laws and institutions” are, after “those who have become gods,” the first to be praised. As to exactly which men “have become gods,” Machiavelli clarifies the point in the chapter of Discourses on Livy in which he defines his own particular hierarchy of glory: Among all men praised, the most praised are those who have been heads and orderers of religions. Next then, are those who have founded either republics or kingdoms.” The “Gods” in the narrowest sense are the chiefs of religions. The founders and reformers of republics and kingdoms are very similar to them, and close to them in terms of glory. Whatever the degree of resemblance and proximity in the hierarchy of glory, there are men that become “gods” and other men who become very similar to “Gods.” One of these was Scipio Africanus whom Machiavelli—following in the footsteps of Dante, who had referred to him in these terms in The Banquet—explicitly described as “a divine man” sent “by heaven.”151 He was a “divine man” because he constantly offered examples of “virtue, strength, integrity at once” and was able on numerous occasions to save his fatherland from drastic ruin. In Machiavelli’s view, Scipio is an unparalleled example: “Among the dead and those who’re still around, / Among the ancient and the modern nations, / Another Scipio cannot be found.” Those who love their fatherland and act on its behalf become godlike, and God honors them with His friendship. Machiavelli criticizes the scorn for worldly glory that is present in the Christian tradition, preferring to it another interpretation that he saw as more genuinely Christian, which existed in the Florentine historical context.

Machiavelli’s ties with the tradition of Florentine Christianity, finally, emerges quite clearly when he speaks of charity and the love of the fatherland. In his Exhortation to Penitence he cites St. Paul’s first Letter to the Corinthians: “Those who are ungrateful to God—it is impossible that they are not unfriendly to their neighbors. Those are unfriendly to their neighbors who are without charity. This, my Fathers and Brothers, is the only thing that takes our souls to Heaven; this is the only thing that has more worth than all the other virtues of men; this is that of which the Church says at such length that he who does not have charity does not have anything.” The faith of Christ is founded on charity, and it is from charity that we derive the principles of civil ethics. Anyone who lacks charity “must necessarily be unfriendly to his neighbor: he does not aid him, he does not endure his faults, he does not console him in tribulation, he does not teach the ignorant, he does not advise him who errs, he does not help the good, he does not punish the evil.” A man cannot be full of charity, he emphasizes, “who is not full of religion.”152

Machiavelli composed the Exhortation for some members of a secular or religious confraternity. But when he writes that those who lack charity cannot be a friend to their neighbor, and therefore cannot be a good Christian and a good citizen, he is reiterating concepts that are entirely his own. In The Art of War he writes that those who show contempt for God cannot show reverence for men and that one must “oblige the citizens to love one another.”153 In his Dell’ingratitudine (Tercets on Ingratitude or Envy), he contrasts, as he does in the Exhortation, charity with ingratitude, which is an enemy to the “life of good service” of the “good citizen.”154

Charity is, for Machiavelli, as for all the political writers of Christian and classical inspiration, the heart of the love of the fatherland. In the Florentine Histories, for instance, he speaks of citizens who took action to exhort the Signoria to annul “the orders that nourish sects” and to establish new ones that are in keeping with “a free and civil life,” and in his description of the passion that drove them to work for the common good, he emphasizes that they were not moved by “any private passion,” but rather by their “charity for our fatherland.”155 The concept of charity of the fatherland appears as well in the chapter of Discourses on Livy entitled “That a Good Citizen Ought to Forget Private Injuries for Love of His Fatherland.” The senate, Machiavelli recounts, sent two ambassadors to the consul Fabius to ask him, after setting “private hatreds aside,” to nominate “for the public benefit” Papirius Cursor. Fabius, “moved by charity for his fatherland,” accepted the senate’s request, “even though by being silent and in many other modes he gave sign that such a nomination grieved him.” From the behavior of Fabius, Machiavelli comments, “all those who seek to be held good citizens ought to take example.”156 Comment and narrative together show Machiavelli’s intent to exalt the charity of the fatherland, since Livy never uses that expression at all, and limits himself to writing that the emissaries of the senate were sent to persuade Quintus Fabius to “lay aside all feeling of enmity for the sake of his country” (“patriae remitteret”), which the consul showed by his silence had cost him great pain: “Showing by his conduct what a painful effort it had cost him” (“insignem dolorem ingenti comprimi animo”).157

The most eloquent and exhaustive analysis of love of the fatherland as a passion that drives citizens to set aside their private and particular interests in order to serve the public good, nonetheless, is found in the chapter of Discourses on Livy in which Machiavelli describes the death of Manlius Capitolinus. Guilty of having incited tumults “against the Senate and against the laws of the fatherland,” Manlius was brought to trial before the people. Although the people of Rome were “very desirous of its own utility and a lover of things that went against the nobility,” that same people, “from defender having become judge, without any respect condemned him to death.” Manlius was a citizen “full of every virtue, who publicly and privately had performed very many praiseworthy works.” To sentence him to death for his acts of sedition against the Senate and against the laws of the republic was a decision that demanded wisdom and courage. The people of Rome, Machiavelli emphasized, found both wisdom and courage because “love of the fatherland was able to do more in all of them than any other respect.”158 Once again, Machiavelli attributes to the Roman people a love of the fatherland as a principal cause of its wisdom and courage. Livy never speaks of it. In his account, he mentions the tribunes of the plebs who count on the people’s hatred for the monarchy and their love of liberty, and points out that Manlius’s “loathesome hankering after kingly power” made the people forget his merits, and even manage to render those merits detestable.159

Though he was no saint, Machiavelli always served the fatherland with all his strength, and with absolute honesty he consoled, aided, taught, and advised. He wrote all his great political works to teach, and in those books he explained how to found and preserve political orderings that really could help the good and punish the wicked. During the saddest days of his life, when he learned from Francesco Vettori that there was no hope of his obtaining a position in Rome or in the new Medici regime in Florence, he considered going to “some deserted spot to teach reading to children.”160 When he was nearing the end of his life, at a time when he had to think day and night about how to halt the onrushing horde of lansquenets descending toward Florence or Rome, he still managed to console his son Guido, afflicted with the fear that he might lose his young mule, with words that only a man capable of true charity could devise.161

Like the writers and poets of humanism, Machiavelli knew very well that love of the fatherland, inasmuch as it is a form of charity, is different from erotic love, of which he was a refined theorist and a connoisseur.162 Erotic love, like love for the great affairs of politics, is a passion that envelops, overwhelms, transforms, and allows one to forget: “I have renounced, then, thoughts about matters great and grave. No longer do I delight in reading about the deeds of the ancients or in discussing those of the moderns: everything has been transformed into tender thoughts, for which I thank Venus and all of Cyprus.”163 From the first poems that he composed, in all likelihood in the years 1494–98, Machiavelli emphasized the tremendous power of love to inflict suffering, fear, and weeping:

I hope, and oh, to hope is to ache more:

I weep, and my tears feed my weary heart:

I laugh, but there’s no laugh I now can store:

I burn, but no one sees my flaming smart:

All that I hear and see I most deplore,

And everything can a new grief impart:

Thus hoping, I do burn and laugh and cry,

And am afraid of both my ear and eye.164


Machiavelli liked to abandon himself to thoughts of love. We have evidence of this in his letter dated 10 December 1513. When his friend dangled images of lightsome and easy loves before his eyes, Machiavelli responded that he reads about the loves of Dante, Petrarch, Tibullus, and Ovid, and that as he reads the works of these poets, he recalls his own loves, and lingers at length in those memories, allowing himself to be swallowed up by the past: “Upon leaving the woods, I go to a spring; from there, to one of the places where I hang my birdnets. I have a book under my arm Dante, Petrarch, or one of the minor poets like Tibullus, Ovid, or some such. I read about their amorous passions and their loves, remember my own, and these reflections make me happy for a while.”165 When he is suffering from the harshness of men’s ingratitude and squalor, he reveals to Vettori that his feelings about love are similar to those of those poets.

In another exchange of letters with Vettori, Machiavelli returned to the subject of the irresistible power and sweetness of erotic love. As was the case in late 1513, it was Vettori who brought up the subject with an explicit praise of the delights of carnal love. After describing once again the languor and boredom of his days in Rome, he reveals the way that he wards off melancholy: “Of necessity one must endeavor to think of pleasant things, but I know of nothing that gives more delight to think about and to do than fucking. Every man may philosophize all he wants, but this is the utter truth, which many people understand this way but few will say.”166 Once again, in the letter of 10 December, Machiavelli responds to his friend with the love of the poets, and becomes a poet himself, imitating Petrarch:

The youthful archer many times had tried

To wound me in the breast with his arrows;

He takes his pleasure thus—spite for all

And harm to everyone is his delight.


Though no diamond exists that might withstand

His arrowheads brutal and keen,

Yet now they’ve struck an obstacle so strong

It took little account of their power.


So, full of rage and anger, in order

To demonstrate his consummate skill,

He made a change of quiver, bow, and shaft;


With such great force he let one fly,

That I feel its painful wound still; thus I

Confess and recognize his power.167


A few months earlier, in order to describe the power of love, he had used the metaphor of golden nets.168 The beauty of the beloved makes the chains gentle, and for no reason would the lover wish to free himself: “And even though I may now seem to have entered into great travail, I nevertheless feel so great a sweetness in it, both because of the delight that rare and gentle countenance brings me and because I have laid aside all memory of my sorrows, that not for anything in the world would I desire my freedom—even if I could have it.”169

In the face of the power of love, one can do nothing but surrender. “As for your love affair,” he writes to Vettori in the summer of 1514, “let me remind you that Love tortures only those who attempt to clip his wings or to fetter him whenever he flies into their laps. Because he is a young, fickle boy, he gouges out the eyes, livers, and hearts of such people. But those who rejoice at his arrival and pamper him, and then let him go whenever he wants to, gladly welcoming his return visits—those he always reveres and cherishes: under his command they triumph. Consequently, my dear friend, do not try to control one who flies or clip someone that grows a thousand feathers for every one lost, and you will be happy.”170

And why resist, even if it were possible, an amorous passion for a woman of rare beauty? Vettori writes that he met such a woman but also reveals that he is torn between the desire to abandon himself to this passionate love and the fear of stumbling into “a thousand annoyances.”171 Machiavelli, in contrast, has no doubts: “I have no response to your letter, except that you should give your love full rein and that whatever pleasure you seize today may not be there for you to seize tomorrow; if things still stand as they did when you wrote, I envy you more than the king of England. I beg you to follow your star; do not let things slide—not even an iota—for anything in the world, because I believe now, I have always believed, and I shall continue to believe that what Boccaccio says is true: it is better to act and to regret it than not to act and to regret it.”172

Machiavelli was quite familiar with sexual passion, even in its most animalistic forms. He leaves us an unforgettable description in the letter that he wrote to Luigi Guicciardini from Verona on 8 December 1509. Luigi Guicciardini must have sent Machiavelli a description of some amorous exploit of his that had left him “want[ing] another fuck,” eager to “take another turn at it.” With his distinctive taste for paradox and self-deprecation, Machiavelli answered his friend with a description of having allowed himself to be lured, because he had been “hopelessly horny,” into a horrifying “amorous” assignation with a grotesque old woman. So grotesque that for a good while he was no longer “horny.”173

He also knew that erotic passion is an essential part of love. He knew it so well that when he realized that he no longer had the vigor to love a woman as she deserved, he resigned himself to abandoning all thoughts of love, as he writes, for the all-too-beautiful and young Barbara Salutati:

If worthiness in me

Were as immense as my desire,

Pity, now still asleep, awake would be.


But since desire and strength

Do not go well together,

Suffer I must the length

Of all my woes, my lord.

And you I do not blame—

I blame myself for this:

Great beauty finds its bliss—

I see and I confess—

In a much greener age.174


Love does not live without desire and without yearning. This is a general rule from which derives an admonition that is as harsh as it is ineluctable:

How beautiful is love in youthful heart!

And how incongruous

In one who’s long since passed life’s flowering part.

Love’s power parallels declining years,

And in our time of bloom evokes esteem,

But with old age its honor disappears.

And so, ye amorous old men, ’twould seem

The better part of valor to leave wooing

To ardent youths’ pursuing,

For they’re a fitter butt for Cupid’s dart.175


I have lingered on the idea of love in Machiavelli, and I hope that this has not annoyed the reader; let us agree, it is a fascinating theme, and because I hope that the texts on which I have commented do show that for Machiavelli love of the fatherland and erotic love were different passions. The love of the fatherland lives, when it lives, in both young people and old people. Even though it is a natural sentiment, it does not have the overwhelming and all-encompassing power of erotic love.
