It can instill sensations of sweetness (Machiavelli speaks of the “sweetness of free life”),176 but it is less intense than love proper. The love of the fatherland is a passion for a good that belongs to many people, can easily be extinguished, must be educated and stimulated, and never springs up in corrupt souls. Erotic love is always for a single person, it is difficult to extinguish, it is born without any intention or will on the part of the person who is in love, and everyone experiences it. It is true that just as a citizen sacrifices himself for the fatherland that he loves, the lover is ready to sacrifice himself or herself for the person he or she loves, but in the case of the lover, that is often simply a grand declamation.177 The lover’s sacrifice springs from the power of the amorous passion, the sacrifice of the citizen springs from charity and a sense of duty. Love for a woman envelops Machiavelli in sweetness, allows him to forget the troubles of life, and leads him away from the thought of great things. The love of the fatherland likewise allows him to forget the troubles of life, but it certainly doesn’t lead him away from thoughts of politics, and indeed it gives him the strength to do great things for the common good.


4. Moral and Religious Reform


Within the Florentine intellectual milieu there still thrived, even during Machiavelli’s time, a profound and diffuse demand for religious and moral reform.178 The supporters of that movement harked back to biblical and classical sources.179 In Latin texts, the words reformatio and regeneratio were equivalent. Ovid, a poet with whom Machiavelli was very familiar, called Jolaus, who is reinvigorated and rejuvenated, now able to fight in battle, “reformatus primos in annos.”180 Apuleius mentions the witch Pamphilia who transforms herself into an owl, “magicis suis artibus reformatur,” and in describing religious rebirth, he uses the term renatus.181 The most powerful ancient myth of rebirth, however, was that of the phoenix. Ovid writes in his Metamorphoses that times change, “et nova sunt semper,” and as an example of the capacity to be reborn through one’s own power, he cites the phoenix.182 The symbol of resurrection is Christ, but every human soul is also reborn, as is every people that is able to find its way back to the right path of living.183

In the New Testament, reformari means to transform oneself into the proper form to the Christian ideal.184 The force, or efficient cause, of this transformation is caritas. In the Vulgate there is an exhortation not to adapt to the corruption of the secular world but to transform, regenerate one’s entire ethical and spiritual life. The text reads: “Et nolite conformari huic saeculo, sed reformamini in novitate sensus vestri, ut probetis, quae sit voluntas Dei” (“be ye transformed by the renewing of your mind,” Rom. 12:2).”185 The image of rebirth appears as well in Paul’s Epistle to the Ephesians, with assuming a direct reference to the rebirth of the after-world. In the Epistle to the Colossians renewal means becoming once again godlike.186

To be reformed and reborn, in the sense of returning to the authentic form of the Christian religion, and to renew one’s way of life in literal keeping with the Gospels, and in accordance with the example of poverty and charity set by Jesus and the apostles, were the principles of the religious movements of the twelfth century.187 All the same, it was Dante, in The Banquet, who fully expressed the fundamental principle of the reformatio: “The supreme desire of each thing bestowed by Nature in the first place is to return to its first source.”188 Rebirth meant returning to the ideal, pure, original form. “Incipit Vita nova”: it is with these words that Dante begins the collection of poems that express the poet’s internal renewal through the spiritualizing and purifying force of love. In the Divine Comedy he explores and reformulates with great expressive power the ideas of rebirth, renewal, and the ideal elevation of man and humanity: “The ages are renewed; / justice and man’s first time on earth return; / from Heaven a new progeny descends,” he writes in Purgatorio (22, 70–72), translating Vergil almost to the letter.189 After Matilda had him drink from the Eunoe, the poet used the following words to express the significance of his inner renewal, achieved through his having “come so far,” right up to the very threshold of paradise: “Remade, as new trees are / renewed when they bring forth new boughs, I was / pure and prepared to climb unto the stars.”

The Christian ideal of rebirth through the power of faith became a myth of social and political reform in the Middle Ages. The most eloquent example is the restoration of the republic that Cola di Rienzo (1313?—54) attempted in Rome. As brief as that experiment may have been, it had immense repercussions. The forces that drove Cola di Rienzo were a love of glory, the cult of the sacred Roman people, a fervent desire to restore Rome, and with Rome all of Italy, to its ancient greatness. He himself presented his tribuneship, after the expulsion of the Roman barons, as the dawn of a new era and the revival of a fossilized life, not unlike an act of grace. The power behind moral rebirth is charity: just as the soil is broken up by a plow, likewise the church of God must be renewed through the acts and seeds of charity.190 The goal is to revive justice, and the rebirth is not the act of a prince but of an entire people, which thus gains new vigor; it is not a simple return to a state of perfection, or the birth of that which existed before, in an identical state, but rather a bringing to light of a new and higher way of life.191 The people, morally deformed by tyranny, must be reformed and liberated, and it must return to the ideal form that is proper to it. The reform, therefore, is a liberation from corruption and a redemption from decadence.192

Likewise Petrarch, felt and expressed quite forcefully the myth of rebirth.193 In a poem that may have been dedicated to Cola di Rienzo, he describes Italy as a lazy, sleepy, befuddled old woman, who fails to respond to the cries of men, until a new redeemer seizes her by the hair, lifts her out of the muck and stands her on her feet, and helps her to regain her old beauty and vigor. Petrarch portrays Italy as a “gentil donna” (‘gentle lady’) awaiting rescue at the hand of a husband or a father, and promises deathless glory to her redeemer. Rebirth is the renewal of the spirits that takes place when men rediscover devotion, charity, and virtue. His sources are Psalms: “Thou sendest forth thy spirit, they are created: and thou renewest the face of the earth” (103:30); and Revelation: “And I saw a new heaven and a new earth: for the first heaven and the first earth were passed away; and there was no more sea…. And he that sat upon the throne said, Behold, I make all things new” (21:1–5).194 He also finds—which Machiavelli was unwilling or unable to do—the words of a prophet to herald the rebirth of virtue and the return of the golden age:

Beautiful souls, the friends of virtue,

shall hold the world, and then we shall see the age

become golden and full of the ancient worthiness.195


Coluccio Salutati, who was in many ways a pupil of Petrarch, perceived indications of a new age in the victorious war that Florence waged against the papacy.196 Leonardo Bruni, in turn, adopted—as an interpretative criterion of human affairs—the cycle of birth, growth, perfection, and decay. Civilizations decay and die due to the effects of the corruption that dissolves the original virtue of the peoples, and especially because of the loss of liberty.197 States are reborn, in contrast, when they learn how to rediscover virtue and they reconquer liberty, as is demonstrated by the example of Florence, which not only was able to endow itself with a free republican government, but which established itself as a protector of Italian liberty and therefore became the providential place for the rebirth of literature and the arts, the rediscovery of the ancients, and the reconquest of civil living.

Giovanni Nesi, in his Oraculum de novo saeculo (1496), interprets the new century as a time of political rebirth for Florence and a rebirth of Christianity:

What does the new age mean if not the conversion and the renewal of the church?… Indeed, after so many different cases, I summon you to that golden age…. Whoever is an adherent of Christ, come into the kingdom of Christ…. Do you want the prophet? Here he is, truthfully announcing the future, admirable always and everywhere…. Italy will be devastated by the barbarians; the Florentine realm, today in crisis due to the ambition, perfidy, and frivolity of its citizens, will extend its name and its powers, endowed with growing wealth, power, and glory. Rome will soon come to ruin, invaded by foreigners; but the church will save itself through divine intervention and will then triumph; the Mohammedans will soon convert to the Christian faith. And, finally, there will be only one flock and only one shepherd.198


The demand for moral and religious reform drew some of its vigor from the comparison between the bad customs of the Italians and the good customs of the German peoples. Poggio Bracciolini, who lived in the Roman curia and was chancellor of the Florentine Republic, has left us an illuminating description of the “pleasant way of life due to the extreme freedom of customs” that he noticed in the baths of Baden. Everyone did their best to live happily and to enjoy life’s pleasures. They are not obsessed with “splitting up common goods,” but rather tend to “share things that have been divided up.” In the diverse crowd at the baths “there are never quarrels, disorders, divisions, murmurings, or cursing.” They are contented with very little, live one day at a time, and are not busy pursuing riches they will never obtain. The contrast with Italian customs is very clear: we “indulge in slander and backbiting,” we are entirely focused on getting and our appetites, never satisfied with what we earn and what we own, always unhappy, always frantic.199

In Germany, Poggio was brought into direct contact with the world of heresy. In Konstanz, he witnessed the trial of Jerome of Prague, a pupil of John Huss. He was greatly impressed by the defendants eloquence, worthy of the great masters of antiquity. He had serious doubts about the legitimacy of the charges of heresy, and he could find nothing to criticize in the faith that he proclaimed in the presence of the judges of the Inquisition: “He said nothing that would be unworthy of a faultless man, and if his faith truly was that which he professed, then he could not be found guilty of anything deserving—let alone a verdict of capital crime—even of the slightest punishment.” Poggio quoted the words of Jerome of Prague in defense of John Huss with evident approval: “He said that he has not held anything against the Church, but only against the abuse committed by the priests, against the overweening pride, the magnificence, and the pomp of the prelates,” and against all those things that were “unworthy of the religion of Christ.” Poggio, a Catholic, saw in Jerome of Prague, about to be burned at the stake as a heretic, a man who was “eminent beyond all belief,” and who knew how to die like a philosopher.200

Even before they began to hear about Martin Luther, the Florentines had an idea of Germany as a land of peoples who lived in accordance with a sincere Christian faith, which translated into a powerful civil conscience. Francesco Vettori had already noted this in 1512, when he narrated an account of his diplomatic mission to the court of Maximilian I from June 1507 to March 1508. Vettori’s account is a savory and impressive document of the corruption rampant among priests and monks. In nearly every tavern and inn at which he stopped, he heard tales of priests and monks guilty of every sort of wrongdoing.201 In the district around Verona, he was able to hear, from the mouths of several Germans returning home, the disgust with which true Christians judged the papal court: “If you ask me the reason that I am leaving Rome, I will tell you that we from the Rhine-land are good Christians, and we have heard and read that the faith of Christ was founded with the blood of the martyrs upon good customs, and strengthened by many miracles, so that it would be impossible for someone from the Rhine to question the true faith. I have been in Rome for a number of years, and I have seen how the prelates and others live, such that I was afraid that if I lingered there any longer, not only might I lose the faith of Christ, but that I might become an epicurean and lose my immortal soul.”202 Years later, in his Sommario della istoria d’Italia, Vettori wrote that the Roman papacy had “created a new religion with nothing in it of Christ save for the name”: Christ “commanded poverty, and they desire wealth, Christ commanded humility, and they pursue pride, Christ commanded obedience, and they want to command everyone else.”203

In the lands through which he was traveling, Vettori also met street artists, charlatans, and frauds, like the one he encountered near Verona who claimed that he had miraculously survived hanging in Bologna and was going to see the Blessed Simon in Trent to fulfill a vow. With this fairytale, he managed to pry money out of “poor men,” which he then cheerfully spent in the nearest tavern. The episode, in itself of minimal importance, caused Vettori to think about the countless modes, arts, industries, and wiles that men, first and foremost among them the religious, employ to deceive their fellow men.204 Alongside the corrupt prelates and the charlatans were those heralding new religions meant to restore the authenticity and simplicity of the evangelical message and to dissolve the wicked religion that popes and priests have established through deception and force. In Mirandola, for instance, he heard the story of Piero Bernardino who “was proselytizing a certain new religion,” and for that reason was burnt at the stake.205

Vettori talks about the things of the world and religion in a cold and detached manner. He notes, all the same, as he walks toward Germany, that the message of Christ is quite another matter from the corruption of the clergy and casuistry of theologians:

The theologians are the first in our religion who have made and continue to make many books, many disputations, many syllogisms, many subtleties, and not only all the libraries are filled with them, but also the booksellers’ shops. Nonetheless, our Savior Jesus Christ says in the Gospels: “You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your mind, and your neighbor as yourself.” In these two precepts we find all the laws of the prophets. What need have we, then, of so many disputations over the Incarnation, the Holy Trinity, the Resurrection, the Eucharist? These are things that we Christians must believe by our faith, and by believing become worthy, and reasons need not be added.206


The reform of the church had also been the topic of discussion, admittedly with objectives that were anything but noble, of the prelates who met in Pisa to take part in the Gallican Council held at the behest of the king of France in opposition to Pope Julius II, at the grave peril of the city of Florence. In the session of 7 November 1511, which Machiavelli may have attended (he had been sent to Pisa by the Signoria), the abbot Zaccaria Ferreri read a passage from the Gospel of John, all about the “reform of the Church,” and concluded that “all the most reverend cardinals and prelates should first reform themselves before attempting to reform the church.”207

A text that puts us into the direct presence of how Machiavelli’s Florence was experiencing the hope of a religious and moral reformation is the Dialogo della mutatione di Firenze, which Bartolomeo Cerretani, a pro-Medici moderate, wrote around 1520. The protagonists of the dialogue, Lorenzo and Girolamo, both fervent followers of Savonarola, set off on a journey to Germany to meet “Martino Luter,” and they explained to Giovanni Rucellai, whom they chanced to meet near Modena, the reasons for their pilgrimage: “To you, as our brother, our desire need not remain secret: we are going to Germany, attracted by the fame of a Venerable Religious there whom they call Brother Martino Luter, whose writings have been published in Italy and especially in Rome, as I know you are aware, and which suggest that this man must be in his way of life, his doctrine, and his religion very admirable, and it strikes us that his conclusions are quite proper and in keeping with the opinions and way of life of the early church militant.”

On hearing these words from the wayfarers, Rucellai responded: “Ah, then, you are victims of the same superstition as you were before with Fra’ Girolamo from Ferrara.”208 In the mentality of the Florentine pilgrims, Martin Luther overlapped with their memory of the monk who had been burnt at the stake twenty years earlier, and became the prophet of a renovatio that some yearned for, others feared, and still others mocked. “Don’t you have in Rome,” asks Girolamo, “a great fear of this renewal of the church out of love for your temporal possessions?” Even if “everyone is calling for a renovatio,” Giovanni Rucellai responded, “and your nuns and peasants and friars and other two-bit prophets talk about it all the time these days, everyone laughs at it in Rome, and you are living on dreams.”209

All those who were calling for religious reform were in favor of a regeneration of the body of the church through a return to the authentic beginnings of the Christian religion. Savonarola, to cite the most significant example, preached the need to renew and rebuild the church with poverty, simplicity, unity, and peace.” His renovatio had above all a moral and spiritual significance, based on a rediscovery of justice, a return to the beginnings of the apostolic life, and the republican and communitarian political values that Florence preserved by divine will: “Thus the church of Christ, which was founded by His apostles in poverty and simplicity and unity and peace, and you see today how it could be said to be destroyed, as the temple of Jerusalem was. And yet God chose to renew and rebuild it, and He wants Florence also to be renewed, whence comes the renewal of many other places.”210 To draw back the church or a city toward its beginnings means correcting the distortions in such a way that the work will be consistent with the purpose for which it was founded.211 In order to revive a people that has become dead through sin requires not only natural wisdom, but also the aid of God, the gift of prophecy, and the warmth of charity.212 Pico della Mirandola, who listened to Savonarola’s sermons in fascination, summarized the meaning of the aspiration to renovatio when he wrote that man can “descend to the lower, brutish forms of life” or regenerate himself in accordance with his will in the higher things “whose life is divine.”213

How and why the church needed to regenerate itself was explained during those same years by the Venetian reformer Gasparo Contarini (1483–1542) in a report on Savonarola written in 1516.214 In the conclusion of that text, Contarini explicitly states that reform is needed to keep the church alive and explains that reform can only take place as a return to the beginnings: “This renovation of the church I do not know through prophecy, rather natural and divine reason dictates it to me. Natural reason because human affairs do not follow an infinite straight line but follow a circular line; although they do not all follow a perfect circle and when they attain a certain point of growth, then they decline.”215

Machiavelli shared with the culture of his time the ideal of the renovatio. In the last pages of The Art of War, which he wrote in order to restore to life the ancient orders and the ancient Italian military virtue, he expresses his belief that Italy “seems born to raise up dead things, as she has in poetry, in painting, and in sculpture.”216 In the Canto degli spiriti bead (The Blessed Spirits) he harks back to the myth of the return of the golden age and the revival of ancient virtue with words similar to those of Petrarch:

Oh, throw away all fear,

All enmity and hate.

All greed and pride and cruelty away!

And in your hearts once more

Let love of true and noble virtue rise,

And bring the world back to that ancient day.

This is the only way

To open up a path to paradise

Where flame of valor never dies.217


As did Petrarch, Machiavelli saw Cola di Rienzo as the creator of a rebirth, however fleeting, of ancient virtue. In the Florentine Histories, he writes that “at this time a memorable thing happened in Rome: one Niccolò di Lorenzo, chancellor at the Capitol, drove out the Roman senators and made himself, with the title of tribune, head of the Roman republic, which he restored in its ancient form with such a reputation for justice and virtue that not only the towns nearby but all Italy sent ambassadors to him. So the old provinces, seeing how Rome had been reborn, lifted their heads; and some being moved by fear and some by hope, they honored him.”218

Machiavelli harshly criticizes the corruption of the church with words similar to those used by so many others before him. He accuses the church of Rome of having divided Italy and of keeping Italy divided, of having brought about the death of “all devotion and all religion,” and of instilling in the minds of the Italians the especially malign wickedness of those who are without religion: “Thus we Italians have this first obligation to the church and to the priests that we have become without religion and wicked.”219 Because they have no fear of God, nor the sense of modesty that arises from true religion, the Italians are convinced that they can always escape, in one way or another, their just punishment, and that they can be wicked without consequences.

Fra Timoteo, a model in The Mandrake of a friar who has only the appearance of a religious, makes the reader and the audience laugh, and is after all a jovial soul. The bad moral education that the Christian religion diffuses, however, deserved a serious condemnation. “Our religion,” writes Machiavelli, “has glorified humble and contemplative more than active men…. It has then placed the highest good in humility, abjectness, and contempt of things human,” and if “our religion asks that you have strength in yourself, it wishes you to be capable more of suffering than of doing something strong.”220 The religious tradition, therefore, bears a heavy burden of responsibility for having suffocated in modern men the love of republican liberty, of having rendered “the world weak” and “given it in prey to criminal men, who can manage it securely, seeing that the collectivity of men, so as to go to paradise, think more of enduring their beatings than of avenging them.”221

Machiavelli contrasted with Christian education, as an example to be emulated instead, the pagan education that taught the young to esteem the honor of the world and to place in it “the highest good,” which beatified only men who were “full of worldly glory, as were captains of armies and princes of republics,” which celebrated “greatness of spirit,” strength of body, and “all other things capable of making men very strong.” Many conscientious critics and writers therefore judged him to be a pagan.222 They failed to notice that Machiavelli also found in modern Christian peoples examples of a religion that instills good moral and political customs. Like others before him, Machiavelli looked to Germany, where he had traveled in 1508, passing, in part, through the same hamlets and cities that Vettori mentions in his Viaggio in Alamagna. Of the religiosity of the Germans, and the good customs that derive therefrom, Machiavelli wrote in Discourses on Livy to emphasize that in the “province of Germany” there was still a great “goodness” and a “religion” that were very similar to that of the Romans. Thanks to that goodness of customs, and that good religion, those peoples lived securely and in freedom. The contrast between Italy and Germany was quite distinct: on the one side was irreligion and wickedness, on the other was religion and goodness. This, however, is not a contrast between Christianity and paganism, but rather between two ways of being Christians.

Machiavelli finds in the Christian religion those moral and political principles that he admires and which he yearns to see reborn in the modern world. It is not that the principles of Christianity are wicked, rather it is that the interpretation that the church has given of those principles is wicked: “And although the world appears to be made effeminate and heaven disarmed, it arises without doubt more from the cowardice of the men who have interpreted our religion according to idleness and not according to virtue.”223 What is needed, then, is to return to the principles of the Christian religion, not to abandon it in order to revive paganism, nor to invent a new religion out of whole cloth, as other republican political theorists would attempt to do after him. “If such religion had been maintained by the princes of the Christian republic as was ordered by its giver,” he writes in Discourses on Livy, “the Christian states and republics would be more united, much happier than they are.”224 Neither a pagan nor the creator of a new religion, Machiavelli sought and found in the religious tradition a God who teaches us to love the fatherland and liberty and identified that God as the principle for a moral and religious reform.

A religious reform would have to be in his view a genuine renovatio, that is, a return to the original principles, betrayed by bad interpreters and offended by corrupt followers. “If one wishes a sect or a republic to live long, it is necessary to draw it back often toward its beginning,” he wrote in the first chapter of the third book of Discourses on Livy. And he explained that those changes “that lead them back toward their beginnings” are beneficial to republics and religions because “all the beginnings of sects, republics, and kingdoms must have some goodness in them, by means of which they may regain their first reputation and their first increase.”

As examples of a religious renewal implemented through a return to the beginnings, Machiavelli cites Saint Francis and Saint Dominic: those two saints, “with poverty and with the example of the life of Christ,” caused to bloom again in “the minds of men” the Christian religion that “had already been eliminated there.” He adds, however, that the reform undertaken by Saint Francis and by Saint Dominic ultimately left the corruption of the clergy without any restraint. This happened not because they preached and practiced a return to the principles of Christ, but because they taught in their sermons that “it is evil to say evil of evil,” that it is good to obey the prelates, and that when they are in the wrong, one should “leave them for God to punish,” with the consequence that “they do the worst they can because they do not fear the punishment that they do not see and do not believe.” Whatever the negative effects of the “renewal” of Saint Francis and Saint Dominic, the fact remains that if the two preachers had not “drawn it back toward its beginning,” the Christian religion would have died.225

The idea of renovatio that Machiavelli defends and sets forth as a religious and political ideal is the same as that found in the Christian tradition: it is a return to the true form. In the case of Christianity it was a return to poverty and charity, which are the true path of Christ, just as for Machiavelli the true path was that of virtue. He had no interest in issues of dogma. This is the difference between his idea of moral and religious reform and the idea put forth by the proponents of the Protestant Reformation. He knew about Luther and the Lutherans, but that Reformation was different from his approach to reform.226 He was not interested in indulgences, predestination, divine grace, free will, or the presence of Christ in the Eucharist. He wanted a God who would help the men of his own time, and those of times still to come, to rediscover their love of liberty and the inner strength demanded by a free way of life.227 He was not seeking a new theology, but a new way of living in the world. For that reason, he spoke of a God capable of entering into the hearts of men and of giving them the strength to overcome moral corruption.228

Bad religion has rendered the Italians wicked, keen to do evil and to deceive, as well as to oppress others without shame. Only a good religion can change them, even if the disease is so serious and so far advanced that there is little real hope of a cure. A religion that has been renewed in keeping with the ancient virtues was needed for all modern peoples in order to recover that old love of freedom that among the ancient peoples had brought about the foundation, and had kept alive, many free republics. Without becoming either a prophet or a promoter of a religious reform, Machiavelli worked with the power of words to bring about the birth of a new religious and moral custom.

Free will was in Dante’s view the greatest gift that God gave to mankind: “The greatest gift of Gods largesse when He / created all, most prized by Him, and best, / as most akin to His own quality, / Was the will’s freedom, crown of all the rest, / Whereof all creatures made intelligent, / They all, they only, were and are possessed”; Paradiso, V, 19–24. English translation: Dante Alighieri, The Comedy of Dante Alighieri, the Florentine, edited by Dorothy Leigh Sayers, Barbara Reynolds, Penguin Classics, Penguin Books Harmondsworth, Middlesex 1957, pp. 90–91.

Guicciardini’s irony was well founded, even though finding good preachers for Lent was a serious matter for both townships and guilds. See Francesco Bruni, La città divisa. Le parti e il bene comune da Dante a Guicciardini, Il Mulino, Bologna, 2003.

32 Opere di Niccolò Machiavelli, vol. III, pp. 519–20. English translation: Atkinson and Sices, Machiavelli and His Friends, p. 336. Machiavelli expresses his love of the fatherland as well in the opening to his A Dialogue on Language [Discorso o dialogo intorno alla nostra lingua], in Opere di Niccolò Machiavelli, vol. IV, p. 261: “Whenever I have had an opportunity of honouring my country, even if this involved me in trouble and danger, I have done it willingly, for a man is under no greater obligation than to his country; he owes his very existence, and later, all the benefits that nature, and fortune offer him, to her. And the nobler one’s country, the greater one’s obligation. In fact he who shows himself by thought and deed an enemy of his country deserves the name of parricide, even if he has a legitimate grievance.” English translation: The Literary Works of Machiavelli, with Selections from the Private Correspondence, edited and translated by J. R. Hale, Oxford University Press, New York 1961, p. 175.

33 Opere di Niccolò Machiavelli, vol. III, p. 422. English translation: Atkinson and Sices, Machiavelli and His Friends, p. 261.

In his translation into the vernacular of the Istoria by Poggio Bracciolini, Iacopo Bracciolini explains that when they stood up to Pope Gregory XI the Florentines were obliged to choose between “the charity of the fatherland” and “the fear of religion,” and as it was just that they should do inasmuch as they were good citizens and good Christians, they placed the charity of the fatherland above and ahead of all other obligations, ties, or considerations; Iacopo Bracciolini, Istoria di M. Poggio Fiorentino: tradotta di latino in volgare da Iacopo suo figliuolo, Filippo Giunti, Florence 1598, p. 35.

35 Niccolò Machiavelli, Lettere a Francesco Vettori e a Francesco Guicciardini, edited by Giorgio Inglese, Rizzoli, Milan 1989, p. 384, n.9.

36 See in this connection the fine commentary by Hannah Arendt: “We, who no longer take for granted the immortality of the soul, are apt to overlook the poignancy of Machiavelli’s credo. At the time he wrote, the expression [to love one’s native city more than one’s own soul] was no cliché but meant literally that one was prepared to forfeit an everlasting life or to risk the punishments of hell for the sake of one’s city The question, as Machiavelli saw it, was not whether one loved God more than the world, but whether one was capable of loving the world more than one’s own self And this decision indeed has always been the crucial decision for all who devoted their lives to politics. Most of Machiavelli’s arguments against religion are directed against those who love themselves, namely their own salvation, more than the world; they are not directed against those who really love God more than they love either the world or themselves.” On Revolution, Viking Press, New York, 1963, pp. 289–90.

In his exposition of the Nicomachean Ethics, Donato Acciaiuoli writes that the “civitatis summum bonum” is considered more beautiful and divine (“divinius et pulcrius”) than the good of the individual because it is assimilated to the first cause that works universally for the good of all; Donato Acciaiuoli, Expositio libri ethicorum Aristotelis, Sanctum Iacobum de Ripoli, Florence 1478, p. 9. Acciaiuoli’s book came into the hands of Niccolò’s father: “I here note that on the 20th of February I received from Piero Gualterotti and through him from Bartolomeo Tucci the bookseller, the commentary by Donato Acciaiuoli upon Ari[stotle]’s Ethics, unbound, to see and purchase if I liked it”; Bernardo Machiavelli, Libro di ricordi, edited by Cesare Olschki, Le Monnier, Florence 1954, p. 141.

See also Charles T. Davis, “An Early Florentine Political Theorist: Fra Remigio de’ Girolami,” Proceedings of the American Philosophical Society LIV (1960), pp. 662–76.

Davis, “Remigio de’ Girolami and Dante: a Comparison of Their Conceptions of Peace, Studi Danteschi 36 (1959), pp. 105–36, and Davis, “An Early Florentine Political Theorist.”

St. Augustine condemns the love of the fatherland of the ancients in De civitate Dei, V, 12; see also Charles T. Davis, “Ptolemy of Lucca, and the Roman Republic,” Proceedings of the American Philosophical Society 118 (1974), pp. 30–50.

86 Dante Alighieri, Opere minori, book I, part II, edited by Cesare Vasoli and Domenico De Robertis, Ricciardi, Milan—Naples 1979–84, pp. 571–73. English translation: Gutenberg, Translated by Elizabeth Price Sayer, http://www.gutenberg.org/files/12867/12867-8.txt. Petrarch considered on the other hand the love (charity) of the fatherland among the ancients to be an imperfect love and did not admit a specific Christian patriotism. See Charles Trinkaus, In Our Image and Likeness: Humanity and Divinity in Italian Humanist Thought, Constable, London 1970, vol. I, pp. 37–38. In De vita solitaria he maintained nonetheless that nothing is more worthy of a man and nothing renders a man more godlike than acting on behalf of the common good: “quid aut homine dignius aut similius Deo est, quam servare et adiuvare quam plurimos?,” in Prose, edited by Guido Martellotti, Ricciardi, Milan-Naples 1955, pp. 322, 328.

I, p. 21;“caritas, que maior quam erga patriam esse non debet in terris”: Ibid., vol. II, p. 87;“patriam…cuius caritas non solum omnes necessitudines amplexa est, sed preterit et excedit”: ibid., vol. III, p. 638.

117 Ibid., p. 150

118 Ibid., p. 151.

119 Ibid., p. 142. The religion of charity put down roots as well in other republican settings. In Vicenza, for instance, the myth of Athens, and of Vicenza as a latter-day Athens, kept alive in the most advanced intellectual circles and in the populace at large the ideal of political liberty linked to the ideal of religious liberty. Cicero and the Bible coexisted without difficulties. The religion of the caritas set forth first by Ficino, and later by Erasmus and Martin Luther, was accepted as a valid alternative to the teachings of the Catholic Church. In this context, the yearning for an internal reform flourished, understood as a necessary condition for a Christian way of life and as a prerequisite to the practice of the art of successfully governing the city. See Achille Olivieri, Riforma ed eresia a Vicenza nel Cinquecento, Herder Editrice e Libreria, Rome 1992, pp. 13, 37; see also Silvana Seidel Menchi, Erasmo in Italia 1520–1580, Bollati Boringhieri, Turin 1987. In the view of Marc’Antonio Sabellico (1436?—1506), for instance, who taught eloquence in Udine and in Venice, charity is a sentiment of the soul that gives richness to one’s inner life, translates into the custom of “tolerantia,” inspires the behavior of a citizen who suffers at the destruction of political liberty and intellectual liberty, and who wishes to act in the defense of both. The symbol of charity is Hercules, a pagan hero, who slays monsters, defeats abuse and overweening pride, wards off dangers from the world of men, and gives new life to the hope for immortality; his opposite is Democritus, the philosopher who laughs at the evils of the world and does nothing to alleviate them; Marc’Antonio Sabellico, “Exemplorum,” in M. Antonii Coccii Sabellici Opera omnia, ah infinitis quibus scatebant mendis, repurgata & castigate…in Tomos quatuor digesta:…per Caelium Secundum Curionem, non sine magno labore iudicioque confecta, per Ioannem Hervagium, Basel 1509, vol. 4, col. 87. See also col. 86: “Christus rex, Deus idem & homo docuit humanum genus charitatem esse omnium virtutum maximam,” and col. 84;“Charitas ut ea sublata nulla constare possit virtus homini.”

120 See Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, De hominis dignitate, edited by Eugenio Garin, Vallecchi, Florence 1942, pp. 106–7.

121 See in this connection Donald Weinstein, Savonarola and Florence: Prophecy and Patriotism in the Renaissance, Princeton University Press, Princeton 1970, pp. 324–33. Weinstein emphasizes the presence of “a characteristically civic set of religious modalities arising out of the special experience of Italian communal life”; Weinstein, “Critical Issues in the Study of Civic Religion in Renaissance Florence,” in The Pursuit of Holiness in Late Medieval and Renaissance Religion, edited by Charles Trinkaus and Heiko Oberman, Leiden 1974, p. 265. See also David S. Peterson, “Religion, Politics, and the Church in Fifteenth-Century Florence,” in Girolamo Savonarola: Piety, Prophecy and Politics in Renaissance Florence, edited by Donald Weinstein and Valerie R. Hotchkiss, Bridwell Library, Dallas, TX 1994, pp. 75–83; Christianity and the Renaissance: Image and Religious Imagination in the Quattrocento, edited by Timothy Verdon and John Henderson, Syracuse, NY 1990.

162 Sebastian de Grazia has stated that for Machiavelli the love of the fatherland is similar to the love for a woman. Love, explains de Grazia, “is a word [Machiavelli] uses to refer to sentiment for country, an ancient usage, transferring the term from concrete persons and things to the abstraction of country”; Sebastian de Grazia, Machiavelli in Hell, Princeton University Press, Princeton 1990; Italian translation Machiavelli all’inferno, Laterza, Rome-Bari 1990, p. 175. Similarly to a love for a woman, the love of the fatherland chains, enslaves, and can even demand the sacrifice of one’s life, experienced not as an imposition but as a joyful and willing gift: “As love can deny nothing to love, so love can deny nothing to country”; Ibid., p. 189.

168 “Those with which he has bound me are so strong that I am in absolute despair of my liberty and I am unable to conceive of any means of unfettering myself; and even if fate or other human stratagem should open some path for me to get out of them, perhaps I should not wish to go down it; so do I find these fetters—now sweet, now light, now heavy—and they make such a tangle that I believe I cannot live happily without this kind of life”; Niccolò Machiavelli to Francesco Vettori, 31 January 1515, in Opere di Niccolò Machiavelli, vol. III, p. 489 English translation: Atkinson and Sices, Machiavelli and His Friends, p. 312. “I ought to tell you, as you did me, how this love began, how Love ensnared me with his nets, where he spread them, and what they were like; you would realize that, spread among the flowers, these were nets of gold woven by Venus, so soft and gentle that even though an insensitive heart could have severed them, nevertheless I declined to do so. For a while I reveled within them, until their tender threads hardened and locked into indissoluble knots”; Niccolò Machiavelli to Francesco Vettori, 3 August 1514, ibid., p. 465 English translation; Atkinson and Sices, Machiavelli and His Friends, p. 293.

169 Ibid, English translation; Atkinson and Sices, Machiavelli and His Friends, p. 293. It is worth noting that Machiavelli had women friends, real or imaginary, which whom he had (or imagined he had) erotic relations. Quite real was Lucrezia, known as La Riccia, a Florentine courtesan, with whom Machiavelli had a lengthy relationship, Lucrezia was at his side in many of the most difficult moments of his life. We know this for certain from a letter written by Vettori: “But you tell me something that astonishes me; you have found so much faith and so much compassion in La Riccia that, I swear to you, I was her supporter for your sake, but now I have become her slave, because most of the time women are wont to love Fortune and not men, and when Fortune changes, they change as well”; ibid., p. 487. English translation: Atkinson and Sices, Machiavelli and His Friends, p. 311. In his reply, Machiavelli called La Riccia “amica” (‘his friend’) and adds that she and Donato del Corno “are the sole havens and refuges for my skiff bereft of rudder and sail because of the unending tempest,” English translation: Atkinson and Sices, Machiavelli and His Friends, p. 312. In contrast, an imaginary figure was the beautiful handmaiden of Circe who in L’Asino [The Golden Ass] (VI, 22–24) welcomes the poor Niccolò (the poem is openly autobiographical) into her house, shares her supper with him, and then invites him to share a sweet night of love with her. After making love, the maiden and the hero of the story speak at length about many important matters (“Then we began to speak of many things, / just as a friend does with another friend”): Opere di Niccolò Machiavelli, vol. IV, p. 308. English translation: Tusiani, Lust and Liberty, p. 83.

The need for spiritual renewal and for a revolt against the corruption of the clergy was strong as well in such highly religious men as the Galateo (Antonio de Ferrariis, 1444–1517) who wrote pages of invective against the hypocrisy of the monks, denouncing them not only for doing wicked deeds, and for protecting “usurers, the unjust, those who steal the property of others, and those who batten off the people,” but also for “making God an accomplice to their thefts,” and thus establishing through fraud a religion that “does not remove sin but conceals it.” See Antonio de Ferrariis also known as the Galateo, “Esposizione del Pater noster,” Collana di scrittori di Terra d’Otranto, vol. IV, Tecce 1868, pp. 145–238; vol. XVIII, Lecce 1871, pp. 5–104; Galateo, L’Heremita, vol. XII, Lecce 1875, pp. 3–134. During the years in which Machiavelli was writing the Discourses on Livy there were circulating, within the Roman church, ideas of reform based not on the idea of looking back to the original principles, but rather to looking upward, to the ideal of the celestial church. See O’Malley, Praise and Blame in Renaissance Rome, pp. 201–3.

English translation: Atkinson and Sices, Machiavelli and His Friends, p. 395. See also Simoncelli, “Preludi e primi echi di Lutero a Firenze,” pp. 674–744; and Silvana Seidel Menchi, “Le traduzioni italiane di Lutero nella prima metà del Cinquecento,” Rinascimento XVII (1977), pp. 31–108. Corrado Vivanti sees a reference to the Reformation in the passage of the Florentine Histories (I, 9) where Machiavelli writes: And you will see how the popes—first through censures, then by censures and arms together, mixed with indulgences—were terrible and awesome; and how, for having used them both badly, they have lost the one altogether and as regards the other remain at the discretion of others.” English translation: Banfield and Mansfield, Florentine Histories, p. 20; Introduction to Opere, vol. III, pp. XL—XLI. Machiavelli was familiar, at least in part, with the debates over the reformation of the church because he had attended, as an envoy sent by the Ten, the Gallican Council that was held in Pisa from 2 to 9 November 1511; see Opere, vol. II, p. 1371. With reference to Machiavelli’s mission to the Pisan Council, Delio Cantimori wrote: “For that matter, Machiavelli’s compatriots and superiors considered him to be competent as well in religious and ecclesiastical matters.” When the Florentine commissioners in Pisa had to report back on the progress of the council, they explicitly stated that as far as matters were concerned that they had failed to understand or that they had overlooked, they freely recommended making recourse “to the wisdom of Nicolò [sic] Machiavelli, who was also present, and who is more knowledgeable in these matters than we are”; Delio Cantimori, “Machiavelli: il politico e lo storico,” in Storia della Letteratura Italiana, edited by Emilio Cecchi and Natalino Sapegno, Garzanti, Milan 1966, vol. 4, II Cinquecento, pp. 13–14. See also Augustin Renaudet, Le Concile gallican de Pise-Milan. Documents fiorentins (1510–1512), Bibliothèque de l’Institut Français de Florence, Paris 1922.

CHAPTER TWO


THE POWER OF WORDS


1. Rhetoric and Civil Religion


In Machiavelli’s Florence, the power of words pervaded both political and religious life. With public orations magistrates educated the populace to love the republic; preachers taught the love of Christ with their sermons. Both magistrates and preachers were well aware that words were capable of educating the people to a love of liberty and justice, encouraging faith, stirring them to devotion, and most of all, that words could impress in the hearts of men and women a love of virtue and a hatred of vice. In terms of the themes they treated, the arguments that they used, and the authorities that they cited, civil and religious eloquence frequently overlapped, and together they made of Florence a city in which rhetoric was the queen of the arts.1

In the opinion of Coluccio Salutati, who was a statesman and a political writer, mastery of eloquence was an essential aspect of the duties of a citizen. Political experience had taught him that the persuasive power of words is essential to the preservation of civil life and especially to the reformation of moral life.2 What could be more important, he writes, than to control the passions, move those who listen to you as and where you wish. and then return them, with courtesy and kindness, to the position from which you first took them? It is useful to know how to adorn and embellish one’s writings, but it is much more important to manage to bend the souls of those who listen to you.3 By means of eloquence we can help our fellow citizens and moderate the most pernicious passions.4 Rhetoric must be considered the queen of the arts and the sciences, but it should never be split off from reason, because eloquence without wisdom has always brought only serious harm to mankind.5 Salutati saw no contrast between eloquence and Christian truth.6 Indeed, eloquence should be placed in the service of the wisdom that Christ revealed to us. Man depends on God for all his excellent works, and eloquence is no exception.7 If used properly, it can bring Christian truth into the souls of men and reinforce civil virtue.8

Leonardo Bruni too believed that eloquence was necessary in order to foster the religion of the fatherland. On the most important occasions in the life of the republic, such as, for instance, funerals of citizens who gave their lives for the fatherland, eloquence adds the ornamentation of words to the solemnity of the ceremony. With its splendor, eloquence illuminates the virtues and the works of the dead person, highlights the religious nature of the love of the fatherland, and moves the spirits of the audience to understand that the fatherland is sacred (“patria sua cuique sanctissima”) and that those who have given their lives for its liberty and its safety will receive eternal rewards in the heavens.9 The true model that a citizen must follow in order to be capable of fulfilling properly his duties is that of Cicero, who was able to combine eloquence and wisdom, and who added to Roman power eloquence, the “mistress of human affairs.” For those reasons, Bruni concludes, it was proper and fitting to call him both the father of the fatherland and the father of our [Italian] language and literature.10 Eloquence not only aids the religion of the fatherland, but also the Christian religion. In his De studiis et litteris, written between 1422 and 1429 for Battista Malatesta, the wife of the lord of Pesaro, Galeazzo Malatesta, Bruni explains that “the Christian woman” should meditate on Holy Scripture with the assistance of eloquent authors, such as Augustine, who “offers us a learned text worthy of being heard,” and Lactantius, “undoubtedly the most eloquent of all Christians.”11

The author who felt more than any others that religion was fostered by eloquence was, in any case, Lorenzo Valla, who from his earliest youth devoted himself to the study of oratory, guided by the conviction that if an orator is, as he ought to be, “a good man skilled in the art of speaking” (“vir bonus dicendi peritus”), then the study of oratory brings great help to men and is pleasing to God.12 Valla theorized a rhetorical theology based on modesty, a great understanding of divine matters, integrity in the way of life, and gravity of character. These are the qualities of the Ciceronian orator, adapted to the Christian orator. But the Christian orator, in contrast to the orator of antiquity, must also possess a sincere faith and lead a Christian life. Just as an orator cannot move his listeners to wrath or pity if he does not himself feel wrath and pity, likewise a religious orator cannot instill a love for divine matters in the minds of others if he himself does not feel that love within.13 By means of the power of words, a Christian orator can lead men toward God in a true and easy manner. Men cannot see heavenly bliss, they can only imagine it, and they can accept faith in imitation of those who already live in accordance with that faith. The only art that is able to stimulate men’s imagination and move them to act through imitation is eloquence. It is, therefore, Valla concludes, the best ally of religion.14

Religion, he writes in the Of Elegancies, makes use of persuasion, not demonstration, and persuasion is far more powerful than demonstration.15 Even the Fathers of the Church and the Apostles were outstanding in their eloquence. Many of them were skilled at ably setting the jewels of the divine word in gold and silver, without putting aside theology in favor of eloquence. Monks and friars, too, though Valla rails at them for their pretense at superiority to other men merely because they have taken holy orders, ought to speak “more in the style of orators than that of philosophers,” if they truly wish to correct men and lead them away from sin, free them from false beliefs, and guide them toward piety and true knowledge and understanding.16 Those who do not know how to use the persuasive power of words, Valla states, are “unworthy of speaking about theology.” The true pillars of the church have been men of eloquence, and foremost among them, Saint Paul. Thus blame should be laid not on those who are masters of eloquence but those who lack skills in that field.

Both civil eloquence and poetic and religious eloquence are worthy of unconditional praise. The former can be compared to the song of a swallow that lives on city rooftops; the latter is like the song of the nightingale that lives in trees and in the woods. Although they have different aims and use different means of expression, one marvelously completes the other.17 It is from these considerations that Valla derived his outspoken subordination of philosophy to rhetoric. Philosophy, he writes, is like a soldier or a tribune serving under the command of rhetoric.18 Orators have explored and treated moral questions with far greater clarity, profundity, and magnificence than have the obscure, tawdry, anemic philosophers. More important, they treated them in the midst of mainstream civil life, long before philosophers debated them in their nooks and crannies. The orator, Valla assures us, and not the philosopher, is humanity’s guide, a true civil man, a true scholar, and a true politician.19

In response to those who criticized him for exhorting readers to explore books dipped in the poison of eloquence, Valla shot back that “the books of the ancients are all so eloquent when they teach wisdom, they possess the utmost eloquence, and that when they teach eloquence, they possess the utmost wisdom.” It is therefore necessary to condemn them all or absolve them all, without exception. If an orator is vir bonus, the art that he teaches—“how to find and how to arrange the bones and the sinews of the discourse, and how to adorn it, that is, how to apply flesh and color to it and, finally, teaches you to commit it to memory and how to deliver it elegantly”—cannot be harmful in any manner.20 Indeed, it is the only art that is able to instill and foster a religious sentiment and a civil awareness in a people.

Eloquence flourishes in a free city and languishes under tyranny. When Florence was free, writes Alamanno Rinuccini (1426–99), a figure of prime importance from the period of Florentine civic humanism, freedom of speech was the rule in assemblies, and with that freedom the eloquence of the citizens flourished. Since the city has been subjugated, silence reigns; and if from time to time the voice of the usher rings out giving the floor to someone who has requested it, “everyone understands that it is an empty sound, since no one dares give their true views and speak openly, because of fear.”21

In the years of Machiavelli’s youth, the learned men summoned to teach in the Studio Fiorentino (the nucleus of what would later be the University of Florence) explained to their young students who were preparing for public life that a good citizen, in order to perform his duties well, should cultivate with all his will and determination the art of eloquence. Angelo Ambrogini, also known as Politian (Poliziano), for instance, began his course in 1480 with an introduction that is a fervent exaltation of the importance of eloquence as the necessary foundation of civil life:

What could be finer than to succeed in outshining other men precisely in that quality whereby men outshine the other animals? What could be more marvelous, when speaking before great multitudes, than to intrude to such a degree into the souls and minds of men as to propel and draw their will as you like, so as to render their sentiments either gentler or more violent, and ultimately to master the wills and the emotions of one and all? What could be more admirable than to be able to embellish and exalt with one’s words men who excel in their virtues and their admirable actions, and in contrast to defeat and discourage the evil and harmful, heaping shame and crushing their foul deeds? What could be more useful and productive than to be able to persuade with one’s words all one’s fellow citizens, dear to one’s heart, in all the matters that you consider crucial to the state, and guiding them at the same time away from pointless and evil purposes? What could be more necessary than always to have ready the armor and the sword of eloquence with which to protect oneself, attack one’s adversaries, and defend one’s innocence, besieged by evil men? What could be more magnanimous and in keeping with a well-educated soul than to be able to console the unfortunate, succor the afflicted, aid the supplicants, and procure and maintain friendship and patronage?22


The natural venue for eloquence was political life.23 Citizens summoned to deliver political orations attempted with the power of their words to infuse a civil faith based on the love of the fatherland and of liberty. There are some very significant documents on the use of eloquence to infuse and keep alive a civil faith in the orations delivered in the presence of the Signori when the new gonfalonier of justice took his office. The orations, described as protestatio de iustitia, gave a religious significance to the political principles of justice and liberty, and with that end in mind, the orators would quote from both the Bible and the Fathers of the Church as often as from the Greek and Latin masters.24

Bono Boni, for instance, in the oration delivered in 1461, emphasized that living in accordance with justice is “complying with God” and that Christ himself recommended cultivating justice in order to ensure that our earthly home does not collapse as a result of disunity and discord.25 Anyone who respects and promotes justice becomes godlike, and after death a place in heaven is set aside for him where he can enjoy eternal beatitude, Boni explains, referring to the theme of the Somnium Scipionis, which was so popular in Florentine humanism.26 Donato Acciaiuoli (1429–78), who was gonfalonier of justice in 1474, emphasizes that justice is a divine virtue that God “sent to earth into our hands and the hands of others who rule and govern.” Because of this divine origin it is so magnificent that if we could see it with our own eyes, it “would fill all our senses and ignite in our souls an ardent love and desire toward its beauty and dignity.”27

Similarly, Giannozzo Manetti (1396–1459) speaks of justice as a divine virtue, inasmuch as it imitates the work of God, who punishes evil with mercy and rewards through grace. The cities that practice justice tend to prosper, and God Himself in the Bible commands us to love justice and to honor those who defend it.28 Bernardo Canigiani (1443–97), who was a close friend of Marsilio Ficino, admonishes those who govern a city and those who administer justice, urging them to “make a profession of standing watch, so that others may sleep, to work hard, so that others may rest, and to put themselves at risk, so that others may live in safety,” and he exhorts them to emulate the Good Shepherd of the Gospels who “ponit animam pro ovibus suis” (“giveth his life for the sheep,” John 10:11). In order to urge his readers to consider public honors as a form of service, he quotes from Saint Paul, who “showed by his own example that it is the duty of the superior to administer to the inferior [the] washing of the feet, with the apostles.” Finally, he points out that “immortal rewards are reserved to those whose soul is received pure and immaculate by God.”29

When the Florentine Republic began to develop into a de facto principality under the rule of Lorenzo de’ Medici, the orators accentuated the religious content of the civil faith. Evidence of this can be seen in the oration that Filippo Pandolfini delivered in 1475, entirely built around the idea that man “was commanded by the almighty God only to perform justice in this present life.” Pandolfini develops the theme of justice as a religious principle based on the premise that God created the universe out of love for man and created man so that he could love God. The “thing created” must consequently perform the will of his creator, because anyone who “truly does not know God and does not love God, lives contrary to nature and goes against the statute and divine law, and therefore manifestly we see that man was created solely in order that he might live with religion and justice.” Just as Plato taught, too, it is only by means of justice that man can become godlike and continue to live even after death. Justice also ignites in the souls of men “an ardent and vast love of the fatherland,” which makes him think “of the common good of the rep[ublic]” and gives men the necessary wisdom to establish good orders and good laws, as well as the strength to defend the common liberty. Even the charity of the fatherland, which demands that one govern with justice, is a religious principle: “This is the thing that above all others pleases the omnipotent God, who commands us to do no other thing, and exhorts no other thing from us save that we administer justice.” For those reasons we must necessarily conclude, Pandolfini emphasizes, that “God created us to perform justice.”30

Alamanno Rinuccini, in the oration delivered on 15 July 1493, describes justice as a “celestial and divine virtue” that descends from the Creator to all creatures, not only in the perceptible world but also in the “intelligible, angelic, and spiritual” world. Justice is one of the attributes of God. For that reason, when men acknowledge “Madonna Justice,” and submit the perceptible, brutal, and inferior powers to the superior, intellectual, and rational powers, governing their works with “just imperium,” they conform with the will of God. Justice, Rinuccini emphasizes, is the virtue that, more than any other, is necessary to “human living and especially political and civil life.” Those who are just become similar to the highest monarch God, “who not only governs the universe with the greatest justice” but also rewards the just with great generosity.31 Justice is the inspiration of the great legislators who gave their peoples blessed laws for the common good; for justice the martyrs of the Christian faith sacrificed themselves, and it was thanks to them that fatherlands preserved themselves in freedom and became great and powerful. From justice is born “peace and delight,” the two goods that are especially acceptable to God and Christ, who reward those who behave in accordance with justice with final and eternal happiness. Justice permits men to step beyond their mortal condition and makes creatures “similar to the creator,” capable of “boundless love.” Thanks to the “holy piety” and the “singular justice” that makes us think of the “common good,” celebrated men from history were able to found great undertakings.32

Justice is also invoked in the Council of the Eighty (Consiglio degli Ottanta), and often the orators place special emphasis upon its religious significance. To respect justice, Bernardo di Carlo da Diacceto stated, for instance, on 28 November 1499, is the true way of respecting God: “He encouraged at some length the Signoria to exert all diligence in ensuring that justice is observed, saying that where justice is observed, there God is, and where God is, there is every good and every thing, and where justice is lacking, there can be no good at all.” In the same case, Cappone Capponi, in order to encourage the Signoria to ensure that magistrates practice proper justice, quotes from the Psalms (84:11): “Pax et iustitia oscula sunt” (“For the LORD God is a sun and shield: the LORD will give grace and glory: no good thing will he withhold from them that walk uprightly”), while Giovanni Buondelmonti quotes from the Epistle to the Hebrews 1:9:“Dilexisti iustitiam et odisti iniquitatem / propterea unxit te Deus Deus tuus oleo exultationis prae participibus tuis” (“Thou hast loved righteousness, and hated iniquity; therefore God, even thy God, hath anointed thee with the oil of gladness above thy fellows”).33

Aside from diplomatic affairs, the natural venue for political eloquence was in the public councils.34 When they were summoned to deliberate on political decisions, the citizens of Florence employed their eloquence to persuade, admonish, exhort, evoke dangers, and stir hope. In the speeches that they made, even in the most concrete ones, those furthest from the magniloquent style, they did their best to bring to life the principles of the republican political ethic. A genuine commonplace was the exhortation to virtue as the necessary foundation for political freedom and the true path to glory. Another recurring topos, or theme, which also emphasized the religious nature of the republican ethos, was the idea that God was a friend to republics, like Florence, that defended justice, even if they were thus obliged to battle against the temporal power of the pope.

A truly illuminating example of this is the speech that Gonfalonier Pier Soderini (1452–1522) delivered before the Consiglio Maggiore (Major Council) in favor of gaining approval of a law that required the clergy of Florence to pay a large sum of money for use in the defense of the city from the impending arrival of the troops of the Holy League under the command of Pope Julius II. Aside from being a response to the need to find resources to pay for the defense of the city, the law was also a response to the interdiction that the pope had imposed against Florence, for having allowed the inauguration of the Council of Pisa in November 1511—a council that the king of France had instigated in opposition to the papacy.

The interdiction forbade the city’s clergy to celebrate Mass, to administer certain sacraments, to perform religious burial services for the dead, and gave anyone the right to avail themselves of the goods of Florentine merchants wherever they might be. It was a powerful weapon, and it exerted pressure on both the souls and the material possessions of the Florentines. In order to win approval of the law, Pier Soderini appealed not only to the supreme necessity of defense, but also to divine law, maintaining that the safety of the fatherland was a religious obligation, even if the enemy was the church:

Nor let the Fear of offending the divine Majesty restrain you; for the Danger is great and evident, and such are our Wants and Necessities … that we are permitted to assist ourselves not only with that Part of the Revenues which is not converted to pious Uses, but it would even be lawful for us to lay our Hands on things consecrated; because Self-Defence is allowed to all Men by the Law of Nature, and approved by Almighty God, and the Consent of all Nations; had its Beginning and will end with the World, and can suffer no Derogation by Civil or Canon Laws, which are founded on the Will of Men, and being written on Paper cannot invalidate a Law, not made by Men, but written, engraved and infixed in the Breasts and Minds of all human Generations.35


The true way of being Christians and acting in a way that is acceptable to God, Soderini insisted, is to defend the liberty of the city: “It is the common Opinion of all Sages that God is highly pleased with the Liberties of Cities, because by them the common Good is more consulted than under any other Form of Government, Justice is administered with greater Impartiality, the Minds of the Citizens are more incited to virtuous and honourable Actions, and more Respect and Reverence are paid to Religion. And can you believe that the Almighty will be displeased at your defending a Thing so precious as Liberty, for which whoever spends his Blood meets with the highest Praises, by employing a small Part of your temporal Profits and Revenues for that Purpose?” There is no “service more pleasing and acceptable in the Sight of God Almighty,” Soderini concludes, that a citizen can perform than to help the fatherland.36

Another equally important aspect of the Florentine civil religion was the belief that the republic owed its birth and preservation to God, and therefore had an obligation to respect God and those who convey His message, even at the risk of great perils. This theme emerges with particular power in the dramatic session (or pratica, which was an advisory council summoned by the Signoria to discuss matters of particular importance; the leading citizens were invited to attend) of 14 March 1498, convened to debate how to respond to the papal brief ordering the city to prevent Savonarola from preaching. While some orators held that the city had to obey the papal orders for fear of an ecclesiastic interdiction and because he is the “High Pontiff, vicar of God, lord of the Christians [who] has jurisdiction over the whole world,” others exhorted their listeners to “preserve the liberty of this city of ours, which has given so much to recover it” and emphasized that “it did not strike them that it was honorable for a city to insist, against a servant of God, such as Fra’ Girolamo, that he should abstain from preaching,” while others still pointed out that to prevent Savonarola from preaching would be tantamount to a sin of ingratitude, would offend God, and thus draw down on the city ills far worse than an interdiction.37

The civil religion that lived on in the minds of the Florentines taught them to seek comfort in God in order to decide wisely and to act courageously and firmly in times of danger; likewise Baldassarre Carducci exhorted the Council of Eighty (Consiglio degli Ottanta) on 17 June 1512, when the demise of the republic was clearly imminent, “to turn to God with prayer, oration, and charity that he should give us the grace to take all the advice and decisions that are beneficial to the city.”38 The appeal to God as a guide to good decisions when the liberty of the city is in danger recurs as well in the speech that Pier Soderini delivered before the Great Council (Consiglio Grande), summoned to make a decision concerning the ultimatum of the Holy League that was demanding that Florence allow the return of the Medici. Soderini exhorted the Great Council to reject the ultimatum and to invoke the aid of God to guide the hearts and minds of the citizens. The Great Council (Consiglio Grande), Soderini emphasized, is the “the prince of this city.” The life of the republic depended on its decisions. The citizens need the aid of God in order to understand the decision that will lead to the salvation of the republic and in order to avoid the decision that will lead to its ruin: “Examine how much the Demand of the Viceroy concerns your Liberty, and may God give you Grace to illuminate your Understanding, and cause you to make Choice of the better Part.”39


2. Machiavelli, Orator


From this cultural context, Machiavelli drew, and put into practice, the belief that the civil religion lived on the power of words. Like his compatriots, he knew that without a religion that teaches the people to love liberty, and without the eloquence that touches the souls of the citizens, a free republic cannot live, no matter how well ordered its political institutions. He also knew that the Christian religion, in contrast with the pagan religion, “which was founded on the responses of the oracles and on the sect of the diviners and augurs,” lived on the power of words. He had seen Savonarola persuading the Florentines, even though they were in no way “coarse men,” to accept his religious and political ideas. He acknowledged that Saint Francis and Saint Dominic were able to persuade people with their preachings and with “the example of the life of Christ.”40

He also saw, however, the hypocrisy and fraud that lurked beneath prophetic and apocalyptic preaching. When he received rumors of preachers of that sort, he pretended with his friends that he had been terrorized by the dark predictions, in order to ridicule the seers and those who believed in their visions. The letter to Vettori dated 19 December 1513 is an exemplary document:

In this city of ours—a magnet for all the world’s pitchmen—there is a friar of Saint Francis who is half hermit and who, to increase his standing as a preacher, professes to be a prophet; and yesterday morning in Santa Croce, where he preaches, he said “many things great and wonderful”: that before much time elapses, so that whoever is ninety years of age will be able to see it, there will be an unjust pope created against a just pope, and he will have false prophets with him, he will create cardinals, and he will divide the Church. Item, that the king of France was to be crushed and someone from the House of Aragon was to be master of Italy. Our city would go up in flames and be sacked, the churches would be abandoned and would crumble, the priests dispersed, and we would have to do without divine services for three years. There would be pestilence and widespread famine…. These activities demoralized me so much yesterday that I was supposed to go this morning to see La Riccia, but I did not go; I am not at all sure whether, had I been supposed to go see Riccio, I would have been concerned. I myself did not hear the sermon, for I do not observe such practices, but I have heard it told about in this manner throughout all of Florence.41


Even Savonarola’s sermons were, in his view, filled with hypocrisy and often inspired by tactical political concerns. In a letter to the anti-Savonarola Ricciardo Becchi dated 9 March 1498, Machiavelli emphasizes that “in my judgment, [Savonarola] acts in accordance with the times and colors his lies accordingly.” More than twenty years had passed since he wrote that letter, and Machiavelli, this time from Carpi, recalled the monk again as a “wily” man, agile and quick to shift position and attitude according to what a specific situation demanded: such a quality was positive for Machiavelli in a politician, less so in a religious.42

And yet, in the same letter, Machiavelli showed that he was well aware of how much good preachers might do if they truly imitated Saint Francis:

They would like a preacher who would teach them the way to paradise, and I should like to find one who would teach them the way to go to the Devil. Furthermore, they would like their man to be prudent, honest, and genuine, and I should like to find one who would be madder than Ponzo, wilier than Fra Girolamo, and more hypocritical than Frate Alberto, because I think it would be a fine thing—something worthy of the goodness of these times—should everything we have experienced in many friars be experienced in one of them. For I believe that the following would be the true way to go to Paradise: learn the way to Hell in order to steer clear of it. Moreover, since I am aware how much belief there is in an evil man who hides under the cloak of religion, I can readily conjure up how much belief there would be in a good man who walks in truth, and not in pretense, tramping through the muddy footprints of Saint Francis.43


Machiavelli was aware that the word, similar in this aspect to amorous passion, entrances, binds up, and chains. Savonarola, he wrote in the First Decennale, held the Florentines “bound to his prophetic word.”44 He was equally aware that persuasion was able to arouse and renew religious faith but was not sufficient on its own to found states and govern peoples. The reason, as he explains in The Prince, is that “the nature of peoples is variable; and it is easy to persuade them of something, but difficult to keep them in that persuasion. And thus things must be ordered in such a mode that when they no longer believe, one can make them believe by force.” For this reason, Savonarola, even though he knew how to keep the Florentines “bound to his prophetic word,” “was ruined in his new orders as soon as the multitude began not to believe in them, and he had no mode for holding firm those who had believed nor for making unbelievers believe.” Moses, Cyrus, Theseus, and Romulus, who were able to use, alongside the power of words, the force of arms, on the other hand, succeeded in holding their peoples in their persuasion, and therefore to “observe their constitutions for long.”45

These passages, so frequently cited, by no means indicate that Machiavelli considered persuasion to be of little importance in political action. He knew the limitations of persuasion, but he was also convinced that the power of words was indispensable for the founding, preservation, and renewal of states. Unarmed prophets come to ruin; but arms alone are not sufficient to educate and emancipate a people. The very birth of cities was in many cases the work of men who knew how to use the power of words. When men lived dispersed “in many small parts,” Machiavelli explains, they were “moved either by themselves or by someone among them of greater authority” to band together to defend themselves. Those who succeeded in moving them to live together possessed authority, that is to say, power, but also knew how to move them with the power of words.46

To an even greater degree the founders of new political orderings were skilled at using the power of words. The power of words is more effective with “coarse” men than with civil peoples; but even civil men can be persuaded to accept “a new order or opinion,” especially if the founders are able to persuade them that the new orders will bring advantages. If the “evident reasons” of the goodness of the new orders were not sufficient to persuade the people to accept the new orderings, the founders will need to pretend that they have been inspired by God.47 In either case, the foundation of new political orderings requires the persuasive power of words.

Machiavelli criticized with blistering words those princes who believed that they could preserve their states with superficial and external eloquence alone:

“The common belief of our Italian princes, before they felt the blows of Transalpine war” —he writes in The Art of War—

was that a prince needed only to think of a sharp reply in his study, to write a fine letter, to show quickness and cleverness in quotable sayings and replies, to know how to spin a fraud, to be adorned with gems and with gold, to sleep and eat with greater splendor than others, to be surrounded with wanton pleasures, to deal with subjects avariciously and proudly, to decay in laziness, to give positions in the army by favor, to despise anybody who showed them any praiseworthy course, and to expect their words to be taken as the responses of oracles. It did not enter the minds of these wretches that they were preparing themselves to be the prey of whoever attacked them.48


All the same, he exhorts the prince to make use of eloquence for the purpose of simulating. A prince, he admonishes, should seem “merciful, faithful, humane, honest, and religious” and in order to succeed in this, he must be a master in the art of using words: “A prince should thus take great care that nothing escape his mouth that is not full of the above-mentioned five qualities and that, to see him and hear him, he should appear all mercy, all faith, all honesty, all humanity, all religion.”49

Even more than a prince must, those who lead armies should be capable of using the power of words. A good commander, Machiavelli explains, must know how to speak to the whole army: “To persuade or dissuade a few about a thing is very easy, because, if words are not enough, you can use authority and force; but the difficulty is to remove from a multitude a belief that is unfavorable and contrary either to the common good or to your belief, when you can use only words proper to be heard by all, since you are trying to persuade them all. Therefore an excellent general is usually an orator because, unless he knows how to speak to the whole army, he will have difficulty in doing anything good; but anything of the sort is in these times of ours completely obsolete.”50
