Only words can instill in the soul of soldiers the conviction and passions necessary for them to be good fighters: “This speaking lightens fear, sets courage afire, increases determination, uncovers deceptions, promises, rewards, shows perils and the way to escape them, reproaches, begs, threatens, fills with hope, praises, berates and does everything through which human passions are extinguished or excited.” A prince or a republic that wishes to establish a new militia and give luster to the military art must accustom “its soldiers to hearing their generals speak, and must accustom its generals to speak skillfully.”51

Like the political writers of humanism, Machiavelli considered eloquence to be the very soul of civil life, especially in free republics.52 He praised liberty of speech in the assembly as an order that was well suited to a republic of good citizens. Before Rome became corrupt, he writes, “A tribune, or any other citizen whatever, could propose a law to the people, on which every citizen was able to speak, either in favor or against, before it was decided. This was a good order when the citizens were good, because it was always good that each one who intended a good for the public could propose it; and it is good that each can speak his opinion on it so that the people can then choose the best after each one has been heard.” In contrast he deplored, as a bad custom typical of corrupt republics, the degeneration of the freedom of speech for all into the freedom of speech only for the powerful.53

He knew very well that eloquence has the power to conceal and mask evil intentions. In order to describe this aspect of rhetoric, Machiavelli used the terms adonostare or colorare, that is, to give evil intent the appearance of honesty and present it in a positive light. In classical rhetoric, to “color” or to “justify” were terms that indicated an orator’s skill at presenting facts or propositions in a favorable way.54 Machiavelli cites examples of skill in “coloring,” taken both from international politics and domestic politics. The Venetians, he writes in Florentine Histories, knew that they could not start a war against Florence without at least attempting to justify their actions with some pretense of justice.55 In order to “color” their plan, they said that Florence had gravely damaged them and had made friendship between the two republics impossible.56 Among the examples drawn from the political life of Florence there was the oration of certain citizens who exhorted the Signori to institute a reform of the orderings of the city to end factional strife. The chiefs and promoters of the factions that are destroying the republic, the orator stated, “give decent appearance to their intention and their end with a pious word,” and although they are all enemies of freedom, “they oppress it under color of defending the state either of the best or of the people.”57 Another example is that of Corso Donati, an enemy of the popular government, who “thought he would make the indecency of his intent appear decent with a decent cause,” and in this manner succeeded in persuading many citizens that he was acting out of love of the fatherland.58

Machiavelli was not afraid of eloquence. He believed that the citizens of a free republic were perfectly capable of deciding the truth, even in an assembly in which eloquent men faced off: “As to judging things, if a people hears two orators who incline to different sides, when they are of equal virtue, very few times does one see it not take up the better opinion, and not persuaded of the truth that it hears.”59 When the people deceive themselves, eloquence can return it to the right path: “The desires of free peoples are rarely pernicious to freedom because they arise either from being oppressed or from suspicion that they may be oppressed. If these opinions are false, there is for them the remedy of assemblies, where some good man gets up who in orating demonstrates to them how they deceive themselves; and though peoples, as Tully (or Cicero) says, are ignorant, they are capable of truth and easily yield when the truth is told them by a man worthy of faith.”60 The eloquence of “a good man,” as a true orator must be, can lead back “to the good way” even a “licentious and tumultuous” people.61 When words are inadequate, then weapons decide. In the face of a wicked prince, words are of no use, “nor is there any remedy other than steel.” The social conflicts in Florence, which had not been settled by “disputation,” were decided through the use of violence.62

Machiavelli wrote to educate. He composed The Prince in order to bring back to life with his words a redeemer similar to Moses capable of arousing “obstinate faith,” and “piety,” and to revive the “ancient valor” in the hearts of the Italians. He set down on paper Discourses on Livy in order to persuade his readers to change their way of life, and in order to shape “the spirits” of youths to encourage them to eschew their own times, filled with “every extreme misery, infamy, and reproach” and to emulate the times of antiquity, so filled with virtue and religion. In The Art of War he wished to encourage his contemporaries and posterity to “bring back” the militia into its ancient orders and to rediscover the age-old virtue. He laments the fact that he himself was unable to undertake the work of redemption and, as in Discourses on Livy, hopes that others, in a new age, might be enabled to implement his teaching. He believes that Italy “seems made to revive the dead things of the past” and hopes to inspire the same faith in those who read his pages, so that they will be freed from their fear and timidity in the face of great enterprises. All his greatest works, then, were designed to shape souls, to teach, to revive forgotten ways of life, and to bring back dead ideas and principles. Like the humanist political writers, he knows that education is above all persuasion. For that reason he wrote all his political and historical works in accordance with the rules of classical rhetoric.

He knew how to use the power of words in a masterly fashion because he was a writer by nature and possessed a true civil passion, and because he had studied the art of rhetoric from his earliest education. In the home where Machiavelli grew up, the canonical texts of classical rhetoric were found. “I here record,” writes Bernardo Machiavelli, Niccolò’s father, “that today, 16 December, I returned to Matteo the stationer the Rhetorica ad Herennium by Tully [Cicero] that he lent me several days back, and also I returned to Zanobi the stationer Tully’s De Oratore that he lent me several days ago.”63

While it is highly unlikely that a Florentine from the late fifteenth century could write about politics in a nonrhetorical manner, that the secretary of the second chancellery could do so was entirely impossible.64 Among Machiavelli’s responsibilities was the writing of letters and the composition of political orations. In both the former and latter occupations, he would be expected to demonstrate to a discerning audience that he knew every detail of the art of rhetoric, which is not even to mention the expectation that he would be a skillful practitioner of eloquence on his diplomatic missions. The instructions that the Signoria gave him often contained exhortations to find words and rhetorical devices that would be effective in persuading rulers and princes. The assignment for the legation to Caterina Sforza, dated 12 July 1499, for instance, contains these instructions: “Upon these several points you will enlarge in the most effective language and in the best terms that may suggest themselves to you; so as to convince Her Excellency of our sincere desire for an opportunity to benefit her, and to acknowledge the services which she has rendered to our republic, as also of our entire confidence in her. At the same time, you will point out the necessity of the union of our states, employing the most acceptable language in your endeavor to persuade her to that effect.”65 When he met Caterina Sforza in Forlì, a few months after being appointed secretary of the second chancellery, Machiavelli was skillful at minimizing Florence’s responsibilities in the eyes of the Contessa (“And that if the refusal to accept her services now seemed contrary to these assurances,… the reason of it was the impossibility of providing the means, because of the heavy charges to which our republic was already subjected”) and emphasize on the other hand the willingness to help her (“I pointed out to her Ladyship that it had never been your intention to refuse to do anything that might be agreeable to her”).66

The instructions of the Signoria, dated 18 July 1500, for the legation in France even contained explicit recommendations to use properly the rhetorical devices of amplificatio and extenuatio, two techniques of redescription treated extensively by the Roman theorists of eloquence.67 The extenuatio is used when we wish to attenuate our responsibilities concerning matters or events that could prove damaging, while the amplificatio is used to amplify our merits or the value of the cause that we are defending.68 “It will, furthermore, be proper for you to speak of the capture of our commissioner,” the letter states, “of the persons guilty of this outrage, and of the manner in which it was done, and of the outrages and insults we have had to bear, even from the lowest private soldier. You must make a summary of all these matters, which will go to prove that we have been treated by them more like enemies than friends, amplifying or extenuating these matters as will best serve our cause.”69

The fact that they wrote in this manner meant that the Signori were well aware that Machiavelli was familiar with rhetoric and was capable of using it with prudence.70 And indeed, when Machiavelli and Francesco della Casa succeeded in obtaining an audience with the king of France and his courtiers, they vastly overstated, as the Signori of Florence had recommended, the guilt of the French army in the failure of the Pisa operation: “We began by submitting that inasmuch as the enterprise against Pisa and the siege of the city had, to our great prejudice and the great discredit of his Majesty’s army, an entirely different issue from his other most fortunate and successful enterprises,… and more particularly all that related to the departure from camp of the Gascons, the outrages of the Swiss, and the carrying off of our commissioner, and to the constant parleying of the French commander with the enemy.” And they cunningly understated the shortcomings of the Florentines: “We said that your Lordships had never failed to furnish the most abundant supply of provisions, and that there never had been any deficiency, but that they had been wantonly wasted, and that those who had brought them into camp had been overwhelmed with all sorts of insults and bad treatment.”71

A full-fledged synthesis of rhetorical instructions is contained in the commission that Marcello Virgilio Adriani, secretary of the first chancellery and teacher of eloquence, wrote to Machiavelli to explain how he should address Giampagolo Baglioni, tyrant of Perugia. His speech should have a preamble that would put Baglioni in a well-disposed state, and a proper arrangement of the arguments, in accordance with the classical technique of the dispositio.72 Then it would have as its central theme the harm that can be caused to a prince by gaining the reputation of being an ingrate, it will be measured in its amplifications and extenuations so as to move the interlocutor to reveal his inner thoughts. Adriani wrote, “You will shape your remarks to him in such wise manner as to make it appear that this is the only object of your mission”; you must then “stir [Baglioni] up in some way,” explaining to him the blame that will fall on him for the “the ingratitude that may be imputed to him for all the benefits he has received in the past, and his want of faith as a soldier, the two essential and fundamental points that men look to”; finally, “you must endeavor to remove the apprehensions which he pretends to have” and “you must try to bring him to the point of revealing to you the real motive for his determination.”

The account that Machiavelli sent back to Florence 11 April 1505 showed once again that he was capable of deploying the tools of rhetoric in a masterful manner, even though he was unable, as had been easy to foresee, to dissuade Baglioni from his decisions. “I said to him,” wrote Machiavelli, “that those who learned of his reputation for ingratitude and faithlessness

would regard him as a stumbling horse which nobody would ride for fear of getting his neck broken; that matters of this kind were not to be judged by learned doctors, but by gentlemen [Baglioni had adduced in his own defense the opinions of Perugian jurists]; and that whoever attached any value to wearing armor, and desired to win honor by his arms, could lose nothing that was prized so much as the reputation for good faith, and that it seemed to me that in this case he staked his very lightly. And as he persisted in saying that he could justify his conduct, I answered that men ought to do everything in their power never to be obliged to justify themselves, for justification presupposes error or the suspicion of error…. And thus I pricked him right and left, speaking to him as a friend, and as though merely coming from me; and although I noticed several times that he changed countenance, yet he gave no indication that could make me hope he would change his determination.73


3. Creator of Myths


Machiavelli continued to reflect, even many years later, on the characters that he had met and known and, based on their stories, he developed fullfledged political myths. He wrote about Caterina Sforza in Discourses on Livy, narrating and commenting on an episode that was at the time quite notorious. “Some Forlí conspirators,” wrote Machiavelli.

killed Count Girolamo, their lord, and took his wife and his children, who were small. Since it appeared to them that they could not live secure if they did not become masters of the fortress, and the castellan was not willing to give it to them, Madonna Caterina (so the countess was called) promised the conspirators that if they let her enter it, she would deliver it to them and they might keep her children with them as hostages. Under this faith they let her enter it. As soon as she was inside, she reproved them from the walls for the death of her husband and threatened them with every kind of revenge. And to show that she did not care for her children, she showed them her genital parts, saying that she still had the mode for making more of them.74


In reference to the passage from Discourses on Livy that I quoted, which gave origin to the “leggenda della rocca,” or “legend of the fortress,” I believe that we can safely rule out the possibility that when Machiavelli wrote those lines he did so merely to offer an example of female nobility and strength of character to set up against male ferocity. If that had been his chief intent, he would certainly have found in the sources that were available to him more eloquent subjects than the story of Caterina. He might well, for example, had cited the story of Epicharis from Tacitus’s Annales. While the organizers of the conspiracy hesitated in uncertainty, writes Tacitus, “a certain Epicharis (how she informed herself is uncertain, as she had never before had a thought of anything noble) began to stir and upbraid the conspirators. Wearied at last of their long delay, she endeavoured, when staying in Campania, to shake the loyalty of the officers of the fleet at Misenum, and to entangle them in a guilty complicity.” This “freedwoman,” who had not previously distinguished herself with honorable deeds, when betrayed by Volusius Proculus, gave an example of strength and dignity that Tacitus describes with great eloquence:

Nero, meanwhile, remembering that Epicharis was in custody on the information of Volusius Proculus, and assuming that a woman’s frame must be unequal to the agony, ordered her to be torn on the rack. But neither the scourge nor fire, nor the fury of the men as they increased the torture that they might not be a woman’s scorn, overcame her positive denial of the charge. Thus the first day’s inquiry was futile. On the morrow, as she was being dragged back on a chair to the same torments (for with her limbs all dislocated she could not stand), she tied a band, which she had stript off her bosom, in a sort of noose to the arched back of the chair, put her neck in it, and then straining with the whole weight of her body, wrung out of her frame its little remaining breath. All the nobler was the example set by a freedwoman at such a crisis in screening strangers and those whom she hardly knew, when freeborn men, Roman knights, and senators, yet unscathed by torture, betrayed, every one, his dearest kinsfolk.75


Despite her magnificence, Machiavelli placed no emphasis on the figure of Epicharis, which Tacitus had offered to him, as it were, on a silver platter. He wrote merely that “in the Pisonian conspiracy there was a woman called Epicharis, who in the past had been the mistress of Nero. Judging that it would be to the purpose to put among the conspirators a captain of some triremes whom Nero kept as his guard, she communicated to him the conspiracy but not the conspirators. Hence, when that captain broke his faith and accused her to Nero, so much was Epicharis’s audacity in denying it that Nero, left confused, did not condemn her.” Instead, he places a great deal of emphasis on Caterina’s act, and even adds a few details of his own to the accounts that were already circulating during the time of his legation to Forlì.

The historical record and legend present us with two different images of Caterina.76 The record describes Caterina as a loving mother who might, out of maternal charity, compromise the interests of the state and who was therefore left out of the negotiations with the conspirators by the castellan; the legend depicts her as a woman who did not hesitate, in order to preserve her own power, to risk the lives of her children and even to consider them as replaceable by others to which she might give birth. Precisely because Machiavelli had met Caterina in person, he should have put more stock in history than in legend. During his mission to Forlì, he had seen with his own eyes Caterina neglecting affairs of state because she was “indisposed and in bad spirits over the great illness that befell Lodovico, her and Giovanni de’ Medici’s son.”77 He preferred however to accept, and hand down to posterity, the account of Caterina smothering her own love for her offspring, knowing full well that it was a legend.

Why did he prefer legend over history? And why did he select from among all the various versions of the story the most theatrical one? According to the chroniclers of Forlì, the popular story had two variants: in one, it is said that Caterina answered in manly fashion to the conspirators that they should do what they wanted, because she was perfectly capable of having new children; in the other variant, “hiking up her skirts, she showed them that she had the equipment to make new babies.” The narrative in Discourses on Livy clearly draws on the second variant and, indeed, accentuates its expressive power: he does not merely say that Caterina raised her skirts and showed them the equipment, but writes that “she showed them her genital parts,” a detail that is not found in the other common versions. In Niccolò’s account, the gesture with which Caterina challenged the conspirators became a direct one: she did not simply raise her skirts; she displays her genitalia to the male conspirators so that they can see her power of engendering new life.

The image of Caterina standing behind the battlements of the fortress and hiking up her skirts was itself sufficiently vivid to satisfy Machiavelli’s purpose. Indeed, in the context of the reasoning that he was putting forth, even the milder version of the episode would have been sufficient. Machiavelli is interested in explaining that it is highly dangerous, after killing a prince in a conspiracy, to leave the children, the brothers, or any other close relatives alive who may hope to take revenge. In order to present this argument he might perfectly well have limited himself to narrating the story in the chaster tones that he employed just a few years later in the Florentine Histories, where he limited himself to writing that “as soon as [Caterina] was inside, she threatened them with death and every kind of punishment in revenge of her husband; and when they threatened to kill her children, she said that she had with her the mode of producing more of them.”78

It may be that when he tells the story in Discourses on Livy of the episode of the fortress, Machiavelli had classical sources in mind and wished to imagine Caterina as one of those great women of antiquity described by his historians. One of Machiavelli’s sources might very well have been Tacitus, who in his Histories recounts the heroism of a Ligurian woman in the face of the ferocious soldiers of Otho:

In the field indeed they had secured no plunder; their rustic adversaries were poor, and their arms worthless; nor could they be taken prisoners, for they were swift of foot, and knew the country well. But the rapacity of the troops glutted itself in the ruin of an innocent population. The horror of these acts was aggravated by a noble display of fortitude in a Ligurian woman; she had concealed her son. and when the soldiers, who believed that some money had been hidden with him, questioned her with torture as to where she was hiding him, she pointed to her bosom, and replied, “It is here that he is concealed”; nor could any subsequent threats or even death itself make her falter in this courageous and noble answer.79


It is all the same more likely that Machiavelli had in mind a passage from Plutarch’s Moralia concerning the courage of the Persian women.80 The Persian women, Plutarch recounts, “ran out to meet them before the city, plucking up their petticoats to their middle, saying, Ye vilest varlets among men, whither so fast? Ye surely cannot find a refuge in these parts, from whence ye came forth.” Mortified by what they saw and by the words they heard, the Persian men regained their courage and turned back to face the enemy that was chasing them.

Both Tacitus’s narrative and that of Plutarch are nonetheless different from Machiavelli’s. The Ligurian woman challenges the men, displaying her power to engender life, but she does so in order to protect her hidden son. Her act is a gesture of resistance in the face of male ferocity, which ultimately crushes her. The Persian women are not challenging their enemies; they are doing their best to make their men ashamed of their display of cowardice: in this case the generative power of the female sex is not opposed to savagery, but rather to immaturity. In his narrative of the episode concerning Caterina Sforza, her open display of her female sex organs strikes terror into the males present and reveals the cowardice concealed behind their savagery. By displaying their sex organs, the Persian women in Plutarch redeem their men from their cowardice; Caterina in Machiavelli’s account, on the other hand, uncovers the cowardice that accompanies male savagery. The Ligurian woman in Tacitus challenges male ferocity, but she loses; Caterina challenges male ferocity and she wins. Like Tacitus and Plutarch, Machiavelli also wishes to leave to posterity a eulogy of the virtue of women, but his narrative contains elements that are absent in the classical sources.

Niccolò intended to build a narrative of the virtue of women. Caterina has greater courage and more “heart” than the males who threaten her and her sons. She acts as a true prince, but she does not transform herself into a male in order to be a prince. She affirms her virtue by displaying the generative power that is specific to her as a woman, and in this way she wins for herself that honor that was commonly perceived as a male quality par excellence.81 In the “leggenda della rocca,” Machiavelli in other words employs a rhetorical invention in the classical sense. He finds among the numerous narratives available to him elements that allow him to recount with unusual expressive power a story that reverses the commonplace of his time that identified virtue and honor as male qualities, and in this way he presents Caterina Sforza as a great example of princely virtue.82

The best-known example of Machiavelli’s skill at mythologizing the characters he met during his diplomatic missions is, in any case, his description of Cesare Borgia. In the dispatches that he sent back to the Signoria, Machiavelli placed an extraordinary emphasis on the figure of the duke. Instead of using indirect discourse, as he normally did to summarize diplomatic exchanges, he had him speak directly, in the first person, to ensure that the Ten of Liberty and Balia perceive the power of his words. In the letter dated 26 June 1502, for instance, he quotes these words of the duke directly: “I am determined to understand first of all with whom I am negotiating our agreement, and next I wish to have good assurances from you, and if that is done, you will always have me in agreement with your intentions; if not, I will be obliged to continue my undertaking and make sure I am safe from you, lest you present a danger to me. Because I am all too well aware that your city is not well disposed toward me, indeed, it has murderous impulses as far as I am concerned; and they have tried to raise very serious charges against me with the Pope and before the King of France.” After reporting that both he and Francesco Soderini denied and refuted the last portion of the duke’s statements and asked him to be more explicit in his views of Florence, he returned to the duke’s direct discourse in order to ensure that the government of the republic should clearly hear the terrible threat: “I am quite sure that you are wise and you can understand me, but I’ll state it again in simple words: I dislike this government and I cannot trust it; you must change it and you must give me reassurance that you are keeping your word to me; otherwise you will soon understand that I do not mean to live in this manner. And if you choose not to have me as a friend, you will see what it is like to have me as an enemy.”83

The Florentine envoys, in their response, defended the honor of the republic and tried to persuade the duke to set aside his threatening plans, availing themselves of the cliché of Florence as a city that always kept its pledges, even at the cost of burdensome consequences: “We answered that the city had the finest government that could be found, and if she was satisfied with it, her friends could be just as satisfied with it. And as for keeping our pledges, we did not think that there was anyone in Italy who could claim to have done better, nor any that had suffered for it as much as her; and that his Excellency should decide for once to be the good friend that he claimed to be and he would find a reliable ally.”84

They could not find more suitable words than these, but the duke “would not shift” from his position. Indeed, he replied, with even more derisive words: “And what did you think, in faith, that I intended to justify myself?” Machiavelli and Soderini appealed to his magnanimity, one of the virtues that treatises of rhetorical hail as proper to princes, and they insisted on Florence’s loyalty and interest: “We said to him that, given the greatness of his soul and considering your friendship and obedience toward him, we expected that he should begin to offer you some great benefit, as we knew was in his power, especially since the lord Vitellozzo was his man.” The duke replied that Florence had not deserved any benefits, to the contrary, it had forfeited any such claims. The last resource of which the two Florentine orators availed themselves was an appeal to justice: “What reason have we given you to have your generals and men to attack us?” In response to this argument, the duke attributed all responsibility for the attacks on the Florentine possessions to Vitellozzo Vitelli, then reiterated that he wished to have Florence as either an enemy or a friend: “Decide which it is to be soon, because I cannot keep my army here [Urbino], since this is a mountain town, and it would be damaged thereby; there can be no half measures between me and you: you must either be friends to me, or enemies.”85

What emerges from Machiavelli’s account is a duke who is a master of the rhetorical art of “coloring” his actions and changing the names of things. He wished to deprive Florence of part of its dominion but he told the Florentine emissaries that “he did not think that he was taking anything from them”; he wished to make himself the master of a large state in central Italy, but he stated that his goal was not to “tyrannize,” but to “destroy tyrants.” Machiavelli, however, was also familiar with rhetoric, and he immediately realized that the duke was “coloring” reality in order to justify his actions, and immediately wrote to that effect to the Ten:

The Duke did not accept those places for himself, nor will he make terms with them, but has referred them to Rome, saying that all he aimed at was to have them return to their obedience to the Church, and to suppress the factions in those places, and to deliver them from their tyrants. And to give more color to these professions, he has refused until now permission to the banished to return to Perugia. It seems even that he has promised to the deputies who came to him from Perugia that the banished shall not return there, telling them that his intention had not been to deliver them from one tyrant for the purpose of replacing him by ten others.86


Skillful in “coloring” and changing the names of things, the duke knew how to use silence and secrecy effectively. Machiavelli did not fail to note this aspect as well: “As I have mentioned to you before, the Duke is so secret in all he does that he never communicates his designs to any one. His first secretaries have repeatedly assured me that he never makes his plans until the moment of his giving orders for their execution, and he gives these orders only when forced by necessity, and on the spur of the moment, and never otherwise.” Machiavelli attempted with the most effective methods he knew to persuade the duke to talk, but he was unsuccessful. “Yet not-withstanding my efforts to get at his real thoughts, he always avoided the subject, so that I did not succeed in getting more from him than what I have written,” he wrote to Florence on 7–8 October; and again on 20 November: “More than this I could not by any further remarks of mine obtain from him.”87 In order to delve into the soul of the duke, he attempted to interpret the significance of his deeds, such as the spectacular execution of Ramirro de Orco. In his letter from Cesena dated 26 December 1502, he reported that “Messer Ramiro was found today cut into two pieces in the public square, and his body still remains there, so that the whole population has been able to see it. The cause of his death is not precisely known, other than that it was the pleasure of his Excellency thus to show that he has the power to make and unmake men at his will, and according to their merits.”88

With his actions and his words, the duke imparted to Machiavelli invaluable lessons in the art of statesmanship. He taught him that when necessity forces his hand, a prince can use any and all means to preserve his state, and that God will forgive him for his actions. He says it to him openly in Urbino: “He wished to be excused with God and men, if he used whatever means were at his disposal to assure himself of his state”; he repeated that idea to him a few months later at Assisi: “He should be sorry to be obliged to injure others, but he felt that he would be excused by God and by men, and even by the Siennese themselves, as being forced to it by necessity.”89 These words remained impressed in Machiavelli’s mind. Ten years later, when he was writing The Prince, he repeated them with reference to cruelties well employed, “that are done at a stroke, out of the necessity to secure oneself.” Those who act in this way, he writes, “can have some remedy for their state with God and with men.”90 This is what the true prince should be like: a master of the art of coloring and simulating with words, certain that he can rely on God’s pardon, and godlike in the grandeur of his soul and his certainty that he can overcome all obstacles: “For on the one hand there is the Duke with his unheard of good fortune, with a courage and confidence almost superhuman, and believing himself capable of accomplishing whatever he undertakes.”91

Machiavelli also showed that he was a skilled master of the rules of the ars dicendi (the art of speaking) in the orations that he composed during his years as secretary. One example of this is the oration entitled Words to Be Spoken on the Law for Appropriating Money, after Giving a Little Introduction and Excuse (Parole da dirle sopra la provisione del danaio, facto un poco di proemio et di scusa), which he wrote, probably for the lifetime gonfalonier Pier Soderini in March 1503, to be delivered before some assembly or special session, or in the presence of the Consiglio Maggiore (Major Council) or the Council of the Eighty.92 The specific purpose of the oration was to urge the approval of a new tax to ensure that Florence could defend herself from the attacks of her enemies and at the same time improve her reputation among her allies.

In order to convey to the members of the Council the need to disburse money, Machiavelli set forth a general principle that, since it applied to the security of all states, applied to Florence as well: “All the cities that ever at any time have been ruled by an absolute prince, by aristocrats or by the people, as is this one, have had for their protection force combined with prudence, because the latter is not enough alone, and the first either does not produce things or, when they are produced, does not maintain them. Force and prudence, then, are the might of all the governments that ever have been or will be in the world. Hence any man who has considered change of kingdoms and the destruction of provinces and of cities has not seen them caused by anything other than failure in arms or in good sense.”93 Now that it had endowed itself with an institution that ensured greater prudence in political decisions, by naming a gonfalonier for life, Florence should waste no time equipping itself with the military forces needed for self-defense.

Having set forth the proposal, Machiavelli moved on, again in accordance with the rules of the art of rhetoric, to refute the belief of the citizens of Florence who were opposed to budgeting money and who adduced the argument that, in case of danger, the king of France would hasten to the city’s rescue. In order to move the Florentines away from this facile and deeply rooted opinion, Machiavelli availed himself of another general principle of political wisdom, soundly corroborated by the experience of history: “Every city, every state ought to consider as enemies all those who can hope to take possession of her territory and against whom she cannot defend herself. Never was princedom or republic wise that was willing to let her territory stand in the power of others or which, so letting it stand, thought she held it securely.”94 In order to make that same concept more evident and persuasive, he made use of an image: “It is not always possible to put your hand on another’s sword, and therefore it is good to have a sword at your side and to gird it on when the enemy is at a distance, because afterward another man is too late and you have no recourse.”

Well aware that the Florentines were in serious danger of losing their liberty, Machiavelli attempted to overcome their greed with the fear of death. To that end, he recounts a terrifying historical example with which his fellow-citizens were quite familiar:

Many of you can remember when Constantinople was taken by the Turks. That Emperor foresaw his ruin. He called upon his citizens, not being able with his organized forces to make proper provision. He showed them their dangers, showed them the preventives, and they ridiculed him. The siege came on. Those citizens who had before had no respect for the exhortations of their lord, when they heard within their walls the thunder of the artillery and the yells of the army of their enemies, ran weeping to the Emperor with their bosoms full of money; but he drove them away, saying: “Go to die with this money, since you have not wished to live without it.”95


Last of all, loyal to the rules of political rhetoric, he concluded his oration with an exhortation. He knew very well that his fellow citizens had a deep-rooted pride in their liberty. He therefore did his best to exert leverage on this emotion to urge them to make those decisions that are incumbent on a people that have always cherished their liberty: “Such a fall I cannot believe in, when I see that you are free Florentines and that in your own hands rests your liberty. For that liberty I believe you will have such regards as they always have had who are born free and hope to live free.”96

Machiavelli used the force of eloquence not only to sustain the love of liberty but also to attempt to remove from the minds and souls of the Florentines the bad old religious mentality that taught them to trust in the aid of God in times of danger rather than seeking active remedies in wisdom and strength, as was the case when the duke Cesare Borgia left Rome with his armies “and everybody prayed God to give us time.” He placed before the eyes of his fellow citizens the image of a God that never provides aid, but who punishes those who—despite being able—refuse to see and refuse to understand: “And seeing that you can learn and see, and yet that you do not learn and see things about which even your enemies are astonished, I am convinced that God has not yet punished us as he intends, and that he is keeping us for greater chastisement.” And he warned, in his final exhortation, that “the heavens do not wish or are not able to support a city that is determined to fall in any case.”97

Like the authors of the protestatio of justice from which I have quoted, Machiavelli described a God who is the friend of those who are wise and strong, emphasized the religious significance of the virtue of justice, and insisted that the Christian religion is an ally of liberty. In his Allocuzione ad un magistrato, which he composed around 1519–20, he made use of a parable: “Men in the first age were so good that the gods were not ashamed to come down from heaven and inhabit the earth with them. Later, as virtues vanished and vices abounded, they gradually began to withdraw to heaven, and the last to leave the earth was Justice.”98 From that time on, Justice no longer returned to the world of men, with the exception of a few fortunate cities that, thanks to Justice, have become peaceful and united. Justice engenders “unity in states and kingdoms; unity provides power and the preservation of states and kingdoms; justice defends the poor and the powerless, represses the rich and the powerful, humiliates the arrogant and the bold, restrains the rapacious and the greedy, chastises the insolent, and disperses the violent; unity engenders in the states that equality that is desirable in a state to ensure its preservation.” For that reason, justice is the virtue that “pleases God above all others.” In the conclusion of the oration, quoting from the “golden and divine” verses of Dante, he reiterates once again that “God loves justice and piety.”99

Machiavelli’s tie with the rhetoric of a civil religion also emerges in the Discursus florentinarum rerum post mortem iunioris Laurentii Medices (November 1520-February 1521). In this piece, he reaffirmed with particular force the idea that God rewards those who have loved and served their fatherland: “I believe that the greatest honor that men can achieve is that which is voluntarily given to them by their fatherland; I believe that the greatest good that can be done, as well as the most acceptable in the eyes of God, is that which is done on behalf of the fatherland.”100 It is true that here Machiavelli was writing to persuade Cardinal Giulio de’ Medici to restore a republican government in Florence, but the topos that God loves those who serve the fatherland is a rhetorical artifice that Machiavelli uses in other writings as well.

His goal was to bring about the reopening of the hall of the Great Council (Consiglio Grande) and to restore the republican regime, and that is why he makes use of the conventional image of a God who loves those who desire the best interests of the fatherland. To invoke the name of “almighty God and His glorious mother, ever virgin, and the holy Saint John the Baptist, and any and all other advocates and protectors of the city of Florence” at the beginning of the Minuta di provvisione per la riforma dello stato di Firenze, as did Machiavelli in 1522, is a simple and straightforward piece of convention. It is not however a piece of conventional ritual to invoke God, who loves those who reform republics in order to endow them with a free and peaceful way of life. Without that God, men will not know how to, indeed cannot become, creators of great political enterprises. And that is why he calls on that God in his exhortations: “As our Magnificent and Excellent Lordships may consider that no law and no order is more laudable among men or more pleasing in God’s sight than that which ordains a true, united, and holy republic, in which advice is freely given, deliberations prudently undertaken, and orders faithfully carried out.”101


4. His Finest Piece of Oratory


The most important of all his political orations is The Prince. Machiavelli wrote the work to show his expertise in the art of statesmanship, but he was not writing as a detached expert. He felt a religious veneration for the art of statesmanship. He clearly shows it when he speaks about the art of founding and guiding states as “that food that alone is mine and for which I was born.”102 Having derived it from the great men of antiquity and from his own direct experience in Palazzo Vecchio and in the courts, Machiavelli wished to donate that food to someone who could truly savor it and translate it into a work of creation and political redemption. He wishes to persuade and motivate, and it is with that goal in mind that he composed the entire work, from the first page to the last, in strict compliance with the rules of ars retorica.103

In order to understand the rhetorical structure of The Prince it is quite sufficient to focus our attention on the Exhortatio. Many writers have judged it to be useless, harmful, unsuccessful, and poorly linked to the rest of the work.104 It is, however, a perfect conclusion to the text. The rules of classical rhetoric prescribe that, for a political oration to be persuasive, it must end, after a brief summation of the theses put forth, with a peroratio (conclusion) or an exhortatio (exhortation) in which the orator touches the passions of the listeners to ensure that they decide or act in accordance with his advice. The orator must use above all indignation (indignation), if he intends to arouse disdain in his listeners, and conquestio (lamentation), in order to arouse compassion.105 In the first case, he must emphasize that something is grim, cruel, nefarious, and tyrannical;106 in the second case, he must insist especially on the innocence of the victim and emphasize the victim’s weakness.107 Skilled orator that he was, Machiavelli diligently put into practice the teachings of the classical masters. The book’s concluding chapter is a full-fledged exhortation, constructed in accordance with the techniques of the indignatio and the conquestio: to move a potential redeemer to indignation, he emphasizes the “barbarous cruelties and insults”; in order to arouse compassion, he describes an Italy which is “more enslaved than the Hebrews, more servile than the Persians, more dispersed than the Athenians: without a head, without order, beaten, despoiled, torn, pillaged, and having endured ruin of every sort.”

Without the Exhortatio, The Prince would be stripped of one of the most effective devices for arousing just passions, and would therefore be ill suited to attain the objectives for which Machiavelli wrote it, first and foremost to persuade a redeemer to take on the challenge of emancipating Italy. Among these devices are the reference to God who loves justice and a civil way of life, a God who is the friend of those who wish to found and reform states and redeem peoples. Machiavelli writes that those who attempted to liberate Italy but failed seemed as if they had been “ordered by God for her redemption,” though in reality they were not. Those men who succeeded in their efforts at redemption, on the other hand, such as Cyrus, Theseus, and especially Moses, truly were ordered by God. God was “a friend to them,” as He would be a friend to others who truly wished to aid in the redemption of Italy. The signs of the friendship of God were evident, especially in the case of Moses: “Here may be seen extraordinary things without example, brought about by God: the sea has opened; a cloud has escorted you along the way; the stone has poured forth water; here manna has rained.” The alliance between religion and eloquence concludes his greatest political oration.

The book’s rhetorical structure is revealed as well in the dedicatory letter to Lorenzo de’ Medici. It serves as a preamble and as such it is intended to render the reader well disposed, docile, and benevolent. In order to achieve this result, on which the successful outcome of the entire discourse depends, Machiavelli emphasizes, as the classical authors of rhetoric advised, his good qualities, his experience, his knowledge of affairs of state, the sacrifices that he has had to make to obtain that experience and knowledge, and the bad fortune that has persecuted him, keeping his merits from attaining adequate recognition. He writes that he has condensed in this book “the knowledge of the actions of great men, learned by me from long experience with modern things and a continuous reading of ancient ones,” attained “in so many years and with so many hardships and dangers.” To be sure to make the reader properly disposed to accept his advice and his exhortations, Machiavelli must also remove the doubts or hostile opinions concerning his own person and his authority in providing political advice: first of all, the prejudice, which was current in the Florence of his time, that a man of the people could not properly offer advice in affairs of state, and that such a privilege was exclusively the domain of the powerful and great. In order to remove this prejudice, he describes his status as a man of the commonfolk as a condition that better enables him to see political matters than the great and mighty: “To know well the nature of peoples one needs to be prince, and to know well the nature of princes one needs to be of the people.”

The former secretary of the Florentine Republic under Soderini, imprisoned just a few months previous on charges of conspiracy, Machiavelli cannot fail to express his loyalty to the house of Medici: “Therefore, your Magnificence, take this small gift in the spirit with which I send it. If your Magnificence considers and reads it diligently, you will learn from it my extreme desire that you arrive at the greatness that fortune and your other qualities promise you.” Nor does he miss the opportunity counseled by the classics to point out that he has suffered though innocent and that his merits have been overlooked: “And if your Magnificence will at some time turn your eyes from the summit of your height to these low places, you will learn how undeservedly I endure a great and continuous malignity of fortune.”108

He promised—and it was a promise that he kept—not to adorn or fill his book “with fulsome phrases nor with pompous and magnificent words, nor with any blandishment or superfluous ornament whatever” as so many other political writers had done. “For I wanted it either not to be honored for anything or to please solely for the variety of the matter and the gravity of the subject.” This choice of style was not a rejection of rhetoric, but rather a result of his thorough knowledge of the rules of the art, in particular the rules that govern the selection of style and the usage of ornamentation (“ornamenta”).109 The Roman master had explained that an oration of the deliberative genre should be in a simple and grave style (“simplex et gravis”), because the material under discussion itself possessed magnificence and splendor.110

The simple, grave style nonetheless does not exclude the ornaments that make an orator’s discourse clear and persuasive. Among the various ornaments in question, examples taken from history are especially effective. History, Cicero had written, is “the evidence of time, the light of truth, the life of memory, the directress of life, the herald of antiquity.”111 Quintilian in his De institutione oratoria had explained that history seems to repeat itself and that the experience of the past is a valid aid to reason. The orations delivered before the people and the opinions discussed in the senate offer valuable examples of persuasion and dissuasion.112 History, moreover, has the power of instilling a love of virtue with its narrative of great examples from antiquity, and for that reason, history is far more effective than philosophy.113

The belief that history and historical examples were the best source of political wisdom was a commonplace in Florentine culture of the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries. History, writes Salutati, teaches us what we need to do, both in private life and in political life. Political advice based on historical examples is much more persuasive than advice based on abstract principles. Human affairs tend to repeat themselves, and men do not change over time, just as the laws of the world remain the same. Thanks to the knowledge of history, therefore, it is possible to understand the present and make conjectures about the future.114 The Florentine historians of the fifteenth century were of the same opinion. In the preface to his history of the Florentine people Leonardo Bruni emphasizes that writing about history is a “highly useful” thing because history allows us to know the actions and thoughts of ancient people in various eras (“partiti presi”) and therefore teaches us prudence (“fare prudenti”).115 Moreover, by placing before our eyes examples of excellent men, history encourages us to pursue virtue.116 Iacopo Bracciolini, in the preface to the translation into Italian of his father Poggio’s Istoria Fiorentina observed:

Hence it becomes clear how useful it [history] is for the human race and to what degree, if we wish to express our gratitude, we have an obligation with it; as it is the sole faithful custodian of our actions, and it is the way that reminds us of the works of singular men, urging us to work to become immortal, and through the actions of others shows us the lives of each one: and councils deliberating, and positions taken, and customs of the republics, and great variety of fortune, and the various events of wars, so that through the examples of others, we can choose that which is useful to us, and to the fatherland.117


Machiavelli, once again, in The Prince follows the teachings of the classic and the Florentine historians. He considers history as a source of political wisdom because he believes that “in all cities and in all peoples there are the same desires and the same humors, as there always have been.”118 He supports all his political advice with historic examples, and he explains the reason why:

No one should marvel if, in speaking, as I will do of principalities that are altogether new both in prince and in state, I bring up the greatest examples. For since men almost always walk on paths beaten by others and proceed in their actions by imitation, unable either to stay on the paths of others altogether or to attain the virtue of those whom you imitate, a prudent man should always enter upon the paths beaten by great men, and imitate those who have been most excellent, so that if his own virtue does not reach that far, it is at least in the odor of it. He should do as prudent archers do when the place they plan to hit appears too distant, and knowing how far the strength of their bow carries, they set their aim much higher than the place intended, not to reach such height with their arrow, but to be able with the aid of so high an aim to achieve their design.119


Therefore Machiavelli’s examples are not scientific, but oratorical. They do not serve the purpose of demonstrating the empirical validity of a scientific law, but rather to render more persuasive a piece of political advice and to stimulate the desire to emulate a specific way of acting.120

The best way of explaining a concept, the classics of rhetoric teach us, is to render it visible to the audience by means of similes, images, and metaphors. Machiavelli was a faithful pupil in this as well. When he wishes to convey that a new prince should be capable of using both force and deceit, he avails himself of the images of the fox and the lion: “Thus, since a prince is compelled of necessity to know well how to use the beast, he should pick the fox and the lion, because the lion does not defend itself from snares and the fox does not defend itself from wolves. So one needs to be a fox to recognize snares and a lion to frighten the wolves.”121

In order to explain that a prince should never entrust his self-defense to the armies of another prince, he relies on a “figure” of the Old Testament: “When David offered to Saul to go and fight Goliath, the Philistine challenger, Saul, to give him spirit, armed him with his own arms—which David, as soon as he had them on, refused, saying that with them he could not give a good account of himself, and so he would rather meet the enemy with his sling and his knife.”122 Machiavelli reads the Bible judiciously.”123 He is not interested in grasping the authentic sense of the stories, but rather in extracting from them the “flavor that they have within them,” that is to say, teachings that penetrate deep within and instill a desire to act and to emulate.124 The act of Saul giving David his armor has been interpreted as one of the finest acts of friendship in the Bible: “The exchange of clothing and arms between the two men expresses the gift of what is most intimate in man, and shows that it is necessary to be willing to give anything to one’s friends.”125 But this time, Machiavelli is not interested in friendship. His imagination does not halt with the gesture of Saul who gives his arms to David but focuses instead on David’s gesture, as he strips off the armor that Saul gave him. This figure, in Machiavelli’s view, teaches that “the arms of others either fall off your back or weigh you down or hold you tight.”

Machiavelli also uses with complete confidence the technique of irony as a means of derision. As the classics had taught us, he describes persons and actions with the greatest seriousness, but intends precisely the reverse.126 One example is the chapter on ecclesiastic principalities where he writes that those states maintain themselves without virtue and without fortune,

for they are sustained by orders that have grown old with religion, which have been so powerful and of such a kind that they keep their princes in the state however they proceed and live. These alone have states, and do not defend them; they have subjects, and do not govern them; and the states, though ungoverned, do not care, and they neither think of becoming estranged from such princes nor can they. Thus, only these principalities are secure and prosperous. But as they subsist by superior causes, to which the human mind does not reach, I will omit speaking of them; for since they are exalted and maintained by God, it would be the office of a presumptuous and foolhardy man to discourse on them.127


What he is really thinking, on the other hand, is that ecclesiastical princes, given the way in which they live and govern, deserve to lose their states, and that they will lose them, even if they proclaim that they derive their power from God.128

Machiavelli also respects the rules of classical rhetoric, which focus on the subdivision of the oration. As Cicero taught, following the exordium (introduction) comes the partition (outline), in which the orator must explain in a clear, concise manner the theme of the speech that he is about to deliver.129 Before concluding with the peroratio (peroration) or with the exhortatio (exhortation), he must then reiterate and summarize the main points and emphasize that he has demonstrated what he set out to show at the beginning of his oration. At the outset of The Prince, he writes: “I shall leave out reasoning on republics because I have reasoned on them at length another time. I shall address myself only to the principality, and shall proceed by weaving together the threads mentioned above; and I shall debate how these principalities may be governed and maintained.”130 As he nears the conclusion of the work, then, in order to reiterate that he has kept that promise, he observes that “when the things written above have been observed prudently, they make a new prince appear ancient and immediately render him more secure and steady in his state than if he had grown old in it.”131

If we keep in mind classical rhetoric then we can understand not only the form, but also the content of The Prince, that is, the end that Machiavelli wished to attain with his advice. Concerning this point, the position of the classic authors vary. In Cicero’s view, political oration should have as its goal both honesty and utility.132 Actions are honest that respect the virtues of justice, wisdom, strength, and temperance; useful actions are those that aim at the security, liberty, and greatness of the republic. In Quintilian’s view, deliberative oratory should have as its goal that which is honest (“quod honestum”).133 The orator, however, must often address an ignorant audience made up of people incapable of understanding that that which is honest cannot be in conflict with that which is useful. In these cases, he must distinguish between that which is useful and that which is honest, and adopt his oratory to common opinion.134 The objective of deliberative oratory must therefore be both honesty and utility. In the opinion of the author of the Rhetorica ad Herennium the goal that an orator has to set for himself when offering advice in political deliberations (“in civili consultatione”) should only be interest. He adds, however, and this is an important point, that interest (“utilitas”) consists of two parts: security (“tuta”) and honesty (“honestas”).135 Then he subdivides security into might and craft (“vim et dolum”).”Might is determined by armies, fleets, arms, engines of war, recruiting of manpower, and the like”; craft “is exercised by means of money, promises, dissimulation, swiftness, deception.”136 Honesty is subdivided into the right and the praiseworthy (“rectum et laudabile”).”The Right is that which is done in accord with Virtue and Duty. Subheads under the Right are Wisdom, Justice, Courage, and Temperance.” On the other hand, “the Praiseworthy is what produces an honourable remembrance, at the time of the event and afterwards.”137

The Roman masters of eloquence were aware that in political deliberations, utility often conflicts with honesty, and they advised various ways in which an orator could resolve this problem.138 In De inventione, Cicero emphasizes that a true orator must put honesty first, second safety, third interest. He adds, however, that in cases in which the safety of the state is truly in danger, the orator can modify the order and place safety first, especially if honesty can be recovered in a later phase through courage and diligence.139 He may also defend a political decision that violates the principle of honesty by claiming that it is necessary for the safety of the state.140 He can also claim that without safety and security it is not possible even to obtain honesty.141

Another way of dealing with the contrast between security and honesty is that found in the Rhetorica ad Herennium. Even if an orator must not propose abandoning virtue, he can still state that in given circumstances virtue does not take the form of those actions that we normally consider virtuous, and he can describe the action that he is recommending in a different way. He may, for instance, state that the actions that others consider courageous are actually cowardly.142 Above all, he can state that “nothing is more useful than salvation”; that no one can make use of virtues if they have failed first to consider security; that not even the gods are of any help to those who recklessly place themselves in peril; that it is not possible to consider honest any action that fails to ensure safety.143 Quintilian’s suggestion points in the same direction: if an orator must place interest above honesty, he must not openly admit that his advice is dishonest (“inhonestum”), but change the names of things.144

In the Florentine intellectual context there were fervent advocates of the primacy of honesty over interest who made no effort at all to express and defend their point of view in public debates over political matters of the utmost gravity. Piero Aldobrandini, speaking during a discussion on foreign affairs, emphasized that “it is best not to break promises made to the Most Christian [king of France], because it has always been an ancient tradition of this Republic not to break our word to others.” In the same debate, Bono Boni exhorted the Ten of Liberty to keep the promises that they had made “because, this city has never broken its word to anyone, and that he would rather die altogether than break his word, because at least that would be a great-spirited death.”145 Another orator reiterated the same point of view: “Those forefathers of mine… always held the same determination, that once you made a promise, you must keep it, indeed, even if it might be dangerous; from which maxim I do not wish at all to vary, because honesty should be put before utility, if necessity, which obeys no law, did not compel us to act otherwise.”146

There were even those who maintained with the same degree of certainty that there are cases in which utility comes before honesty. One faithful follower of Cicero, Matteo Palmieri, wrote for instance:

The truth approved by the greatest minds and by the authority of stern and grave philosophers in no way divides or separates honesty from utility, indeed, it joins them together; and they insist that that which is honest must also be useful, and that which is useful must also be honest, nor do they admit in any way that the two qualities be divided: the wisdom of which is certainly approved and true. But matters are judged otherwise when in disputation the truth itself is examined more closely, and otherwise do words find application in the common opinion of the great masses. To us, speaking at present in the vernacular, it is fitting to employ words in accordance with the usage of the commonfolk, and to leave aside the well-honed subtleties of absolute truth. And as the many say, so shall we, at times something that is not honest may be useful, and something that is not useful may be honest.147


When he is exploring the relationship between the useful and the honest, Machiavelli is probably referring to the political debates in Florence’s public councils, rather than treatises of advice to princes, which he did not know well and which he considered of minor importance.148 In the central section of his oration, he closely hews to Rhetorica ad Herennium and he rejects the Ciceronian doctrine that what is useful is also honest and that what is honest is also useful.149 The end of his advice is utility, but by utility he means both security (understood as force and dolus [cunning]) and honesty (that which is praiseworthy and that which is in keeping with virtue).150 He stated, that “my intent is to write something useful to whoever understands it.” In chapter XXIV, nonetheless, he explicitly refers to security as the objective of his advice. In chapter XVIII he insists on honor and praise: “So let a prince win and maintain his state: the means will always be judged honorable, and will be praised by everyone.” In chapter XV, he explicitly states that his advice is in keeping with virtue, even if it appears to be vice. He explicitly recommends what is just in the Exhortatio: “Their undertaking was not more just than this one, nor easier…. Here there is great justice: ‘For war is just to whom it is necessary’”

If we analyze in a more detailed manner the arrangement of the themes, we can see that he deals, in correspondence with the scheme of the Rhetorica ad Herennium, with “vim et dolum,” that is, weapons, money, promises, simulation and dissimulation; and the “rectum et laudabile,” that is, those actions for which princes are praised or blamed; and honesty.151 In particular, Machiavelli discusses the contrast between that which is honest and that which is useful, in four chapters that occupy the central section of The Prince: “Of Those Things for Which Men and Especially Princes Are Praised or Blamed” (XV); “Of Liberality and Parsimony” (XVI); “Of Cruelty and Mercy, and Whether It Is Better To Be Loved than Feared, or the Contrary” (XVII); “In What Mode Faith Should Be Kept by Princes” (XVIII). In order to resolve the contrast between security and honesty, Machiavelli avails himself both of the argument for the need to postpone considerations of honesty in favor of safety, and of the technique of redescription.

He uses, for instance, the argument of necessity in chapter XV: “Hence it is necessary to a prince, if he wants to maintain himself, to learn to be able not to be good and to use this and not use it according to necessity,” and in chapter XVIII, which concludes the discussion of actions for which princes are praised or blamed: “This has to be understood: that a prince, and especially a new prince, cannot observe all those things for which men are held good, since he is often under a necessity, to maintain his state, of acting against faith, against charity, against humanity, against religion. And so he needs to have a spirit disposed to change as the winds of fortune and variations of things command him, and as I said above, not depart from good, when possible, but know how to enter in evil, when forced by necessity.” He resorts to the technique of the redescription of actions in chapter XV: “And furthermore one should not care about incurring the reputation of those vices without which it is difficult to save one’s state; for if one considers everything well, one will find something appears to be virtue, which if pursued would be one’s ruin, and something else appears to be vice, which if pursued results in one’s security and well-being.”152

The hidden plot of The Prince is the Rhetorica ad Herennium. Machiavelli teaches utility in all its components because he advises the prince not only to equip himself with good weapons and to know how to be deceptive, but also teaches true virtue and the true way of obtaining praise and glory. In this book, wrongly considered to be Machiavelli’s most scientific work, whereas it was in reality a political oration, Machiavelli teaches a new prince the true virtue that does not hesitate in the face of evil nor does it stop in evil, but rather makes it possible to found and preserve a state. He explains that entering into evil is necessary, and that this is not a vice but a virtue, and even he promises the friendship of God to those who are capable of doing so in order to emancipate Italy. In his finest oration Machiavelli puts into practice the teachings of classical rhetoric and the image of God who was a friend of the founders of states, retrieved from the Bible and from the Florentine context.


5. Republican Eloquence and Wisdom


The context of classical rhetoric allows us to understand not only the meaning of The Prince, but also, and to a greater degree, Discourses on Livy. A book like Discourses, Machiavelli writes in the preface, demands virtue, judgment, and eloquence (“discorso”), in order to free men from error and ensure that they can “taste” from episodes from ancient history “that flavor that they have in themselves.”153 “Tasting that flavor”: Machiavelli uses once again the metaphor of food, as he had done in the letter to Vettori (“that food that alone is mine”), in order to convey the idea that he wishes to teach something that will enter into the soul, nourish it, and give it renewed strength and new life. In The Prince he wants to move a founder of states; in Discourses on Livy he wants to encourage the hearts of the young to emulate the virtue of the ancients and to eschew the corruption of the moderns. Both one and the other are undertakings of education, not chilly issuings of precepts.

The objective that Machiavelli pursued in Discourses on Livy is even more ambitious than that which he pursued in The Prince. This time he intended to emancipate the souls of the many from wicked moral education and from bad religion. “For it is the duty of a good man,” he writes in the preface to the Second Book, “to teach others the good that you could not work because of the malignity of the times and of fortune, so that when many are capable of it, someone of them more loved by heaven may be able to work it.”154 But a good man was, according to the classical Romans, the true orator.155 Only the true orator can educate men who can found new and free political orders, if they are beloved of the heavens. Machiavelli wanted to teach young people republican political wisdom and an understanding of political and military institutions. But he also wished to renew their souls by instilling in them with the power of words a love of a free way of life, a hatred of tyranny, and a yearning for true glory, because he was convinced that only a man who has these passions in his soul can hope to be loved by God and thus to achieve great things.

Machiavelli employs with confidence the devices of rhetoric, first and foremost the technique of the laudatory genre for praising men, institutions, and periods. Cicero and Quintilian had explained that when we wish to praise someone, it is a good idea to focus above all on the virtues that confer benefits on others.156 Aurelio “Lippo” Brandolini (1454–97) in his De ratione scribendi libri tres, completed around 1485, emphasized that a substantial portion of the laudatory genre has to do with the praise of great men, especially those who managed to distinguish themselves for their virtue in times of corruption.157 Machiavelli begins Discourses on Livy with a consideration of men who deserve praise and those who merit reproach. In the dedicatory letter to Zanobi Buondelmonti, he writes that while writers often “praise him [the prince] for all virtuous qualities when they should blame him for every part worthy of reproach,” he has chosen instead to dedicate his composition to a man who for his qualities would deserve to be a prince.158 In The Art of War, in order to praise as highly as possible Cosimo Rucellai, he writes that he had all the virtues that can be desired “in a good friend by his friends” and “in a citizen by his native city.”159 The clearest example of his mastery of the technique of praise and blame, nonetheless, is found in his preface to the Second Book of Discourses on Livy: “Men always praise ancient times—but not always reasonably—and accuse the present; they are partisans of past things in such a mode that they celebrate not only those ages known to them through the memory that writers have left of them, but also those that once they are old they remember having seen in their youth.” But one born in Italy in the time Machiavelli was writing “has reason to blame his times and to praise the others” because, he explains, “in the latter there are very many things that make them marvelous and in the former there is nothing that recompenses them for every extreme misery, infamy, and reproach.” And the first defect of the times that Machiavelli blames is the decline of religion: “There is no observance of religion.”160

To move anyone who was “born of man” to be terrified “away from every imitation of wicked times” and to be “inflamed with an immense desire to follow the good,” Machiavelli employs the technique of transforming his readers into spectators, placing before their eyes a series of powerful images (“ante oculos ponere”). The classical Romans had insisted a great deal on this essential rhetorical technique. Cicero had recommended speaking in such a way that listeners would see the events with their inward eyes, the eyes of the imagination.161 Quintilian, in his turn, had emphasized that one of the chief qualities of an orator is to narrate events as if painting a picture with his words.162 With explicit reference to Horace’s famous phrase, “ut pictura poesis,” the theorists of eloquence and the political writers of the fifteenth century repeated insistently the concept that a true orator is very much like a painter, and that the most effective way of amplifying an argument, and to describe men in order to instill a desire to emulate them, is to portray them as if depicting them on a canvas.163

Machiavelli in The Prince explicitly admits that he considers his work to be similar to that of the painter: “Nor do I want it to be thought presumption if a man from a low and mean state dares to discuss and give rules for the governments of princes. For just as those who sketch landscapes place themselves down in the plain to consider the nature of mountains and high places and to consider the nature of low places place themselves high atop mountains, similarly, to know well the nature of peoples one needs to be prince, and to know well the nature of princes one needs to be of the people.”164

I have already shown how Machiavelli designed the figure of the prince with metaphors and comparisons. In Discourses on Livy he used the same technique to move his readers to eschew tyranny and imitate good princes. In chapter X of book I, for instance, he writes:

Thus, let a prince put before himself the times from Nerva to Marcus, and compare them with those that came before and that came later; and then let him choose in which he would wish to be born or over which he would wish to be placed. For in those governed by the good he will see a secure prince in the midst of his secure citizens, and the world full of peace and justice; he will see the Senate with its authority, the magistrates with their honors, the rich citizens enjoying their riches, nobility and virtue exalted; he will see all quiet and all good, and, on the other side, all rancor, all license, corruption, and ambition eliminated. He will see golden times when each can hold and defend the opinion he wishes. He will see, in sum, the world in triumph, the prince full of reverence and glory, the peoples full of love and security. If he then considers minutely the times of the other emperors, he will see them atrocious because of wars, discordant because of seditions, cruel in peace and in war; so many princes killed with steel, so many civil wars, so many external ones; Italy afflicted and full of new misfortunes, its cities ruined and sacked. He will see Rome burning, the Capitol taken down by its own citizens, the ancient temples desolate, ceremonies corrupt, the cities full of adulterers. He will see the seas full of exiles, the shores full of blood. He will see innumerable cruelties follow in Rome, and nobility, riches, past honors, and, above all, virtue imputed as capital sins. He will see calumniators rewarded, slaves corrupted against their master, freedmen against their patron, and those who lacked enemies oppressed by friends. And he will then know very well how many obligations Rome, Italy, and the world owe to Caesar.165


In Discourses on Livy as well, Machiavelli uses the commonplaces of utility understood as safety and honor. For instance, when he discusses whether it is better to place a guard on liberty (“Where the Guard of Freedom May Be Settled More Securely, in the People or in the Great; and Which Has Great Cause for Tumult, He Who Wishes to Acquire or He Who Wishes to Maintain”), he argues his case entirely from the point of view of security. But when he discusses the problem of whether an aristocratic republic is preferable to a popular republic, he concludes his argument with an appeal to honor: “In ordering a republic there is need to think of the more honorable part.”166

Finally, lest there be a proliferation of examples, in the chapter in which he offers advice on the best way of expanding territory, he makes use of the useful, security, praiseworthiness, and virtue. He describes expansion by means of simple conquest as “useless in armed republics” and “very useless” in unarmed republics, and concludes by pointing to the methods employed by the ancient Tuscans as an example to be followed, because they were secure, praiseworthy, and a cause of good customs: “And if the imitation of the Romans seems difficult, that of the ancient Tuscans should not seem so, especially to the present Tuscans. For if they could not, for the causes said, make an empire like that of Rome, they could acquire the power in Italy that their mode of proceeding conceded them. This was secure for a great time, with the highest glory of empire and of arms and special praise for customs and religion.”167

Aside from explaining which institutions and decisions are truly useful to a republic, Machiavelli also wished to rekindle in modern people the love of liberty that history books tell us was so strong in the ancient peoples of Italy. In order to achieve that result, he amplified the contrast between the beauty of a free way of life and the misery of lives spent in servitude:

It is an easy thing to know whence arises among peoples this affection for the free way of life, for it is seen through experience that cities have never expanded either in dominion or in riches if they have not been in freedom. And truly it is a marvelous thing to consider how much greatness Athens arrived at in the space of a hundred years after it was freed from the tyranny of Pisistratus. But above all it is very marvelous to consider how much greatness Rome arrived at after it was freed from its kings. The reason is easy to understand, for it is not the particular good but the common good that makes cities great.
