And without doubt this common good is not observed if not in republics, since all that is for that purpose is executed, and although it may turn out to harm this or that private individual, those for whom the aforesaid does good are so many that they can go ahead with it against the disposition of the few crushed by it. The contrary happens when there is a prince, in which case what suits him usually offends the city and what suits the city offends him. In this mode, as soon as a tyranny arises after a free way of life, the least evil that results for those cities is not to go ahead further nor to grow more in power or riches but usually—or rather always—it happens that they go backward.168


Below, again to instill in his readers a love of freedom and a contempt for servitude, he reiterates with equally strong words the same contrast:

It is an easy thing to consider whence that order arose and whence this disorder proceeds; for it all comes from the free way of life then and the servile way of life now. For all towns and provinces that live freely in every part (as was said above) make very great profits. For larger peoples are seen there, because marriages are freer and more desirable to men since each willingly procreates those children he believes he can nourish. He does not fear that his patrimony will be taken away, and he knows not only that they are born free and not slave, but that they can, through their virtue, become princes. Riches are seen to multiply there in larger number, both those that come from agriculture and those that come from the arts. For each willingly multiplies that thing and seeks to acquire those goods he believes he can enjoy once acquired. From which it arises that men in rivalry think of private and public advantages, and both the one and the other come to grow marvelously. The contrary of all these things occurs in those countries that live servilely; and the more they decline from the accustomed good, the harder is their servitude.169


In The Art of War, Machiavelli uses rhetoric to teach the political and military wisdom of the ancients and in order to instill a new attitude in the souls of the young. “And I repine at Nature, who either should have made me such that I could not see this or should have given me the possibility for putting it into effect. Since I am an old man, I do not imagine today that I can have opportunity for it. Therefore I have been liberal of it with you who, being young and gifted, can at the right time, if the things I have said please you, aid and advise your princes to their advantage.” He wishes to see not only the rebirth of the military discipline of the ancients, but also a new moral life: “To honor and reward excellence, not to despise poverty, to esteem the methods and regulations of military discipline, to oblige the citizens to love one another, to live without sects, to esteem private less than public good, and other like things that could easily fit in with our times.” He believes that his effort can be successful because he is convinced that he is teaching the truth: “About these customs, it is not difficult to be persuaded when one thinks about them enough and takes them up in the right way, because in them so plainly can be seen the truth that every public-spirited nature is capable of receiving. He who accomplishes such a thing plants trees beneath the shade of which mankind lives more prosperously and more happily than beneath this shade.”170

Machiavelli made use of the teachings of classical rhetoric in his plays as well, when he wants to move the audience to laughter. As Aristotle and Cicero taught, the subjects of laughter are persons or actions that have a degree of imperfection or turpitude but are neither excessive nor grave, nor do they cause grief or damage. For that reason, one should not laugh at outsized evil or hateful crimes, nor a grave hardship or misfortunes that arouse sentiments of pity, but rather at common vices and shortcomings, especially vainglory, greed, ambition, cunning, and the folly of lovers.171 When he writes plays “to benefit and to please the audience,” Machiavelli makes intelligent use of these teachings and parodies “an old man’s avarice, a lover’s madness, a servant’s tricks, a parasite’s gluttony, a poor man’s distress, a rich man’s ambition, a harlot’s flatteries, all men’s faithlessness.” Nicia, in The Mandrake, makes the audience laugh because of his excessive greed and vainglory, though neither one nor the other cause any real lasting pain or harm and they are not grave sins. Machiavelli laughs at himself as well, and puts himself in the comedy in full view. The Nicomaco of the Clizia is a little bit like the unfortunate Niccolò Machiavelli who, nearing his sixties, falls in love with the young Barbara Salutati. Nicomaco deserves the derision to which he is subjected because he has behaved excessively, but he also deserves some human understanding because until this point he has always been a model man and citizen.172

To move his audience to laughter, he sets aside “language serious and solemn” and fills the comedies with words that are “either stupid, or biting, or amorous.”173 A different style than that used in his political works, but with the same objective, which is to teach wisdom that can be used in life:

For though the aim of a comedy is to hold up a mirror to domestic life, the way it does this, all the same, is with a certain urbanity and with expressions which excite laughter, so that the men who come eagerly to enjoy themselves, taste afterward the useful lesson that lay underneath. This is why it is difficult to use serious characters; for there can be no gravity in a cheating servant, a ridiculous old man, in a love-crazed youth, in a wheedling harlot, in a greedy parasite, yet their actions can convey various lessons, useful to our daily life.174


In Discourses on Livy he condemns with grave words the religion that makes men wicked and irreligious; in The Mandrake he derides that religion, using this time the technique of irony. He describes Fra Timoteo with serious language, as if he were a person worthy of respect. When a noblewoman reveals (with ill-concealed longing) what her “insistent” husband forces her to undergo, Fra Timoteo responds impassively: “Don’t worry, God’s mercy is great.” In response as well to Ligurio’s suggestion that a scandal be covered by arranging an abortion, the monk offers words of great piety: “Amen, in the name of the Lord. Let it be as you will, and may everything be done for God and for divine Charity. Tell me the name of the convent, give me the potion, and, if you like, the money, too, and then we can begin to do some good.”175

Fra Timoteo, after his own fashion, is a harsh critic of the decline of religion:

I couldn’t close my eyes all night long, I have been so eager to hear how Callimaco and the others have made out. I did everything I could to pass the time: I said my matins, I read a Life of the Holy Fathers, I went to church and relit a candle that had gone out, and I changed the veil on a miraculous statue of the Virgin. How many times have I told those friars to keep her well cleaned! And then they wonder why attendance is going down. I remember when there were five hundred holy images, and nowadays there are barely twenty. It’s all our own fault, because we haven’t been smart enough to keep up the publicity. We always used to hold a procession every evening after vespers, and we would have hymns sung there every Sabbath. We used to take vows there ourselves, so that people would see fresh, new plaques all the time, and we would encourage the men and women to make vows when they came for confession. Now nobody bothers to do any of these things, and then we wonder why business has fallen off! Oh, my fellow friars really don’t have any brains!176


In the face of such devotion on the part of a “venal” friar, we can only laugh. But the smile of those who believe, as did Machiavelli, that religion should teach us to be truly good and strong in our souls is a smile filled with bitterness. Just as we laugh at the goodness of Lucrezia, who first allows herself to be led docilely by the monk, by her mother, and by her husband, but when she discovers the qualities of the lover that they have introduced into her bed, actually declares that she can no longer resist what heaven has ordained: “Since your cleverness, my husband’s stupidity, my mother’s silliness, and my confessor’s guile have led me to do what I would never have done by myself, I have to judge that this comes from a divine providence that willed it so. I am not capable of refusing what heaven itself wants me to accept.”177

Machiavelli uses a comic and burlesque narrative as well to mock the religion that instills a fear of Hell and of devils in its followers. In the story of Belfagor a saintly man, “transported in prayer,” believed that many of those who “had died in the displeasure of God” descended to Hell through the faults of their wives. To such pious men, Machiavelli explained in his fable that the devils were decent and upright men, first and foremost Pluto, king of the underworld, himself. The words that Pluto addresses to his infernal advisers, and his method of treating the thorny question, are worthy of the finest of earthly princes:

Beloved friends! By celestial decree and the irrevocable course of destiny I am the ruler of this kingdom and thus cannot be bound by any judgment, earthly or celestial. Nevertheless, the wisest rulers are those who, though they have the greatest power, are keenest to submit themselves to the laws and are most inclined to respect the judgment of others. Therefore I have concluded that I would do well to be counseled by you on a matter that could bring shame to our reign. When all the souls of men who enter our kingdom proclaim that they have been driven here by their wives—a thing that seems impossible to me—I fear that if I should pass a judgment that is too severe, I might be accused of being too cruel, and if I should pass a judgment that is not severe enough, I might be accused of being too lenient, and not a lover of justice. The first sin is that of a frivolous ruler, the second of a ruler who is unjust. As I wish to avoid either charge but cannot find the means of doing so, I have called you together so that with your counsel you can offer assistance and be the reason that our kingdom will once again prevail without infamy!


The true devils are probably women like Onesta, who are perfectly willing, under the guise of virtue, to ruin a man out of pure ambition and an unbridled love of money; or men like that monk who “for more than four years had kept a woman in his cell dressed as a novice”; or like Gianmatteo del Brica, the laborer described by Giovanni del Bene, who “had outwitted the devil.”178 The devils, writes Machiavelli, were expelled from heaven for their pride.179 But he hastens to add that “he who sees the devil in very truth, sees him with smaller horns and not so black.”180

Even if he was not accustomed to listening to sermons, Machiavelli was familiar with the rhetorical technique for the composition of thematic sermons. According to the rules of the ars praedicandi, a sermon should begin with a quote from Holy Scripture, known as the “theme.” After the theme, the preacher should provide a short section intended to capture the attention of the audience by evoking subjects designed to arouse wonder or fear. He should then subdivide the sermon into three parts and develop each one with appropriate examples, references to the authorities, and amplifications. Finally, it was advisable to end with a summary or an admonition.181

The Exhortation to Penitence faithfully follows the canonical scheme. It begins with a quote from Psalms: “De profundis clamavi te, Domine; Domine exaudi vocem meam” (Out of the depths have I cried unto thee, O LORD. Lord, hear my voice, Psalm 130, 1–2). It continues with words that explain the theme and attract the attention of listeners, evoking the fear of sin and an admiration for divine mercy:

Since this evening, honored Fathers and Superior Brothers, I am to speak to Your Charities, in order to obey my superiors, and am to say something on penitence, it has seemed to me good to begin my exhortation with words of that teacher of the Holy Spirit, David the Prophet, so that those who have sinned with him may, according to his words, hope they can receive mercy from God all-powerful and all-merciful. And that they can obtain it, since David obtained it, they should not fear, because neither greater transgression nor greater penitence for a man than in his instance can be conceived, nor in God can greater generosity to pardon be found.


He then develops the theme, amplifying the gifts of God to man and quoting from Saint Paul, Saint Matthew, Saint Francis, and Saint Jerome, and concluding with the admonition: “Hence, to get out of [the snares of sin], we must resort to penitence and cry out with David: ‘Have mercy upon me, oh God!’ and with Saint Peter weep bitterly, and for all the misdeeds we have committed feel shame ‘And repent and understand clearly / that as much as pleases the world is a short dream’ [Petrarch].”182

In the Florentine Histories, Machiavelli’s last great work, he uses eloquence to teach once again political wisdom and to instill a love of liberty. Like the classic Romans and the humanists, he was well acquainted with the persuasive power of history: And if every example of a republic is moving, those which one reads concerning one’s own are much more so and much more useful.”183 Even if he cannot magnify examples of virtue, and must instead recount stories of corruption and political foolishness, the history of Florence still preserves its value intact: “And if in describing the things that happened in this devastated world one does not tell about either the strength of soldiers, or the virtue of the captain, or the love of the citizen for his fatherland, it will be seen with what deceits, with what guile and arts the princes, soldiers, and heads of republics conducted themselves so as to maintain the reputation they have not deserved. It may, perhaps, be no less useful to know these things than to know the ancient ones, because, if the latter excite liberal spirits to follow them, the former will excite such spirits to avoid and eliminate them.”184 This history of times of decline is more useful than philosophical considerations not only because it is capable of evoking passions, but also because it explores details and is therefore better suited to teach true political wisdom. In the specific case of Florence, Machiavelli explains, the historical narrative allows one to describe in a detailed manner the serious problem of civil discord and factional strife: “For if nothing else delights and instructs in history, it is that which is described in detail; if no other lesson is useful to the citizens who govern republics, it is that which shows the causes of the hatreds and divisions in the city, so that when they have become wise through the dangers of others, they may be able to maintain themselves united.”185

In the Florentine Histories Machiavelli allows the protagonists to speak directly, and he uses imagined orations to persuade or dissuade. In this way, he lets us witness deliberative rhetoric in action, and he can have the Florentines of the past speak to the Florentines of his own time and of times to come, in order to exhort them to avoid the errors that caused the decline of their city. Their arguments are almost always based on the topoi of utility, honesty, or necessity.186 An example of a political oration built around the theme of interest is the speech that a member of the Signoria delivers before the Duke of Athens to persuade him to abandon his ambition to become tyrant over the city. It is not our intention, emphasizes the orator, “to oppose your designs with any force, but only to point out to you how heavy a weight you are taking on your back, and how dangerous the course you are selecting, so that you can always remember our advice and that of those who counsel you otherwise, not for your advantage but to vent their rage.” After working to reduce the duke’s hostility by pointing out that their objective is to offer him a way of behaving that better corresponds to his own interests, the orator continues his speech, emphasizing the point that it would be difficult, indeed impossible, to impose a tyranny over a city like Florence, which has a long tradition of liberty. Anyone who wished to become a tyrant in Florence would be obliged to face the hostility of the entire city, and “amidst universal hatred one never finds any security, because you never know from whence evil may spring, and he who fears every man cannot secure himself against anyone. If indeed you try to do it, you aggravate the dangers, because those who remain burn more with hatred and are readier for revenge.” It would therefore be much safer not to try to become a tyrant over Florence but rather to live contented with “the authority that we have given you” because “that dominion is alone lasting which is voluntary. Nor should you, blinded by a little ambition, be led to place yourself where, unable either to rest or to rise higher, you must necessarily fall with the greatest harm to yourself and to us.”187

The duke’s reply is an example of the rhetorical technique of arguing on both sides (“in contrarias partes”).188 The Duke of Athens states that it is not his intention to deprive Florence of her liberty, but rather to restore that liberty by destroying sects and factions. As for the dangers that the orator of the Signoria had pointed out to him, he answered that “he did not regard them, because it was the office of a man not good to set aside the good for fear of evil, and of a pusillanimous man not to pursue a glorious undertaking because the end was doubtful.”189 Nor were the words of the duke persuasive in their turn, and not because the Signori had hardened their hearts, but rather because those words were nothing more than a poor attempt to conceal wicked intentions. What ultimately resolved the disagreement was the use of weapons, as a demonstration of the principle that when words prove inadequate, the sole remedy is force.190

An example of an oration based on the commonplaces of humanity, honesty, and a love of the fatherland is the speech of the Gonfalonier Luigi Guicciardini in the name of the Signoria. “And truly, if we had believed that during our magistracy our city had to be ruined, either by opposing you or by gratifying you, we would have avoided these honors with flight or exile; but as we hoped to have to do with men who might have in them some humanity and some love for their fatherland, we accepted the magistracy willingly, believing that with our humanity we could conquer your ambition by any mode.”191

After the appeal to humanity and the love of the fatherland, Machiavelli puts in the magistrate’s mouth a strong contrary argument, constructed in accordance with the rhetorical figure of the interrogatio, that is, a question posed not in order to obtain information but in order to give an argument greater persuasive force.192

What end will these demands of yours have, or how long will you abuse our liberality? Do you not see that we tolerate being conquered with more patience than you tolerate victory? To what will your disunions lead this city of yours? Do you not remember that when it was disunited Castruccio, a vile citizen of Lucca, defeated it? That a duke of Athens, one of your private condottieri, subjugated it? But when it was united, neither an archbishop of Milan nor a pope could defeat it, and after many years of war they were left in shame. Why, then, do you want your discords to make a slave of a city in peace that so many powerful enemies left free in war? What do you get out of your disunion other than servitude? Or of the goods that you have stolen or would steal from us other than poverty? For those are the things that, with our industry, nourish the whole city; and if it is despoiled of them, they cannot nourish it; and those who will seize them, as things ill acquired, will not know how to preserve them: from this, hunger and poverty will come to the city.193


In perfect keeping with the classical model, the oration of the gonfalonier concludes with an exhortation built around the principles of decorum and moderation: “These Signori and I command you, and if decency permits it, we pray you to still your spirits for once and be content to rest quietly with the things that have been ordered through us, and if ever you wish something new, be pleased to ask for it with civility and not with tumult and arms. For if they are decent things, you will always be granted them, and you will not give occasion to wicked men, at your charge and to your cost, to ruin your fatherland on your shoulders.”194 Luigi Guicciardini’s oration is a model of the discourse that a “good citizen” ought to deliver in order to calm the evil passions. The words of the gonfalonier, because they “were true,” “moved the spirits of those citizens very much.”195 Because of the way in which he skillfully spoke, Machiavelli notes, Luigi Guicciardini acted not only as a “good citizen,” but also as a “good Signore,” two expressions that reveal in Machiavelli the presence of the old language of prehumanist theories of republican self-government.196

Aside from the oration to the Duke of Athens, Machiavelli presents in the Florentine Histories other examples of words that fail to persuade. Among them, we should cite the description of the conspiracy against Maso degli Albizzi, who had established a regime that was hated even by the citizens who belonged to his faction.197 A group of citizens that Maso had banished decided to enter Florence secretly, kill Maso, summon the populace to revolt, and establish a free government.” With loud voices [the conspirators] urged the men to take up arms and escape the servitude they hated so much. They asserted that the grievances of the malcontents of the city, more than their own injuries, had moved them to want to free them.” Their words, Machiavelli notes, “even though true, did not move the multitude in any way”: either because of fear or else because the murders that the conspirators had committed had made them hateful and odious.198 In any case, the example confirmed the truth of the maxim that it is very dangerous “to want to free a people who want in every mode to be enslaved.”199

The Florentine Histories also provide examples of orations built on the theme of compassion and the fear of God, such as the speech that the citizens of Seravezza delivered before the Ten of War (Dieci della guerra, a magistracy in charge of international affairs) of Florence. In order to protest against the sack of their city perpetrated by the Florentine commissioner Astorre Gianni, and in a request for compensation, the citizens of Seravezza appealed to their audience’s sense of justice: “We are sure, magnificent Signori, that our words will find faith and compassion in your Lordships when you learn the way in which your commissioner seized our country and in what manner we were treated afterwards by him.” They then told the story, in moving words, of the atrocities they had suffered. and concluded their plea with an appeal to the fear of God: “And if our countless ills do not move you, may fear of the wrath of God move you, for He has seen His churches sacked and burned and our people betrayed in His bosom.” The oration attained its purpose, Machiavelli comments, because “the atrociousness of the thing, first learned and then understood from the living voices of those who had suffered it, touched the magistracy; and without delay Astorre was made to return and was then condemned and admonished.”200

A typical oration based on the theme of necessity is the speech by the anonymous worker who spoke to his comrades during the mutiny of the Ciompi to exhort them to continue the revolt and to increase the level of violence. After a brief preface, the orator comes to the essential point: “But because arms have been taken up and many evils have been done, it appears to me that one must reason that arms must not be put aside, and that we must consider how we can secure ourselves from the evils that have been committed. Certainly I believe that if others do not teach us, necessity does.”201 In order to overcome the sense of inferiority felt by many of his fellow mutineers toward the city’s nobility, the orator appealed to the natural equality of all men: “For all men, having had the same beginning, are equally ancient and have been made by nature in one mode. Strip all of us naked, you will see that we are alike; dress us in their clothes and them in ours, and without a doubt we shall appear noble and they ignoble, for only poverty and riches make us unequal.”202 He then attempted to assuage their moral scruples by explaining that victory, however they might obtain it, never brings shame, and that it was not advisable to take the voice of their conscience too seriously: “For neither conscience nor infamy should dismay you, because those who win, in whatever mode they win, never receive shame from it. And we ought not to take conscience into account, for where there is, as with us, fear of hunger and prison, there cannot and should not be fear.” Finally, he concludes his speech by returning to the concept of necessity: “I confess this course is bold and dangerous, but when necessity presses, boldness is judged prudence; and spirited men never take account of the danger in great things.”203 This oration, Machiavelli comments, was effective. The orator was able to enflame the spirits of his fellow mutineers and persuade them to take up arms again. What rendered the oration so persuasive was the appeal to necessity, a motive that is more powerful than any other in urging men to undertake dangerous acts.204

As in Discourses on Livy, in the Florentine Histories too Machiavelli makes use of the technique of contrast to move his listeners to eschew bad examples and to emulate good examples. One exemplary case is the comparison between the social conflicts in ancient Rome and those in Florence:

The grave and natural enmities that exist between the men of the people and the nobles, caused by the wish of the latter to command the former not to obey, are the cause of all evils that arise in cities. For from this diversity of humors all other things that agitate republics take their nourishment. This kept Rome disunited, and this, if it is permissible to compare little things with great, has kept Florence divided, although diverse effects were produced in one city and the other. For the enmities between the people and the nobles at the beginning of Rome that were resolved by disputing were resolved in Florence by fighting. Those in Rome ended with a law, those in Florence with the exile and death of many citizens; those in Rome always increased military virtue, those in Florence eliminated it altogether; those in Rome brought the city from equality in the citizens to a very great inequality, those in Florence reduced it from inequality to a wonderful equality.205


Machiavelli mentions God in many accounts of historical events. He speaks of God in one of the first chapters in which he recounts the turmoil occasioned by the barbarian invasions and the religious conflicts during the times of Honorius and Arcadius. The need to draw a broad historical outline in order to be able to describe the origins of Florence did not require that he focus on the “variations of religion” (transitions from one religion to another) and on the conflicts among the churches and among the sects. And yet Machiavelli includes a consideration on the consolatory power of religion:

But among so many changes, change of religion was not of lesser moment, because in the struggle between the custom of the ancient faith and the miracles of the new, the gravest tumults and discords were generated among men. If indeed the Christian religion had been united, fewer disorders would have followed; but the struggles among the Greek Church, the Roman Church, and the Church at Ravenna—and even more, the struggle between the heretical and catholic sects—afflicted the world in many modes. Witness to this is Africa, which suffered more anguish on account of the Arian sect believed in by the Vandals than through their avarice or natural cruelty. Living thus, among so many persecutions, men bore the terror of their spirit written in their eyes, because, aside from the infinite evils they endured, for a good part of them the possibility of seeking refuge in God, in whom all the miserable are wont to hope, was lacking. Therefore, as the greater part of them were uncertain as to which God they ought to turn to, they died miserably, deprived of all help and all hope.206


When the subject matter allows him to point this out, Machiavelli reiterates the concept that he had already expressed in Discourses on Livy, that is, that without religion and a fear of God, republics are corrupted. One of the evident signs of the “common corruption of all the Italian cities,” states an anonymous orator, is that “because religion and fear of God have been eliminated in all, an oath and faith given last only as long as they are useful.”207 Instead, citizens who love liberty appeal to God: “Nor do I wish to say more to you than this: may God keep this city that no one of its citizens becomes prince of [Florence].” This is a God that in extreme circumstances helps the city: “But God, who in such extremities has always had a particular care for it, made an un-hoped-for accident arise that gave the king, the pope, and the Venetians something greater to think about than Tuscany.” The help of God, at times, is manifested through defeats and tragedies that force the city to rediscover virtue: “But, since God had willed the troops to be defeated, the loss would be that much more grave if others were abandoned; but if they faced fortune and applied what remedies they could, neither they would feel their loss nor the duke his victory.” At other times God prevents a city from expanding its dominion, even if in this way He causes a great displeasure to the citizens: “God and men had not wanted the Lucchese to come under their rule.”

Machiavelli often has the protagonists of events say that God is an enemy to wicked man. He places the following words in the mouths of the citizens of Milan, in despair over Francesco Sforza’s betrayal:

And even if ambition blinds you, the whole world as witness to your wickedness will open your eyes; God will open them for you, if perjuries, if violated faith and betrayals displease Him, and He does not always wish to be the friend of wicked men, as up to now He has done for some hidden good. So do not promise yourself sure victory, for that will be kept from you by the just wrath of God; and we are ready to lose our liberty with death; if ever we cannot defend it, we will submit to any other prince rather than you. And even if our sins have been such that we have fallen into your hands against every wish of ours, have firm faith that the kingdom you have begun with deceit and infamy will come to an end for you or your sons with disgrace and harm.208


In response to these words, Francesco Sforza replied that “whether this was true or not, that God upon whom they called to avenge their injuries would demonstrate at the end of the war; through Him they will see which of them is more His friend, and which has fought with greater justice.”209 In that circumstance, God was friend to a wicked man: Francesco Sforza entered “Milan as prince the twenty-sixth of February in 1450,” and “was received with utmost and marvelous joy by those who not long before had defamed him with so much hatred.”210 At the time of the War of the Eight Saints, God on the other hand helped the Florentines, who were fighting for justice and against the pope: “But if God was just, if acts of violence were displeasing to Him, then those of his vicar must have displeased Him, and He must be glad that offended men, finding no refuge in that place, would have recourse to Him.”211

Machiavelli interpreted extraordinary events as punishments or warnings from God to men. In connection with the fire that burned the Church of Santo Spirito during the visit of the Duke of Milan in 1471, he mentions. without discounting it, the popular belief that the fire was a punishment of God for the corrupt customs of the duke’s court:

At that time was seen a thing never before seen in our city: this being the season of Lent, in which the Church commands that one fast by not eating meat, his court, without respect to Church or God, all fed on meat. And because many spectacles were held to honor him, among which was represented the giving of the Holy Spirit to the Apostles in the church of Santo Spirito, and because that church burned down as a result of the many fires that are made in such solemnities, it was believed by many that God, angered against us, had wished to show that sign of his wrath.212


Similar considerations come into play when he describes an extraordinary storm that ravaged Tuscany:

Thereupon, when arms had been put away by men, it appeared that God wished to take them up Himself: so great was a wind storm that then occurred, which in Tuscany had effects unheard of in the past and for whoever learns of it in the future will have marvelous and memorable effects…. Without doubt, God wanted to warn rather than punish Tuscany; for, if such a storm had entered into a city among many and crowded houses and inhabitants, as it did enter among few and scattered oaks and trees and houses, without doubt it would have made ruin and torment greater than that which the mind can conjecture. But God meant for them that this small example should be enough to refresh among men the memory of His power.


With his comments, Machiavelli meant to remind men that heaven is by no means unarmed, as the Catholic religion would have us believe, but is indeed inhabited by a God that threatens and chastises. He puts an armed God back in heaven and has God intervene in human affairs because he wants to combat a bad moral and religious education, and replace it with a way of living that is based on a love of liberty and virtue. Even though his friends jokingly tell him that he is “a greater prophet than the Hebrews or any other nation ever had,”214 he is no prophet, nor does he want to be, either armed or unarmed, but he uses the power of words to revive the religion that republics need.

Plutarch, Machiavelli’s likely source, writes: “Now, after the death of his father Aegeus, forming in his mind a great and wonderful design, he gathered together all the inhabitants of Attica into one town, and made them one people of one city, whereas before they lived dispersed, and were not easy to assemble upon any affair for the common interest. Nay, differences and even wars often occurred between them, which he by his persuasions appeased, going from township to township, and from tribe to tribe. And those of a more private and mean condition readily embracing such good advice, to those of greater power he promised a commonwealth without monarchy, a democracy, or people’s government, in which he should only be continued as their commander in war and the protector of their laws, all things else being equally distributed among them;—and by this means brought a part of them over to his proposal. The rest, fearing his power, which was already grown very formidable, and knowing his courage and resolution, chose rather to be persuaded than forced into a compliance”; Plutarch, Vite parallele, edited by Carlo Carena, Einaudi, Turin 1958, vol. I, pp. 19–20. English translation by John Dryden can be found at http://classics.mit.edu/Plutarch/theseus.html.

81 Machiavelli presents Caterina Sforza as an example of virtue gleaming in contrast with the ferocity of the males as well in the Art of War [Arte della Guerra], which he completed a few years after the Discorsi. The reference to Caterina is inserted into the text as if by force, in the context of a purely military consideration on the utility of fortresses. After explaining the manner in which Cesare Borgia, with the army of the king of France, took possession in January 1500 of the fortress of Forlì, Machiavelli recounts: “So the badly planned fortress and the incapacity of its commander brought shame upon the Countess’ bold undertaking. She would have had the spirit to await an army, as neither the King of Naples nor the Duke of Milan had done. But though her efforts did not turn out well, nonetheless she carried off such esteem as her valor deserved. This was testified to by many epigrams made in her honor at the time”; Machiavelli, Dell’arte della guerra [The Art of War], in Opere vol. I, pp. 670–71. English translation: Gilbert, Machiavelli: The Chief Works, p. 706.

The locus classicus of Florentine political rhetoric concerning faithfulness and respecting one’s undertakings is the Laudatio florentinae urbis by Leonardo Bruni: “Joined to this beneficence and liberality is the admirable loyalty that this Republic has always preserved inviolate with an extraordinary constancy. It has always felt that before promising it is necessary to reflect thoroughly; but once it has made a promise it refuses to go back on its word. Having adhered to this principle from the very beginning and having judged that to do this is right, never for any sort of advantage has it been possible to induce it to break its pacts, agreements, treaties, oaths, and promises. In its judgement, nothing suits the dignity of a state more than to maintain firmness in all its words and all its deeds; nothing is less fitting than not to keep its promises”; Leonardo Bruni, Laudatio florentinae urbis, in Opere politiche e letterarie, edited by Paolo Viti, Utet, Turin 1996, pp. 619–21.

In The Prince Machiavelli interprets the execution of Ramiro as a gesture dictated by the need to placate the populace: “And because [the duke] knew that past rigors had generated some hatred for Ramiro, to purge the spirits of that people and to gain them entirely to himself, he wished to show that if any cruelty had been committed, this had not come from him but from the harsh nature of his minister. And having seized this opportunity, he had him placed one morning in the piazza at Cesena in two pieces, with a piece of wood and a bloody knife beside him. The ferocity of this spectacle left the people at once satisfied and stupefied”; The Prince, VII. English translation: Mansfield, The Prince, p. 30.

92 In this text, written to be read by someone else, as Jean-Jacques Marchand has pointed out, “it would be erroneous to imagine that Machiavelli expresses ideas contrary to those of the government; indeed, we may conjecture that it was his job to put in an oratory and argumentative format the political ideas of the high officials close to the Gonfalonier, in order to obtain the broadest possible consensus among the representatives of the various currents or interests (optimates, commoners, Savonarolians, Mediceans, republicans, artisans, bankers, or major businessmen) within the councils. Nonetheless, the technique of argumentation, given the original aspects that we have emphasized [the dilemmatic structure], should be entirely attributed to Machiavelli”; in Niccolò Machiavelli, L’Arte della guerra. 104 Federico Chabod, beginning with his Introduction to The Prince (1924), believed that the book contained twenty-five “rigidly logical” chapters, and another chapter, chapter 26, “which is an expression of the author’s feelings, passion, and imagination”; Federico Chabod, Scritti su Machiavelli, Einaudi, Turin 1964, p. 18. In his commentary on the invective against tyrants in chapter 10 of Book I of the Discorsi, Luigi Russo wrote that “we read with a certain astonishment this page, which lacks the sobriety and the quality of scientific analysis that is usually found in Machiavelli’s thinking and style. We find in it an oratorical and literary raptus, that goes well beyond the writer’s scholarly perceptions”; Luigi Russo, Machiavelli, 3d ed., Laterza, Bari 1949, p. 224. Fredi Chiappelli writes that “Machiavelli is in fact an unusual writer of treatises, at once a writer of treatises and an artist”; Fredi Chiappelli, Studi sul linguaggio del Machiavelli, Le Monnier, Florence 1952, p. 9. See also Chiappelli, Nuovi studi sul linguaggio del Machiavelli, Le Monnier, Florence 1969.

CHAPTER THREE


THE REPUBLIC AND ITS RELIGION


In Machiavelli’s view, a republic is a political constitution based on the rule of law and the common good, as well as a way of life that adheres to those principles: a “free way of life,” as he frequently wrote. In a republic the people are sovereign, even though in the republics of his own time, including the Florentine Republic, many were excluded from political rights, and governing was a privilege accorded only to a tiny minority. The people had the power to approve laws and to select the magistrates who would govern. A republic is created through the extraordinary virtue of its founders; it survives if it is well ordered and possesses good laws and good customs, and it is reborn, if and when it is reborn, through the virtue of its redeemers. The extraordinary virtue of both its founders and its redeemers, as well as the customs of its citizens, are in need of a religion that exhorts them to seek glory here on earth, that infuses them with courage, and that teaches them to serve the fatherland and to love liberty. Without this religion, republics cannot be created; they would be incapable of withstanding either external aggression or the evils of corruption from within; they would not have the moral strength to be reborn and to rediscover the basic principles of justice. The religion of virtue is not an accessory to the free life; it is, rather, its very soul. That is why Machiavelli places the founders of religions above the founders of states in his hierarchy of excellent men, and singles out first and foremost in the ranks of detestable men those who undermine and dissipate religions.


1. Republican Government and Religion


The idea of the republic as a self-governing political community, based on the rule of law and on the common good, has a long history in Machiavelli’s intellectual and political context. According to republican political thought, cities that are self-governing (“proprio regimine”) are free. A free city is a city that recognizes no other greater power (“civitas quern superiorem non recognoscit”).1 The power to approve laws and appoint magistrates belongs to the citizens, in accordance with the principle of Roman law that “what concerns a large number of individuals must be decided by the entire sovereign body of the citizenry, acting with respect for the law and the procedures established by statute,” as Leonardo Bruni wrote. Only a self-governing republic can produce laws intended for the common good, thus enjoying a true civil life and true liberty.2

The most significant consequence of this approach to the conception of political liberty is that equality in the eyes of the law must be accompanied by an equal liberty to take part in the government of the republic. This was stated very clearly by Poggio Bracciolini in a letter to the Duke of Milan in 1438: “Our republic is not governed either by a number of citizens, nor by the aristocrats; rather the entire populace is allowed equal access to public office; this ensures that both the great and the lowly citizens, nobles and non-nobles are joined in the service of liberty, and in order to defend it, they will spare neither expense nor effort.”3

For republican theorists, the right to take part in sovereign deliberations and to stand for elections to public office is a fundamental aspect of liberty. They emphasize that this liberty is particularly dear to the interests of the citizens of the republics because it gives them an opportunity to achieve their own legitimate ambitions and refine their better moral and intellectual qualities.4 A well-ordered republic, then, must encourage the participation of the citizens by rewarding virtue and entrusting public office to those individuals who have shown an ability to serve the common good. As stated in the declaration that served as the preamble to the law that introduced election by lot in 1458, citizens who have shown prudence and rectitude should be gradually promoted to the highest public offices, while those “who live in such a way as to be unworthy of public honors should not be chosen for the responsibilities of government.”5 If a republic wishes to remain free, republican political theorists warn, it must have a ranking or hierarchy of honors based solely on virtue.

In order to ensure that participation in the public deliberations was truly an exercise of political freedom, the citizens must be able to express their ideas without fear. Freedom of speech in deliberative councils, stated one of the participants in the debates concerning the reform of the city statutes in November 1458, is the most precious possession. Another participant emphasized that the difference of opinions that took place in the council chambers was not to be taken as a signal of civil discord but rather as the best possible way to discover the truth.6 A republic cannot claim to be free, wrote Alamanno Rinuccini in his Dialogus de libertate, if through fear, corruption, or for any other cause, the citizens are forced to refrain from openly and freely stating their views.7 The preamble to the draft of the constitutional reform dated 23 December 1494 that established the new republican government following the expulsion of the Medici clearly and precisely documents the fact that the Florentines considered freedom of speech in public councils, and the rightful allocation of honors, to be two fundamental conditions of political liberty. According to that document, the “public and universal good” consisted “of advising, arranging, and ordering freely in public and private cases and in the establishment of the best and most carefully considered provisions and laws and in the just distribution of honors and weights such as to create a well established republic.”8

Along with self-government, one fundamental principle of the republic is the rule of law. In the view of the humanist and prehumanist political theorists, the republic, or civitas, is an association of individuals bound together by shared rules of justice.9 Men established civil communities, writes Brunetto Latini in his influential Tresor (Book of the Treasure), in order to live in peace and security under the protection of laws. A civil community (“cite”), then, is, to use the words of Cicero, an association of men who live in accordance with a single law.10 Following the translation into Latin of Aristotle’s Politics, around 1260, political writers used the concept of “civil life” as a synonym for “political life.” Giles of Rome, in his De Regimine Principum Libri III, completed in 1280, speaks of a political community, or civitas (“communitatem politicam sivi civitatem”), and calls it a necessary condition for living well, that is, for living in accordance with virtue. Likewise, in the neo-Aristotelian version, the fundamental nature of political life is the rule of law. To live politically (“vivere politicum”), wrote Giles of Rome, means living in accordance with good laws and good statues.11

The rule of law marked the boundary between civil or political life and tyranny. Since no tyrant accepted the restraint of laws, a tyrant’s rule violates the fundamental prerequisite of civil or political living. While a monarchic government can still be defined as a political government, writes Coluccio Salutati in De Tyranno, tyranny cannot be so described because the “principatus politicus” is the only one in which sovereign power is limited by laws.12 Thirty years later, around 1430, Giovanni Cavalcanti described the custom of making the most important political decisions in the homes of the powerful citizens rather than in the public buildings as a practice typical of a “tyrannical and non-political way of life.”13

The jurists of the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries also provided a significant, if often overlooked, contribution to the development of the theory of republican self-government. They defined the political man as a man who lives in a civil community, emphasizing that to live politically or in a civil fashion means living in accordance with the rules of civil law and justice.14 The civil or political life is something different from the political regime, that is, from republican government. Giles of Rome maintains, for instance, that the form of government most in keeping with political life is an hereditary monarchy. Tolomeo da Lucca, in contrast, writes that the form of government best suited to achieve and preserve political life is the rule of magistrates elected by the citizens for limited periods of time. Republican government (“politia” or “principatus politicus”), Tolomeo explains, is the appropriate government for civil communities (“civitates”) and goes on to specify that by republican government he means the form of government in which the citizens elect rulers who are subject to the laws and statutes of the city.15

Republican political theorists of the fifteenth century identified civil or political life with mixed government, understood as that form of government that wisely combines the virtues of popular government, aristocracy, and monarchy The argument that mixed government is the most appropriate way of encouraging true political life is present above all in the writings of both theorists and historians, who identified the constitution of the Republic of Venice as the best model to imitate. The historian Lorenzo de’ Monaci summarized the prevailing notion of his own time when he wrote that the finest political constitution is one in which the laws are sovereign and that the best way to ensure the rule of law is to establish a mixed government emulating the example of Venice.16 In a public oration delivered in 1493, which Machiavelli might well have listened to, Alamanno Rinuccini emphasized, with a reference to Cicero, that justice and good laws are the foundation of all “humano vivere et maxime politico et civile”—“human, and especially political and civil, life.”17 Less than a year later, in the Treatise on the Government of the City of Florence (Trattato circa el reggimento della città di Firenze) that served as a theoretical and political foundation for the republican government that would then summon Machiavelli to take the office of secretary, Girolamo Savonarola explained in a detailed fashion that the true civil government is a government “for all the people,” and that as such, it is the most reliable shield against tyranny.18

Before examining Machiavelli’s writings, we should analyze the relationship between civil life and the liberty of the city and of the citizens: this theme was of great importance in the political thinking of the Renaissance. For the jurists and political philosophers of the fourteenth century, the essential element of political liberty was independence. They understand independence as a city’s power to endow itself with statutes and laws; its opposite was the state of dependence on the will of the emperor, in which a city was given statutes and laws by the emperor, or was, at best, required to ask his approval. Much as with the liberty of the city, the liberty of a citizen consisted of independence from the will of another individual. It can exist only if the laws govern men, rather than men governing the laws. The existence of a true political and civil life in a free city, then, is a necessary precondition to the liberty of a citizen.19

The source that jurists use to interpret political liberty as the absence of personal dependency is Roman law, and in particular those passages in which the status of a free individual is defined as the status of a person who is not subject to the power (“dominium”) of another person. The opposite of the free condition is the servile condition, that is, the condition of the individual who is dependent on the will of another.20 Just as a city or a people are free as long as they live under the rule of their own laws, likewise an individual is free as long as he is not subject to the will of another individual (“sui iuris”).21 From this principle, Roman political writers derived the corollary that if a people receives its laws from a king, it is not free but enslaved: it lives, not in a state of liberty, but in a servile condition, that is, in a state similar to that of a slave with respect to his master.22 While monarchy in the narrowest sense—that is, in the sense of an absolute monarchy—is similar to dominion (“dominium”), the republic is the form of government and the way of life of a free people.

One significant consequence of political liberty understood as the condition of an independent people that lives in accordance with its own laws is that liberty entails constraints or hindrances on the actions of individuals. This interpretation of political liberty is described eloquently in three classic texts that became the core of modern republicanism. The first is Livy’s statement that the liberty that the Romans won for themselves following the expulsion of their kings consisted first and foremost in the fact that laws were more powerful than men.23 The second is the speech, reported by Sallust, in which Aemilius Lepidus proclaimed that the Roman people are free because they obey no one but the law.24 The third is a passage from Cicero’s Pro Cluentio: “legum… idcirco omnes servi sumus ut liberi esse possimus” (“We are slaves of the laws in order that we may be free.”)25

These aspects of Roman political wisdom were adopted and reformulated by Florentine civic humanism. Liberty, writes Coluccio Salutati, is a “gentle brake” (“dulce libertatis frenum”) that the law imposes on all citizens.26 True liberty, Leonardo Bruni reiterates, consists of the equality ensured by law, and it remains intact as long as the laws are more powerful than the citizens.27 In the second half of the fifteenth century, it was chiefly the opposition to the Medici that warned that the foundation of civil liberty is the rule of law. A republic that wishes to “live in freedom,” writes Alamanno Rinuccini in his Ricordi, should never allow a citizen to have “more power than the laws.”28 A few years later, in the Dialogus de libertate, he reiterated the same concept with the words of Cicero: “Legibus parere summa Libertas est” (“To obey the laws is the greatest liberty”).29

True laws have a divine character for the humanists. The objective of laws is to supervise human affairs, and their goal is the good, and not just any good, but that most divine good, which is the common good.30 Since republican government is better suited than any others to promote the common good and liberty, God prefers it above any other form of government.31 With perfect consistency in terms of these principles, the fifteenth-century humanists maintained that the Christian religion teaches the true morality of the citizen. Matteo Palmieri, in his Vita civile, explained that the “human nation” is subject to two laws: the “almost divine and natural” law, and the laws, similar to them, that have been written and approved by men. Religion derives from divine and natural laws. Men recognize themselves in religion and keep it alive because, in their souls, there lives “a supernal essence, eternally perfect, in divine union,” and it commands them to consider their duties to the republic and toward humanity as duties to God.32

In order to instill and preserve in citizens the sentiment of social duties as an obligation to God, Palmieri emphasizes, the republic must take great and diligent care of religious worship and teach its people to love justice and the fatherland by educating good and venerated priests and by using ceremonies that capture and stir the emotions of the multitude.33 Palmieri further expands on his ideas concerning the republic and religion in his Libro del poema chiamato Città di Vita, which he wrote between 1455 and 1464. This work had a troubled existence because of the strong suspicions of heresy, suspicions that grew even stronger immediately after Palmieri’s death in 1475.34 In Palmieri’s view, religion is the inseparable companion of justice and the virtue that, more than any other, is crucial to civil life.35 To Palmieri, a city that lived in accordance with this religion would be a perfect republic, like the one that Plato chose to imagine. Since human corruption does not allow such perfection, civil wisdom advises us to attempt to attain here on earth not necessarily Plato’s republic, but the republic that Cicero depicted, “as clearly as can be obtained here on earth.” In the earthly republic as well, men can live and rule in accordance with justice and, for those who live according to justice, Palmieri assured, the reward will be the eternal city.36 Just as other political writers of civil humanism had done before him, Palmieri insisted on the belief that God loves the honest life and wishes to ensure that it is preserved and that without the restraints of religion, an honest life is not possible. God, last of all, rewards as an excellent human endeavor “the extermination of tyrants for the good of the many” and “the establishment of just governments and of peace.”37

Bartolomeo Sacchi, also known as the Platina (1421–81), was of the same opinion, and in his De optimo cive, an exemplary text of fifteenth-century Florentine civil thought, he reiterated that religion is necessary to the preservation of republics, and that the Christian religion is the best suited to the education of stalwart and good citizens. Platina sets off from the assumption that the ultimate goal of republics, as Aristotle tells us, is to allow the citizens to live well. No man is capable of governing so great and significant an entity as a republic unless he has the guidance of God.38 Since religion is the true foundation of the republic, a ruler must take great care to ensure that in the city the worship of the God of the Christian religion is piously and sacredly observed.39 For that matter, it is clearly demonstrated just how great the power of religion and piety toward God is by the fact that those peoples who preserve their religion and respect the cult of the divine, as the Roman people did as a result of the ordinances introduced by Numa, have achieved astonishing progress. If this is true for the false religion of the ancients, it is therefore all the more true of the sacred and chaste Christian religion.40

Our religion, Platina explains, instructs us to pursue justice, strength, and modesty; it teaches us shame, respect for promises made, constancy, and honesty. It commands us to shun carnal violence, deceit, wicked actions, rage, turpitude, lust, injustice, sensuousness, sloth, and foolhardiness. Far more than the ancient religion of the pagans, based on fables, our religion, proven by the blood of countless martyrs and by the opinion of the most learned men whom the church honors, is well equipped to sustain the life of the republic. It is therefore the duty of those who rule to encourage in any way and with all efforts devotion to the Christian religion and to ward off superstition, that is, the cult of false gods. With this end in mind, a prince must protect himself from hypocrites and flatterers who have no faith of their own and who work in countless ways to appear highly religious.41

Love of the fatherland is a duty that comes second only to one’s duties toward God, and a good ruler must devote the same effort and care to love of the fatherland as he devotes to religious worship. Inasmuch as it is an expression of charity (“charitatem patriae”), Platina points out, love of the fatherland is not only entirely in keeping with the ethical and civil teachings of the Christian religion, but is even a necessary part of it.42 Concerning love of the fatherland there is no intellectual division between Roman republican thinking and Christian ethics and therefore there is no need to hark back to pagan beliefs in search of a religion that teaches the love of the fatherland that is so necessary to a republic.

The idea that a republic has need of the Christian religion to serve as its moral essence and to regulate its customs encountered, at the end of the fifteenth century, its most steadfast, but also its most extreme, formulation in the sermons of Girolamo Savonarola and in Savonarola’s Treatise on the Government of the City of Florence, written in 1494 at the behest of the Signoria. Savonarola openly hoped that the republican government that was established on his advice following the expulsion of the Medici would operate in a devout manner to make Florence into a latter-day Zion, living in unity in the love of God and in a mutual love among the citizenry, and becoming a powerful source of inspiration for the moral and religious reformation of Italy and the world.43 Florence, he urged, must make Christ its king: not only because, once Christ was king, no one else would be able to become prince of the city, but also because taking Christ as its king meant taking on the solemn commitment to live in accordance with divine law.

Savonarola reiterated frequently that he was preaching religious reform and the establishment of a civil government at God’s behest. The new republican government that Florence had established for itself was a part of God’s plan to save the city. Its principles and its institutions were entirely in keeping with both natural law and the teachings of the church.44 Both natural law and Christian doctrine agree in condemning tyranny as an enemy to true religion and as the greatest political and moral evil. A tyrant feigns piety and religiosity but pursues and cultivates only the surface aspects of devotion, rewards bad prelates, and rejects the good ones, thus destroying all sincere religious sentiment.45 True civil government on the other hand guides the city toward the beatitude that Christ promised men, encourages proper living, and supports religion understood not as a set of external practices related to worship, but rather as a deeply felt truth, experienced with sincere faith through the teachings of good ministers.46

A civil or republican government derives from God and receives all its benefits from God. The religiosity and good customs of citizens render perfect the government of a city, both because they draw on the favor of God, and because the citizens summoned to govern will be capable of giving good advice, once again as a result of the enlightenment and illumination of God.47 At the same time, Savonarola did not fail to point out that the first duty of good government, and the most reliable bulwark against tyranny, is the true religion. Tyranny offends all cities, but more than all the others, it is an offense to the cities in which the citizens cultivate Christian piety with sincere love. Florence was profoundly Christian, and if tyranny was finally eradicated, it could become a perfect Christian city; as long as tyranny could rule the city or threaten it, however, Florence would be unable to live in accordance with the true religion. The warning that Savonarola launched in the pages of the Treatise on the Government of the City of Florence, and from the pulpit, was urgent and peremptory: either the true religion, or tyranny.48

Savonarola recommended four principles for good government: the fear of God, the love of the common good, peace and reciprocal love among the citizens, and the respect of justice.49 He wanted the citizens of Florence to look to the Consiglio Maggiore (Major Council), the soul of the republic, as a special gift from God, and therefore that they view it with profound devotion. They must sense their duty to make the republican government increasingly perfect and just as an obligation that each of them owes to God:

Every Florentine citizen who wants to be a good member of his city and to lend his support to it, as everyone should, must believe that this Council and civil government have been mandated by God, as is indeed true, not only insomuch as every good government proceeds from Him, but also through the special providence God has at present for the city of Florence; concerning this, anyone who has been here for the past three years and who is not blind and totally without judgment clearly understands that if it had not been for the hand of God, there would never have been such a government in the face of so much and such strong opposition, nor would it be able to maintain itself up to this day against so many traitors and so few champions.


But because God wants us to exercise our intellect and free will, which He has given us, He makes those matters appertaining to human government imperfect at first so that we with His help might make them perfect.50

If Florence is just, Savonarola assured, it will also be great and powerful because the people will happily accept the rule of a just republic that lives in accordance with a Christian manner. Rome was not Christian, but it was just, and God, who wishes the peoples to be governed with justice, gave that city dominion over the world. Florence, if it so desires, can follow Rome’s example. From civil government come the most precious treasures: true liberty, security of property and person, the honor of women and children, the progress of the arts and commerce, prosperity, free marriages, and the desire to produce children. The conclusion of the Treatise on the Government of Florence is worth quoting, because it summarizes the idea that Savonarola had of the divine value of the republic:

Given then, as we have proved, that the one who rules well is happy and like to God, and that the one who rules badly is unhappy and like to the devil, every citizen ought to forsake his sins and selfish concerns and make the effort to rule well and to preserve and augment and make perfect this civil government for the honor of God and the salvation of souls (all the more so considering that He has especially given us this government on account of the love He bears for this city), in order that he may be happy in this world and the next, through the grace of our Savior, Jesus Christ, King of kings and Lord of lords, Who with the Father and the Holy Ghost lives and reigns for ever and ever. Amen.51


The prose of Savonarola fails to make it entirely clear whether it is a good religion that creates a good civil government or whether it is necessary to have a good civil government before it becomes possible to see a good religion flourish in the city.52 This would be a problem for a philosopher, not for a preacher and prophet like Savonarola, who had every right, as such, to say in one case that religion demands good civil government and helps to preserve it, and in other cases that good government encourages good religion. In contrast with philosophers, preachers and prophets are chiefly interested in being effective, and care little for doctrinal consistency. From a more purely theoretical point of view, the relationship between republican institutions and religiosity has to do with the relationship between political orderings and ways of life. Religion forms part of a people’s customs and at the same time it is a force that can change those customs: for the better (when it encourages hostility to a tyrannical or princely government); or for the worse (when it is used to justify corruption).

A republic must eject bad priests and encourage and preserve intact and uncorrupted religious worship, and indeed it should establish “a most perfect cult” and an “excellent Christian way of life.”53 In this way, Florence will restore the way of life of the early Christians; it will become the New Jerusalem, mirror of true religion in the world, “an earthly paradise” that lives “in jubilation and song,” where the children will be as angels. Supported by Christian living, the civil government will become more celestial than earthly.54 In Savonarola’s millenarian vision, the republic and religion are two aspects of the same reality. The city taken as a whole, its institutions, and its way of life become a true church, placing themselves in the service of good Christian living; at the same time, the church is fully realized in the republic into which it instills its spiritual patrimony.

The union of religion and republic is emblematic of the victory of the divine plan on earth and the reconciliation between heaven and earth, between the human and the divine. The problem of the liberty of other peoples and the other cities under Florentine domination is of no concern to Savonarola. His overriding objective is to ensure that the Florentines devote themselves wholeheartedly to political and religious reform. He seeks not only a new republican leader, but a redeemer, a politician who can attain a religious and moral reformation, and who is himself a prophet. For that reason, he condemns the saying commonly attributed to Cosimo de’ Medici, that states are not held with paternosters, as an example of the irreligious cynicism that destroys both civil living and Christian living. For that same reason, he emphasizes the idea that the more a republic is spiritual, the stronger it becomes and that those who govern become true rulers only inasmuch as they obey Christian morality.55 He states specifically, however, that electing to public office men who are good but foolish is a way of undermining “the honor of God, the common good, and the honor and reputation” of a city.56

The Florentine political elite shared only in part the millenarian vision of Florence as a New Jerusalem. It accepted, on the other hand, and almost without exception, the idea that the republican constitution of 1494 was a divine gift, that God had a special love for Florence, and that the city’s respect for the law of God and for religious worship assured it of God’s friendship. When the most respected and influential citizens of Florence took the floor in sessions and in deliberative and advisory bodies to offer their carefully thought-out advice on problems of domestic politics and international affairs, they would emphasize the idea that Florence was a religious republic and that it needed religion.

For the Florentines, God is all-powerful and intervenes whenever He wishes in human affairs. Fortune is often malevolent, but God “can alter fortune,” as an orator explained in the session of 19 August 1505.57 We must turn to God for wise advice. At times of grave danger, it is necessary that priests organize processions, that the republic increase its charity to the poor, and that the miraculous image of the Madonna dell’Impruneta be solemnly conveyed into the city. During the crisis in the spring of 1512, which led to the dissolution of the republican regime, a number of members of the advisory body suggested delaying all their deliberations for one day so that they could gather in prayer.58

The Florentines believed that governing on behalf of the public good was a religious duty, in the precise sense of a duty to God. To fail to perform that duty entailed divine punishment. When someone works toward a just cause, on the other hand, “God must favor his efforts.”59 Even in cases where the defense of the republic has been entrusted to men who have erred through their sins, God will still be at their side. For the followers of Savonarola, it was necessary in all public deliberations always to put Christian principles above the principles dictated by human reason.60 They called the Great Council (Consiglio Grande) the “soul” and “the life of our city.”61 Since it was a gift of God and dear to God, the Great Council had succeeded in withstanding the attacks of the enemies of free living.62 The belief that republican institutions and civil living were a gift of God had as its political consequence the belief that those institutions were perfect and that the only way to reform them would be to draw them back to their original principles.

A significant document of the republican religion in Machiavelli’s Florence is the speech by Lamberto del Nero Cambi in favor of the law imposing a tithe on the revenues of priests. His oration is entirely inspired by the religion of the fatherland, taken not as an alternative to the Christian religion, but as the true and right interpretation of that religion. After a brief preamble, Lamberto del Nero explains that

Republics and kingdoms, and more generally all states, as they are acquired, thus they are maintained, either by force through arms, or by love through religion, or with both one and the other thing taken together; and in my own view, if one of those two elements were to be absent, I would prefer to be deprived of the force of arms, rather than being deprived of religion, as I judge it not only more pious but also more secure to entrust oneself to the will of God than to place oneself in the power of men. And it is a true thing that, both religion and arms need—indeed, demand—not merely good laws, which are important, but good men, who set their hands to those laws, and ensure that they are justly and inviolably observed and executed on both one side and the other.


In order to reinforce his argument, he cites the Romans and urges his listeners to follow their example:

If the Gentiles so greatly revered—as we see in the Roman republic, better ordered than all other republics—their own religion, which was manifestly false, then what should we Christians do with respect to our own religion, which is indubitably true? And if they punished so harshly those who violated or besmirched their ceremonies, then what chastisement can be administered that is not trifling to those who violate or besmirch our own ceremonies? It strikes me that I have observed, while reading ancient histories, that men have been—not merely better—but greater and more prudent, the more they have emphasized and always followed the matters of religion; nor do I believe that there is a greater sign, or better argument that a certain city or region must choose between either quickly changing or falling into ruin, than seeing in that place either changes in or scorn of a divine cult.


As he draws to his conclusion, he levels against the papacy the charge that it has corrupted the Christian religion:

Those who say that the popes have been absolutely the prime and foremost cause of our ruin and miseries, as well as of the servitude of Italy, do not speak the truth; because it was not the popes, but the ambition of the popes, and the avarice of the popes, and the infinite lust and cruelty of the popes that caused and continue to cause all of our ills. Their vast greed and incredible wickedness, not the popes themselves, have annihilated and virtually extinguished the Christian faith, which is in and of itself holy and good, but which is doubtless also highly useful, indeed, entirely necessary, not only so that we may live in beatitude in the world beyond, but also to live securely in this world.


Last, in the peroration, he appeals to the tradition of Florentine republican Christianity: “Read your histories, and you will find that eight men, who were at the time the most religious and Catholic men to be found, having shown greater respect for the common good than to the private interests of a few priests, won so much favor among the populace, and so much benevolence among one and all, that they are were generally called, and are still known today, when we think or talk of them, as the Otto Santi (Eight Saints).”63

When, at the end of the third decade of the sixteenth century, the Florentine experiment with republican government was on the wane, there was a corresponding decline in the faith in a God who loved republics. One piece of evidence of this decline is found in Guicciardini’s reflections in his Cose fiorentine (1528). In this text, Guicciardini reports the belief that God loves the freedom of peoples as a commonly held opinion in Florence, but he cautiously abstains from adopting that opinion as his own.64 He neither criticizes confiding in God nor expressing gratitude to Him, but he exhorts his reader to be wise and to hope for God’s help as a reward for wisdom rather than as a remedy for foolishness or madness: “It is quite appropriate to put one’s hopes in God, to recommend oneself with prayers and good deeds, but then to follow the advice of reason and to hope that God will help you if you behave sagely, and not to do wrong in order to have things turn out well, and behaving in accordance with the call of reason, and in all other things resign oneself to God; by doing otherwise you do not placate Him, rather you irritate Him and you give Him good reason to transform His mercy into righteous wrath.”65 These are words that express a stern criticism of the religion of indolence, and they tell us that even a strictly observant Catholic and a clear-eyed realist like Guicciardini sensed the link between the republic and a civil religion.


2. The Religion of Liberty


Machiavelli’s republican theory, like that of the humanists, strongly believes in the ideal of “political and civil way of life.”66 Any form of government, including a republican or popular government, that fails to satisfy the requirements of civil life, is either a tyranny or a corrupt republic. In full agreement with the tradition of political thought that I have summarized here, Machiavelli considered the rule of law to be a fundamental principle of civil life. In Discourses on Livy, he contrasts a “political way of life” with tyranny understood as “absolute power.”67 In other places, he separates political life and corruption: “Two things are to be considered here: one, that one has to seek glory in a corrupt city by modes other than in one that still lives politically.”68 In a corrupt city, he explains, “well ordered laws do not help”69 and “neither laws nor orders can be found that are enough to check a universal corruption.”70

When he speaks of the rule of law, Machiavelli always means a government of just laws and statutes that tend to promote the common good. In complete agreement with the Ciceronian doctrine and with the interpretation that the humanists gave of it, Machiavelli claims that a law is a legitimate command that respects the principles of fairness and equity. As an anonymous orator eloquently explains in the Florentine Histories, in order to restore the “true free and civil life,” Florence needs new laws and new statutes that will protect the common good against the power of factions and will replace the laws and statutes ordered “not in accordance with free life but by the ambition of that party which has come out on top.”71 The same orator, though it is of course Machiavelli himself who is speaking, states as well that the laws against the common good were one of the effects of a bad religious mentality. In contrast, the good laws and the good orders of the Romans sprang from the love of the common good that their religion instilled.
