Since the rule of law and the common good are the essential features of a civil way of life, the best government will be one that is most likely to ensure these two goods. A “political way of life” can be attained either through a republican form of government or else through a monarchic government, on the condition that the sovereign power is, in either case, regulated by laws: “a prince who can do what he wishes is crazy; a people that can do what it wishes is not wise.”72 Between a people “fettered” by the laws and a prince likewise “obligated to the laws,” Machiavelli believes that the former is preferable to the latter, because it is more apt to choose better rulers and to safeguard the common good. In his defense of the superiority of a republican government over a monarchy, Machiavelli makes use of the classic argument that if the deliberations over matters of general interest are entrusted to the many, it is more likely that the common good will prevail over narrow and individual interests. “And without doubt,” he writes, “this common good is not observed if not in republics, since all that is for that purpose is executed, and although it may turn out to harm this or that private individual, those for whom the aforesaid does good are so many that they can go ahead with it against the disposition of the few crushed by it.”73

Even when he is discussing the matter of “Where the Guard of Freedom May Be Settled More Securely, in the People or in the Great”—that is, the establishment of a specific magistracy charged with monitoring the legality of political decisions—his chief concern is defending the rule of law.74 If we consider the aims of the nobility and the aims of ordinary citizens, he writes, we clearly see that the nobility desires power, while the commonfolk wish only to avoid domination, and therefore to live freely: “So when those who are popular are posted as the guard of freedom, it is reasonable that they have more care for it, and since they are not able to seize it, they do not permit others to seize it.” He then presents the point of view of those who support the idea of government by the aristocracy: they declare that it is safer to entrust the nobility with a dominant role in the public institutions because in that way the nobles will be satisfied and will have no reason to agitate in opposition to the republic. After conceding that “he who discourses well on the one thing and the other could remain doubtful as to which should be chosen by him as guard of such freedom,” Machiavelli concludes his analysis by examining the question of which are more dangerous in a republic: those who wish to obtain more than what they already have, or those who fear losing the wealth and honor that they already possess. Both the former and the latter, Machiavelli comments, can trigger grave disturbances, but the nobles who fear losing their honors and their wealth are the more dangerous because “it does not appear to men that they possess securely what a man has unless he acquires something else new. There is this besides: that since they possess much, they are able to make an alteration with greater power and greater motion.” If we carefully weigh the arguments on behalf of both the first and the second thesis, it is clearly wiser to entrust the populace with the task of guarding freedom than to assign that task to the nobility.75

The aspect of Machiavelli’s republicanism that reveals in an especially clear fashion his debt to the Roman tradition is his analysis of political liberty. Like the Roman political writers, and using a language similar to that used by jurists, he defined as “free men” those men who do not “depend on others,”76 drawing a contrast between the status of the free citizen and that of a slave: “born free and not slave.”77 Along the same line, he calls free states those states that “are accustomed to living by their own laws and in liberty.”78 Like republican political writers, he believes that citizens can securely enjoy their liberty only in an independent republic where political life is sufficiently protected. If the rule of law is restored, and if the laws that protect the interests of factions are replaced with laws and statutes that aim at the common good, states a citizen driven by love of the fatherland in the Florentine Histories, Florence will once again have a “true free and civil life.”79 In contrast, a city cannot be free if there is a citizen who is “feared by the magistrates.”80

As he explained in the Florentine Histories, a city “can be called free” only if it has good laws and good constitutional orderings capable of restraining both the ill humors of the nobles and those of the commonfolk, that is, the insolence of the former and the license of the latter.81 Free republics must be capable of moderating the passions and the desires of the citizens in such a way that they respect the boundaries of civil life. One example of this is the Roman republic, where the people “never served humbly nor dominated proudly while the republic lasted uncorrupt; indeed, with its orders and magistrates, it held its rank honorably.”82 Another example, in modern times, are the free German cities, which “observe their laws so that no one from outside or inside dares to seize them.”83 In another chapter of Discourses on Livy he reiterates the necessary connection between law and freedom with words that require no comment: the republic degenerates when “one begins to corrupt a law that is the nerve and the life of a free way of life.”84

A monarch or a prince can satisfy the wishes of individuals to live securely if they introduce laws and constitutional provisions that protect both their power and the tranquility of their subjects, and if they respect the laws and prevent others from breaking them. If a prince does these things, the people will feel secure and contented, as in France, where the kings are “obligated to respect countless laws, which preserve the security of all their peoples.”85 Political liberty, nonetheless, is secure and the citizens can enjoy it fully only in a republic, on the condition that the republic has not been corrupted. In Discourses on Livy, Machiavelli speaks of the “common utility that is drawn from a free way of life,” and he describes it as “being able to enjoy one’s things freely, without any suspicion, not fearing for the honor of wives and that of children, not to be afraid for oneself.”86 Aside from ensuring security, a good republic also allows its citizens to enjoy a degree of liberty that the subjects of a monarch cannot attain, that is, the freedom to take part in sovereign deliberations, the freedom to be summoned to serve in public office, and even to win the highest honors. In a republic, he writes in Discourses on Livy, the citizens “can, through their virtue, become princes”; in a principality this possibility is not available to them because the prince “cannot honor any of the citizens he tyrannizes over who are able and good since he does not wish to have to have suspicion of them.”87 A free way of life “proffers honors and rewards through certain honest and determinate causes, and outside these it neither rewards nor honors anyone”88 A prince, in contrast, frequently gives honors to corrupt men.89

In a well-ordered republic, the poor citizens must have the same possibilities as the others to win public honors; in Rome, Machiavelli writes, “the way to any rank whatever and to any honor whatever was not prevented for you because of poverty, and that one went to find virtue in whatever house it inhabited.”90 If it was the citizens, in their councils, who freely elected the magistrates, it is more likely that they would select honest and capable men, as a true political order demands.91 Finally, Machiavelli accepted without reservation another political principle that the Roman theorists and their modern disciples had indicated as one of the distinctive characteristics of a free city, that is, the possibility of freely expressing one’s own opinions in public assemblies. Machiavelli cited as an example the Roman republic, where “a tribune, or any other citizen whatever, could propose a law to the people, on which every citizen was able to speak, either in favor or against, before it was decided.” It is a good thing, he comments, that “each one who intended a good for the public could propose it; and it is good that each can speak his opinion on it so that the people can then choose the best after each one has been heard.”92

Because a republic makes possible the secure enjoyment of all aspects of political liberty, Machiavelli identifies republics with freedom and draws a contrast between the republic and not only tyranny and license, but also with monarchies and principalities. In the well-known opening of The Prince he writes: “All states, all dominions that have held and do hold empire over men have been and are either republics or principalities.” A few lines below that, he reiterates the same distinction, replacing the term republic with “being free”: “Either in the habit of living under a prince or used to being free.”93 In the fifth chapter, he writes that “when cities or provinces are used to living under a prince, and his blood line is eliminated—since on the one hand they are used to obeying, and on the other they do not have the old prince—they will not agree to make one from among themselves and they do not know how to live free.” In the ninth chapter he cites three mutually exclusive possibilities: “principality or liberty or license.”

In Discourses on Livy the examples in which he equates republican government with liberty are of course far more numerous. In book 3, chapter 12, when writing about the mainland cities subject to Venetian rule, he notes that they were “used to living under a prince,” “not free,” and “accustomed to serving.” Living under the rule of a monarch or a prince, as is demonstrated by the passage that I have quoted here, not only means being not free in the sense that the subjects are excluded from all sovereign deliberations and from public office, but also that they are dependent on the will of a single man, and therefore not a free people. Under the rule of a monarch, if that monarch is just, the subjects can enjoy a certain security, but that security remains precarious at best, because they nevertheless remain dependent on his will. Since they have no power to prevent the monarch from imposing laws that favor his own interest or other private interests, they may at any moment be oppressed.

Machiavelli’s republicanism was inspired by the ideal of a well-ordered popular government. By well-ordered popular government he meant, in accordance with the Ciceronian conception of order as moderation and proper placement of the parts within the whole, a republic in which each social component played a role of its own.94 As an example, he cites Sparta, where Lycurgus “ordered his laws so as to give their roles to the kings, the aristocrats, and people and made a state that lasted more than eight hundred years, achieving the highest praise for himself and quiet in that city”; and Rome, which became a “perfect republic” only when, with the creation of the tribunes of the plebs, “all three kinds of government there had their part.”95 As an example of a badly ordered republic, Machiavelli points to Florence, which never had a political constitution that recognized to each social group a role of its own, and for that reason remained unstable, over the course of its history, oscillating between governments that were either excessively popular or excessively aristocratic. The too-popular governments prevented the nobles from attaining public honors, and therefore the city became increasingly humble and abject; in the excessively aristocratic governments, “the people did not play a part of its own,” and the republic was therefore shaken by a permanent state of instability.96

Religion is an essential aspect of Machiavelli’s republicanism. Although some founders, such as Romulus, were not obliged to avail themselves of the “authority of God” in order to establish new civil and military orders, many others, “such as Numa Pompilius, Lycurgus, and Solon,” in order to persuade the citizens to accept their extraordinary laws, had recourse “to God.” Numa, in particular, “pretended to be intimate with a nymph who counseled him on what he had to counsel the people,” and thanks to this charade he succeeded in persuading the Romans to accept the new laws. Savonarola succeeded in persuading the citizens of Florence, who were neither ignorant nor coarse, to accept the new republican orderings of 1494 because he convinced them that he “spoke with God.”

Founders such as Moses, Cyrus, Theseus, and Romulus, obtain the friendship of God as a result of their remarkable virtue, and they succeed, with divine aid, in the exceedingly difficult task of founding a state. Lawgivers are successful in the almost equally difficult challenge of introducing new orderings because they too possess extraordinary qualities—“the goodness and prudence” of Numa; “the life and learning” of Savonarola”—and, thanks to their qualities they are believed when they claim to have been inspired by God. Other men as well could do what Numa and Savonarola did before them, if they possessed similar qualities: “No one, therefore, should be terrified that he cannot carry out what has been carried out by others, for as was said in our preface, men are born, live, and die always in one and the same order.”97 The founder who succeeded, through his virtues, in persuading the people that he had been inspired by God, and therefore achieved his goals, was not in Machiavelli’s eyes an ancient myth, but rather a possibility of modern politics, attainable on the condition that men, or at least some men in their midst, free themselves of the mistaken belief that it is impossible to imitate the great statesmen of the past.

To Machiavelli, both founders and lawgivers are similar to God or else sent directly by God, as was the case with Numa. In his narrative, Livy wrote that “when they heard the name of Numa, although the Roman fathers [the senators] perceived that the balance of power would incline to the Sabines if a king were chosen from them, yet none of them ventured to prefer himself, or any other member of his party, or, in fine, any of the citizens or fathers, to a man so well known, but unanimously resolved that the kingdom should be offered to Numa Pompilius.” When he reached Rome, Numa determined that the gods should be consulted, but Livy says nothing of divine inspiration.98 Machiavelli, on the other hand, focuses on the divine influence in the senate’s decision: it was “the heavens,” he wrote, that “inspired in the breast of the Roman Senate” the sound counsel that brought infinite good to Rome, advising them to elect Numa as successor to Romulus “so that those things omitted by him might be ordered by Numa.” Since this is a task that falls to men who are friends of God or to men who are inspired or sent by God, the birth and ordering of states has a religious characters and prompts reverence and veneration. All states, Machiavelli observes, “have some reverence in the beginning,” and the founders of states who succeed in making their peoples observe “their constitutions for long” obtain “veneration.”99


3. Customs and Religion


Even the best political orders established by the wisest lawgivers are incapable of protecting a free life if the customs are corrupt: “For as good customs have need of laws to maintain themselves, so laws have need of good customs so as to be observed.”100 The jurists of the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries had emphasized in their treatises that the objective of the laws is to shape customs in order to make men well suited to civil living (“viventem civiliter”), and in order to forge individuals who know how to keep a republic alive.101 Coluccio Salutati, in his De nobilitate legum et medicinae, emphasizes that the goal of lawgivers “is to make the citizens good and obedient to the laws”; he also adds: “Politics is intended to preserve human society; the law has the same objective. Politics wants a good citizen, and what else do the lawgivers desire, with their institutions?”102

Machiavelli was familiar with this tradition of thought and he accepted its fundamental principle. In Discourses on Livy he reiterates the common belief that “hunger and poverty make men industrious, and the laws make them good,” that is to say, disposed to obey the laws and to perform their civic duties. The task of the law is therefore to correct customs: “Where a thing works well on its own without the law, the law is not necessary; but when some good custom is lacking, at once the law is necessary.”103

Following the lead of the political writers of humanism, Machiavelli stresses that religion is more effective than laws in moderating the customs of a people. Those who carefully consider Roman history will see, he warns in Discourses on Livy, how useful religion was as a way “to keep men good.” Laws “keep” men good through fear of human punishment, or through the desire for human reward; religion keeps them good with the fear of divine punishment or the hope of divine reward. The good customs upheld by religion remain vigorous even when laws and orders lose their power. Religion, therefore, is stronger than laws and even than the virtue of the rulers. For that reason, it is “a thing altogether necessary if [one] wished to maintain a civilization,” whether that civilization takes the form of a kingdom or a republic.104

The sovereign people of a republic has, when it is not corrupt, a special kind of wisdom that makes it godlike: “A people is more prudent, more stable, and of better judgment than a prince. Not without cause may the voice of a people be likened to that of God; for one sees universal opinion produce marvelous effects in its forecasts, so that it appears to foresee its ill and its good by a hidden virtue.”105 Through the sovereign people, the voice of God speaks secretly to ensure that common good is attained in the world and free living is established. Machiavelli recognizes in the sovereign people of a free republic a divine aura that is never attributed to the prince. He takes divinity from the prince and assigns it to the sovereign people, thus blazing a path for intellectual and political developments of great importance in the history of republican political thought.

Religion is more necessary in republics than in monarchies because in republics the people is master of the laws, and therefore there is no fear of a prince to restrain men from their natural insolence: “As the observance of the divine cult is the cause of the greatness of republics, so disdain for it is the cause of their ruin. For where the fear of God fails, it must be either that the kingdom comes to ruin or that it is sustained by the fear of a prince, which supplies the defects of religion.”106 That fear would in any case be extinguished on the death of a virtuous prince, while the fear of God will survive, whether or not the prince is alive. In order to ensure that a republic lives on for a long time, it is wiser to rely on religion than on the fear that a virtuous prince is capable of instilling. A people that has no true and good religion can live only under a prince or under a tyrant. We shall see what the moral content should be of the kind of religion that is needed by republics, but that a free life cannot exist without a religious underpinning is an undoubted matter for Machiavelli.

All the free peoples that Machiavelli points out as examples are profoundly religious. The first is the Roman people, where for many centuries “there was never so much fear of God as in that republic; which made easier whatever enterprise the Senate or the great men of Rome might plan to make.”107 Another such people were the Etruscans, one of the “very free peoples” of antiquity, who lived “secure for a great time, with the highest glory of empire and of arms and special praise for customs and religion.”108 Yet another such people were the Samnites, who were also a free people of antiquity, who “Had Recourse to Religion” as a last “remedy from which they could take hope of recovering lost virtue,” providing evidence of “how much confidence can be had through religion well used.”109 Among the modern examples, we should note the free cities of Germany: “In the province of Germany this goodness and this religion are still seen to be great in those peoples, which make many republics there live free, and they observe their laws so that no one from outside or inside dares to seize them.”

The peoples without religion, on the other hand, are corrupt and enslaved. The Italians, who have become, thanks to the church of Rome. “without religion and wicked,” are the most corrupt and servile people.110 The second example are the French and the Spanish, likewise Catholic, but without the religion and the goodness of the ancient Romans and the modern Germans. And so they live in servitude inasmuch as they are subjects of kings, and they are corrupt, though less so than the Italians, not so much because of their goodness, “which is in good part lacking,” as much as because they have “one king that maintains them united not only through his virtue but through the order of those kingdoms, which is not yet spoiled.”111

Those who are religious can live a free way of life; those who are not religious can only live in servitude. In order to fully understand the significance of this alternative, one should nonetheless keep in mind that Machiavelli is praising as a foundation of political liberty that religion that teaches virtue and condemns idleness:

To think that without you for you God fights,

While you are on your knees and nothing do,

Has ruined many kingdoms, many states.


Prayers are necessary as a rule,

And he who from his people snatches rites

And orisons is utterly a fool.


Perfect control and union they keep,

And if you have all this, it is quite true

You will good fortune ultimately reap.


But let your brain prove not so small and short

By thinking that, should your house fall and crumble,

God would soon save it with no man’s support:


You would soon die beneath its very stones.112


Machiavelli calls for a religion that educates the people to a genuine goodness of heart. Just what he means by “goodness,” he explains with two examples drawn from the history of the peoples that he admires for their religiosity and their good customs, the Romans and the Germans. The Roman example refers to an episode from the war against the inhabitants of Veio. The spoils of war, in part intended for Apollo, fell instead into the hands of the Roman plebs. In order to recover the booty, the Senate issued an edict ordering all those who had taken the loot to return a tenth of it. This edict proves that the Senate had full faith in the plebs and was certain that none of the commoners of Rome would refuse to return what was ordered. And yet that restitution did not take place, in part because the plebs mutinied against the edict with “open indignation.” That open rebellion, and that rejection of deceit, demonstrated in Machiavelli’s view, “how much goodness and how much religion were in that people, and how much good was to be hoped from it.”113

The example of the peoples of Germany is even more eloquent because it proves that in those peoples there still lives “that ancient goodness” and because their goodness “is so much more to be admired in these times as it is rarer; indeed one sees it remaining only in that province.” “When it occurs to those republics,” Machiavelli writes, “that they need to spend some quantity of money for the public account, they are used to having those magistrates or councils that have authority for it assess on all the inhabitants of the city one percent or two of what each has of value. When such a decision has been made, each presents himself before the collectors of such a duty according to the order of the town; and having first taken an oath to pay the fitting amount, he throws into a chest so designated what according to his conscience it appears to him he ought to pay. Of this payment there is no witness except him who pays.” History demonstrates to Machiavelli that in the peoples of Germany there was still much “goodness” and much “religion.”114 To perform one’s duties in accordance with “conscience,” to eschew “fraud,” to possess a sense of individual responsibility, this is the goodness that an uncorrupted Christian religion teaches, and which Machiavelli considers necessary to a free way of life. And in order to find these qualities, it is not necessary to go back in time to ancient paganism, nor is it the realm of dreams to think of reviving it. Those citizens of the free German republics were Christians, not pagans. And yet the civil ethos of religion lived on in them. The same ethos survived, though more feebly, in his own Florence. What Machiavelli was lamenting was the fact that the ancient goodness had not survived in the princes of the Christian religion: “If such religion [of the Romans],” he writes in a significant passage of Discourses on Livy, “had been maintained by the princes of the Christian republic as was ordered by its giver, the Christian states and republics would be more united, much happier than they are.”115

Christianity, on the other hand, did all it could to destroy not only the pagan religion, but even the memory of that religion: “For when a new sect—that is, a new religion—emerges, its first concern is to extinguish the old to give itself reputation; and when it occurs that the orderers of the new sect are of a different language, they easily eliminate it. This thing is known from considering the modes that the Christian sect took against the Gentile. It suppressed all its orders and all its ceremonies and eliminated every memory of that ancient theology.”116 Along with those orders, ceremonies, and theology, it also extinguished—and this is what Machiavelli deplored—the civic virtue that religion had kept alive.

The religion that is necessary to a free way of life is the religion that makes men good and brings “shame to the wicked.” Machiavelli here adopts another theme that was beloved by the humanists. Salutati had written in this connection that it is necessary to distinguish between the shame that fills men before guilt, and leads them to “act to conceal,” and the shame that “makes them abhor vicious and dishonorable actions.” While the first kind of shame encourages the guilty to act stealthily and to “lurk in the shadows for the turpitude that they commit,” the second kind wards them away from evil, and should therefore be considered a virtue.117 The shame that drives us to refrain from evil is an obligation to the conscience that “accuses us, persuades us, judges us”; and internalization of the sentiment of the duty that a true Christian must feel more deeply than others, as Saint Paul teaches when he writes that “for our rejoicing is this, the testimony of our conscience.”118

When he speaks of the shame that religion manages to produce within the souls of the guilty, therefore helping to uphold the laws, Machiavelli too is referring not to the fear of the reproof of other men or of punishments, but rather of that internal feeling that springs from the awareness that one has violated the duties that one’s conscience and God impose on one. Machiavelli praises the religion that is able to enter into the hearts of men and keep alive a sense of duty, like the pagan religion of the ancient Romans and the Christianity of the modern Germans. The charge that he levels against the Catholic religion of having made the Italians wicked should be interpreted together with his praise of the religion that makes men good. Wicked are not only those men who break the law, deceitfully, even more than those who use force, but especially those men who no longer have any morals or conscience in the strict sense of the term.

If men are to be taught to be good, and aided effectively to live in a civil manner, then religion must keep its distance from political power and not become subordinate to the powerful. Machiavelli obtains this teaching from the religion of the Romans. “The life of the Gentile religion,” he observes, “was founded on the responses of the oracles and on the sect of the diviners and augurs. All their other ceremonies, sacrifices, and rites depended on them; for they easily believed that that god who could predict your future good or your future ill for you could also grant it to you. From these arose the temples, from these the sacrifices, from these the supplications and every other ceremony to venerate them; through these the oracle of Delos, the temple of Jupiter Ammon, and other celebrated oracles who filled the world with admiration and devotion. As these later began to speak in the mode of the powerful, and as that falsity was exposed among peoples, men became incredulous and apt to disturb every good order.119 The history of the church confirms the same teaching. In the first centuries of Christianity, the pontiffs had authority and veneration “for the holiness of their lives and for the miracles” as well as for “reverence for their customs and their learning.”120 When they became powerful through the weakening of the emperor and of the barbarian kings in Italy, and through their arms, their censures, and their indulgence, they were “terrible and awesome,” but they lost their moral authority. In subsequent centuries, they also saw their temporal power weakened, and having rendered the Italians unbelievers and without religion, they are now “at the discretion of others.”121

Machiavelli, like others before him, wanted to see an end put to the temporal power of the popes. One piece of evidence among many is his account of the unsuccessful conspiracy of Stefano Porcari, who attempted to free Rome from the “hands of prelates” and “restore it to its ancient way of life.” Even though Porcari failed in his attempt, Machiavelli remarks, his intention can be praised by some.122 Machiavelli’s desire to see the punishment of the corrupt popes and prelates also emerges in his account of Julius II’s visit to Perugia. When he reached the city, with the intention of expelling therefrom Giovampagolo Baglioni, Machiavelli recounts, Julius II “did not wait to enter that city with his army, which was guarding him, but entered it unarmed, notwithstanding that Giovampagolo was inside with many troops that he had gathered for defense of himself.” Giovampagolo, despite the fact that he was a man capable of all sorts of wickedness, failed to take advantage of the pope’s mistake. If he had, Machiavelli observes, “everyone would have admired his spirit and that would have left an eternal memory of himself as being the first who had demonstrated to the prelates how little is to be esteemed whoever lives and reigns as they do; and he would have done a thing whose greatness would have surpassed all infamy, every danger, that could have proceeded from it.”123

Machiavelli praised the princes and captains who used rituals and auspices as they saw best, in the tradition of the Romans, who “Interpreted the Auspices According to Necessity, and with Prudence Made a Show of Observing Religion When Forced Not to Observe It; and If Anyone Rashly Disdained It, They Punished Him.”124 He did not, however, accept the idea that popes could humiliate kings. A private individual, he writes in the Florentine Histories, would have been ashamed to subject himself to the conditions that Pope Alexander III imposed on the king of England.125 Machiavelli was indifferent to the question of whether the church was run as a republic or a monarchy. What mattered to him was whether the prelates were good and if they nourished and sustained religious sentiment.

Princes and republics, in their turn, must maintain “the foundations of the religion they hold,” and support “all things” that encourage religion, even if they judge them false.126 Machiavelli did not want a religiosity that was reduced to the mere exterior practices of the cult and which absolved those who did evil, much as did Fra Timoteo, who was concerned with processions and statues, and who was false, greedy, tawdry, and shameless. Nor did he wish a religion that inspires the reverence of cowards, who know neither how “to be honorably wicked or perfectly good” and who are incapable of performing those acts of noble, great-spirited wickedness that are necessary to the success of great political undertakings.127 What he wanted, in other words, was a religion made up of faith and the fear of God, a religion that instills sincere devotion, that penetrates into the soul, that educates the faithful to a sense of duty, that teaches its adherents to love liberty, and which helps men to find within themselves the moral strength to defend the free way of life. He was willing to let others worry about the forms of worship and the internal organization of the church.


4. Religion, War, and Social Conflict


Aside from serving to reinforce and maintain civil virtue, religion is necessary in order to have good armies: “For where there is religion, arms can easily be introduced, and where there are arms and not religion, the latter can be introduced only with difficulty,” as the history of Rome teaches us.128 Good armies are in Machiavelli’s view the necessary foundation of any state. “The principal foundations that all states have, new ones as well as old or mixed, are good laws and good arms. And because there cannot be good laws where there are not good arms, and where there are good arms there must be good laws, I shall leave out the reasoning on laws and shall speak of arms.”129 In The Art of War, he reiterates the same point: “Because all the arts that are provided for in a state for the sake of the common good of men, all the statutes made in it so that men will live in fear of the laws and of God, would be vain if for them there were not provided defenses, which when well ordered, preserve them, even though they themselves are not well ordered.”130 Because religion helps to establish and preserve the good arms needed to defend the lives of states, without religion, or with a bad religion, states cannot survive. For this reason as well Machiavelli placed on the highest step of men worthy of praise the “heads and orderers of religions,” and beneath them, “those who have founded either republics or kingdoms,” and believed that Rome owed its fortune more to Numa, who introduced the religious orders, than to Romulus, who introduced the political and military institutions.

Religion is necessary to the creation of good armies, especially because it renders sacred the oath that soldiers take before going into battle: “Also very powerful in keeping the ancient soldiers well disposed were religion and the oath sworn when they were taken into service, because in all their transgressions they were threatened not alone with the ills they could fear from men but with those they could expect from God. This condition, mingled with other religious customs, many times made every sort of undertaking easy for the ancient generals, and always will make them so, where religion is feared and observed.”131 In Italy, by contrast, where men have no religion, and are “corrupt” and “unaccustomed to any honorable obedience,” the oath taken by soldiers is of no value, and it is therefore impossible to establish a good army.

Machiavelli believed that the oaths that soldiers swore were an essential aspect of good military discipline. When he was secretary, he had inserted in the Provision of the Ordinance of the Militia (Provisione della ordinanza) that established the militia (1506) a detailed description of the solemn ceremony of the administration of the oath:

This commissioner, or any deputy as described above, should the following morning, as agreed the previous day, have a solemn mass of the Holy Ghost said in such a place that all those assembled can hear it. And after this mass has been celebrated, the deputy should speak to them such words as are appropriate in such a ceremony; then he should read to them the regulations and duties that they must observe, and administer a solemn oath to them to that effect, causing each of them, one by one, to place his hand on the book of the Holy Gospels. And before they swear that oath he should read to them all the capital penalties to which they will be subject, and all the admonitions ordained by said officials in the preservation and reinforcement of their union and oath; rendering more majestic the oath with all the obligatory words of body and soul that are found to be most effective; once this has been done, they will all be free to go back to their own homes.132


The soldiers of the militia that Machiavelli had established at the cost of such great effort fled in terror when they were faced with the attack on Prato of the Spanish infantry, in 1512, and they died without honor. Little seemed to count the solemn oath that they had sworn to defend with their lives the freedom of the republic. Fifteen years later, in the words that he places in the mouth of Fabrizio Colonna in The Art of War we can perceive all the despair of a man who, by now drawing near to the end of his life, understands how much harm was done to Italy by the wicked religion that stripped that oath of all value: “By what God or by what saints can I have them take oath? By those they worship or those they blaspheme? What one they worship I do not know, but I know well that they blaspheme them all. How can I believe they will observe their promises to those for whom every hour they show contempt? How can those who feel contempt for God respect men?”133

Religion, profoundly experienced as an obligation to God, helps peoples to gather their moral forces to react to military defeats that endanger the liberty and lives of one and all. “For after the defeat that Hannibal had given to the Romans at Cannae,” Machiavelli writes, “many citizens gathered together and, terrified for their fatherland, agreed to abandon Italy and move to Sicily. Hearing this, Scipio went to meet them and with naked steel in hand constrained them to swear they would not abandon the fatherland.”134 Livy emphasizes the terror that Scipio struck into the hearts of those citizens who wanted to abandon the fatherland: “Terrified, as though they were beholding the victorious Hannibal, they all take the oath, and deliver themselves to Scipio to be kept in custody.”135 Machiavelli focuses rather on the importance of religion in the face of extreme peril, when laws, and the love of the fatherland, strong though that was among the Romans, are no longer enough to win out over fear and to preserve the determination to defend liberty, even at the cost of one’s life: “So those citizens whom the love of fatherland and its laws did not keep in Italy were kept there by an oath that they were forced to take.” Likewise, in his account of another episode from the history of Rome, that of how Lucius Manlius was saved by his son, Livy emphasizes that the tribune of the plebs Marcus Pomponius decided to abandon charges against Lucius Manlius out of fear of that man’s son, who had gone to confront him with a knife in his hand.136 Machiavelli interpreted this episode as yet another demonstration of the power of religion: “This arose from nothing other than that religion Numa had introduced in that city.”137

When he speaks of arms, Machiavelli always means arms under the laws of the republic. The republics that educate citizens to military discipline without ever allowing them to wage war as an art are successful in warding off corruption. As long as Rome “continued without reproach,” Machiavelli writes, “no great citizen ever presumed, by means of such an activity, to retain power in time of peace, so as to break the laws, plunder the provinces, usurp and tyrannize over his native land and in every way gain wealth for himself. Nor did anybody of low estate dream of violating his oath, forming parties with private citizens, ceasing to fear the Senate, or carrying out any tyrannical injury.”138 A well-ordered republic, then, must “decree that this practice of warfare shall be used in times of peace for exercise and in times of war for necessity and for glory.”139 If the exercise of the art of war is firmly under the control of the laws, the republic need in no way fear that armed citizens will become seditious. Its arms-bearing citizens, quite to the contrary, are the soundest foundation of liberty.140

Religion is necessary as a way of teaching soldiers to experience shame at doing wrong. To Machiavelli, men without shame are bad soldiers: “What can I do to make them modest, who are born and bought up without modesty?” Fabrizio Colonna complains in The Art of War. However respected a commander may be, and however skilled he is at inspiring love, he will be unable to instill in his men a sense of shame, if they are not religious. Machiavelli clearly states the horrors of which soldiers without religion—and therefore without discipline and shame—are capable, and he is willing to cite examples of crimes perpetrated by militias in the pay of Florence.141 Roman soldiers also sacked and looted, as Machiavelli notes in Discourses on Livy, but “being men full of religion,” they stopped before temples, and when they did enter, they did so “without tumult, all devoted and full of reverence.”142

The Christian religion is a brake, hindering unjust wars and a warning to seek peace. God approves only necessary wars as just;143 He abhors wars waged “for least cause,” as he writes in the canzone The Blessed Spirits (Gli spiriti beati), probably composed around the time of the war between François I and Charles V for domination over Italy (1521–23). Machiavelli also explains in this canzone that “it is our Lord’s desire / That peace should prosper, and mankind cease fire.”144 Although they meet with God’s displeasure, wars are a constant scourge: “For as long as I can remember, people have always been either making war or talking about going to war; it is now being talked about and in a short while it will be declared; when it is over, people will start talking about it again,” he wrote to Guicciardini on 3 January 1526. The causes of war are either the ambition that drives princes and peoples to conquer and expand their domains, or else necessity, “when an entire people, with all its families, removes from a place, necessitated by either famine or war, and goes to seek a new seat and a new province, not to command it like those above but to possess it all individually, and expel or kill the ancient inhabitants of it.”145 Whatever the cause, war cannot be warded off by calling for peace: “I agree with the friar who said, ‘Peace, peace, there will never be peace!’” he wrote in a letter to Francesco Vettori dated 26 August 1513.146 In order to ward off war, or to ensure that it ends as soon as possible, diplomatic initiatives and good weapons are necessary. In short, it is necessary, to use the words of Fabrizio Colonna in the Art of War: “To love peace and to know how to make war.” But one element that is useful to an ability to wage war is a good religion that teaches men to be both strong and respectful of the laws of a civil way of life and of the laws of God.

In Machiavelli’s view, peace is always preferable to war, unless one can only obtain peace at the price of one’s liberty.147 The lust to conquer out of the mere desire to expand one’s power is a pernicious vice that leads, not toward greatness, but rather to the ruin of a state. The cause of the fall of so many republics and kingdoms, he writes in The Golden Ass, is that “all rulers here on earth / with wealth and power never feel replete.” The lust for conquest “ruins all states”; and even though one and all are well aware of this pitfall, “yet into it [they still] fall.” While Venice, Sparta, and Athens fell because they conquered too extensively, in Germany every free city “lives secure and safe from harm, / Barely encompassing six miles of ground.”148

States must be able to expand if they are to remain free, but conquest and rapacious expansionism lead to their ruin. In all his writings on the topic, Machiavelli condemns the policy of expansion by means of conquest and the subjugation of conquered peoples. This policy, he writes, is “useless,” and for those republics that do not have their own arms, as was the case with Florence, “Very useless.” He condemned Florentine expansionism toward Pisa, Siena, and Lucca and praised its policy of protection of Pistoia: the former policy ensured that the Pisans, Siennese, and Lucchese “have exerted and exert all their force” to resist the Florentines; the policy of protection, on the other hand, “has made the Pistoiese run willingly under their rule.” The words with which he concludes the chapter require no other comment: “And without doubt if the Florentines by way either of laws or of aids had tamed their neighbors and not made them savage, they would without doubt at this hour be lords of Tuscany. This does not mean I judge that arms and force do not have to be put to work, but they should be reserved for the last place, where and when other modes are not enough.”149 In the Florentine Histories, finally, Machiavelli praised the Florentines’s decision to regain the control of Arezzo, offering their protection to the city.150 On the other hand, the policy, so frequently pursued by the Florentines, of insistent expansion of their domains without offering the people adequate protection from external aggressors and evildoers, was reckless and the cause of countless evils.151

The example to be followed in pursuing a policy of expansion is that of the Romans who established alliances, while reserving to themselves “the rank of command, the seat of empire, and the title of the enterprises,” but extended Roman citizenship to their “partners” (“who in many things lived with it under equal laws”). Machiavelli praised the expansionism of the Romans, also because “they let those towns they did not demolish live under their own laws, even those that surrendered not as partners but as subjects. They did not leave in them any sign of the empire of the Roman people but obliged them to some conditions, which, if observed, kept them in their state and dignity.” He likewise praised the method of expansion employed by the Etruscans, based on the leagues of free cities. The Etruscans, he writes, obtained considerable “greatness,” not by means of military conquest, but rather by forming leagues in which the various republics all enjoyed equal rights and authority. Well aware that it was impossible for Florence to follow the example of the Romans, he recommended that the Florentines should follow the example of the Etruscans: “And if the imitation of the Romans seems difficult, that of the ancient Tuscans should not seem so, especially to the present Tuscans. For if they could not, for the causes said, make an empire like that of Rome, they could acquire the power in Italy that their mode of proceeding conceded them.”152

When he composed orations intended to persuade the Florentines to equip themselves with a proper army, Machiavelli did not appeal to their hunger for conquest and expansion, nor did he invoke the cliché of Florence’s right to lord it over the other cities of Tuscany.153 Instead he used all his rhetorical skills to persuade his fellow citizens that the republic needed a good army in order to protect effectively its own liberty.154 No prudent kingdom or republic, he explained, has ever placed its own independence at the discretion of other states or considered itself to be secure when it was forced to rely on the protection of other great powers: “Because every city, every state ought to consider as enemies all those who can hope to take possession of her territory and against whom she cannot defend herself. Never was princedom or republic wise that was willing to let her territory stand in the power of others or which, so letting it stand, thought she held it securely.”155 If a republic wishes to protect its own freedom, it must attain sufficient territorial size to allow it to defend itself through adequate alliances and federations, while protecting and treating justly the cities and peoples under its dominion and making war only as a last resort. This is what political wisdom advises; this is what God commands; He will not help those peoples in which “all zeal in you forever dies”;156 and this is how every citizen who loves liberty should consider these matters.

Whom does Machiavelli consider good? Certainly not docile men, but rather the citizens who, like the ancient Romans, want “neither to serve humbly nor dominate proudly.” That people, “which never served humbly nor dominated proudly while the republic lasted uncorrupt; indeed, with its orders and magistrates, it held its rank honorably. When it was necessary to move against someone powerful, it did so, as may be seen in Manlius, in the Ten, and in others who sought to crush it; and when it was necessary to obey the dictators and the consuls for the public safety, it did so.”157 The religion that Machiavelli admires brings “shame to the wicked” but is also effectively able to “animate the plebs,” that is to say, giving the people the courage to withstand the powerful who wish to crush without however inciting them to become oppressors in their turn. Machiavelli on the other hand scorns the corrupt Christian religion which puts the highest value on “humility” and “abjectness” and which, if it “asks that you have strength in yourself, it wishes you to be capable more of suffering than of doing something strong.”158

The goodness that religion taught to the Romans ensured that social conflicts would strengthen the liberty and the power of that republic, while the wickedness that the corrupt religion had instilled in the souls of the Florentines helped to make Florence servile and humble. In ancient Rome, Machiavelli points out, the conflicts between the plebs and the senate never, or very rarely, violated the boundaries of civil life and they were resolved “by disputing”; in Florence they were resolved “by fighting.”159 Machiavelli condemned those social conflicts that degenerate into armed combat and culminate in the imposition of the interests of one group or faction to the damage of some other group or faction, as was the case in Florence. He praised only those that culminated with laws that incorporated the demands of the various groups, and therefore served to promote the common good, as was the case in republican Rome. Social conflicts therefore serve to uphold the common freedom if they do not violate the fundamental requirements of a civil way of life, and this is possible if the people, through a good religion, is neither docile nor proud.

Machiavelli harshly criticized the arrogance of the nobility; but he also denounced with equal emphasis and firmness the ambitions of the commoners. Not content with having reinforced its position vis-à-vis the nobles, Machiavelli observed, the Roman people “began at once to engage in combat through ambition, and to wish to share honors and belongings with the nobility.”160 The excessive ambition of the commonfolk led to conflicts over the agrarian law and to the ultimate dissolution of the republic.161 The ambitions of the nobles would have caused the ruin of the republic much sooner, if the people hadn’t successfully kept those ambitions in check for three hundred years. In many cases, however, it was also necessary to restrain the demands of the tribunes of the people, as they had become dangerous to the common good and to the safety of the fatherland.162

An even more eloquent example is that of Florence, where the people wished to exclude the nobility entirely, so as “to be alone in the government.” While the desire of the Roman people to share access to public office with the nobles was reasonable, the desire of the people of Florence was “injurious and unjust.”163 With their exaggerated demands, the commoners of Florence obliged the nobility to resist with all its power, and as a result the social conflicts degenerated into armed combat and civil wars. As long as the commoners shared the highest public offices with the nobles, they acquired those virtues that were peculiar to the nobility; but once the nobility was excluded from the government, Florence became increasingly humble and abject.164

Was the disrespect for justice—equally distributed between the nobility and the people—also a consequence of the Florentines’ irreligiosity? Machiavelli does not explicitly draw this conclusion. However, he has openly told us that whereas the Romans were religious, the Italians of the early modern times were not. If we put together the two pieces of Machiavelli’s analysis, we can rather safely conclude that the destructive effects of social conflicts in Florence were the consequence at least in part of the widespread irreligiosity of the city.

Precisely because he was a republican, Machiavelli did not advocate any form of social conflict that attempted to impose a partisan interest of any kind against the rule of law and the common good. In this connection, it would be appropriate to comment on a text that at first glance appears to bring support to the thesis of Machiavelli’s populism.165 I am referring to his account of the revolt of the Ciompi in the Florentine Histories. In this book, Machiavelli emphasizes that the cause of that uprising was the unjust manner in which the lowest classes, with no guild of their own, were treated. From injustice, sprang “the hatred that the lesser people had for the rich citizens and princes of the guilds, since it did not appear to them that they had been satisfied for their labor as they believed they justly deserved.”166

After this general consideration, Machiavelli presents a speech by an anonymous leader of the uprising, and has him deliver a denunciation of social inequality that culminates with an exhortation to increase the violence in order to crush the Signoria and the magistrates and become “princes of all the city.”167 This text is a radical critique of populism in the name of the republican principle of the common good. The oration delivered by the anonymous plebeian, Machiavelli writes, “strongly inflamed spirits that were already hot for evil on their own.” Why hot for “evil”? Because the revolt of the commoners was not directed toward the common good, but rather the victory of a faction; the goal was not a general emancipation from dominion, but rather the substitution of one form of dominion with another: “Now is the time not only to free ourselves from them but to become so much their superiors that they will have more to lament and fear from you than you from them.” The plebeian regime that culminated in the gonfaloniership of Michele di Lando was an unjust government, just as the oligarchic regime that followed it was unjust: “This state was neither less injurious toward its citizens nor less oppressive in its beginnings that that of the plebs had been.”168 The plebs that Machiavelli condemned because it imposed a government that was contrary to the common good—and this is an important aspect to keep in mind—were plebs that had abandoned the conscience that would have made them repent of the acts of violence they had committed and “abstain” from committing other, graver acts of violence.

The problem of Florence is that its social conflicts do not lead to the establishment of a good republican regime, but rather to an alternation of servitude and license: “Cities, and especially those not well ordered that are administered under the name of republic, frequently change their governments and their states not between liberty and servitude, as many believe, but between servitude and license. For only the name of freedom is extolled by the ministers of license, who are the men of the people, and by the ministers of servitude, who are the nobles, neither of them desiring to be subject either to the laws or to men.”169 This is a vicious circle that can only be halted by a wise and powerful man capable of restraining the humors of both the plebs and the great: “A wise, good, and powerful citizen by whom laws are ordered by which these humors of the nobles and the men of the people are quieted or restrained so that they cannot do evil, then that city can be called free and that state be judged stable and firm.”170

Licentious commoners lead the state toward a principality. The mutiny of the Ciompi only reinforced the power of Salvestro de’ Medici, and Salvestro de’ Medici led to Cosimo de’ Medici, the founder of a government that was a principality under the guise of a republic, just as the disorders that broke out in Rome as a result of the agrarian law led to the rule of Caesar, who “was the first tyrant in Rome.”171 Stern in his condemnation of the social conflicts that aim to impose the dominion of one party over another, Machiavelli becomes very stern when discussing conflicts among sects, or factions. Government by sects is the opposite of free government, as Machiavelli has a good citizen say, speaking out of love of the fatherland.172 While the conflict between humors may have positive results for a republic, the conflict among sects is always harmful because, by its very nature, it is contrary to the common good: “It is true that some divisions are harmful to republics and some are helpful. Those are harmful that are accompanied by sects and partisans; those are helpful that are maintained without sects and partisans. Thus, since a founder of a republic cannot provide that there be no enmities in it, he has to provide at least that there not be sect.173

How then to prevent the formation of sects and partisans, and the destructive conflict that they engender? The chief causes of sects are fear and corruption. Machiavelli speaks of fear especially in the chapter of Discourses on Livy entitled “How Far Accusations May Be Necessary in a Republic to Maintain It in Freedom.” After praising the fact that the tribunes of the plebs had spared Coriolanus from lynching by summoning him to defend himself in the tribunal, Machiavelli observed: “Everyone may consider how much ill would have resulted to the Roman republic if he had been killed in a tumult; for from that arises offense by private individuals against private individuals, which offense generates fear; fear seeks for defense; for defense they procure partisans; from partisans arise the parties in cities; from parties their ruin.”174

He also deals with corruption as a cause for sects and partisans in the Florentine Histories when he distinguishes between the “public ways” and the “private modes” of winning reputation in a city:

And therefore it is to be known that citizens in cities acquire reputation in two modes: either by public ways or by private modes. One acquires it publicly by winning a battle, acquiring a town, carrying out a mission with care and prudence, advising the republic wisely and prosperously. One acquires it in private modes by benefiting this or that other citizen, defending him from the magistrates, helping him with money, getting him unmerited honors, and ingratiating oneself with the plebs with games and public gifts. From this latter mode of proceeding, sects and partisans arise, and the reputation thus earned offends as much as reputation helps when it is not mixed with sects, because that reputation is founded on a common good, not on a private good.175


The remedy for private offenses, and against the fears that they engender, is the unyielding defense of the rule of law. The remedy against the “private modes” of winning fame is either to prevent the existence within the city of men so wealthy that they could hire many supporters or else to ensure that the citizens are so virtuous that they would be unwilling to serve powerful men. Both the former remedy and the latter one are exceedingly difficult to put into practice. And the obvious consequence is that republics often die, either from weakness, because they are incapable of imposing the rule of law and protecting citizens from private harm, or else from corruption, because they reward those men who pursue their own self-interest.

There is of course the alternative of a principality which imposes civil peace. A principality however is nothing other than a sect that has become far more powerful than the others. Caesar, who became the leader of what had been the party of Marius, “remained superior” and became the “first tyrant of the city”; Cosimo de’ Medici “surpassed every other man of his times not only in authority and riches” and became the de facto lord of Florence. But neither Caesar nor Cosimo were heroes to Machiavelli. Instead, he recommended establishing good political orders capable of restoring the common good and the rule of law. But he also knew that, in order to uproot completely the evil of the sects and to prevent social conflicts from destroying liberty, it is necessary that religion and goodness be rekindled in the soul of the people. The “common corruption” of all the cities in Italy flourishes and spreads because, aside from the political factors, “religion and fear of God have been eliminated in all, an oath and faith given last only as long as they are useful.” Only the rebirth of a civil religion could truly free the republics from the evil that is undermining them and could help them to return to a truly civil way of life.176


5. The Reform of Orders and the Reform of Customs


The path that Machiavelli was pointing out to his contemporaries, and to posterity, for the rebirth of republican liberty, was political reform. To conquer the disease of corruption, it is necessary to annul “the orders that nourish sects,” and to put in their place those “that do in truth conform to a free and civil life.” He reiterates that concept as well in the chapter of Discourses on Livy in which he explores the possibility of maintaining or ordering a “free state” in corrupt cities. In order to restrain corruption in Rome, he explained, it was necessary to cancel the laws that the powerful had imposed “not for the common freedom but for their own power,” and “if Rome wished to maintain itself free in corruption, therefore, it was necessary that it should have made new orders, as in the course of its life it had made new laws. For one should order different orders and modes of life in a bad subject and in a good one; nor can there be a similar form in a matter altogether contrary.”177

New political orders and new laws that restore a civil way of life can be established with peaceful and legal means or else with violence and absolute power. In sharp contrast to a lengthy interpretative tradition, Machiavelli’s advice is to attempt to follow the former approach rather than the latter. In order to restore a civil way of life in a corrupt city with extraordinary means, a man of exceptional virtue is required, capable of winning absolute power that will allow him to dispose of the city “in [his] own mode.” To reorder a city to a political way of life, then, demands a “good man,” but to win absolute power in a corrupt city requires “a bad man.” “It very rarely happens,” Machiavelli warns, “that someone good wishes to become prince by bad ways, even though his end be good, and that someone wicked, having become prince, wishes to work well, and that it will ever occur to his mind to use well the authority that he has acquired badly.” Reform, on the other hand, could be implemented peacefully by a wise and powerful citizen who can see the shortcomings of the existing institutions and who manages to persuade his fellow citizens of the need to change them. Such an undertaking however is very difficult because men who are “used to living in one mode do not wish to vary it.”178

Precisely because it is difficult, the restoration of the rule of law is an achievement that brings glory more than any other. The two eulogies to potential redeemers that Machiavelli composed for the conclusions of, respectively, The Prince and the Discursus florentinarum rerum are both eloquent on that subject. War and arms, he writes in The Prince, quoting Livy, are just when they are necessary, and become sacred when there is no hope other than them. He adds, and this is an important point, that “nothing brings so much honor to a man rising newly as the new laws and the new orders founded by him. When these things have been founded well and have greatness in them, they make him revered and admirable”: revered and admirable because his achievement is similar to that of God.179

In the peroration that concludes the Discursus, Machiavelli expresses the same concept with words of even greater eloquence:

No man is so much exalted by any act of his as are those men who have with laws and with institutions remodeled republics and kingdoms; these are, after those who have been gods, the first to be praised. And because there have been few who have had opportunity to do it, and very few those who have understood how to do it, small is the number who have done it. And so much has this glory been esteemed by men seeking for nothing other than glory that when unable to form a republic in reality, they have done it in writing, as Aristotle, Plato, and many others, who have wished to show the world that if they have not founded a free government, as did Solon and Lycurgus, they have failed not through their ignorance but through their impotence for putting it into practice.180


Since he considers it to be the necessary foundation of a civil way of life, and the highest expression of man’s divine nature, Machiavelli exhorts his readers to respect the order of law with the greatest firmness and determination. Once they have been enacted, laws must be enforced without tolerance for privileges or discriminations, and crimes must be punished without any regard for the public or private merits of the guilty party. “No well-ordered republic,” he writes, “ever cancels the demerits with the merits of its citizens; but, having ordered rewards for a good work and punishments for a bad one, and having rewarded one for having worked well, if that same one later works badly, it punishes him without any regard for his good works.” If this principle of civil justice is violated, he concludes, and these are significant words, “any civility will be dissolved.”181

Even if the guilty party is the most evil of men and has committed the worst crimes against the republic, his civil rights established by law must be respected. Machiavelli explains this fundamental aspect of his theory of the republic when he is commenting on the death of Appius Claudius, the chief of the decemviri who imposed a tyranny in Rome, who was denied the right to appeal to the people (“appellatio ad populum”). Given the seriousness of his crimes, Appius deserved the harshest of penalties; all the same, Machiavelli writes, and the words that he uses are important in this case as well, “it was hardly a civil thing to violate the laws, and so much the more one that had been made then.”182

Those who govern must take care to ensure that punishments are always inflicted within a respect for the law by the public authorities and never by private citizens, if they wish to prevent the corruption of the republic. The tribunes of the people, we read in Discourses on Livy, saved Coriolanus from the fury of the mob and hauled him to judgment for having used illicit means to diminish the power of the plebs. If the mob had lynched Coriolanus, Machiavelli comments, his death would have been an “offense by private individuals against private individuals,” which would have undermined the faith of the citizens in the efficacy of the law. They would then have banded together into factions to protect themselves, to the grave detriment of the republic.183 But because the matter was resolved by the public authorities in full respect of legal process, it did no damage to the republic. “For if a citizen is crushed ordinarily, there follows little or no disorder in the republic,” Machiavelli explains, “even though he has been done a wrong. For the execution is done without private forces and without foreign forces, which are the ones that ruin a free way of life; but it is done with public forces and orders, which have their particular limits and do not lead beyond to something that may ruin the republic.”184

In order to protect the city from corruption, magistrates must be inflexible in their defense of the law, especially when the law is in danger from powerful and eminent citizens. Death sentences carried out against men such as Brutus, the decemviri, Maelius, Manlius Capitolinus, the lawsuits against Papirius Cursor and against Fabius, and the charges leveled against the Scipios, Machiavelli comments, were so noteworthy and came in such close succession one after another, that they kept alive in the populace the fear of consequences and induced the people to respect the laws. When such memorable executions became less frequent, men began to break the laws and “corrupt themselves.” It is therefore useful, Machiavelli points out, to remember and to emulate the severity of Junius Brutus, the father of Roman liberty, who not only sat on the jury that condemned his own sons to death for having conspired against the republic, but even witnessed their execution.185 The republic must, in sum, entrust the magistracies to citizens who love only the “common good” and who have no respect for “private ambition.”186

Machiavelli expressed his belief that respect for the law is the basis for civil order as well when he judged the important episodes that took place in the Florence of his own time, where the laws were often broken by means of statues or provisions that served to protect the power and the interests of private groups, or through discrimination and special privileges. In order to justify these waivers of the laws, the rulers, aided by jurists, almost inevitably made recourse to arguments of extraordinary conditions or overriding necessity. In 1501, for instance, the notary Domenico Bonsi, a moderate Savonarolian, advised the Signoria not to be too exacting in their observance of the laws, and emphasized that “to want certitude in everything is impossible, that affairs must be conducted according to accident and circumstance, and that one should not will the ruin of the city by [always] insisting on the observance of the laws.”187

A significant example of the attitudes among the Florentine political elite on the matter of legality and respect for the law was the trial of Paolo Vitelli, the condottiere hired by the Florentines in the war against Pisa. Accused of treason, Vitelli was tried and sentenced to death in October 1499. Niccolò Altoviti, a doctor of civil law and canon law, summed up the views of the jury when he stated that there was no way to spare the life of Paolo Vitelli because he had not denied being in contact with the Pisans; because he could, through his rank and his authority, greatly damage the Florentine Republic; and most important because “this is not the way things are usually done in the affairs of states.”188

The most important debate over issues of legality that was conducted in Florence during Machiavelli’s time, in any case, was the one concerning the trial and subsequent death sentence issued against five eminent citizens—Bernardo del Nero, Niccolò Ridolfi, Lorenzo Tornabuoni, Giannozzo Pucci, and Giovanni Cambi. All five were charged in August 1497 with conspiring to restore the rule of the Medici. According to a law inspired by Savonarola, and approved in March 1495, any citizen who had been sentenced to death, to exile, or to the payment of a fine greater than three hundred large florins, had the right to appeal their case to the Signoria, and the Signoria had the duty of presenting the appeal before the Great Council. The jurists who advocated the decision not to carry out the appeal all invoked a state of necessity.189

We have no documents allowing us to establish clearly Machiavelli’s position in the Vitelli case. In a letter to a chancellor of Lucca dated in early October 1500, he openly supports the republic’s decision and writes that Vitelli’s actions deserve “infinite chastisement.”190 A few years later, in 1504, he commented on Vitelli’s death with these verses: “Soon after, for the trick he played, you [Florence] took revenge in full / inflicting death on him who had caused you such great harm.” These are ambiguous words. Machiavelli speaks of vengeance for a trick, not of justice. He knew full well, as all the Florentines who read his verses knew, that revenge is one thing, and justice is quite another.191 He says no more than that, however. As secretary he was sworn to silence, and he respected that duty scrupulously.

On the other hand, we do know his opinion on the condemnation of the five Florentine citizens sentenced to death in 1497. In contrast with the decision of the high authorities of the Florentine Republic, and differing with the opinions of eminent jurists, he viewed the matter much as he had judged the behavior of the Roman republic in the case of Appius, that is to say, as setting a bad example. Machiavelli writes, “I do not believe there is a thing that sets a more wicked example.”192 In both the case of Appius and in the case of the five Florentine citizens, Machiavelli took a position in favor of respect for the law, even though the death sentences were issued against enemies of republican liberty and on behalf of the interest of the state (“per conto di stato,” or “on the state’s account”). Jurists developed sophisticated and ornate arguments to justify the violation of the law on behalf of the security of the state; Machiavelli, the supposed advocate of the priority of politics over the law, instead took a position in defense of legality.

If constitutional orders are not provided to make it possible for republics successfully to face emergencies, when rapid decisions and exceptional measures become necessary, then those republics will be short lived. Republics must necessarily respect their constitution and perish; or, in order to survive, to violate their constitutions. Machiavelli does not at all recommend relying on a redeemer capable of rescuing the republic with his exceptional virtue, and with violence and cruelty. Instead, he exhorts republics to establish constitutional procedures that allow them to deal effectively with states of emergency: “In a republic, one would not wish anything ever to happen that has to be governed with extraordinary modes. For although the extraordinary mode may do good then, nonetheless the example does ill; for if one sets up a habit of breaking the orders for the sake of good, then later, under that coloring, they are broken for ill.”193 But unless a republic “has provided for everything with its laws,” its rulers must be willing to take on themselves the responsibility for violating the laws and using extraordinary methods. The behavior of the gonfalonier of the Florentine Republic under whom Machiavelli served from 1498 to 1512, Pier Soderini, who refused to employ extraordinary means and to break the laws in order to thwart the plots of the partisans of the Medici, was certainly, wrote Machiavelli “wise and good”; but the consequences of his decision were disastrous, both for him and for the republic: “So much so that, through not knowing how to be like Brutus, he lost not only his fatherland but his state and his reputation.”194

Matters stand differently in a case where laws and orders are good, but the republic lacks the power to impose a respect for them. Where customs are corrupt, Machiavelli explains, “well-ordered laws do not help unless indeed they have been put in motion by one individual who with an extreme force ensures their observance.”195 In order to preserve republican orderings in a corrupt city, he subsequently makes clear, it would be necessary to turn the republic “more toward a kingly state than toward a popular state, so that the men who cannot be corrected by the laws because of their insolence should be checked in some mode by an almost kingly power.”196

These exhortations to draw back the republic more toward the royal state than toward the popular state and to restrain insolence with an “almost kingly power” should not be taken as an abandonment of the republican principles in favor of the principality or the monarchy. They are, instead, a recommendation to give temporarily greater weight to the monarchic element of a mixed government in preference to the popular component.197 As I have previously emphasized, Machiavelli’s republicanism is a theory of a civil way of life that is to be achieved by means of a mixed government that also includes an element that is almost monarchic, as Machiavelli is always careful to point out, in the person of a gonfalonier for life or a doge. The supreme magistrate of the republic has the option, in order to combat corruption, to make use of extraordinary means: in accordance with the statutes, if the statutes allow him to do so; outside of the statutes, however, if necessary.

The most radical, but also the most effective, remedy that Machiavelli recommends as a way of combating corruption is to renew a republic, by drawing it back toward its beginnings. He resorts, that is, to the religious idea of reformation and resurrection through the rediscovery of founding principles:

It is a very true thing that all worldly things have a limit to their life; but generally those go the whole course that is ordered for them by heaven, that do not disorder their body but keep it ordered so that either it does not alter or, if it alters, it is for its safety and not to its harm. Because I am speaking of mixed bodies, such as republics and sects, I say that those alterations are for safety that lead them back toward their beginnings. So those are better ordered and have longer life that by means of their orders can often be renewed or indeed that through some accident outside the said order come to the said renewal. And it is a thing clearer than light that these bodies do not last if they do not renew themselves. The mode of renewing them is, as was said, to lead them back toward their beginnings. For all the beginnings of sects, republics, and kingdoms must have some goodness in them, by means of which they may regain their first reputation and their first increase. Because in the process of time that goodness is corrupted, unless something intervenes to lead it back to the mark, it of necessity kills that body.198


The healthy and necessary renewal of republics can take place through the virtue of a man or through the virtue of good ordering that, all the same, “have need of being brought to life by the virtue of a citizen who rushes spiritedly to execute them against the power of those who transgress them.” This is the “simple virtue” of a man who, “without depending on any law,” does not attempt to replace the rule of law nor eliminate the republican orders. We can grasp the historic significance of Machiavelli’s republican theory only if we keep in mind the fact that the virtue of men and the rule of law integrate one another.
