The virtue of the “good man” is necessary when the rule of law no longer exists or has been undermined by corruption. Once it has been restored, the rule of law still needs virtue and goodness, this time, the virtue and goodness of the many.

The way to “regain new life and new virtue” that Machiavelli proposed for republics was to return to the “observance of religion and justice.” In Rome, he explained on the basis of the text of Livy, religion and justice “were beginning to be tainted”: religious ceremonies were no longer observed; those responsible for grave crimes were no longer punished, such as the three Fabii who had violated the “ius gentium” (‘the law of nations’) while fighting against the Gauls, but were actually rewarded with tribune-ships. The Romans knew how to react against corruption and conquer it, because they restored to life all the good political orders that had been established by Romulus and “by the other prudent princes,” and they understood that it was necessary not only “to maintain religion and justice but also to esteem its good citizens” and “to take more account of their virtue” than of private and personal interests. They were capable of being reborn because they examined their way of life and were able to return to the principles from which they had grown estranged.

The method of rebirth that Machiavelli recommends is a reform that is both political, because it restores good orders that have deteriorated through corruption, and moral, because it changes the way that men judge matters. It is, however, a primarily religious reform, not only because it revives worship, but because it allows the people to rediscover that “reverence” that “all states have… in the beginning,” and the “goodness” that gives states “their first reputation and their first increase.” The rebirth of republics has the same features and physiognomy as the rebirth of religions. Both arise through a reform that brings back to life in the minds of men the original principles.199

Machiavelli understands that the revival of a free way of life requires both political reform and moral and religious reform. One cannot succeed without the others. A political reform without a religious reform would engender good orders and good laws but would be unable to model customs, and would necessarily have a short and troubled existence. A religious reform without a political reform would fail to enforce the moral principles diffused by preaching and example. In the first case, a redeemer would not be a prophet; in the second case, he would be an unarmed prophet. Without a moral and religious reform, the only alternative remains the principality, in which it is the fear of the prince “which supplies the defects of religion.”

Precisely because he is unwilling to resign himself either to the principality or to the corrupt republic, Machiavelli points the way to a religious reform that is capable of rekindling in the minds of the Italians a religion capable of teaching them to love liberty and to punish those that have done wrong, not a religion that, like that of Saint Francis and Saint Dominic, preaches that “it is evil to say evil of evil,” that it is good to live in obedience to dishonest prelates, and that if they do wrong it is best “to leave them for God to punish,” and as a result they “do the worst they can because they do not fear the punishment that they do not see and do not believe.”200

Like the theorists of the religious reformatio of the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries, Machiavelli means by beginnings not the origins in a chronological sense, but the right form, or “segno,” (literally, ‘mark’): “In the process of time that goodness is corrupted, unless something intervenes to lead it back to the mark.” When Sofronia in the Clizia exhorts her husband Nicomaco to “ritornare al segno”—“come back to your mark”—she does not only mean that he should go back to being “that Nicomaco you were a year ago,” but that he must go back to being a husband, a father, and a citizen as he ought.201 In a passage that is difficult to interpret, Machiavelli seems to be saying that in the teachings of Christ what was good in the religion of the Romans had been preserved, and that this good was later lost in the beginnings or the principles of the Christian “res publica,” and most especially, in the church of Rome.202 In another passage, which poses no difficulties of interpretation, he maintains that it is possible to replace the “false” interpretations of “our religion according to idleness” with another interpretation, this one true, in accordance with virtue: “For if they considered how [the Christian religion] permits us the exaltation and defense of the fatherland, they would see that [the Christian religion] wishes us to love and honor it and to prepare ourselves to be such that we can defend it.” While the false interpretations of the Christian religion and the education based on them have made the world weak and therefore ensured that in the modern world “not as many republics are seen in the world as were seen in antiquity; nor, as a consequence, is as much love of freedom seen in peoples as was then,” a renewed religion of virtue would make the world once again strong, good, and capable of rediscovering the love of liberty.203

To reform the Christian religion means, as I emphasized in chapter 1, drawing it back to its true principles, principles ensuring that it lives in the souls of men in the most just and perfect manner, that is to say, in accordance with virtue, charity, and justice. Machiavelli’s reform did not refer to a specific time in the past and it had no specific theological content. Rather it indicates a moral content and a way of life. In this way, but in this way alone, it is similar to the reforms of Saint Francis and Saint Dominic, who renewed and gave new life to the Christian religion, not because they proclaimed a new theology but because they lived in poverty and they followed “the example of the life of Christ.” Machiavelli wished to see the rebirth of a religion that would teach its followers to love one’s fatherland more than one’s own soul, to serve the common good with a spirit of charity, to defend with all one’s might the free way of life, and such a spirit had indeed been alive in the religious sentiment of Florentine and Italian humanism. In part through his writings, that religion continued to live in Italian history as the aspiration of those who wished to enjoy a life of freedom.

Machiavelli’s authority on the religion of the Romans, aside from Livy, was Polybius: “But among all the useful institutions, that demonstrate the superior excellence of the Roman government, the most considerable perhaps is the opinion which the people are taught to hold concerning the gods: and that, which other men regard as an object of disgrace, appears in my judgment to be the very thing by which this republic chiefly is sustained. I mean, superstition: which is impressed with all its terrors; and influences both the private actions of the citizens, and the public administration also of the state, in a degree that can scarcely be exceeded. This may appear astonishing to many. To me it is evident, that this contrivance was at first adopted for the sake of the multitude. For if it were possible that a state could be composed of wise men only, there would be no need, perhaps, of any such invention. But as the people universally are fickle and inconstant, filled with irregular desires, too precipitate in their passions, and prone to violence; there is no way left to restrain them, but by the dread of things unseen, and by the pageantry of terrifying fiction. The ancients, therefore, acted not absurdedly, nor without good reason, when they inculcated the notions concerning the gods, and the belief of infernal punishments; but much more those of the present age are to be charged with rashness and absurdity, in endeavoring to extirpate these opinions. For, not to mention effects that flow from such an institution, if, among the Greeks, for example, a single talent only be entrusted to those who have the management of any of the public money; though they give ten written sureties, with as many seals and twice as many witnesses, they are unable to discharge the trusts reposed in them with integrity. But the Romans, on the other hand, who in the course of their magistracies, and in embassies, disperse the greatest sums, are prevailed on by the single obligation of an oath to perform their duties with inviolable honesty. And as, in other states, a man is rarely found whose hands are pure from public robbery; so, among the Romans, it is no less rare to discover one that is tainted with this crime”; Polybius, Storie [Histories], VI, 56, edited by Domenico Musti, Rizzoli, Milan 2002, vol. III, pp. 387–91.

English translation: Tusiani, Lust and Liberty, pp. 81–81. Evidence of how deeply rooted was the religion of idleness that Machiavelli combated so strenuously can be found in the letter Vettori wrote Machiavelli on 5 August 1526:” We are having very poor harvests here [in Rome], and we hear that they are worse elsewhere. So we judge that the year is going to be very bad, because of war, plague, and famine, and since in times of tribulation people turn toward God, having also heard the saints have conquered as a result of making prayers and processions, we have tried to obtain a Jubilee for the middle of August from Our Lord, to be taken without money, and fasts, confessions, and prayers will suffice to take it”; in Opere di Niccolò Machiavelli, vol. III, Lettere, edited by Franco Gaeta, Utet, Turin 1984, p. 602. English translation: Atkinson and Sices, Machiavelli and His Friends, pp. 395–96.

123 Machiavelli, Discorsi, I, 27. English translation: Mansfield and Tarcov, Discourses, p. 64. Guicciardini is even harsher in his condemnation of the temporal power and the corruption of the church than Machiavelli: “On these foundations and by these means, raised to secular power, little by little forgetting about the salvation of souls and divine precepts, and turning all their thoughts to worldly greatness, and no longer using their spiritual authority except as an instrument and minister of temporal power, they began to appear rather more like secular princes than popes. Their concern and endeavors began to be no longer the sanctity of life or the propagation of religion, no longer zeal and charity toward their neighbors, but armies and wars against Christians, managing their sacrifices with bloody hands and thoughts; they began to accumulate treasures, to make new laws, to invent new tricks, new cunning devices in order to gather money from every side; for this purpose, to use their spiritual arms without respect; for this end, to shamelessly sell sacred and profane things. The great wealth spreading amongst them and throughout their court was followed by pomp, luxury, dishonest customs, lust and abominable pleasures: no concern about their successors, no thought of the perpetual majesty of the pontificate, but instead, an ambitious and pestiferous desire to exalt their children, nephews and kindred, not only to immoderate riches but to principalities, to kingdoms; no longer distributing dignities and emoluments among deserving and virtuous men, but almost always either selling them for the highest price or wasting them on persons opportunistically moved by ambition, avarice, or shameful love of pleasure. And for all these misdeeds, reverence for the papacy has been utterly lost in the hearts of men, and yet their authority is somewhat sustained by the name and majesty, so powerful and effective, of religion; and mightily by the means they have of gratifying great princes and those powerful personages around them, by conferring dignities and other ecclesiastical concessions. Whence knowing themselves to be held in the greatest respect by men, and that whoever takes arms against them gains grave infamy thereby and often opposition from other princes, and in any event profits very little, and that when victors they employ the victory as they will, and when vanquished they obtain whatever conditions they will, and pricked on by the greed to raise their families from private conditions to principalities, the popes have been for a long time, and very often, the instruments of stirring up wars and new conflagrations in Italy”; Francesco Guicciardini, Storia d’Italia [The History of Italy], IV, 12, in Opere di Francesco Guicciardini, edited by Emanuella Lugnani Scarano, Utet, Turin 1981, vol. CHAPTER FOUR


MACHIAVELLI AND THE RELIGIOUS AND MORAL REFORMATION OF ITALY


Francesco De Sanctis writes in the History of Italian Literature that during the years in which Martin Luther gave the world a new theology, religious reform was no longer possible in Italy. A country that had too much culture, accustomed to laughing at the corruption that had aroused the indignation of the entire German world, Italy had already progressed beyond “the age of theology,” believed only in science, and considered both Luther and Calvin as “new latter-day scholastics.” For that reason, De Sanctis concludes, “the Reformation could not flourish in Italy; it never affected Latin culture, which was developing on its own lines. Already emancipated from theology, and holding not only Christianity but all religions and the whole of culture in a single embrace, the Italy of Pico and Pomponazzo, seated on the ruins of the Middle Ages, could not possibly look to theology for the basis of its new edifice, but only to science. The Luther of Italy was Niccolò Machiavelli.”1

Federico Chabod (1901–60) developed this intuition by De Sanctis and proposed a parallel between the need for religious reform and the need for political reform. Chabod writes:

[During the crisis of the sixteenth century] political protest and religious protest came into the world at a single birth pang: exceedingly different, indeed, antithetical as to objective and in terms of means and tone, and therefore not nourished on the same spirit of reaction against the immediate past, but germinating in the same climate mate of general anguish, doubt, and unease; destined, both one and the other, to failure, at least in practical and immediate terms, and to be followed, rather than by the redemption of Italy at the hands of a prince of great virtue, by the definitive establishment of Spanish dominion, for the first form of protest and, rather than by the defeat of the church and of the “human” spirit of the Curia, by the victorious Counter Reformation, for the second form of protest; they did share, then, from the very outset, a mindset of rebellion and a culmination in failure. The “return to principles” took place in neither of the two fields: Machiavelli was succeeded by Ammirato and Botero, well content that so much of Italy had come under the domination of His Catholic Majesty; while Valdés and Ochino and the other reformers were succeeded by the diocesan synods of the Counter Reformation.2


Delio Cantimori (1904–66) plumbed the matter even deeper, identifying, rather than a straightforward parallel, an interpenetration or an intersection between Machiavelli and the variegated universe of Italian reformers:

The themes set forth by Machiavelli became essential elements of the cultural life of the entire sixteenth century and beyond, right up to the last century, when Francesco De Sanctis, in a transport of historiographic lyricism, went to so far as to declare that Niccolò Machiavelli was the Martin Luther of Italy. This oratorical impetus on De Sanctis’s part may appear, even to those with ears attuned to the voices of the men of the centuries past, a little excessive, rhetorical, provincial, and antiquated. But if we base ourselves purely on the facts, there was a certain continuity, though it may have been a literary continuity…: and a historian must record it. Suffice it to recall symbolically, that Mazzini’s favorite nom de guerre as a conspirator was none other than that of Filippo Strozzi: the hero, it is true, of a tragedy by Niccolini, but also a friend of Machiavelli’s. With these names of De Sanctis and Mazzini, a cycle comes to an end. The fact remains that this cycle existed, and that it began, as far as we are concerned as historians, with the themes put forth in the works of Machiavelli.


For that matter, notes Cantimori, “it was Italians that were in exile for religious issues who translated and printed, or had printed in Latin, The Prince”3

The meditations of De Sanctis, Chabod, and Cantimori all point out Machiavelli’s role in the long and fascinating history of the ideas of the religious and moral reform of Italy. In some cases, Machiavelli’s presence within this history is marginal and springs from the tactical needs of anti-Catholic polemics or from an interest in specific aspects of his work.4 Nonetheless, the attention paid by Italian reformers, heretics, and dissidents to Machiavelli’s thoughts on the subject of religion is too extensive and continued for too long a time to be considered nothing more than a marginal convergence of ideas and intentions that derived from distant motivations and objectives. The ideal common ground was the objective of the reformatio peculiar to humanism, that is, the aspiration to achieve religious reform in the sense of moral reform, rather than an overall plan for the reformation of ecclesiastical institutions or even an ambition to achieve dogmatic revision. The thinkers who perceived these demands for reform from the sixteenth century onward saw Machiavelli as a fellow traveler and a guide.

Machiavelli, as I have stressed, had neither proposed a new system of dogma nor outlined the structure of a new church. He had however pointed out the need to reform the Christian religion by drawing it back to its original principles and had likewise urged a reinterpretation of the moral content of the Christian religion in accordance with virtue. He had, in short, called for a religion capable of infusing a sense of duty, strength of soul, and a love of the fatherland and the common good. His thoughts on the subject of religion were indivisible from his political teachings, intended to instill or revive a love of republican liberty and an aspiration to create a national state. He presented religious rebirth and political rebirth as two processes with the same form, which each required the presence of the other. For that reason, his works influenced those who felt the need for religious reform as well as those who felt the need for political reform, and especially those who understood that in Italy there would never be true political liberty and a true civil way of life without a religious reform capable of engendering and rooting a new moral way of life.

Machiavelli’s presence in the history of moral and religious reform allows us to understand the problem that Italy had been dragging forward with it through the centuries: the lack of a profound and powerful moral awareness that prevented, and still prevents Italians, from being a truly free people. The ideal process that traces its roots back to civic humanism, and which had one of its most dogged supporters in the person of Machiavelli, does not come to an end with Mazzini and De Sanctis, as Cantimori believed. It continues right up to the present day and can be seen in the way of thinking and feeling of those who are unwilling to resign themselves to the idea of living in a country that laughs at corruption and has no sense of shame. Despite the few, or the many, who are unwilling to resign themselves, the moral reform of Italy will probably remain an aspiration destined never to be achieved, not even in part. But the history of that aspiration, and the role that Machiavelli played in it, still deserves to be recounted, if for no other reason than to keep it from being forgotten.


1. Heresy and Aspirations for Religious Reform


Machiavelli developed a reputation as an atheist at an early date. One of the first to call him an atheist was Paolo Giovio in his Elogia, written between 1545 and 1546.5 During his lifetime, he was also widely considered to be a heretic, at least to judge from a letter that Giovanbattista Busini wrote to Benedetto Varchi in 1549: “To the piagnoni (followers of Savonarola) he appeared to be a heretic, the good people thought him dishonest, the wicked thought him more wicked and more valiant than them; so that they hated him one and all.”6 That the piagnoni, of all people, who had seen their leader put to death by the church and burned at the stake as a heretic, should accuse Machiavelli of heresy may sound odd. It may have been an inaccurate depiction of events, but it might also be an invaluable piece of evidence that tells us that in Florence there were those who had detected in the writings of Machiavelli heretical ideas that stood in open contrast with church doctrine, and that were still quite distinct from the ideas held by Savonarola.

Still more serious was the accusation of heresy leveled against Machiavelli by Antonio Caracciolo, author of the Life and Deeds of Gian Piero Carafa, Pope Paul IV (Vita et gesti di Giovan Pietro Carafa cioè di Paolo IV Pontefice Massimo). In Italy, wrote Caracciolo, referring to the period of the trial of Piero Carnesecchi (1566–67), “there was practically not a single major city that remained untainted by heresy. Indeed, not only those cities bordering the regions beyond the mountains, but also the other Mediterranean cities, and Rome itself were gravely blighted in this context.” And he added that “the good and wise old gentleman, Signore Pietr’Antonio Bandini, father of the current Cardinal Bandini, told me that in many cities of Italy, and especially in Florence, where Machiavelli and Carnesecchi lived, there was practically not a shred of Catholic faith remaining, which fact has been extensively confirmed to me by numerous older cardinals and prelates, and in particular the cardinals Monreale, Camerino, and Arrigone.”7

Even though the church had placed his works on its Index of Forbidden Books, Machiavelli continued to be read in heretical circles.8 Alessandro Strozzi, for instance, wrote to his brother Carlo: “We spend Carnival here in the usual fashion… and in the evenings I go from time to time to entertainments at the home of Signore Bernardo da Castiglione, where there are a great many gentlemen, and we read the occasional fine book and spend our time in a virtuous manner; and in particular we have taken the liberty of reading Machiavelli and copy his words extensively.”9

Someone who knew Machiavelli very well—specifically, Luigi Guicciardini (1478–1551)—described him as a person who “had difficulty believing the things that should be believed, let alone the things that deserve ridicule.” Luigi Guicciardini wrote those words to his brother Francesco on 30 May 1533 to explain that the Niccolò who appears in his dialogue Del libero arbitrio dell’Huomo was Niccolò Machiavelli.10 Although he was not one of the main protagonists, the Niccolò of the dialogue is a firm proponent of doubt as a point of departure in any quest for the truth: “If I did not doubt, I would have no intellect: nor would I gain any greater merit in finally believing in the truth, than those who never thought about it: Don’t you know that a man is made prudent by restraining and placating?”11 Luigi Guicciardini attributed to the Niccolò in his dialogue thoughts that the real Niccolò Machiavelli rejected, at least to judge from his works. It is all the same instructive that he should have chosen to use Machiavelli as an interlocutor in a disputation over a topic of theological and religious significance.12

In the late sixteenth century, Machiavelli was remembered as a skeptic and a scourge of priests and monks. In a letter from late 1571 or early 1572, the philologist and man of letters Vincenzo Borghini wrote to Ludovico Martelli:

I don’t believe that Machiavelli was interrogated by the Inquisition, and it may be that he was harmed as much by his name as by the fact that he freely and harshly mocked and went after certain friars, whose character he certainly assassinated in that play of his, La Mandragola [The Mandrake] as well as in his novella Belfagor, and so they were always the bitterest enemies to him, and they persecuted him to the point of death, but I think not to the point of subjecting him to the Inquisition. Although I cannot claim to know. Matters in those days were looser in how they were done and freer as to what could be said; people were not concerned with tiny minutiae, and as a result many people did not then refrain from saying certain things in which there was no real evil intent that nowadays they would take great care to avoid saying; and at the time, in Florence, as you may well have heard, people happily mocked one another and they launched at one another the accusation of the peccadiglio.13


The “peccadiglio” in question was the “peccadillo” (literally, petty sin) of being a nonbeliever, an allusion to the story about the Spaniard who, after saying confession went back to the father confessor to tell him that he had forgotten to mention a “peccadillo”: that he did not believe in God. Not to believe in God, or to say that you did not believe in God, in Counter-Reformation Italy, was a very serious fault, especially if you add to that demerit the reputation of being a harsh accuser of the Roman Catholic Church’s responsibility for Italy’s moral and political decadence. The church quickly expressed its hostility toward this aspect of Machiavelli’s criticism. The Roman publisher Blado, keenly attuned as he was to the mood of the court of Pope Clement VII, expurgated his edition of Discourses on Livy of exactly those passages in which Machiavelli writes that “because of the wicked examples of that court, this province has lost all devotion and all religion” and that “we Italians have this first obligation to the church and to the priests that we have become without religion and wicked.”14 In place of those emended passages, we read: “And because there are those who believe that the welfare of matters in Italy depends upon the church of Rome, perhaps one could say the opposite, with all the due respect, that the Roman church does not respect all those precepts that should be observed and in this way it comes to adulterate those holy Catholic institutions that have long been observed.”15

A similar fate befell the Florentine Histories at the hands of Giuliano de’ Ricci, Machiavelli’s nephew, who hoped by so doing to obtain permission from the church to publish the work which, like the others, had been placed on the Index of Forbidden Books. Giuliano de’ Ricci also judged particularly unacceptable to the papal authorities those passages in which Machiavelli accuses the church of being the cause of all of Italy’s ills and so eliminates the lines that read “all the wars waged by the barbarians in Italy were for the most part caused by the pontiffs, and all the barbarians who invaded it were most often called in by them. This mode of proceeding continues still in our times; it is this that has kept and keeps Italy disunited and infirm.”16 Equally dangerous in his view were those passages in which Machiavelli praised the virtue of antiquity in contrast with the customs of the present day. Where Machiavelli writes, “As a result, many parts of the world, and especially of Italy, have become deserted by comparison to ancient times; and it all happened and happens because in the princes there is no appetite for true glory and in the republics no order that deserves to be praised,” Giuliano de’ Ricci suggests replacing the last line with a less harsh accusation.17 Last of all, in the passage in which Machiavelli praises the behavior of the captain Biagio del Melano, who refused to surrender to the enemy, Giuliano entirely eliminates these lines: “An example truly worthy of much-praised antiquity!—and as much more wonderful than those as it is rarer.”18

Giuliano de’ Ricci is particularly harsh on those passages in which Machiavelli presented and approved examples of behavior guided by a form of morality that rejected the humility and resignation that the church taught. From the well-known oration delivered by one of the leaders of the mutiny of the Ciompi, Giuliano de’ Ricci expunged both the passage in which we read, “And we ought not to take conscience into account, for where there is, as with us, fear of hunger and prison, there cannot and should not be fear of hell,” and another passage in which the anonymous orator declares:

And those who, out of either little prudence or too much foolishness, shun these modes always suffocate in servitude or poverty. For faithful servants are always servants and good men are always poor; nor do they ever rise out of servitude unless they are unfaithful and bold, nor out of poverty unless they are rapacious and fraudulent. For God and nature have put all the fortunes of men in their midst, where they are exposed more to rapine than to industry and more to wicked than to good arts, from which it arises that men devour one another and that those who can do less are always the worst off.19


Giuliano de’ Ricci also decided it was unadvisable to leave in the passage in which Machiavelli praised the Florentine magistrates who loved their fatherland more than their souls, and were thus to be held up as examples of a religiosity that taught a sense of civic duty. He cut out the passage in which Machiavelli wrote: “And they were called Saints even though they had little regard for censures, had despoiled the churches of their goods, and had compelled the clergy to celebrate the offices—so much more did those citizens then esteem their fatherland than their souls. And they showed the Church that just as before they had defended it as friends, so now as enemies they could afflict it.”20

During the same years in which the belief that Machiavelli was a heretic and an enemy of the church was taking root, there are also traces of a presence of his ideas in the thoughts of those who felt a deep disgust for the corruption of the church of Rome and of its prelates. Around 1523, when his ties to Machiavelli were particularly close, Francesco Guicciardini wrote in his Ricordi: “Naturally, I have always wanted to see the ruin of the Papal State. But as fortune would have it, I have been forced to support and work for the power of two popes. Were it not for that, I would love Martin Luther more than myself, in the hope that his sect might demolish, or at least clip the wings, of this wicked tyranny of the priests.”21 Like Machiavelli, Guicciardini focused on the corruption of the “ecclesiastical state” and advocated either its dissolution or any type of reform that might put a halt to the moral corruption of the clergy. In the last version of the maxim (1530), he wrote: “I should have loved Martin Luther as much as myself… to see this bunch of rascals get their just deserts, that is, to be either without vices or without authority.”22

Guicciardini denounced in the priests a mixture of vices that can rarely be found even in the rich variety of human wickedness, a mixture that is particularly detestable in men who claim to live in accordance with the laws of God.23 And yet he does not level an accusation against the Catholic interpretation of the Christian religion, nor against the moral education that derives from that interpretation. Rather, he hastens to point out that he does not love Luther “so that I might be free of the laws based on Christian religion as it is generally interpreted and understood,” but only so that he may witness the end of the tyranny of the priests. A form of tyranny, he explains, that is doubly violent “because [the priests] use both the temporal and the spiritual arm to subjugate us.”24 He shares with Machiavelli a harsh criticism of the states governed by the pope and by the priests, whom he also refers to as “ecclesiastics,” but does not accept Machiavelli’s idea of a religious reform to be carried out by drawing the church back to its beginnings.

In 1530, when he had an opportunity to read and comment on the manuscript of Discourses on Livy in Rome, his Considerations (Considerazioni), though incomplete, focus on the pages with political content, not on religious issues. On more than one occasion, he greatly moderates his friend’s judgments of the importance of religion to the birth and preservation of republics. In his considerations concerning chapter X of book 1, for instance, he does not note that Machiavelli places first among the men who are “most praised” those men who have been “heads and orderers of religions.”25 Also, concerning Machiavelli’s opinion that Rome owed its good fortune more to Numa than to Romulus, Guicciardini objects that “if the first king of Rome had been Numa and not Romulus, certainly the city would have been threatened in its beginnings by its neighbors and Numa would not have left room for Romulus to put armies in, as Romulus allowed Numa to put religion in.”26

If we read the series of Ricordi written just two years earlier, in 1528, the traces of Machiavelli’s thoughts about religion are even more evident. “It was said truly that too much religion spoils the world,” writes Guicciardini, “because it makes the mind effeminate, involves men in thousands of errors, and diverts them from many generous and virile enterprises.” It is not possible to say for certain, but it is very likely that Guicciardini had in mind the passage from Discourses on Livy (II, 2) in which Machiavelli accuses the Christian religion of having made the world “effeminate” and “weak” and of having denied the value of the “greatness of spirit” that leads men to undertake strong and generous deeds.

After speaking of how the Christian religion made the world weak, Machiavelli had referred to the possibility of an interpretation of that same religion, not “according to idleness” but “according to virtue” that teaches “the exaltation and defense of the fatherland” and exhorts men to love the fatherland, to honor it, and to ready body and soul to “prepare ourselves to be such that we can defend it.” Guicciardini too levels an accusing finger against the Christian religion as the cause of the weakness of the world, but proposes only a generic reformation: “I do not hereby wish to derogate from the Christian faith and divine worship, but rather to confirm and augment them by distinguishing what is excessive from what is sufficient, and by stimulating men’s minds to consider carefully what should be taken into account and what may safely be ignored.”27

Guicciardini condemned Luther’s Reformation, even though he had confessed that he was willing to embrace it in order to see an end put to the rule of the priests.28 What he glimpsed behind the Reformation and a free examination of the Bible was less a return to the principles of the Christian faith than a collapse into a licentious way of life. He did not believe in the possibility of a religious reformation that would revive the authentic principles of Christianity and bring about a rebirth of the love of liberty. He judged Italy too corrupt to be reformed. In contrast with Machiavelli, Guicciardini’s propensity for reform was merely a vague thought, or perhaps an impulse.29 He limited himself to a reference to the usefulness of correcting and reforming both faith and worship in order to ensure that Christianity was no longer a source of weakness in the world, but would rather encourage men to undertake stronger and more altruistic deeds. That reference, however tenuous, appears in any case in a text in which we can clearly detect the influence of Machiavelli.

A far more powerful influence can be sensed in the writings of the supporters of the last Florentine Republic (1527–30). Donato Giannotti (1492–1573), who, like Machiavelli, served as secretary of the Ten of Liberty and Peace, invoked the religion of virtue that teaches that God helps only those peoples who are capable of fighting to defend their liberty. In the name of that religion, and of the need to protect republican government, Giannotti denounced the wicked customs of the young and the hypocrisy and corruption of the friars. In order to perfect the Florentine Republic, he writes, it is necessary that the young be “temperate, grave, reverent toward the old, lovers of goodness, enemies of evil, attentive to the public good, observant of the laws, fearful of God, and in each and every action, gladsome and merry.”30

Likewise, Bartolomeo Cavalcanti, when he spoke about the reconstituted Florentine militia, on 3 February 1530, repeated concepts and ideas from Machiavelli:

But can you not see how even those ancient sages who established kingdoms and republics determined that their armies should be guided and governed by the reins of religion? Consider Numa, who as soon as he took over the kingdom of Rome thought of nothing other than to fill with religion the too-savage souls of that bellicose people, since he clearly understood that that ferocious army devoid of religion could not save that city nor lead it to happiness. And you know that Rome, from then on in all public affairs, and especially in matters military, was such a diligent observer of religion that those who scorned the augurs and the sacred laws of war, and other ceremonies were sternly punished by that city, and their actions, however good their outcomes, were rejected; and that the Romans judged the observance of religion to be of greater importance to the safety of the city than defeating their enemies.31


Cavalcanti borrowed a theme typical of republican Christianity when he emphasized the necessity of religion in order to establish a good army:

Your King [Christ] desires nothing more from you than that your souls be inflamed with his love, and that there be among you, with the most holy bond and the indissoluble knot of charity joined together and bound. This is the religion that, if it reigns within you, O Florentine people, you will be always defended by that same [Christ] as his devoted and faithful servant as well as freed from the domain of your enemies, as well as exalted victorious and triumphant above all other peoples: otherwise let none of us confide in their own virtue and hope to achieve successfully anything; because our works will turn out wrong, if we are devoid of the light of divine religion, which guides us along the right path; our daring will be reckless, if it depends not upon confidence of divine help, but that of our own valor; our forces will be weak if they are not supported by the immense power of our King; and ultimately all our hopes will be vain if they are not based on him who governs the universe.32


In another oration Cavalcanti focuses on republican liberty as a gift from God: “Because on that day, 16 May 1527, the tyranny of the Medici left without being expelled, out of the fear and trembling that God put into them in order to fulfill the prophecy uttered by the prophet, Friar Girolamo, who said: ‘The next time that you regain your liberty, you will regain it from God, and not through your own ingenuity and strength, lest the citizens grow proud in the glory of that liberty, as they did the first time, in the year 1494, when they were unwilling to recognize it as a gift from God, as it was; and they therefore lost it.’”33

An even more eloquent example of the republican religion is the oration that Francesco Carducci delivered in 1529 when he assumed the office of gonfalonier. From the very beginning of that oration, Carducci not only reproduced the concepts, but even the expressions of Machiavelli: “The Roman republic, from which the Florentine Republic is descended, while it remained uncorrupted, distinguished men not by their names, but by their deeds, and went to find and honor virtue even among the bumpkins and the ploughmen.” In perfect harmony with the tradition of republican Christianity, the gonfalonier further insisted on the concept of a God who loved liberty and on the duty that fell to men of working with all their strength to defend it: “It was the work of God that tyranny was expelled from this city, in contrast with the wishes of many, and it will be God’s work if it is kept away, despite the intention of more than a few: and we therefore cannot say that we may or must feel that we are secure and able to relax our guard.”

At the conclusion of the oration he links in a masterful fashion the idea of a God that loves more than other men those who do good deeds on behalf of the fatherland, and the idea that man, by working on behalf of the fatherland, becomes godlike and wins eternal glory on earth and eternal beatitude in the heavens:

For myself, I cannot think of anything more acceptable to God nor more desirable to other men than to work in such a way that those who write histories are obliged to place in their books our names, and the things that we have done, either through prudence or valor, in bright inks, in perpetual celebration; because this is nothing other than a way of evading death and preserving oneself alive for a great age; indeed, it is even a form of never dying, of living eternally in glory. It is not entirely wrong, nor indeed far from our own most true and most holy religion, what the Gentile philosophers and theologists wrote, that the souls of those who have administered republics well and faithfully live on after their death, separate from all the others, in the highest and most resplendent section of the heavens, enjoying sempiternal and blessed life. Because no form of praise can be greater or finer among mortal beings, nor can any make men more godlike, than to help other men, and be the cause of the safety and preservation and the liberty of their republics.34


Toward the middle of the sixteenth century, other republican political writers converted openly to Lutheranism. The most eloquent example is that of Antonio Brucioli. For his ideas about religion, Brucioli was forced into exile, the first time in 1529 and a second time in 1548, in the wake of a search by the Inquisition. In 1555, the Inquisition subjected him to an interrogation that culminated in a condemnation of all his books to be burned and a state of house arrest for him.

Brucioli died either on 4 or 5 December 1566, as noted in the file “in materia heresis” (“on matters of heresy”) that the Inquisition diligently maintained.35 Brucioli was a friend and an admirer of Machiavelli. He met him during the gatherings in the Orti Oricellari (or Rucellai Gardens) and cast him as one of the interlocutors, though a minor one, in his Dialogi. The considerations that were clearly inspired by Machiavelli were those that Brucioli placed in the mouth of Giangiorgio Trissino. In the cities, Trissino explained in the dialogue Della Repubblica, it is necessary to ensure that “divine matters” are entrusted to men who are “impeccable and of only the most laudable customs and godliness.” Without the fear of God, it is difficult to govern cities, “because there are many who care little for the laws of man yet who fear the laws of God.” If the priests are like those of our own times, “wicked and without any religion, so much the worse become the people.”36 True lawgivers must be lovers of the wisdom of God, and must be capable of interpreting that “intimate mind of God” that is the foundation of the rational order of the world. A lawmaker who wishes to construct an excellent republic must ensure that “he piously understands all of God’s matters” because “a man cannot be good nor succeed in doing anything good if he lives without the love and the fear of God.”37

In Brucioli’s view, “our religion” instills in men “great hopes of immortality,” and men, because of that hope, achieve great things and are willing to face “the danger of dying for their fatherland.”38 In order to explain how important religion was to the proper governance of states, he cites first what Numa did “with the name of religion and divine worship,” and then Moses “who, with nothing more than the harness of the holy and true religion, not only led an enslaved people out of Egypt, but then guided that people for forty years through the desert.” Numa and Moses were two of Machiavelli’s heroes, just as it was Machiavelli’s observation that the corruption of the priests made the people afflicted with that corruption both irreligious and wicked; likewise, Machiavelli originated the idea that the Christian religion exhorts its adherents to serve the fatherland in order to win eternal happiness.39 The true greatness of a republic is not found in its powerful walls, its wealth, its magnificent mansions, and its mighty armies. Rather, it is found in the customs of the citizens. Only those republics that have citizens who are “learned, wise, good, reasonable, and properly educated, as well as acceptable to God because of their good actions”40 can be said to be powerful and flourishing. Like Machiavelli, Brucioli, who witnessed the end of the republican experiences, identifies the moral education of the citizens through a proper religious education as one of the necessary foundations of a good republic.41

The idea that the Christian religion teaches the virtues of the citizen was also present in the context of the Genoan aristocracy during the first half of the sixteenth century. One piece of evidence of this is Ludovico Spinola’s De reipublicae institutione, written between 1528 and 1530 to commemorate the ruling republican oligarchy that claimed to represent the entire city community.42 Despite the unmistakable apologetic intentions of the book, the philosophy underlying De reipublicae institutione is a civil philosophy based on the principle that a true human and Christian life is one spent in the service of the fatherland and the common good. Like the Florentine humanists, Spinola defends political action on behalf of the good of the republic as “man’s way of saying yes to the divine plan.” In Spinola’s thought, Erasmus’s miles christianus (Christian soldier) develops into the civis genuensis (Genoese citizen) who works to defend the free republic as an implementation of God’s plan.43 Spinola found comfort in the passage of the Somnium Scipionis that promises those who have aided, preserved, and increased the liberty of the fatherland a place in heaven where they will enjoy eternal beatitude because God loves those who serve the republic.44

From this interpretation of the civil content of the Christian religion, Spinola derives not only an admonition to preserve and uphold religious worship, but also an exhortation to undertake a full-fledged moral reformation based on the principle of charity and justice as a response to divine love:

Our city has always distinguished itself for its faithful adherence to divine worship and its constancy in that pursuit: we wish to persist in that observance and faithfulness, following in the footsteps of our fathers. But our faithfulness to the religious tradition of our city and our fathers is achieved fully and completely in the moment in which we achieve, within ourselves, in response to the love of almighty God, a state of mind disposed to supreme goodness and justice. We can offer God nothing that would be better or more acceptable in response to His love and His redeeming death. He does not look to see whether the hands that are raised toward Him in offering are full: He looks to see whether the hands that are raised to Him in prayer are clean.45


Spinola harmonized a municipal religiosity based on the idea of a God who protects and loves the Republic of Genoa with an Erasmian religiosity based on the idea of Christ, “who is nothing other than charity, simplicity, and patience.”46

The same harmony between civic spirit and Christian authenticity emerges with equal clarity in the meditations of Agostino Giustiniani, the Dominican bishop of Nebbio in Corsica who, during the second decade of the sixteenth century lived in Paris for many years, and traveled extensively, visiting the learned men of Europe, first and foremost Erasmus in Flanders and Sir Thomas More in England. Giustiniani states that he was always faithful to a sober ideal of piety and adverse to superstition. He reiterates that loving one’s fatherland and serving the republic is entirely in keeping with a Christian way of life.47 In the Epistola al Duce, al Senato, e a tutto ilpopolo di Genova that begins his Annali della Repubblica di Genova (1537), he writes that “if we call religious those who examine and frequently reread holy matters, then what could be more religious and divine, than to have given instruction to educate our people to be lovers of the republic, so that they attempt not only to maintain it in life and true unity, but also to increase it in power and glory?”48 To set aside a disorderly love of one’s private interest and to devote oneself to the interests of the public good is neither more nor less than what “God commands.”49

Machiavelli’s thoughts on religion were also present in sixteenth-century Venice, a place abounding in unrest, with Catholic reforms and heretical movements.50 Paolo Sarpi, for instance, shared with Machiavelli a critical view of idleness and hypocrisy, and wrote about religious education as one of the means used by “politicians” to preserve the state.51 Sarpi, like Machiavelli, was aware of the power of religion, and he judged as harmful to the state the corruption of the principles and rites of the Christian religion that had been perpetrated by the priests: “The cunning priests have been able at their leisure to shape religion to their own interests, changing it and twisting it as was convenient to their purposes, introducing on a daily basis innovations, many of them highly prejudicial to the basic principles, which are passed without being noticed.”52

Sarpi set forth these observations in his commentary on the Italian translation of Sir Edwin Sandys’s book Europae speculum: or, A view or survey of the state of religion (Relazione dello stato della religione), printed in Geneva in 1625. In this book the author had emphasized that the religion of the Italians consisted “for the most part in external appearance” and in “intricate and silent ceremonies,” whence sprang the custom of an “exterior and mediocre” obedience to the laws, rather than the “integrity and internal sincerity” that is the product of true religion. From bad religion, Sandys insisted, derives an evident moral corruption:

it is impossible to conceal that in general the country is greatly inundated and almost covered with sins, dishonesty of conversation, squalor of actions, everyone competing against everyone else, especially the priests and the monks, in an attempt to outdo one another in impudence; to such an extent that what would not be tolerated elsewhere is here greatly honored; that which elsewhere even the most dissolute individual would be ashamed to confess, here the priests and monks barely blush to do openly. Indeed, if anyone abstains from such actions, it strikes them as strange, and they look upon integrity as little better than simplemindedness and a lack of spirit.53


The wickedness and shamelessness of the Italians, as Machiavelli had guessed, sprang from religious corruption, indeed from the neglect, the superficiality, and the passivity in the area of religion: “Nothing could be more surprising to those who consider the conversation of the Italians than to observe this nation—acute, prudent, and subtle in all matters, not easy to persuade without good reasons—and yet in the matters of religions so careless, indifferent to knowing or seeing, as if by principle uninterested in knowing, as if it were something of no importance to them.”54

In the Venetian cultural milieu, the ideas of Paolo Sarpi concerning moral and religious reform were given significant development in the thinking of the Brescian friar Fulgenzio Micanzio, who advocated a reform suited to political liberty and the true religious spirit.55 For Micanzio, political power requires the support of religion because religion has proven over the centuries its great ability to lead the minds and the passions of the people.56 It is based on that principle that Micanzio derives a harsh critique of the church for having attempted to subjugate temporal power to the spiritual power, and for having “formed a religion that was no longer indifferent to all states, but shaped to its own state and for the furtherance of that state.” At the same time, he scolds those princes who delegate to the ecclesiastics all theological issues and allow the corruption of ceremonies and religious beliefs.57

In contrast with a religion that claims to subjugate secular power to its own interests, and against those princes who allow the corruption of religion out of self-interest or ignorance, Micanzio provides an interpretation of religion that is in harmony with classical ideals and with Christian ideals and which teaches its followers how to translate the lesson of Christian piety into political engagement in the service of the common good.

“But man,” he wrote,

was not born for himself, but principally for the fatherland and for the common good. The question as to whether a wise man should become involved in governance can be explored by others. Our father [Paolo Sarpi] will give us the example of turning away from neither hard work nor dangers in the service of God and the fatherland; and that a good and wise man is far from the mistaken doctrine, invented by a mob of seditious frauds, who never speak of the secular police except as a bad thing, even though it was established by God, and in which an upright man can well serve his divine Majesty with a vocation that is so pious and excellent, that either no others equal it, or at least do not outdo it, as in homage to the greatest piety, which can be exercised in the church, and to which God has summoned from time to time the greatest heroes of the entire ecclesiastical order.58


If we are looking for a confirmation of the link in terms of ideas between humanism and the Italian reformation movement, then let us consider the fact that Machiavelli’s works, even after they were placed on the Index of Forbidden Books, were present in the libraries of the victims of the Inquisition.59 Francesco Gentile da Fermo, against whom the Inquisition began proceedings in 1568, owned and held on the shelves of his library—as did his disciples Manusso Marano and Giovanni Cassimati—alongside the works of Erasmus, both Machiavelli’s The Prince and Discourses on Livy.60 Luigi Groto, also known as the Cieco di Adria, or the Blind Man of Adria, was ordered to appear on 5 June 1567 before the Inquisition of Rovigo because he had been discovered to be in possession of forbidden books. Among those books was the Florentine Histories by Niccolò Machiavelli.61 The poor apothecary Giovanni Antonio Paterno, dragged before the Inquisition of Naples on 6 November 1569, had in his small library copies of Discourses on Livy, the Florentine Histories, and a book entitled Princeps christianus, which might either have been Machiavelli’s The Prince or Erasmus’s Institutio principis christiani. The old apothecary loved those books so much that he wrote his name on each one of them, without stopping to consider that by so doing he had only worsened his position in the eyes of the Inquisition.62 The bookseller Vilio Bonfadini, interrogated on 13 February 1571, replied that “the works of Machiavelli were among the most sought after books, along with Le cento novelle, the Bibles in the vernacular, and the New Testaments in the vernacular.”63

Machiavelli’s views on religion surface in a broad and diverse array of cultural and political contexts. Their presence, for instance, can be seen in the Dialogue on Religion (Dialogus cui titulus est religio) printed in Lucca in 1539 by the Luccan jurist Enrico Boccella, and in particular in the pages discussing a comparison between modern religion and the religion of the ancient Greeks and Romans.64 Equally significant are the observations set forth by the Calabrian author Tiberio Rosselli in his Apologeticus, printed in 1519, concerning religious worship as a factor of cohesion and good customs among men, and his worried denunciation of the decadence of Christian rituals, degenerating into superstitious and magical practices.65 Even closer to Machiavelli’s ideas are the thoughts of the Brescian Benedictine monk Gregorio Bornato who emphasizes, in his De libero hominis arbitrio, completed in 1537, the practical function of religious rites and the effectiveness of Roman religion at inspiring the souls of the young to emulate both virtue and glory.66

Moving along the same path was the Siennese nobleman Bartolomeo Carli Piccolomini, a follower of the ideas of Juan de Valdés and the author of an uncompleted essay, entitled Trattati nove della prudenza, probably written between 1530 and 1540. In this virtually unknown text, we can clearly see the presence of the idea of religion as a form of human perfection and greatness, and as the foundation of a true civil way of life. Like other Italian thinkers, Piccolomini experienced the religious reformation above all as a demand for moral rebirth,67 and was a profound admirer of Machiavelli.

“In this pursuit,” he writes in the treatise Of the Perfect Chancellor (Del perfetto cancelliere), “men will become expert in many fields, by managing countless affairs, as did Nicolo [sic] Machiavelli in the Florentine Secretariat, where he acquired lengthy and varied knowledge, a deep and rare understanding of the matters of the world, whence derive his highly valued works about the principality, about War, his Discorsi sopra Livio [The Discourses], and his histories, in which it is possible to glimpse a perfect harmony of a great mind, with extensive experience of varied things pertaining to human life.”68

Like Machiavelli, Piccolomini called for a religion that did not encourage living in resignation and idleness, relying on God for help, but rather one that exhorted its followers to work wisely and with strength of spirit. In this connection, he quotes a passage from Sallust: “Not by vows nor womanish entreaties is the help of the gods secured; it is always through watchfulness, vigorous action, and wisdom in counsel that success comes. When you abandon yourself to cowardice and baseness, it is vain to call upon the gods; they are offended and hostile.” From Discourses on Livy he draws the maxim of political wisdom that the people not only need laws, but also the “fear of God,” if a republic is to be preserved good and united. Princes, even at the cost of evincing a devotion that they do not feel, must respect the cult of the divine and preserve religious ceremonies uncorrupted.69

Piccolomini maintains that with religion the ancients achieved great things that would otherwise “not have been possible,” and he cites examples from Discourses on Livy and The Art of War: David, Numa Pompilius, Sartorio. He believes, like Machiavelli, that those men who were considered worthy of speaking with the deities were themselves divine, like Scipio Africanus “in whom was considered to be such divinity that he could speak to God.” But if men, or certain men, have divinity within themselves, then religion becomes synonymous with moral virtue, as Piccolomini himself explains: “Under this heading [of religion] I mean to describe a virtue of the soul that consists of the fear of God, in a perfect conscience, and in a great care to preserve the honor of religion. If those parts are found in a prudent man, they will make him miraculous, and place him in the reverence of one and all, because good men will love him, guilty men will fear offending him lest they be seen to be enemies of the good, and especially of religion, which is a dangerous thing to oppose in any manner.”70

Even the Italian evangelists, though well aware that Machiavelli was quite different from Erasmus, read his books carefully and made use of them.71 Giovan Battista Goineo, who died an exile in Germany where he had sought refuge because he was accused of heresy, in a letter to Pier Paolo Vergerio written in 1539, quoted, in relation to the origin of the secular power of the popes, a passage from the Florentine Histories.72 Lucio Paolo Rosello, a parish priest in the Venetian hinterland, in contact with the philo-Erasmus circles of Padua, a correspondent of Melanchthon, a translator of Luther, and an example of Italian Nicodemism (the hypocritical custom of feigned adherence to Catholic worship while concealing one’s heretical beliefs), used Machiavelli’s ideas extensively in his Ritratto del vero governo del Principe, published in Venice in 1552. Concerning the themes that are most closely connected with religious issues, Rosello nonetheless took the defense of Erasmus’s Christian caritas against Machiavelli. A true prince, he wrote, must show a “religious piety,” as did Numa Pompilius, Hercules, and Alexander the Great, and not move away from the Christian religion, especially where oaths and promises are concerned.73

Machiavelli was the topic of assiduous debate among those who frequented the Calvinist circles that Alessandro Trissino, a Vicenza aristocratic placed on trial by the Inquisition in 1563, and who died around 1609, helped to run in Venice between 1558 and 1561.74 From Holy Office sources we know that in those gatherings the discussions turned to the problem of grace, of human freedom with respect to God, the importance of works, the pope, and the legitimacy of the use of images and the cult of the saints.75 According to available sources, it is difficult to identify what aspects of Machiavelli’s thought most attracted the interest of Alessandro Trissino and the “gentilhuomini” (gentlemen) who attended the Venetian gatherings. It is likely that their interest converged in particular on those passages in which Machiavelli accused the Catholic Church of having undermined the religiosity and the morality of the Italians. It is equally plausible that they applauded the Florentine Secretary’s pessimism with regard to the human condition and the pages he wrote about how fortune dominates so great a part of human affairs and the life of the state.

Because of his Calvinist faith and his cultural education and upbringing, Trissino was profoundly opposed to the exterior and superficial forms of religiosity and harshly condemned the hypocritical practice of Nicodemism.76 This aspect of his religiosity, common to other Italian heretics, was in sharp contrast with Machiavelli’s ideas. Machiavelli had advised princes and the rulers of republics to feign respect and devotion even for those religious practices in which they did not believe.77 If princes and rulers of republics could feign and conceal, then it was all the more reasonable to think that their subjects and citizens could do the same.

A further documentation of the distance that separated the religious sensibility of the reformation from Machiavelli’s prescriptions in the area of ceremonial can be found in the 1578 letter in which the heretic Bernardino Bonifacio, Marchese of Oria, criticizes the treatment of religion in chapters 11 to 15 of book I of Discourses on Livy because it was excessively favorable to superstition. What the heretics considered to be superstitions were for Machiavelli effective ways of keeping alive in the populace a fear and veneration of God. The purity of the faith was a chief concern for heretics and evangelists, but of less interest to Machiavelli, even though they shared a similar focus on a rigorous civil ethic that was deeply rooted in hearts and minds.

That Calvin and the Calvinists were something quite different from Machiavelli was clearly understood by another Luccan, the printer Pietro Perna, who was in religious exile in Basel. With the help of Stefano Tegli of Foligno, Perna published in 1560 the first Latin translation of The Prince, followed by three editions in 1580. In that translation, Perna—in contrast with the theocratic ideas of Calvin and his followers—sang the praises of Machiavelli’s political wisdom. He did so because Machiavelli spoke of the state with no theological presuppositions, and in that manner pointed the way toward civil peace. Machiavelli’s Huguenot enemies, Perna wrote, “have entirely muddled and confused the consciences, the families, the people, and the state. And now I ask you, O readers, whose teachings are better: those of Machiavelli, who shows how it is possible to conquer the government and hold it peacefully, with a minimum of bloodshed… or that of these people, who are neither strong enough to govern nor capable of doing so, and who in the meanwhile advocate for years and years conflict and bloody battles and ultimately send to the inferno so many thousands of bodies and souls, devastating cities and provinces, without ever putting an end to the desolation.”78

In his editions of The Prince, Perna nonetheless inserted powerful antidotes and edited out of the text those passages that most greatly offended Christian morality. His determination to distribute the work of Machiavelli was motivated not only by the desire to encourage the fame of an author whom the Catholic Church had placed on its Index of Forbidden Books or to oppose a secular concept of the state against the Calvinist theocracy. Perna, who like many other exiles brought with him from Lucca the ideals of Italian liberty, the republic, and religious reform, perceived the same aspirations in the pages of Machiavelli. This convergence of political ideals and a demand for moral and religious reform encouraged the translation of works by the Florentine Secretary in the lands of the Reformation, and those works served as a launching pad for a full-fledged rebirth of Machiavelli’s ideas in the history of the conquest of liberty in Europe.


2. The Difficulties of Survival in Counter-Reformation Italy


While the proponents of moral and religious reform accepted and reinterpreted in various ways the idea of a Christian religion that would teach the love of liberty and the fatherland, the political writers who were closest to the spirit of the Counter-Reformation either rejected it or twisted it beyond recognition. They judged Machiavelli—and they were not far wrong—to be more than just another of the many critics of the corruption of the priests, but rather a proponent of a different interpretation of the Christian religion. To accuse him of heresy was ill advised, because Machiavelli never dabbled in theology, and yet they still needed to find a way of answering the charge that the church had infested Italy and the world with indolence, and they still had to respond to the question of whether or not the religion of virtue was true Christianity.

One of the first to speak out against the idea of a religion that would help to build a virtuous republic was the Paduan Sperone Speroni (1500–88). In a text abounding in references to Machiavelli’s ideas, Speroni explains that a civil way of life is always and exclusively guided either by self-interest or the desire for peace, or else by the lust of conquest. The Christian people never “raises its head toward the heavens,” except when it turns to God, “wheedling and entreating.”79 The “civil religion” of the men who live in republics is in every way contrary to the true Christian religion. It is a vain dream, unworthy of those who, like Machiavelli, had stated that they did not wish to discuss “republics and principalities that have never been seen or known to exist in truth,” to pretend that republic has virtue as its ultimate end.80 The only possibility in the view of Speroni, who failed to perceive the evident weakness of his argument, was the Christian prince described by Erasmus, who lived in accordance with the laws of God and who educated his subjects to follow his example.81

Far more refined was the position of Giovanni Botero, the most authoritative Italian political writer of the Counter-Reformation.82 Botero sharply rejected the idea that Christian morality was incompatible with royal majesty. In order to support his view, he appealed to the God of the Old Testament, the “God of the armies,” the same God that Machiavelli (whom he called “homo ingeniosus sed parum Christianus,” that is, ‘an ingenious man, but scarcely a Christian’) had invoked in his writings, in order to install in princes that virtue that those who act merely out of calculation and personal interest cannot hope to attain.83 At the same time, Botero rejected Machiavelli’s claims that the teachings of Christ have made men unsuited for war and for military virtue. He acknowledged that church doctrine prohibited unjust wars, but he also emphasized that the Christian faith teaches us that the soul is immortal and that the glory of eternal life awaits those who fight and die on behalf of justice, religion, and the homeland. It is therefore not the cause of corruption, but rather a school for civil and military virtue. Examples such as that of Godfrey of Bouillon do more than any possible argument to demonstrate that Christians are, thanks to their faith, great warriors.84

Since the Christian religion had not made the world weak, there was no need for any moral and religious reform whatever. Botero failed to understand, or chose not to understand, that Machiavelli had not spoken so much of a decline in military valor, as of the waning of a love of liberty and of the virtue that gives men the strength to resist those wicked men who want to dominate others. Setting aside the essential question of liberty and political virtue in the strict sense, Botero explained with a wealth of references that the real causes of the decline of states are heresy, which foments a spirit of subversion and triggers incurable divisions in the body politic; scorn for the church and the priesthood; and cruelty toward God’s prophets and servants. Machiavelli had praised the Florentine magistrates who in the fourteenth century loved their fatherland more than their souls and placed no importance on a papal excommunication; Botero condemned princes who paid no attention to excommunications as wicked and irreligious men. Machiavelli called for the rediscovery of the God of liberty; Botero was invoking the God of obedience.

In his most important work, Della Ragion di Stato, Botero also rejected Machiavelli’s ideas on religious reform, choosing instead to exalt the religion of humility and an apparent full submission to the Catholic Church and its prelates. He begins his argument by emphasizing the role that religion plays in the preservation of states. Religion, he writes, “maintains the state through the divine aid of God’s grace.” He explains that subjects, even the subjects of a tyrant, do not fear being oppressed by a prince who respects God and are less inclined to mutiny in that case. A prince must therefore cultivate religion in order to ensure that he is beloved of God and in order to preserve his state. With the friendship of God, moreover, he can achieve great things in civil life and in military enterprises. In order to reinforce the hope and the daring of soldiers, nothing is more effective than an appeal to God and the promise of happiness in the next life.85 Like Machiavelli, Botero offers as an example the Romans who “regarded religion as one of the chief bases of government and would not permit it to be altered or violated in any way.” He rejects the idea, however, that a prince can be considered religious even if he lacks a sincere faith. It is unlikely, he observes, “for one who is not truly religious to be thought so, for nothing lasts so short a time as pretence. The prince must prostrate himself in all humility before the Divine Majesty and acknowledge that from Him proceed the power of a ruler and obedience of his subjects. The higher he is raised above his fellows, the lower he should abase himself before the face of God, taking no business in hand unless he is certain that it is in conformity with God’s law.”86

Still clearly waging a polemic with Machiavelli, Botero absolves the church of Rome of all responsibility for the ruin of Italy, and instead levels an accusation against the emperors: “In the times of the Emperor Arnulf, and in the years that followed, religion declined through the faults and bad example of the emperors, who were most insolent in their behavior toward the Church. At this period of moral collapse Italy was looted by the Saracens, and utterly ravaged by the barbarians.” He even refuses to accept a distinction between religion and the religious, a distinction on which all reformers had insisted: “A man who has no respect for the clergy cannot feel reverence for religion.”87 He does not hesitate to praise the more exterior and superstitious aspects of the Catholic religion, including the adoration of relics. Men, he explains, aspire to God; but because God is concealed both from human senses and from human intellect, they must go in search of some “sign of his assistance.” A Christian prince therefore must respect prelates, leaving to them the right of judging matters of doctrine, ensuring that they do not slip into poverty, erecting magnificent churches, and even relying on a “spiritual council” made up of theologians and doctors of canon law.88 The morality of docility and humility in the face of powerful men that Machiavelli and the other reformers had so roundly condemned became in Botero’s view one of the most worthwhile aspects of a Christian education. Christian law “[enjoins them] to obey wicked rulers as well as moderate ones, and to suffer all rather than disturb the peace.” Aside from the docility of the subjects, Christian law, with the aid of the church, allowed princes to extend the dominion of the state to the inner souls of men: “According to [Christian law] not merely the bodies and possessions but even the souls and consciences of his people are subject to him.”89 This is the most straightforward doctrine of religion as Instrumentum regni, with a certain grandeur to its clarity, and Botero set it forth in clear and direct contrast with Machiavelli’s religion of liberty.

During that same period, Paolo Paruta (1540–98) also maintained that the Catholic religion is well and entirely suited to the preservation of states and that there was no need to change it. Since divine worship is a necessary condition for a truly civil way of life, it is indispensable that it should be “excellently established” in the city. Private citizens must obey the magistrates and the magistrates must obey the laws, but both the citizens and the magistrates must respectfully, reverently serve the religion “with which all other good conditions would be impossible and the city would not only no longer be happy, it could not even be considered a city.” If man moves away from God, he loses his “true form.” Even the gentiles, who admittedly were not aware of this truth, ordered that their civil governments should be based “upon this firm foundation of religion” and celebrated the virtue of Numa Pompilius as a latter-day founder of the city of Rome because he “ordered it in religion.” To that much greater a degree, a Christian prince, who knows that he reigns on earth by God’s will, must “devote every attention, and every effort to ensure that his people be educated to the true faith and that in every part of the city, and in every deed of the citizens the role of religion should be magnificently evident.”90 The prince should in no case allow there to be two religions, but should ensure that the city abounds with temples and priests, and that the ceremonies of worship are pious and magnificent and that religious holidays are observed devoutly and solemnly. External appearances, Paruta emphasizes with Venetian wisdom, stimulate “internal effects”; moreover, the prince should foster in the souls of the citizens, along with piety, a love of true goodness “which alone can form that perfect civil virtue, that in vain others attempt to bring into the world through fear of the laws.”91

Tommaso Campanella brought a ferocious attack to bear against Machiavelli who was guilty, in his view, of having reduced religion to nothing more than the art of governing men and for having treated Moses as simply a virtuous and cunning man.92 Religion, Campanella explained, is a necessary foundation of the lives of states and of society,93 and he warned the prince against altering the existing religion and its rites for political ends.94 Campanella also attacked with particular acrimony Machiavelli’s idea that the church bore the principal responsibility for the fragmentation and weakness of Italy: “I know that Machiavelli says that the Pope was the cause of the ruin of Italy, but this statement smacks of ignorance, directed not at the providence of God, nor at the whole, but merely at a part, at a few popes, who summoned the barbarians to Italy.”95 At the same time, he maintained the necessity of religious worship in republics as a tool of government: “Listen, Venice, to your medicine, for now I speak to you out of the reason of state, not out of theology, which in these times is suspect and widely mocked. You certainly will perish in this resolution you have made against religion—understood?—because every republic and empire preserves itself with the auspices and orders of living with which it originated and grew to grandeur and glory, just as every plant preserves itself with the water, land, and sunshine that is natural to it; and as soon as its water or native soil is taken from it, it will languish or die.”96

In a poem that was probably written in 1601, Campanella actually invokes Christ, urging him to “return to earth, armed,” and turn His weapons against the wicked religious who have strayed completely from His law.97 But his armed Christ does not teach the love of liberty, and he wages war at the pontiff’s side. Campanella’s ideal is not a return of the love of a free way of life, but rather the triumph of the pope over the other princes and a victory of Catholic doctrine over heresy and atheism.98 With that goal in mind he judged beneficial that education to humility, docility, and a detachment from worldly affairs that Machiavelli had so strongly condemned. The contrast between the spirit of the Counter-Reformation and Machiavelli’s religion of liberty could not be any more distinct:

Therefore know, O Venice, that the reason why the peoples fail to rebel is the religion and the priests and the monks, because they are the soul of the body of politics and the limbs can never rebel against the body, while it is the very soul that animates them, as philosophers know.
