Now, while the princes behave wrongly and the populace is ill contented and are urged forward by bandits, the religious are the cause for their failure to rebel, and their continued obedience, with their sermons or their secret confessions, admonishing them to live rightly, and distracting them from their vicious intents, and comforting them with Paradise, and terrifying them with the Inferno, and encouraging them to go to church, where they observe the offices and masses and sermons and genuflections and humble faces and bent necks and rough clothing and lessons and exercises, and seeing that they behave according to duty and not according to mockery, they compose their souls and believe in the next world, and happily leave this world: and, if they are outraged, they have in their hearts this comfort that they will be rewarded by God in another life: and these customs have great power in the human heart, making men pious, obedient, loving toward one another, and faithful: and the more churches are built and religion and sermons and such actions, the more the people believe and are filled with piety: otherwise men would be worse than lions and tigers and worse than the demons themselves, because their minds are unconquerable, they lend their forces one to another and they make use of animals, and boldly do as they please.”99


That men should be domesticated sheep rather than ferocious animals, and that religion is the most effective method for molding them in that way, is explained very clearly by Traiano Boccalini in his Ragguagli di Parnaso. In this satirical work, he describes Machiavelli’s trial and sentencing on Parnassus, the imaginary abode of wise men under the government of Apollo; Machiavelli is condemned less for the impious doctrines that he had drawn from the deeds of princes than for having “been caught during the night in the midst of a flock of sheep, working to insert false dog’s teeth into their mouths, with a most evident danger that all shepherds might abandon their posts.”100 A grievous crime inasmuch as it stripped the sheep of their simplicity and tameness and made it impossible for a single shepherd to control large flocks of animals, and in brief “was an attempt to kindle the entire world into flame, to make the simple malicious, and to show the light to those moles that Mother Nature had so judiciously brought into this world blind.”101

Who are the sheep and who are the shepherds? Clearly the Christian people and its priests. As he repeats the clichés of the anti-Machiavellianism of the Counter-Reformation, Boccalini appeared to be attempting to tell his readers that Machiavelli had instigated the people to rise up against the rule of the prelates, who had no other aim than to take wool and cheese from the flock of the faithful; Machiavelli had attempted to enable the simple to see, and therefore to free them from a rule that extended deep into their souls.

Similarly Gaspare Scioppio, who was in the forefront of political thought of the seventeenth century, rejects the idea that a moral and religious reform is necessary, and instead makes a considerable effort to shift Machiavelli’s thought into the center of traditional Catholic doctrine. The decision to place the works of Machiavelli on the Index of Forbidden Books, he explains, developed out of political considerations. His writings, if examined with sufficient care, contain nothing in violation of Catholic faith and morality102 Machiavelli acknowledged in a way that leaves no room for doubt that religion is the foundation of the good orders and the greatness of republics, and that without the fear of God, the fear of a prince is not sufficient to instill a respect for the law.103 Even though Machiavelli placed Moses on the same level with Cyrus, Romulus, and Theseus, and attributed his victories and his laws to armed might and not to God, he did so because as a politician he wished to consider only natural causes and exclude supernatural causes.104 Machiavelli’s admonition to a prince to respect and to encourage religious worship, even if he considers it to be false, is not in Scioppio’s eyes an offense to religion, but rather develops out of an awareness that in politics what counts is appearances and actions, not one’s inward convictions.105 When he accuses the Christian religion of having made the world weak, Machiavelli is speaking to those who wish to live a life that is secure from pain and sacrifice and to cover their ignoble cowardice with religion. Machiavelli explicitly acknowledged that the Christian religion, rightly interpreted, not only allows its followers to defend the fatherland from tyrants, but even exhorts them to do so.106 Scioppio absolves Machiavelli from the charge of irreligiousness, but he also discards the need for a renewed religion of the fatherland and of liberty, and instead insists on the tired theme of the moderating effects of religion.

With tones verging more toward the moralistic than those found in the writings of the theorists of the Counter-Reformation, Scipione Ammirato (1531–1601) also condemns Machiavelli for having subjugated the Christian religion to the demands of the state. Rather than adjust religion to fit themselves, he writes in his Discourses on Cornelius Tacitus (Discorsi sopra Cornelio Tacito), published in Florence in 1594, men, and especially princes, must adjust laws to fit religion, “since in the nature of men in the fields and the caverns, before cities were built, there was a belief in God sooner than there were civil gatherings, on behalf of which laws were made; because it would not otherwise be necessary to say that religion should accommodate to civil life, than who might say that seasons of the year should change to fit individuals rather than the other way round.”107 When he exhorts a prince to feign respect for religion even if he is not a believer, in Ammirato’s view Machiavelli is recommending nothing more nor less than fraud, because “nothing other than fraud is it to cause people to believe one thing in place of another.” The wise prince, to the contrary, should free his soul “from such malice” and hold fast in the knowledge that “since our religion has no need, in order to grow, of our falsehood, therefore lies will be helpful to no one for long, and the Romans, a proud and greathearted people, reputed liars to be servile felons.”108

With comparable determination, Ammirato defends the Christian religion from the accusation that it had attempted in all ways possible to extinguish the pagan religion by eliminating its orders, its ceremonies, and its theology, and even, with Pope Saint Gregory the Great, by burning the works of historians and poets. Ammirato upholds the superiority of religion over state and exalts the moral worth of Christianity as a religion that teaches humility. “This man [Machiavelli] speaks of the Christian religion,” Ammirato writesm, “as if it were a state policy, which right or wrong intends to pursue its thoughts; failing to consider, that since it is different from the ways of the world, it has different approaches than those used by the world, because the reputation of the Christian religion was poverty, humility, chastity, scorn for the things of this world, forgiving one’s enemies, ignoring wrongs done to one, and a willingness to submit to torture and martyrdom in order to win life and glory in the world beyond.”109

It was however the Jesuits, in the context of the Counter-Reformation, who perceived better than anyone else the subversive significance of Machiavelli’s idea with respect to Catholic orthodoxy. They accused him of dethroning God and Divine Providence from the government of the affairs of the world, putting in their place an impersonal fate and the inexorable influence of the stars. Pedro Ribadeneyra in his The Christian Prince (Principe cristiano) explicitly denounces Machiavelli for having spread the impious doctrine that takes political action out of the power of God and Divine Providence.110 In a world ruled by fate, political action could no longer be subject to government by religion and its legitimate ministers on earth.111

Machiavelli is the Antichrist. Saint Hippolytus Martyr, wrote Ribadeneyra:

depicts the Antichrist as a hypocrite and a master of just this kind of politics. He says that when the Antichrist shows himself to the world he will be merciful, humane, religious, a friend to justice and an enemy of corruption and gift-giving and gratuities; that he will not allow idolatry, that he will honor the elderly and the silver-haired, and he will hate dishonest people, those who squander, and rumor-mongers; he will welcome the poor, he will protect widows and children; he will act as a peacemaker, and he will compose disagreements; he will give gifts and distribute wealth, and he will feign in such an astonishing manner that, even though he will do everything imaginable to win the people’s favor and to become the ruler of the world, when the people themselves come to beg him to become their king, he will allow himself to be entreated, signify that he is unwilling, and that he scorns power and preferment, until he finally yields to their requests, accepts scepter and crown and crushes the world.112


Even in the heart of the Counter-Reformation, voices were raised to revive and defend Machiavelli’s ideas concerning moral and religious reform. Giordano Bruno, in The Expulsion of the Triumphant Beast (Spaccio della bestia trionfante), written in 1584 against Luther and his “most deformed” Reformation, invoked a “new civil and natural religion” as a remedy for the corruption of the world that had been brought about by bad religion.113 In Bruno’s narrative, it is Jove himself who felt the need for a reformation that might bring back to life a true religious sentiment and expel from heaven the beasts depicted in the constellations, replacing them with virtues, first and foremost justice, and thus restore the “jaded strength and enervated manliness.”114 Once the fear of God vanished, true religious worship also vanished from the world, and religion, instead of encouraging men to become heroes, rendered them similar to beasts.115

In contrast with the spirit of the Counter-Reformation, Bruno draws on and develops the humanistic and Machiavellian theme of rebirth through a return to the beginnings. In Bruno’s story, Jove recognizes the invincible power of fate that brings death and decay,116 but also states that fate gives the gods the power to rise again: “Let us prepare ourselves, then, let us prepare ourselves for our destinies; because just as Fate has not denied us the possibility of falling, so it has conceded us the possibility of rising again; therefore, just as we were ready to fall, so are we prepared to get back on our feet. We shall be able to depart without difficulty from that suffering into which through our error we have fallen, and from worse than that which could befall us, by means of reparation which lies in our hands.”117 Against Luther, who preached that men cannot become godlike and that all acts of heroism are “nothing more than cowardice,” Bruno revived the idea that men can be reborn, and in that rebirth become godlike, pursue justice, love the fatherland, and seek glory. It matters little that in order to become godlike men must become beasts because “he who does not know how to become a beast cannot maintain his superiority.” We must believe, Bruno has Jove say, that “for man to be divine it is fitting that he have of the beast, and that when he yearns to show himself deeply divine, he makes up his mind to let himself be seen in such measure as a beast.” Honor therefore should be accorded to Chiron the Centaur, a most just man who taught Achilles and showed the way upward to heaven.118

Machiavelli in The Prince had sketched out the image of the founder of states who knows how to be both man and beast and yet becomes godlike and wins God’s friendship. Bruno sets forth the same image in even more vivid hues. For him too it was the Romans who served as examples of men who made themselves similar to the gods, and the gods loved the Romans for their devotion to their fatherland, their magnanimity, justice, and mercy, and they rewarded them by elevating them above all other peoples. With their deeds, Bruno emphasizes, the Romans “knew how to conform with and resemble” the gods:

by pardoning the subdued, overthrowing the proud, righting wrongs, not forgetting kindness, helping the needy, defending the afflicted, relieving the oppressed, restraining the violent, promoting the meritorious, abasing criminals and spreading terror and the utmost destruction among them by means of scourges and axes, and honoring the gods with statues and colossi. Whence, consequently, that people appeared more bridled and restrained from vices of an uncivilized and barbarous nature, more excellent and ready to perform generous enterprises than any other people that has ever been seen. And as long as such were their law and religion, their customs and deeds, such were their honor and their happiness.119


In these words we clearly hear the echo of Machiavelli’s Discourses on Livy, which Bruno in all likelihood read in the edition published in London by John Wolf in 1584. Machiavelli and Bruno shared a radical criticism of the religion of idleness and the exaltation of a religion that would teach men to love the fatherland and perform good deeds on behalf of a civil way of life. The most grievous ills that men commit are those “which have a prejudicial effect upon the republic.” The gods wish to be loved and feared “for the purpose of benefiting mankind.” The gods detest sins that “bring them annoyance.” In the eyes of the gods a solitary and “useless” man is of less value than someone who works on behalf of a civil, “profitable conversation.” The “proud appetite for glory, out of which there results some good for the republic” is more worthy than the “sordid lust for money.” Someone who has “liberated his country and reformed a perturbed spirit” is more deserving of triumph than someone who “has healed a humble and useless lame man who, cured, is worth little, or no more than when he is infirm.” God does not allow “that statues be erected to good-for-nothings who are hostile to the state of republics and who, with prejudice to customs and human life, offer us words and dreams, rather than to those who raise temples to the gods, increase the cult and zeal for such a law and religion, through which are kindled magnanimity and ardor for that glory which is derived from the service to the fatherland and is to the advantage of mankind; whence there seems to have been instituted universities for the disciplines of customs, letters, and arms.”120

Bruno’s God, like Machiavelli’s God, loves and hates, rewards and punishes. While Machiavelli still believed it was possible to bring men back to the God that teaches virtue, Bruno did not believe it was worthwhile to attempt to draw Christianity back to its beginnings. He believed that its very beginnings were corrupt, and that Luther’s Reformation, rather than being a first step toward rebirth, was the final stage of degeneration. The return of the world to “its ancient countenance” cannot take place through a rediscovery of Christian principles, but rather by finding new religious beliefs in the ancient knowledge of the Egyptians and Romans.121 Even though he admits the usefulness of religious ceremonies, Bruno takes his distance from Machiavelli: he accompanies him for a good part of the way, and sets off toward a rational religion.

Although with a lesser degree of philosophical robustness, Francesco Sansovino (1521–83) too keeps alive in his writings the teachings of Machiavelli on religion and political liberty. In his Concetti politici (1598) he writes that “it never happened that a respectable man lost his liberty, unless he lost it along with his soul. Indeed, everyone knows that it is a very difficult thing for those who were taught, raised, and born in liberty to live in servitude, since among all the things in the world, the finest is liberty: whose very name is an indication of virtue, just as servitude is an indication of unhappiness.”122 And he adds that “above all others, God loves free cities. Because it is in those cities, more than in all other kinds of government, that the common welfare is preserved, justice is administered more equitably, the souls of the citizens are more likely to engage in virtuous and honorable acts: and there is more observance and respect of religion.”123

Sansovino reiterates with Machiavelli that religion is necessary if one is to have good laws and good arms: “Arms, laws, and divine worship cannot be separated in a well governed city save with the destruction of some of them, while if joined together they preserve one another, supporting one another, whereas if by chance they are unbound, the one that might have seemed sufficient unto itself without its two comrades, quickly falls.”124 Without mentioning his name, he takes from Machiavelli the maxim that “of all the men who are praised, particularly praised are those who are the chiefs, and the founders of religions. At their side are those who have founded Republics or Kingdoms” and the consideration that “there was never an issuer of extraordinary laws in a people who did not avail himself of the assistance of God, because otherwise those laws would not have been accepted. A prudent man knows many factors that do not possess sufficient reasons to persuade him and convince others. Thus wise men who wish to eliminate this difficulty, attribute everything to the hands of God. Thus did Lycurgus, thus Solon, and thus all the other lawgivers.”125 He also returns to the classical and humanistic theme of the great rulers who receive as their reward glory in the heavens: “The souls of warriors and valorous men, who are fated to die for the fatherland, for their children, and for their religion, once riven by cold steel from the ties of the physical body, are undoubtedly received by the clear star-spangled heavens, as to a haven of blessed quiet.”126

During a time in which principalities were triumphant, Sansovino rediscovered a number of elements of the old civil religion that developed in the context of the republican experiences. When examining the religion of Utopia described by Sir Thomas More, he notes that the inhabitants of that happy republic accept the Christian religion because they have understood that the “fashion of their way of life pleased Christ,” and they judged Christianity to be “very similar to their religion,” which gave them the determination to persevere “in that fashion of good living.”127 Sansovino recalls Machiavelli’s principle that it is republics above all that require religion: “Religion causes good orders, good orders bring good fortune, good fortune engenders the success of undertakings. However the observance of divine worship is the cause of the greatness of Republics, and where there is no fear of God, it is necessary for that Realm to be upheld by a Prince, who provides fear to make up for the absence, where there is no religion.”128

The ideas of Machiavelli concerning religion found especially fertile ground among the Italian freethinkers, first and foremost Giulio Cesare Vanini, who upheld a conception of nature as an incessant and unstoppable cycle of corruption and generation, without purpose and without divine guidance, in every way similar to that of Machiavelli.129 “Everything that is born”—writes Vanini

in any species you care to name, grows, lives for a set period of time, and finally progresses toward corruption: now, in order to ensure that this corruption is followed by a new birth, it is necessary that there be prior provisions that are contrary to the ones that tend toward corruption: and indeed if the mechanism of generation does not outweigh the mechanisms of corruption, birth will not ensue, since nothing can be generated unless there are adequate mechanisms at work in the matter, just as nothing can be corrupted unless the mechanisms for the conservation of the subject are themselves corrupted. This rule is not only true for men in particular, but also for cities, kingdoms, and religions.130


Vanini also shared with Machiavelli a high opinion of the pagan religion for its effectiveness in preserving and expanding the empire. False though it might have been, the religion of the Romans played its role quite effectively, not only because it promised eternal rewards to those who died on behalf of the fatherland but also because it set up simple mortals as gods to be worshiped, and in this way encouraged men to act in such a way that they might hope to rise to the status of “divine dignity.”131 It was therefore through the assistance of deities and “celestial bodies” that Romulus rose from shepherd to king, and Rome became “the capital of the world.” It was from Machiavelli, last of all, that Vanini derived the idea that great lawgivers persuaded the people that they had received the “sanction of the laws” from the gods, and especially the myth of an armed prophet who, unlike Christ, could impose his laws and emancipate peoples: “Christ always found himself helpless and all those who set out to defend the truth unarmed (says Machiavelli) died miserably. Moses, on the other hand, always proceeded armed.”132 Despite the intensity of the polemical attacks on the part of the political writers of the Counter-Reformation, the idea of the religion of virtue never disappeared entirely from the Italian intellectual milieu. It persisted as an ideal aspiration or as a memory, without however succeeding in becoming a point of reference for a full-fledged reform movement.


3. The Redemption of Italy and the Idea of Moral Reform in the Writers of the Eighteenth Century


Machiavelli did finally become such a point of reference, however, in the eighteenth century, especially through the work of writers who considered the emancipation of the conscience to be a necessary condition for the political redemption of Italy. Their Machiavelli was both a critic of the bad education produced by the corrupt Christian religion, and the prophet of the moral renewal that takes place when a people rediscovers itself by rediscovering the principles of a free way of life, true religion, and justice.

Among the first to focus on the necessity of a religion that could teach a love of the fatherland was Paolo Mattia Doria (1662–1746). In his Of Civil Life (Vita civile) Doria placed religion among the most important requirements in preserving good customs in a republic or a kingdom: “It is not possible to establish a law, or set up a custom, that will be accepted by a people, unless they perceive it to be a commandment from God and a consequence of religion.”133 He then adds, in a polemic with Machiavelli, that “it is certainly true, nonetheless, that this must be done rightly, and not with malice, as others have wickedly asserted, but rather with pure and sincere truth: because in the final analysis it is not that religion, as some wicked individuals have believed, is ordered by politics, but that politics is what a religion orders.”134 Following this criticism, Doria nonetheless urges the princes to cultivate a religion that teaches the peoples to love the fatherland: “The preservation of the fatherland must be in the peoples a firm and inviolable point of religion.” This religion must inspire veneration through its mysteries and ceremonies and especially the awe and fear of oaths. On the other hand, “badly administered sacrifices, ceremonies performed with a lack of reverence, priests with bad morals, oaths sworn too frequently, and the love of God neglected” all render religion weak and ineffective at “upholding the orders and the fatherland,” and when a religion is corrupt it is difficult to restore it to life in the hearts and minds of men.135

The second maxim that Doria advises is to ensure that peoples prefer the love of the fatherland “to the love of life, while keeping in mind that if the fatherland is destroyed, then possessions are not safe, nor is one’s life secure, and everything else is lost.”136 It is moreover necessary to reward virtue, in such a way that the citizens are encouraged to pursue the good of the fatherland in order to satisfy their own private passions: “Wisely did the Romans, great masters in the art of governing, put into practice the distribution of so many crowns to their soldiers, so many laurel wreaths to their men of letters, so many triumphs to the generals of their armies, and so many temples to their lawgivers; because they turned in this manner personal interest to the benefit of the fatherland, and they encouraged men, through the love of glory, to suffer those immense efforts that all true virtues demand.”137

The cause of the love of the fatherland and the love of the prince is veneration. In order to obtain veneration, a prince must take great care to appear almost divine: “If princes, magistrates, or senators of the republic wish for the people to love the fatherland that they rule and govern, they should do their best to win that veneration, that allows them to be considered almost divine, and those human virtues that cause them to be seen as heroes.” Men always respect those virtues that resemble the perfections of God, because virtues trigger in them “that wonder that is fed by the divine virtues.”138 Magistrates, therefore, in order to attract the love of their subjects and citizens, should “demonstrate themselves to be almost divine in their virtues.” In conclusion, Doria comments, “true virtue” generates “a true love in the people,” “illustrious vices, or those mixed with virtue, create a love that originates out of a passion for glory; the hypocrisy of virtue produces a false love of the fatherland, which lasts only a short while, until it is uncovered, or until the vices grow to excess,” and “the uncovered vice causes open hatred.”139

The ideas of the Florentine Secretary concerning the religious and moral problem can be seen in the works of Pietro Giannone (1676–1748), and in particular in his Civil History of the Kingdom of Naples (Istoria civile del regno di Napoli), published in 1723, which the Jesuit Giuseppe Sanfelice judged to have been written in order to instruct and form an even more wicked prince than Machiavelli’s and in order to show “that the tree of civil life will also flourish more prosperously in that soil where the Christian religion no longer blooms.”140 Even more visible are the references to Machiavelli, even though Giannone never mentions his name, in his Discorsi sopra gli Annali di Tito Livio, a work that was written during the period of rediscovery of Machiavelli the republican between the end of the seventeenth century and the beginning of the eighteenth century.141

Pietro Giannone points out that the religion of the Romans, despite the fact that it was false and superstitious, was effective in fostering civil and military virtues. The Christian religion, despite the fact that it was “true and certain” and that it taught “a far more perfect and pure morality” than did the pagan religion, is incapable of inculcating in the souls of the moderns the distinguished and sublime virtues of the Romans. For this moral decadence the Christians deserve to be considered the most wicked men on earth: despite being guided by the most perfect of religions, they choose to follow the path of corruption. But the “laxity and gravity of their customs,” Giannone emphasizes, springs from the superstitious, superficial, and hypocritical perversion of Christian teachings. “The greatest evildoers are those who, unfortunately, believe [in eternal life], flattering some in one way, others in another, and abandoning them all to divine mercy; indeed, these are freely willing to pass from religion to superstition, and cover their failings under specious pretexts, and conceal their ambition, avarice, and other vices beneath the cloak of hypocrisy and an affected humiliation and piety.”142

The thoughts of Giannone about ancient religion and the religion of the moderns are very close in their substance to the ideas of Machiavelli.143 Following in Livy’s footsteps, Giannone praises the religion of the Romans because it served “solely for the preservation and expansion of the republic,” to encourage the souls of its adherents to “truly arduous and magnanimous” undertakings, and was in short an effective way of “causing the citizens to observe their duties, among them the preservation of justice and concord; and bound by this tie, they could devote themselves not only to the preservation of the public good, but even to expanding it; and they should not abuse that trust, as some wicked men sometimes do, for their own convenience and private interest.” From these observations, Giannone concluded that “as far as the civil theology is concerned, princes ought not to neglect to maintain in their dominions that religion which the people took in with their mothers’ milk, not only to keep them in the safety of a tranquil civil society, but also because the power of their traditional religion serves greatly in the context of military expeditions to encourage the soldiers to take on difficult and challenging exploits.”144

Against Machiavelli was raised nonetheless the critical voice of Giambattista Vico, who leveled against him the accusation of having failed to understand the role that religion had played in the greatness of Rome and in the birth and life of nations. “And here we find,” Vico explains, “the principle whose effects are narrated in Roman history, though neither Plutarch nor Machiavelli uncovered the basic cause: that religion was what brought about Rome’s greatness.” It was religion, based as it was on the augurs, that first engendered and subsequently kept alive magnanimity in the heart of the plebs and pushed the plebs to prove that they were just as worthy as the patricians in both war and peace. Hence the heroism and greatness of both the patricians and the plebeians, and therefore the greatness of Rome. Machiavelli saw this “great truth” only “in part,” when he identified as a cause of Rome’s greatness the magnanimity of the plebs, but failed entirely to grasp a more general truth, that republics are born and live through the intervention of Providence. Moreover, Machiavelli reasoned about the preservation of peoples, much like Hobbes did after him, “with malice toward God, with disrespect toward the princes, and with injustice toward the nations.”145 Nor did he perceive the gravity and wisdom that contributed so greatly to the glory of that republic: “Thus the cause which produced among the Romans the wisest jurisprudence in the world is the same that made the Roman Empire the greatest in the world. And it is the cause of the Roman greatness, which Polybius, in too general a sense, attributes to the religion of the nobles; Machiavelli, on the contrary, to the magnanimity of the plebs; and Plutarch, envious of Roman virtue and wisdom, to their good fortune.”146

That religion is necessary to the birth of civil communities and to their preservation, Machiavelli had clearly stated. Likewise, he had written that the Roman religion was founded on the augurs, and had been one of the causes, along with the wisdom of the decisions of the Roman Senate, of the greatness and glory of Rome. Vico instead chose to link Machiavelli to the theorists of natural law who had ignored the role of religion in the birth and the life of states.147 He theorized the return of corrupt men from “premeditated malice” to the “primitive simplicity of the first peoples of the world,” and explained that this return meant becoming again “religious, truthful, and faithful” and rediscovering “piety, faith, and truth,” which is to say, the foundations of justice and “the graces and beauties of the eternal order of God.” He does not link, however, this intuition to the pages of Machiavelli, and he fails to sense the need for a religious reform. In his view, the Christian religion is the “best in the world,” it has given rise to a Europe that dazzles with “such humanity,” a culture that is notable for “ministering to the comforts of the body as well as to the pleasures of mind and spirit.”148 Why reform a religion that has produced such positive effects? If a reform is ever necessary, it will be Providence that produces it.

The author who understood and absorbed Machiavelli’s urgent need for moral reform was Vittorio Alfieri (1749–1803).149 He recognized the immense power of religion. Opinion “is the undeniable mistress of the world” because it guides the actions of humanity; but there is nothing that controls men more than religion, nothing “that more greatly impresses in them this or that opinion, nothing that more greatly inspires them to carry out great enterprises.”150 Against the “semi-philosophy” that dominated his century, Alfieri proclaimed that “to believe in God, after all, never harmed any people; indeed it aided many; it deprives strong-minded individuals of nothing; and it is a prop and stay for the weak.”151 For this reason, faithfully following the “divine Machiavelli,” as he liked to call him, Alfieri called the heads of religions, the prophets, the saints, and the martyrs, “superior men” who “deserve, even from the most irreligious of men admiration, devotion, and veneration.” It matters little that they, emulating Moses, who used religion to give “form, liberty, and existence” to the Jewish people brutalized by decades of servitude, happily made use of “the sublime veil of an inspired religion.”152

Alfieri criticized the Christian religion for having persuaded men to accept the absolute power of the prince or the tyrant. The pagan religion, he explains, “must have been, and in fact was, quite favourable to freedom, because it endlessly multiplied its gods, turned heaven into a kind of republic, and subjected Jove himself to the laws of fate, and to other usages and privileges of the celestial court. The Jewish religion, and hence the Christian and the Mohammedan, must have been, were, and still are much more favourable to tyranny, because they admitted one God alone, the absolute and dreadful Lord of all.”153

These are things, Alfieri comments, “which have all been discussed by others,” and it is therefore unnecessary to insist on them. Machiavelli had written about them in Discourses on Livy, and like Machiavelli, Alfieri accused the Catholic religion of being an enemy of liberty and of having taught men to think and live as slaves: “The Christian religion, which is that of almost all Europe, is not in itself unfavourable to a free life, but the Catholic religion is almost incompatible with freedom.” It teaches men to accept the more-or-less unbounded authority of a single man over the great questions of life, and in that way makes the peoples that accept that religion slaves for all time. With the practice of confession, and “the certainty of pardon for any iniquity whatever,” moreover, it encourages, rather than condemns, criminal and immoral behaviors, with the result that it prevents the formation of a true civil morality.154

Alfieri forcefully emphasized that the teachings of Christ are not contrary to a free way of life: Jesus Christ, “politically considered as a man, also desired by teaching through example, truth, and virtue to restore to his own people, and to many others at the same time, by way of a better religion, a political existence independent of the Romans who held them enslaved and debased.”155 He adds that “our own saints” who were also writers, such as Paul, Augustine, Chrysostom, and Jerome; those who taught with words and examples, such as Francis, Dominic, and Bernard; and finally those who died to testify to their faith, such as Lawrence, Stephen, and Bartholomew, triggered in many others a “burning desire to imitate their virtue” and to bring back to life the authentic teachings of Christ.156

After the Catholic Church imposed on the whole world its servile religion, the path to win back liberty could only be that of religious reformation and of heresy. The excessive abuses of the Catholic Church, Alfieri explained, obliged “some peoples who were more wise than imaginative to curb it, stripping it of many harmful superstitions. And these people, later distinguished by the name of heretics, thus opened once more a path for liberty, which was born anew and flourished admirably among them after she had long been banished from Europe; this is proved to us by the Swiss, by Holland, by many cities of Germany, by England, and by new America.”157 He adds that the religious reformation that pointed the way for a number of peoples to a lasting liberty took the form of a return to the beginnings like the one about which Machiavelli had spoken: “Even the Christian religion, though a bitter enemy of worldly glory, is seen to have been if not a stimulant to liberty at least compatible with it and with happiness, and even with a certain greatness of nations in all those countries where it was somewhat modified or, more accurately, led back to its simple primitive principles. This is still evident among the Swiss, the Dutch, the English.”158

In his concluding chapter, which he entitled, in a tribute to Machiavelli, Exhortation to Free Italy from the Barbarians (Esortazione a liberar l’ltalia dai barbari), Alfieri emphasizes that Italy can have a future as a republic and thus become once again free and great. The foundation for his prophecy is Machiavelli’s argument that there is no reason, save for vile cowardice, to believe that what has been done by men in the past can no longer be achieved by other men in the present, especially on the same soil. Italy, therefore, can rise again, if it is capable of rediscovering itself and resuming a life in accordance with the principles of the true Christian religion, as other peoples that enacted religious reforms have done. Reworking Machiavelli’s ideas, Alfieri interprets the rebirth of a people as the rediscovery of a forgotten religion and hands down this idea to the generations that would become the protagonists of the Risorgimento.

The call for moral and religious reform in Italy became especially intense and widespread around the middle of the century. It was a movement, as Franco Venturi has written, that “did not constitute a new, revised, and improved edition of the Protestant reformation,” nor “was it the incipient Enlightenment revolution of the French post-Encyclopédists”; rather, it was “the discovery, on the part of a growing number of Italian intellectuals and politicians, of a new relationship with the church and the state, with peasants and citizens, with law and science.”159 The book that better than any other conveyed the spirit of the reform movement was Carlantonio Pilati’s Concerning a Reformation of Italy, that is, Concerning the Means to Reform the Worst Customs and the Most Pernicious Laws of Italy (Di una riforma d’Italia, ossia dei mezzi di riformare i più cattivi costumi e le più perniciose leggi d’Italia); published in 1767, this work by Pilati (1733–1802) was, in Venturi’s words, “a classic of the Italian Enlightenment,” and it had lasting and widespread repercussions in Italy and in Europe.160 Pilati, who spent nearly his entire life in exile, like so many other proponents of moral and religious reform, denounced religious corruption as the principal cause of the ills of Italy.

The wicked examples set by the clergy, he explained, have ruined the customs of the Italians. With the Inquisition, the priests have deprived Italy of her finest minds; with the cult of the saints and their superstitious practices they have taught the Italians how to cover up the worst vices: “They [the Italians] abandon themselves to robbery, murder, and all other manner of harm to their neighbor, and then they make some pilgrimage to the sanctuary of their advocate saint, paying to say Mass in honor of their saint, as is customary to say, they make an offering of a few coins in the charity box on that saint’s altar, they celebrate that saint’s day, they say confession, and take communion on his behalf, and having done that, they fear nothing, secure in the belief that their saint will protect them from all perils, not only in this life, but in the life to come.”161

Despite the fact that he levels harsh criticisms of the corruption found in Italian religiosity, Pilati considered religion to be a force well suited “to keeping states both good and united, to holding peoples in subjection and quiet, and to inspiring a love of the fatherland in the subjects, along with a predisposition to do things that are useful to their neighbors, and to observe a reciprocal concord among them.”162 If Italy is to be reborn, it is indispensable to oblige the clergy to live in accordance with the Gospels and in keeping with the teachings of the apostles. He clearly states, adopting Machiavelli’s advice, that this moral and religious reform could not be achieved through the power of the laws, but only through the persuasive force of words and examples: “The employment of laws in cases where customs and manners are to be reformed is an entirely tyrannical mode, from which can be hoped nothing other than to astonish and disgust the people without being able to conduct in the direction that is desired.”163

The ideas of the Jansenists contributed to a significant degree to the demands for religious and political reforms in the mid-eighteenth century. Writers linked to this school of religious thought denounced the corruption of the authentic spirit of Christianity in the superstitious and exterior practices of worship that had drained the religious cult of all serious moral and civil content and had driven Italy back into a sort of “religious infancy.”164 The priests, wrote Melchiorre Gioia (1767–1829), have encouraged all the feelings of esteem and respect for actions that are of no use to society and have therefore engendered and perpetuated “a scorn for political and social duties.”165 With their morality based on idleness, they have justified the worst forms of moral conduct and have further caused the poverty that afflicts so much of Italy. “The Protestant citizens,” wrote an Italian Jansenist in 1768, “are nearly all wealthy, and the Catholic citizens are nearly all poor. The reason for this disparity is that the Protestants have no monasteries, and so all of them set themselves to work on something or other, while the Catholics have monasteries, fomenting idleness and laziness which in turn engender poverty.”166

In the Jansenist tradition the demand for liberal and democratic reform of the political institutions sprang from a religious consciousness and awareness that rediscovered the authentic significance of the Christian word against dogma. Lodovico Antonio Muratori (1672–1750) wrote in 1748:

All the laws that this divine master [Christ] has brought from Heaven, if you consider them carefully, were designed to make men happy. Happy primarily in spiritual terms… happy as well in the spheres of the state and temporal government. Thus, if in fact the teachings of the Gospel, so marvelously filled with charity and justice, were commonly implemented, we would enjoy a wonderful peace and concord in this lower world, and the princes would govern us gently, with more concern for the good of their subjects than their own interests…. Read and reread the holy books of the New Testament: you will find nothing in contrast with the wise temporal government of the people. Indeed, everything conspires to improve that government and to render easy the yoke of the Christian religion and to lighten its weight, with the abolition of so many rites and the obligation of so many costly sacrifices of the ancient law…. One of the principal goals of Christianity is the public utility and the good, both spiritual and temporal, of the faithful…. and therefore both ecclesiastical and secular laws must help one another to further, and not to hinder, this public good.167


The principles of Christianity, Muratori concludes, are more active and powerful than human virtues and serve to sustain good political institutions.

Given their religious and moral convictions, it was natural that the Jansenists should consider Machiavelli, the republican who was an enemy of the popes, as their “dearest idol.” The name of Machiavelli appears often in their letters, and in Florence it was the Jansenists who edited, “in crocchio,” (‘in a group’) under the supervision of the Abbot Reginaldo Tanzini, secretary of Scipione de’ Ricci, bishop of Pistoia and descendant of Giuliano de’ Ricci, a new and important edition of the works of Machiavelli (Florence, 1782).168 Like other reformers before them, the editors of the new edition of Machiavelli’s Works looked with special favor on the pages that the Florentine Secretary had written concerning the religious problem, and in the lengthy preface they defend Machiavelli from charges that he was an intractable enemy of the Christian religion. “What he thought of religion can be seen in particular in chapter 12 of the first book of Discourses on Livy, where after establishing that religion is one of the fundamental principles of any well-regulated government, he judiciously assigns responsibility for the deplorable state of affairs in Italy to the corruption and the scorn of the Christian cult.”169

Machiavelli was hardly an irreligious man, the publishers remarked. He always wrote about religion in respectful terms and pointed to it as not only the most secure foundation for any well-ordered government, but also as the origin of the law and the moderation with which modern peoples treat their enemies in war, in contrast with the ancients. The same page in Discourses on Livy in which, to general outrage, Machiavelli had exhorted princes and the rulers of republics to show respect for religious ceremonies even if they considered them to be false, is a pearl of political wisdom, and if that advice had been taken, it would have spared the population the horrors of the wars of religion: “Although he might not have spoken with absolute theological rigor, which would be ridiculous to expect from a man of his profession, it does show how much importance he placed upon religion in good politics, if he took this point to the verge of superstition and scruples. For that matter, he was well aware that all changes in the area of religion were enemies of tranquility and public safety. Tragic experience fully justified his fears.”170

For the editors of the Cambiagi edition, Machiavelli deplored religious “decadence and corruption” and was a good prophet when he foretold the schism that would soon bloody all of Christian Europe. He did not theorize a return to paganism, even though he did admire the courage and spiritual strength of the ancient Romans. He recommended instead a renewed interpretation of Christianity in keeping with that religion’s authentic principles. He had written that the decadence of the ancient political virtues were not a product of Christian principles as such, but of the “false interpretations given of the precepts of the Christian religion.”171

Italian ideas about religious reform were powerfully influenced by the 1762 publication of Jean-Jacques Rousseau’s Contrat Social (Social Contract). In this book, Rousseau set forth a doctrine that differed sharply from Machiavelli’s ideas: no longer a return to the beginnings of the Christian religion, but rather the establishment, through the republic, of a civil religion based on new dogmas. Rousseau was raised in the republican milieux of Geneva where the intellectual heritage of Machiavelli remained quite strong. He sincerely admired the political thought of the Florentine Secretary. In a well-known handwritten notation published in the 1782 edition of the Social Contract, he wrote, “Machiavelli was a proper man and a good citizen; but, being attached to the court of the Medici, he could not help veiling his love of liberty in the midst of his country’s oppression. The choice of his detestable hero, Cesare Borgia, clearly enough shows his hidden aim; and the contradiction between the teaching of the Prince and that of the Discourses on Livy and the Florentine Histories shows that this profound political thinker has so far been studied only by superficial or corrupt readers. The Court of Rome sternly prohibited his book. I can well believe it; for it is that Court it most clearly portrays.”172

From Discourses on Livy Rousseau took the idea that great lawgivers should place in God’s mouth the rules of civil life “in order to constrain by divine authority those whom human prudence could not move,” and he recognized that only men with great souls can persuade people that they have been inspired by God and thus establish enduring laws. He also borrowed Machiavelli’s accusation against the Christian religion of educating its followers to a servile mentality: “So far from binding the hearts of the citizens to the State”; the Christian religion “has the effect of taking them away from all earthly things. I know of nothing more contrary to the social spirit.” His invective, entirely built on Machiavellian themes, concludes with an unappealable condemnation: “Christianity preaches only servitude and dependence. Its spirit is so favourable to tyranny that it always profits by such a regime. True Christians are made to be slaves, and they know it and do not much mind: this short life counts for too little in their eyes.”

Rousseau, taking his distance from Machiavelli, excludes the possibility of an interpretation of the Christian religion in accordance with political virtue. He recognizes that every state, and a republic more than all the others, has need of religion, but he recommends a new religion, a civil religion based not on dogmas but on “social sentiments without which a man cannot be a good citizen or a faithful subject.”173 This is a religion that is to be established not with the power of words but with the force of laws. A republic can banish those citizens who do not believe and sentence to death those who, after publicly declaring that they believe, behave as if they did not. With the Social Contract then begins a new phase of republican thought concerning religious reform, a phase that overshadows the teachings of Machiavelli.

The signals of a new way of thought are evident in the ideas of the Italian Jacobins. Girolamo Bocalosi, for instance, in his book Of the Democratic Education to Be Imparted to the Italian People (Dell’educazione democratica da darsi al popolo italiano, 1797), quotes the entire famous page written by the “great Nicolò [sic]” on the responsibility of the Christian religion for having weakened in the moderns their love of a free way of life and for having made the world weak, and then comments: “This argument of Machiavelli’s is, I believe, unassailable.”174 Bocalosi theorizes not a religious reform, but a full-fledged revolution: he does not suggest a new interpretation of the Christian religion, but an entirely new religion that would completely destroy the principles, the language, and the rites of the old one.

If it were possible, he writes, I would prefer “that we try to preserve all the pious ideas that men now have, nor would I modify anything about the way they now think, however superstitious, but I would want them all to become republicans.” The problem is that the Christian religion as such is unsuited to the education and formation of republicans and to the regeneration and renewal of a people: “I challenge any learned doctor with a degree in the study of Saints Luke, Mark, and Matthew to organize for me a republic of citizens who are moved by powerful zeal on behalf of the earthly glory of their fatherland based only on the precepts found in the writings of those three most reverend writers.” With Christian teachings, he continues, with an observation that smacks distinctly of Rousseau, we would have “men who would be moral and virtuous in the affairs of patience; but can we defend with patience alone our hearths and homes, our families, and the soil that gave us birth and gives us sustenance?” If it is possible to define the moral rules that teach us to be good citizens and good Christians with a new language that is more in keeping with democratic principles, “then why should we rely upon vehicles, voices, phrases, and names that must be associated with other names and other ideas, that only serve to perturb the system of democratic education and which, preserving as they do in our souls the age-old prejudices, they often guide us back to the age-old errors and servitude?”175 A radical political revolution must be matched by an equally radical religious and moral revolution. We must learn, Bocalosi warns, from tyrants how to destroy and from the church the methods that allowed it to reign uncontested throughout the world for centuries.176

The most detailed treatment of a plan for a religious revolution meant to establish a new theology of the Supreme Being and new forms of worship is found in the writings of another Jacobin, Enrico Michele L’Aurora. In the appeals published under the title To Italy in the Shadows Dawn Brings Light (All’ Italia nelle tenebre l’aurora porta la luce, 1796) he denounced “the wickedness and ignorance of the ecclesiastics” who were guilty of having deceived for centuries the people with countless falsehood and of having preached a gospel of resignation: “If your kings tyrannize you, if the powerful oppress you, if the nobility holds you in contempt, and if the wealthy insult you, tolerate all this patiently: life is short, and God will avenge you by punishing your tyrants in the next world.”177 L’Aurora exalted the figure of Christ in contrast to the temporal power and the corruption of the church: “The Christ who founded the Christian religion had no realm, no money, and no possessions; He preached charity and poverty, and both He and His disciples lived and died poor mendicants; intent only on spreading their religion, they did not interfere in temporal matters at all, nor did they play any role in civil government, limiting their ambitions to the conversion of the gentiles and to persuading them to abandon idol worship and to adore only the one and supreme God.”178 From his praise of Christ, L’Aurora did not derive the idea of a reformation that might draw back religious practice to its first principles, but rather the idea, or the dream, of a rational religion that all peoples could accept.179

Matteo Galdi (1765–1821) expressed similar thoughts in his reflections on public revolutionary education. Like the other Italian Jacobins, Galdi began from the consideration that Christianity cannot be reformed. “However pure and innocent this cult may have been in its beginnings, it is now too corrupt to be drawn back to its proper boundaries, too many factions and religious wars would be ignited between the reformed and the reformers, too insolent, avaricious, and ignorant are its ministers to be able to be summoned back to the right way; Christianity ultimately has too many faults with humanity, it has caused too much bloodshed, encouraged too many wars and too much civil discord, it has been too closely allied with despotism, ignorance, and error, and so it always was and always will be the same, an enemy of truth and the republic.”180 It is from this premise that Galdi draws the conclusion that it is necessary to establish a completely new religion that he calls “teofilantropismo” (theophilanthropism), founded on the principle of the love of God and of our neighbors. Only the theophilanthropic cult can destroy superstition and put an end to the political influence of Christianity. Daughter of the republican revolution, the new religion is the only one that is consistent with the progress of the human spirit; it alone is capable of rendering universal the republic.181

Vincenzio Russo (1770–99), martyr of the Neapolitan republic, supported in turn, rather than a religious reform that would educate the people to a different form of morality, a civil morality without religion. He theorized the possibility of uprooting religious superstition through the work of a censor who demonstrated to the people, with unequivocal facts and luminous words, that it is possible to achieve a different form of morality than that produced by theology. He was even convinced that the people would love the new morality and perceive it as being much closer to the truth and more in keeping with human faculties than the old religious morality.182

Russo admits that religion can be very effective politically and can stimulate men to act in ways that are beneficial for a civil way of life, but he emphasizes forcefully that good social institutions can obtain the same results with far less danger.183 It is not true, as Machiavelli believed, that it was the fear of God that gave ancient peoples the strength to perform the great deeds described in history books.184 If anything, then, it is advisable that those who govern democratic republics should set religion aside and work to eliminate it, allowing a rational morality to take its place.

Vincenzio Russo explicitly criticized the “profound Machiavelli” for having spread the belief that political bodies, like natural bodies, are subject to a cycle of birth, growth, decay, and death, with no other remedy than to “draw political institutions back to their beginnings, and thus to restore them as it were to a new life.” This doctrine, aside from being dreary and sapping the enthusiasm of revolutionaries who aspired to create political institutions that would defy time, is false in that there is no reason to believe that social energies must necessarily decline, if they are upheld by good institutions and by good laws. With the proper provisions “political bodies can endure,” and therefore the friends of humanity can sacrifice themselves in order to create “a line of light that will shine for all future time on the happiness of the human race.”185

Giovanni Antonio Ranza (1741–1801), founder of the Republic of Alba (April 1796), was another sincere proponent of religious reform as an indispensable condition of republican liberty. In his appeal For a Republican Lombardy (Per la Lombardia repubblicana) he speaks explicitly of the need for a “religious reform” that would correspond “to the simplicity and dignity of a democratic government.” Such a reform would consist of a return to the equality and the democratic and apostolic conciliar liberty of the earliest centuries of the Christian church and would set itself the task of

reforming abuses, rooting out errors, abolishing superstition, and preserving only those dogmas that are truly Catholic and apostolic; replacing casuistical morality with evangelical morality; instituting, in place of the ridiculous hodgepodge of ceremonies, multiplied endlessly over the course of sixteen centuries, a return to an original simplicity: converting the Latin singsong of the liturgy, incomprehensible to the people, into the Italian vernacular which is understandable to one and all; and last of all, exchanging the luxury and ill-conceived pomp of the sacred furnishings and functions for the modest propriety of the democratic Christians of the good old days in which the married ministers of the sanctuary, with hearts of gold and pure hands, sacrificed with clay vases and woolen garments; and the people, in unison with their sacred ministers in the social virtues, were the object of the admiration and envy of the sterner philosophers of the pagan religion!186


Gaetano Filangieri (1752–88) too theorized the necessity of a civil religion, though he followed the new path blazed by Rousseau rather than Machiavelli’s older path. The pages of the Social Contract and Nicolas-Antoine Boulanger’s Research into the Origin of Oriental Despotism (Recherches sur l’origine du despotisme oriental) had encouraged him to consider the religious experience as something other than a superstitious fraud; instead he saw it was a powerful force for social unification and a response to the demands of the multitudes. Religion is “so inherent to the nature of man, so necessary to the formation, perfection, and preservation of society, and so terrible in its degeneration” that it becomes a powerful political force. Filangieri made a distinction between the context of religion and that of politics. The former was assigned to the internal forum, that is, the interior convictions of individuals; the latter was assigned to the external forum, that is, one’s actions.187

It was from this foundation that Filangieri derived the idea of a civil religion that would be diffused out through the lodges of the Freemasons toward the elites and the populace at large, that would battle fanaticism and irreligiosity, and that would put an end, through legislative measures, to the privileges enjoyed by the clergy and the existence of a church separate and distinct from the civil power as well as hostile to that power.188 In contrast with the Jacobin supporters of religious reform, Filangieri theorized a gradual transition from the old and superstitious religion to a new civil religion. Even in his plans, however, reform was neither a return to the beginnings of the Christian faith nor a project to be undertaken merely with the aid of words and examples, but rather the construction of a new theological and moral edifice.189

Unlike what its proponents fervently hoped, the Jacobin approach to moral and religious reform reinforced traditional religion and weakened republican regimes. The Jacobins made the mistake of believing that the institutions and laws of republics born out of revolution, with the aid of French armies, would be powerful enough to uproot from the minds of the people age-old religious beliefs and replace those creeds with new dogmas, new symbols, and new rites. They failed to realize that, as Machiavelli had taught, redeemers and founders can create new states, but they cannot create new religions. The defeat of the Jacobin republics prompted those who truly desired the rebirth of Italy to rediscover the wisdom of Machiavelli and set aside the works of Rousseau.


4. The Risorgimento and the Religion of Liberty


The defeat of the revolutionary experience brought the most sophisticated political writers into direct contact with the problem of the absence in Italy of a public spirit capable of supporting republican institutions. They realized that even more than reactionary governments and the papacy, the true enemies of republican liberty were bad customs and bad religion. The revolutionary enterprise might be able to change governments and constitutions, but only a large-scale, long-term educational program could change customs and religion.

It was Vincenzo Cuoco (1770–1823) who understood better than anyone else that the Italian problem was first and foremost a problem of public spirit. In a letter to Giovanni Battista Giovio dated 7 March 1804, truly enlightening in reference to the story that I am telling here, he explained that a better order, for him and for the fatherland, could never come about as long as the Italians remained “slothful and cowardly,” weak in their souls even more than they were weak in politics and arms.

It is time—he writes at the end of the letter—to join to the glory of knowing how to say beautiful things also the glory of knowing how to achieve great things, because those nations that do not know how to do great things, sooner or later will forget how to say them as well, and the minds of men will shrink, their hearts will shrink, and since they have no true ideas of the beautiful and the great, they will create a mannered beauty, the beauty of the orator, the beauty of school, and there will be a collapse into the languid, the polished, and the false: just as the Greeks first declined into that decadence, followed by the Romans, and it is there that we seem to be hastening today. I think about these matters much as did Fabrizio Colonna in The Art of War written by our Florentine Secretary.190


Cuoco, who wrote in the same letter that “Machiavelli needs no defense,” was surely thinking of the last pages of The Art of War in which Machiavelli, through the words he placed in the mouth of Fabrizio Colonna, contrasted the corrupt Italian princes of his time, who dealt “with [their] subjects avariciously and proudly,” decaying in laziness and scorning any who showed them “any praiseworthy course,” with the great generals of antiquity who “lived and died gallantly,” adding that if in them, or in some of them, we can condemn “too much ambition for rule,” nonetheless “we shall never find any softness to condemn, or anything that makes men delicate or unwarlike.” Like Machiavelli, Cuoco also believed, or at least hoped, that Italy was “born to raise up dead things.” But that rebirth could only take place if in Italy a public spirit were formed, based on a love of the fatherland and on virtue. The true greatness of the fatherland, Cuoco explained, springs from the virtue of the citizens. Without that virtue, “neither cities nor men can ever be great.” This is once again Machiavelli’s lesson: “Every state has a fixed period to run. All small nations are destined either to grow or to perish. Those do not perish that devote their minds in a timely fashion to the scope of their future destinies; whence, when the course of events presents to them suitable occasions, they are not left helpless due to a lack of preparation. This was the cause of the weakness of the republic of the Venetians, which Machiavelli describes as a ‘lack of virtue,’ and which, to use his vigorous expression, ‘cut from them their legs for climbing to the sky.’”191

Italy’s problem was that there was no longer any trace of that virtue. If we had not been “rendered cowards by idleness,” Cuoco noted, we could accomplish great things, first and foremost conquer our liberty and become citizens instead of subjects. But we have been educated to the idleness that makes us cowards and weaklings by that holy religion which, “handed down from heaven in order to improve the human race,” has become, “through the abuse of many who have preached it, the source of new corruption.” God wishes us to wage eternal war on vices, “foremost among them the vice of abandoning the fatherland when it is in danger; this powerful God does not wish to be tempted in vain, and he does not help cowards and the weak.”192 If Italy was to be reborn, it would be necessary to return “to the ancient ideas and the ancient virtues” and replace the God of the corrupt religion with the God who commands us to be strong in order to be able to protect the fatherland.193 In his thoughts about the defeat of the Neapolitan Revolution, Cuoco rediscovers Machiavelli’s God and proposes his ideals to the generations who will play a leading role in the Italian Risorgimento.

In those same years, Ugo Foscolo also retrieved from the past Machiavelli’s intuition concerning the need for a moral rebirth through a return to the beginnings. In his On Sepulchres (Sepolcri), Foscolo sketches out a lovely image of “that mighty one / who measuring the sceptre of the Prince, / strips him of laurels to reveal a brow / bedewed with tears and dripping gouts of blood.” In the prose he wrote between 1811 and 1816, he emphasized that religion is essential to the people.194 Without the restraint of a fear of God, the people soon fall under the power of despotism: “A people without religion will soon fall under the sway of an absolutely military government; and that government will be instable because, where there is no supernatural restraint, human restraints will be insufficient to prevent revolutions.”195 Employing a typically Machiavellian argument, he reiterates that the problem of Italian liberty is first and foremost a moral and religious problem: “[Italy] cannot have liberty, because there is no liberty without laws; nor can there be laws without customs, nor customs without religion; nor religion without priests.” As long as the Italians continue to experience religion as an “outward ceremony,” they will not be able to hope to win their freedom. Political rebirth can only come about if the Italians, and first and foremost the clergy, realize that “all the institutions of the world, if they are to survive, must be drawn back to their beginnings; and that they should pay heed to the philosopher so unworthily proscribed by them, who was the first to utter and evidently prove this axiom: the religion of Christ is holy in and of itself, and it will endure eternally in its own essence; but, corrupted by men, and especially by the very men who administer it, it has become useless to any civil institution; and as we might say, nowadays more than ever before, more Catholic than Christian.”196 Who “the philosopher so unworthily proscribed by them” might be, at this point in history, it is not necessary to say.

A few years later, J.C. Léonard Simonde de Sismondi (1773–1842), in his monumental A History of the Italian Republics: Being a View of the Origin, Progress and Fall of Italian Freedom (Histoire des républiques italiennes du moyen âge), which enjoyed broad and lasting influence on the political ideas of the Risorgimento, focused on the destructive effects of a bad religious education on the moral sense of the Italians. Sismondi carried out his analysis in the light of Machiavelli’s ideas and leveled accusations against the monopoly over moral education that the Catholic Church obtained with the advent of the Counter-Reformation. He points out that it is impossible to state just how harmful a bad religious education (“une fausse instruction religieuse”) had been to the moral life of the Italians. In all Europe, the Italians are the people that have remained most faithful to the church and most devoted to religious practice. But they are also the people that observed less than any others the duties and virtues of the Christian faith. As a result of the education that they received from the church, the Italians have become masters of the art of silencing the voice of their conscience and of covering up with a surface devoutness the absence of an authentic moral sense. They have all learned not to obey their conscience, but rather to deceive it, and they are all masters in the art of accommodating their passions with indulgence, mental reservations, the intention of penitence, and the hope of imminent absolution. The great religious fervor of the Italians, in the final analysis, is by no means a way of encouraging uprightness; to the contrary, it is advisable above all to be distrustful of the most devout Italians.197

In the first few decades of the nineteenth century, a number of devoutly Christian thinkers rediscovered the lessons of Machiavelli. Machiavelli, wrote Niccolò Tommaseo, for instance, deserves to be included among the Italian thinkers who thought deeply “about the religious and civil education of their brothers.” Tommaseo even acknowledged in Machiavelli the kind of virtue that, distilled to a state of purity through suffering, gives his words a divine power to “move human souls and profoundly change them.”198 Tommaseo relies on Machiavelli, along with Cicero and Vico, in his statement that religion is more powerful than princes, and that it has lost its power among the Italians only because “the priests wanted it to be fruitful for them more than for the people.” The Italian rebirth, then, could take place only through religion, never against or without religion. Every aspect of Italy that possessed greatness, Tommaseo admonishes, “is either itself religion or an effect of religion; it is either republic or else a remnant of republic.”199

It was pointless, and harmful, in Tommaseo’s view, to dream of a new religion that would take the place of the Christian religion. If we carefully examine the words of Christ, he emphasized in the dialogue Concerning Revolutions In Accordance with the Gospel (Delle rivoluzioni secondo il Vangelo), we see that He in no way prohibits “courage and a consciousness of magnanimous things.” It is the duty of the Christian to

spare others suffering, eliminating that suffering or reducing it at the expense of one’s own suffering or danger; prevent the doing of evil. Hence the necessary consequence: raise up the oppressed, suffer and fight on their behalf; provided that out of our suffering or fighting it is certain, or highly likely, that their ills be alleviated. Another consequence: injustices, whatever their source, whether from princes or commoners, must be denounced and admonished; and, in cases where it is necessary and possible, repressed. It is my sacred duty to further public liberty, inasmuch as that liberty tends to alleviate suffering and root out injustice. Patience teaches me to suffer my own ills, not to tolerate the ills afflicting others; and the patience that teaches us to withstand suffering does not teach us to tolerate crimes. And it must be said: oppressive taxes, corrupt and corrupting militias, education that is either distributed in a miserly or abusive manner, justice that is denied or delayed or unreliable, ecclesiastical liberties (liberties, I say, not immunities) that are violated; all these are crimes committed by kings. And therefore, repress them, punish them we must.200


The Gospels, the apostles, the Fathers of the Church, and countless other examples, Tommaseo points out, prove that the Christian faith teaches us a love of liberty. The Italian revolution can only be a “war of religion” that is engendered by love and by faith, not by hatred and blasphemy, because only a revolution sustained and reined in by the true Christian faith will bring liberation, not a new tyranny.201 Tommaseo found confirmation for his ideas in the pages in which Machiavelli explained that religion is the best means to instill in the souls of soldiers an extraordinary persistence and in the pages in which he warns that war must come to a halt at the thresholds of churches and sacred places.202 If an Italian revolution ever proves to be victorious, Tommaseo stated, and he was a good prophet, we can now say, the true difficulty will be to preserve the liberty thus won. The Italians must fear, first and foremost, themselves and the effects that centuries of oppression have had on their minds and their thoughts. The revolutions that have been led by men without religious faith can be adapted to other peoples; an Italian revolution can and must be only a political revolution inspired by a moral and religious reform.

Even more revelatory of the new sensibility toward the problem of moral reform are the thoughts of Giacomo Leopardi (1798–1837) concerning the religion of virtue. Though without taking Machiavelli as his chief source of inspiration and intellectual guide, Leopardi adopted many of the Secretary’s ideas about Christianity, about the positive effects of ancient religion as a prod toward virtue and the love of the fatherland, and about the need for religion in the lives of republics. More than a simple rediscovery, Leopardi’s was a further development of civil religion, and it developed a theory of the rebirth of virtue and of the love of liberty based on a reappraisal of illusions and religion.

Leopardi considered Machiavelli “the founder of modern and profound politics,” an example of that inventive way of thinking “that is typical of the south.”203 He considered Machiavelli to be an equal of those “extraordinary and great [men] who give with their creations an impulse to the human spirit and help it to achieve considerable progress.”204 At the same time, he described him as a man who was capable of freeing himself from the illusions of virtue, beauty, greatness, honesty, and the love of the fatherland, who taught that the world cannot be renewed and that we must adapt to it with a cold awareness.

Is it not true—Leopardi has Machiavelli say—that virtue is the patrimony of the ass: that any young man, however well born and well brought up he might be, if he has just a pinch of brains, is obliged shortly after entering the adult world (if he wishes to achieve anything, and live his life) to renounce the virtue that he has always loved dearly; that this takes place in all cases and most inevitably; that even the most right-minded men, speaking sincerely, would be quite ashamed if they thought that they were incapable of other thoughts and other forms of behavior than those that they set out for themselves when they were young, and yet which are the only ones that can ordinarily be learned from books?205

But he also has Machiavelli say that:

By my nature, and as a young man, to a greater degree than many others, and even down to the bottom of my soul, I was virtuous, and I loved beauty, greatness, and honesty, at first summarily, and later, greatly. Nor when I was a young man did I reject this impulse, indeed I sought opportunities to put these feelings of mine into practice, as are demonstrated by the actions I took against tyranny, and on behalf of the fatherland. But as a clever man, I quickly learned from experience, and once I had learned the true nature of society and of the times in which I lived (which may well have been different from the times in which you live), I did not behave like those foolish men who expect to change the world with their acts and their words, which was always impossible, but to the degree that it was possible, I changed myself.206


While in the imaginary dialogue with Xenophon, Leopardi presents a great and tragic Machiavelli who accepts the fact that the world cannot be renewed, in the Zibaldone he focuses his attention on the Machiavelli who says “if one wishes a sect or a republic to live long, it is necessary to draw it back often toward its beginning.” Leopardi feels a profound ideal tie with Machiavelli: “And I say in the same sense that if we wish to preserve men, that is, make them happy, we must draw them back to their beginnings, that is, to nature,” he writes in August 1820.207 In those same days he noted that man detached from nature is like a tree cut off from its roots. It can neither bloom nor bear fruit. Rather than moving forward along the denaturing path of limitless progress, it is therefore necessary rather to draw back toward first principles, otherwise there is a risk of the demise of humanity: “If we do not return backward,” our descendants will leave this example to their descendants, if they have descendants.208

To rediscover the true principles of man means returning to the illusions that push us toward virtue and heroism, to the good customs of ancient republics, in particular, those of the Greek republics prior to the Persian war, and of the Roman republic prior to the Punic wars. Those republics were “a state that greatly favored illusions, enthusiasm, etc.” Merit and virtue received their just rewards; the yearning for glory was a powerful engine driving men to achieve great deeds, everyone felt they were part of the whole, and there was not much reason for envy. That state, coming after the primitive monarchy, was certainly the best suited to man, “the most fruitful to life, the happiest.”209 Among the principles that humanity must rediscover there is that of the love of the fatherland, the true root of virtue. “There is no virtue in a people devoid of love of country,” he noted on 21 July 1822.
