Leopardi’s direct source was Montesquieu, but his way of linking love of the fatherland, political liberty, and public and private virtues is entirely in keeping with Machiavelli’s doctrine: “Wherever true and warm love of country has existed, and especially where most, that is amongst the free peoples, the customs were always as proud as they were serious, firm, noble, virtuous, honest, and full of integrity. This is a natural consequence of the love of country, of the sentiment that nations, and hence the individuals have of themselves, of liberty, of valor, of the strength of nations, of the rivalries that they have with foreigners, and of those great and constant and persuasive illusions that spring from all that is produced thereby.”210 However difficult this is for modern people to comprehend, to live with a love of the fatherland, and to be ready even to give one’s life for it, made the citizens of the ancient republics happier than the moderns.211 Along the path back to the principles of virtue and love of the fatherland, nonetheless, lies the formidable stumbling block of the Christian religion. Ancient religion taught its followers to love the public and temporal good, to live for the fatherland and to seek out glory.212 It also taught men to elevate themselves to a point just below the gods” and to have a high opinion of human nature.213 Christianity, on the other hand, has lowered man considerably with respect to God and has fought and continues to fight the tendency to consider great men as divine beings; it rendered men inactive, contemplative, and willing to accept despotism. It calls those who suffer blessed, it preaches patience and endurance, making them useful and necessary.214 By principle, Leopardi makes clear, Christianity neither praises tyranny nor forbids its followers from fighting against it. But men who consider life on earth to be an exile and have no concerns other than “a fatherland situated in the other world” are more likely to tolerate tyranny than were the men who, in keeping with the “ancient religion” considered the earth to be their true homeland.215 These seem like the words of Machiavelli, but they are Leopardi’s. Leopardi nonetheless goes beyond Machiavelli, especially when he ponders the possibility of a rebirth through the rediscovery of the true principles of Christianity and of ancient virtue. Although Christianity, like all religions, is an illusion and bears the great responsibility of having made the world weaker and having enfeebled the love of liberty, a rebirth and a return to the natural principles of a human way of life demands other illusions, that is, persuasions concerning the reality of things that can only be real and important with respect to another life.216

In order to engender and nurture illusions, religion is necessary. It alone is capable of establishing “a great many of those qualities that were proper to the men of antiquity or who were closer to nature, it satisfies our imagination with the idea of infinity, it preaches heroism, and gives life, body, reason, and foundation to thousands of those illusions that constitute the state of average civility, the happiest state of the social and incurably corrupt human being: a state in which as much is conceded to nature as is compatible with society.”217 The state of a Christian people is a state midway between the state of nature and the state of corruption: “Life, activity, the pleasures of domestic life, heroism, sacrifices, love of the public good, the private and public loyalty of individual and nations, public and private virtues, the importance placed on things, compassion and charity, and so on and so on…. All the illusions that rendered sublime the peoples of antiquity, and that render sublime children and the young, acquired new vigor and power in Christianity.” “Pure Christianity” has beneficial effects on the virtue of a people, as is shown by the example of the heroic resistance of the Spaniards to the French; corrupt Christianity on the other hand destroys good customs and takes a people away from the happy state of average civility, as is attested by the example of Spain in the “low times.”218 Like Machiavelli, Leopardi therefore identifies, as an alternative to the subservient way of life of corrupt Christianity, not a return to the “state of ancient civility” and to the ancient religion, but rather a return to the state of “average civility” and to “true and pure Christianity.”219

In the nineteenth century, the most surprising instance of an adoption and reelaboration of Machiavelli’s religion of virtue in opposition to the wicked religion of laziness was, in any case, that of Vincenzo Gioberti (1801–52), standard-bearer of the crusade to unify Italy under the aegis of the pope. Surprising because Gioberti knew perfectly well what Machiavelli had written about the role that the papacy and the Catholic religion had played in Italian history, and he specifically accused Machiavelli of having gravely damaged the patriotic spirit of the Italians because he considered the pope “to be a supplement to Italian civilization, indeed, an impediment, not to say a scourge,” and to have conceived the absurd idea of “renewing in the Christian age a set of institutions founded upon paganism.”220 Machiavelli was in Gioberti’s view the best example of the philosophical revival of the fifteenth century that was, basically, “a renewal of paganism.” For that reason, he held in scant consideration “the orders of Christianity,” and despite his wisdom, failed to understand that the true civil science is one that harmonizes “the profound study of events and men with the ideal illuminations, in keeping with the style of the Pythagoreans and the dictates of the Gospel, similarly steering clear of the atrocities and dreams, and working earnestly to improve human institutions, without aspiring to a chimerical perfection.”221 Machiavelli even seemed to regret the affirmation of Christianity, and in his political theory he separated the sacred element from the profane in his consideration of events.222 And from these premises, he finally and inevitably went so far as to theorize the absurd notion of Italian unity without Roman supremacy.

Despite these criticisms, Gioberti is a supporter of Machiavelli’s idea that the true Christian religion preaches neither idleness nor detachment from the world, but rather virtue. Idleness, which Gioberti attributes primarily to the Jesuits, is a contradiction of the will of God, who “created man to do rather than to enjoy” and “cuts off morality from its roots.” Virtue, in contrast, is in keeping with the will of God, glorifies man and renders him divine, making him godlike, within the confines of his finite nature, and directing him along the path that leads to eternal life.223 The causes of the unfortunate predisposition of the Italians to idleness and to “cowardly indolence” are the “wicked rulers” and religion misunderstood as “ascetic quietism,” in a radical contradiction of the “evangelical principles.” These are Machiavelli’s ideas, and Gioberti explicitly acknowledges this:

It should be no source of astonishment that Christianity, twisted and conceived in such a manner that its essence is inertia, is favorable to sloth; and that patience—a sublime virtue when it is conjoined with the evangelical activity, with faith enlivened by works, with an ardent love of one’s neighbor, with the worship of everything that can engender happiness and improvement—can become a source of vice when it is isolated from its intrinsic surroundings. And that is why Machiavelli, critical of the corrupt asceticism, which wishes you to be capable more of suffering than of doing something strong, adds that this disorder comes not from Christianity, but from the cowardice of the men who have interpreted our religion according to idleness and not according to virtue.224


The religion that Christ preached in Gioberti’s view emphasized “benevolent magnanimous” acts “of social virtue,” and of “civil humanity,” and command us to work for the victory in the city of human of liberty and on behalf of justice:

[Christ] came and went, benefiting and carrying out in an immediate, extraordinary manner, worthy of the power that created the world, those same miracles of charity that in a natural and gradual way are carried out by the process of refinement and civilization that takes place over the centuries. That process, by improving the legal, judicial, and governmental orders, by abolishing the dominion of man over man and violent, capricious, despotic lordships, making wars less frequent and less terrible, by unifying the nations and increasing the brotherhood among and between them, by encouraging and expanding knowledge… glorifies and elevates to the heavens the sincere and just cooperators of the common good on earth.225


The most influential apostle of the religion of the fatherland in the Italian Risorgimento, Giuseppe Mazzini (1805–72), did not perceive in the pages of Machiavelli the call for moral and religious reform. He was capable of distinguishing between Machiavelli and Machiavellianism, between the many “who have in their souls Machiavelli’s doubt, and the few who have his genius.”226 He inveighs against those mediocre courtiers “who study the secret of the third life of the nation in the pages written by Machiavelli on the cadaver of that nation” and against those who quote Machiavelli “to prove that politics knows no principles, but merely calculations of immediate utility.”227 He recognizes in him the prophet of the unification of Italy who served as something like an ideal bond linking Dante and Alfieri.228

He perceived the greatness of the man who suffered because of Italy’s corruption, but he considered his ideas inadequate to the needs of a rebirth of the fatherland:

Nations are not regenerated with lies, Machiavelli, whom the false prophets of liberty have long imitated, profaning his knowledge, lived in a time when church, principality, and foreigners had extinguished an era of Italian life and after braving the greatest dangers on behalf of the fatherland and having undergone prison and torture to see if there might be some way of extracting a spark of action, proceeded, God only knows with what misunderstood and uncomforted pain, to undertake an anatomy of the cadaver, to mark its scars, to enumerate the princely, courtly, and priestly worms squirming within it, and offered that spectacle to the better posterity that he foresaw, just as the Spartan fathers led their young before the drunken Helot in order to teach them to avoid the shame of intemperance. And we are at the dawn of an era, stirred by the breath of a new life; and what could we ever learn from the pages of Machiavelli save for an understanding of the tactics of the wicked to avoid them and disappoint them?229


Peoples are reborn through virtue and the “religion of the true,” not by means of Machiavellian contrivances or Jesuitic cunning.230 The true masters that we should follow are Socrates and Jesus Christ, more than the disillusioned Florentine Secretary:

No; we cannot revive through Jesuitism, we cannot regenerate a people with lies. Jesuitism is the tool of dying religions; lies are the art of a people condemned to servitude. Socrates and Jesus both died, at the hand of an executioner, a corporeal death, but their souls live on immortally, transfused from century to century in the finer life of ensuing generations. All moral and philosophical progress that has been achieved over the past two thousand years hearkens back to the name of Socrates, and an entire era of emancipatory civilization drew for fourteen centuries its auspices from the sainted name of Jesus: all of Machiavelli’s learning was nothing more than a funereal lamp that illuminated the tomb of the second life of Italy; and if the powerful anatomist of a period of shame and decay were to glimpse the pygmies who today busy themselves in imitating him around the cradle of the third life, he would fume with a greathearted wrath against them.231


Mazzini senses that Machiavelli would be on the side of those calling for the rebirth of Italy but cannot establish the presence of a convincing theory of emancipation even in Machiavelli’s pages on the rebirth of religions and states through a return to the beginnings. He wrote to Pietro Giannone in December 1832: “There may exist between us certain differences in our political and literary beliefs; you perhaps believe that it is necessary in order to regenerate Italy to comply with Machiavelli’s prescription, and draw it back to the centuries whence come the origins of its civilization; I believe that all the efforts of valiant men cannot revive a period that has run its course, and that instead of wasting energy and strength on what is impossible we should look to Italy, take possession of the tendencies now brewing in it, and turn them to good purpose, as best we can.”232

Without accepting Machiavelli’s insights, Mazzini developed a complete theory of religion as a necessary means for the emancipation of a people from political servitude and from moral corruption. For Mazzini, religion is a faith that provides the strength to translate a moral ideal into concrete action, and as such it is a regenerator of peoples. It is therefore a source of the drive to act ethically, that is, to achieve a harmony between thought and action. Religion does not mean a system of dogmas or truths written in one holy book or another, but rather a sentiment, a principle that drives men to find new political and social forms, a concept that elevates an individual, purifying him of selfishness and making him capable of acting in history in order to achieve a moral idea.233

Mazzini is profoundly convinced that without a religious sentiment there has never been, nor will there ever be, redemption or emancipation:

Man is higher than the earth upon which he stands. His lives on its surface, not at its center. His feet touch the soil, but his forehead is turned upward toward the heavens, as if he were trying to elevate toward them. High above, glittering in the clear sky or concealed behind storm clouds, is his polestar. From the depths of his soul he aspires to a future that he cannot hope to attain as things now stand, but which is the object of every pursuit of his life, the secret of his being, the guarantee of progress; and every great era of humanity makes that aspiration more intense, and spreads a new light on the concept that he forms of that future. Corresponding to that newly spread light is a social renewal—a new land made in the image of the new heaven. I do not know, speaking in historical terms, of a single achievement of the human spirit, a single important step taken along the path of the betterment of human society, that does not sink its roots in a strong religious belief; and I say that every doctrine in which an aspiration to the ideal is neglected, which fails to include, to the degree that its time allows, a solution to this supreme necessity for a faith, this everlasting problem of the origin and the fate of humanity, is and always will be powerless to bring to fruition the concept of a new world. It may succeed in shaping magnificent forms; but it will lack the spark of life that Prometheus brought to his statue from the heavens.234


For the emancipation of peoples religion has a much greater value than does philosophy.235 Evidence of this is the French Revolution, which Mazzini interprets as the victory of an ideal experienced as a faith over facts and lifeless institutions, and therefore as a manifestation of the most genuine religious spirit. The French Revolution, he writes, was “the work of Luther in the realm of politics,” and it is in this aspect that its glory and its power are to be found.236 However great his soul may have been, and however profound his love of the fatherland, what Machiavelli lacked was the true religious spirit that is capable of regenerating a people. And yet, precisely as he is reiterating the distance that separates Machiavelli from the religion of truth, Mazzini writes words that summarize the concept of the religion of virtue that Machiavelli had outlined:

Truth! The nascent Italy desires nothing else, and cannot live without it. The nascent Italy seeks in the present day its purpose, the rules governing its life in the future, a moral criterion, a way of choosing between good and evil, between truth and error, without which there can be no responsibility for Italy, and hence no liberty. Centuries of enslavement, centuries of selfishness, the only foundation of a slave’s life; centuries of corruption, slowly and cunningly instilled by a Catholicism devoid of any consciousness of a mission, have ruined, perverted, practically eradicated the instinct for the great and holy things, that God placed in Italy… The nascent Italy needs to strengthen itself by acquiring an understanding of its own duties, its own strength, of the virtue undermined by sacrifice, of the certainty of triumph that lies in logic: and you give it a theory of interests, opportunities, fictions; a poorly understood Machiavellianism revived by pupils to whom Machiavelli, were he alive, would say: I was in the presence of a people’s tomb; you, fools, are in the presence of a people’s cradle. The nascent Italy needs men who incarnate in themselves the truth in which Italy must find its identity; let them preach that truth aloud, represent it in their actions, and profess it, whatever may happen, until they are in their grave.


Without men of this sort, Mazzini concludes, and his words were tragically a prophecy that proved all too accurate, Italy will fall “beneath the yoke of the first foreign and domestic master who might choose to saddle it with tyranny, a weak, indecisive Italy, lacking confidence in itself or in others, with no notions of honor or glory, with no religion of truth, lacking the courage to translate it into action.”237

Toward the middle of the century, Edgar Quinet (1803–75) reopened the discussion in The Revolutions of Italy (Les Révolutions d’Italie) of the question of what role Machiavelli played in the history of religious reform in Italy. In the view of Quinet, a man who had received a Calvinist education, Machiavelli’s thought had an intellectual and political foundation in the defeat of Savonarola’s religious reform. In his opinion, Savonarola had committed the error of applying to the ills of Italy the remedies of the early church and had preached the message that aid should be sought only in God, that his listeners should arm themselves with the sword of prayer and await a miracle.238 Machiavelli, on the other hand, attempted to revive Italy from its moral death by means of a politics carried on “without God, without providence, without religion, neither pagan, nor Christian.” He tenaciously insisted upon the idea of “founding and preserving the fatherland without God and separate from God.”239 Because of the wicked example set by the papacy, the Italians were not only morally corrupt but also devoid of any sense of remorse or shame. In the face of this total moral depravity, Machiavelli did not raise the banner of a moral and religious reform but rather an appeal for a redeemer who would employ force to bring about a rebirth. His violent and impassioned theory left no room for the art of conquering the human soul with religion.240 Sustained by a love of the fatherland equal to that of the great men of antiquity, Machiavelli accepted neither the death of Italy nor a sense of Christian resignation. He attempted to make an heroic intellectual effort to find a cure for the ills of Italy in both political intelligence and in force. He felt no pity for the corpse he wished to revive. He showed in an unmistakable and irrefutable manner the absolute incompatibility between Roman Catholicism and liberty, and on this awareness based his exclusively political therapy for the rebirth of Italy.241

A few years later, Jules Michelet (1798–1874) pronounced a similar verdict in The History of France (Histoire de France, 1855). The prophet of the rebirth of Italy through religious reform was Savonarola, and his defeat provided proof that it is not possible to reform the Catholic Church by drawing it back to its beginnings, and that democracy and Christianity are incompatible. Machiavelli understood Savonarola’s defeat with great lucidity and took a radically different approach to the salvation of Italy. Seeing that God was doing nothing for Italy, he invoked a politics without God: if heaven turned a deaf ear, then he would invoke hell.242 Jacob Burckhardt (1818–97), in turn, reinforced in his The Civilization of the Renaissance in Italy (1860) the image of an Italy caught between an absurd call for moral and religious reform inspired by the ideals of the Middle Ages, and the clear-eyed but excessively cold diagnosis offered by Machiavelli. Savonarola, wrote Burckhardt, had brought together in his preaching both patriotism and moral and religious regeneration. His remedy for Italy’s moral corruption, however, was a theocracy in which men bowed down to God and lived for the salvation of their souls. Machiavelli also knew that Italy’s ill’s consisted of a particular kind of moral corruption, but he failed to realize that its cause was the individualism that had driven Italy outside of “the limits of morality and religion.”243

The ideas of Quinet, Michelet, and Burckhardt on Machiavelli and the failed religious reform in Italy were adopted and developed by Francesco Fiorentino (1834–84) who, in his 1868 book on Pietro Pomponazzi (1462–1525), focused on the profound similarities of religion of the Mantuan philosopher and those of the Florentine Secretary, Pomponazzi’s contemporary. In his De incantationibus, written in Bologna in 1520, Pomponazzi stated that religions, including the Christian religion, are subjected to natural cycles of birth and decay, determined by the influence of the celestial movements on human affairs: “And let the truth be told, if religions are born and die in a necessary alternation caused by the revolutions of the stars, it is inevitable that the time will come as well for the Christian religion; and Pomponazzi makes no secret of the matter, indeed he foretells the imminent waning of faith, and the diminishing frequency of miracles; and the end of that religion will be nigh when faith and miracles will have ceased entirely.”244 The influence of the stars also affected both the founders of religions and the great lawgivers, justly called “sons of God.” And the latter, in order to carry out their work, made use of miracles and religion, just as Machiavelli had maintained.245 With the difference that, while Pomponazzi considered religion to be an “instrument of virtue,” Machiavelli considered it to be an “instrument of the regime.”246 For that reason, Machiavelli, who was an especially knowledgeable critic of the “decay of religion in Italy,” refused—or was unable—to become a promoter of religious reform. When it emerged onto the Italian political and intellectual scene, the possibility of reform vanished with the pyre that consumed Savonarola:

Girolamo Savonarola lacked neither enthusiasm, nor learning, nor personal integrity of customs for his role as an apostle, yet all the same he failed in his undertaking due to a shortage of faith among his followers. The Florentine people might in a moment of great exaltation applaud the fervid sermons of the Friar of San Marco and make a bonfire of the monuments to worldly vanity and corruption; but after rethinking matters, they could not approve of that barbarous destruction; they sensed that art was their life, their glory, and that they could not abandon that art to follow Savonarola on his flight to a life devoid of beauty.247


And so, while religious reform took root in Germany and in other nations of northern Europe, the Italians instead abandoned themselves to criticism and to irony, and with their criticism and their irony they remained servants of popes and princes.

The manifold reflections on the failure of religious reform in Italy were given an effective summary in the pages of Francesco De Sanctis. Once the attempt at religious reform advocated by Savonarola proved to be in vain, what remained for De Sanctis was the scientific path identified by Machiavelli: “The Renaissance came, and science truly believed that it was capable of restoring life: the science was called Machiavelli, Campanella, and Sarpi; and the life was Cesare Borgia, Leo X, and Philip II.” But “thoughts remain thoughts, and facts remain facts.”248 Both Machiavelli and Savonarola wanted to reform Italy. Savonarola “relied upon enthusiasm for his reform”; Machiavelli relied on science: “Savonarola employed fanaticism; uncomprehending and intolerant wrath: Machiavelli employed the tolerance that understands and absolves… the tolerance of the scientist…the irony of the man who is above anger.”249 The former failed because he wanted to enact a de facto replacement of social indolence with the indolence of monasteries, the latter failed because science lacked the power of redemption and wound up feeding the spirit of intelligent adaptation to corruption that celebrated its triumph in the man depicted by Guicciardini, wholly absorbed in the care of his “particulare”:

To restore the Middle Ages and obtain a reform of customs and conscience through a religious and moral restoration was the conception of Savonarola. The Council of Trent adopted this same conception, and refined it: it was the one most easily understood by the masses and most easy to present to them, for the common people looked to the past for their remedy. But Machiavelli, looking at the corruption, saw it from another and a higher standpoint. Pensive and ill at ease in the middle of the Italian carnival, he saw this corruption of Italy as nothing else than the Middle Ages in putrefaction—dead in men’s consciousness, but still surviving in forms and institutions—and far from wishing to plunge Italy back into the past and to revive the Middle Ages, he took his share in destroying that past.250


In De Sanctis’s view, Machiavelli attempted to find the essence behind the appearance, and raised the banner of Italian unification and of the separation of church and state. In this sense, he begins the tradition that later continued with Sarpi, Giannone, Mazzini, Gioberti, and Cavour. He was not however the promoter of a genuine religious reform. If he had any religion, it was that of the fatherland, and because of his deep and abiding love of the fatherland, Machiavelli deserves earthly glory. On the day of the liberation of Rome, De Sanctis set forth a sincere and moving eulogy:

What Machiavelli looks to see is whether he is a man; his aim is to revive the roots of the plant called “man,” which is drooping…. The seriousness of earthly life with its instrument, which is work; its objective, which is one’s country; its principle, which is equality and liberty; its moral link, which is the nation; its agent, which is the State, autonomous and independent…. Scholasticism dies: science is born. This is the real Machiavellism; alive, indeed young, even today. Developed, corrected, simplified, and in part realized, Machiavellism is the program of the modern world, and the great nations are the nations that come nearest to realizing it. Let us be proud of our Machiavelli. Whenever a part of the ancient building crumbles, let there be glory to Machiavelli, and whenever a part of the new is built, let there be glory to Machiavelli! Even as I am writing these words the bells are ringing far and wide, unceasingly, telling that the Italians are in Rome: the temporal power is falling, the people are shouting, “Long live the unity of Italy!” Let there be glory to Machiavelli!251


De Sanctis admired Machiavelli the patriot but looked with suspicion, good liberal that he was, on the religion of the fatherland and the elevation of the state to a divine principle. “Country to Machiavelli,” he writes,

was a god, higher even than morality, and higher than law…. God had come out of Heaven and descended to the earth…. Liberty meant the participation, in some degree, of all the citizens in public affairs. The rights of man did not enter as yet into the code of freedom. Man was not as yet an autonomous being, an end in himself: he was the instrument of his country, or what was even worse, of the state… the individual absorbed into society, or as they called it then, “the omnipotence of the state.” Machiavelli puts these ideas forward not as things discovered or analyzed for the first time by himself, but as things accepted by long tradition and backed by classical culture. They have the spirit of ancient Rome in them…. In ancient times “liberty” had meant the sharing by citizens in the government, and this was the sense in which Machiavelli had understood it. But in modern times, besides this question of political liberty there is also the thing called “liberty of conscience.”252


The fatherland of Machiavelli is, in short, a divinity that suffocates, that “absorbed religion, morality, and individuality”; and his state is not content with being autonomous itself, but “wants to cut away autonomy from everything else.”253 With his state, and with a leader, Machiavelli believed he could found the Italian nation, failing to realize that it was necessary also to “re-create the people, re-create thought, re-create man, the Roman man of whom he dreamed.” If political reform were to become a reality, it needed to be accompanied by a religious reform that could recognize in an unambiguous manner the value of the individual.

With the end of the Risorgimento, the scholars who were linked intellectually to that period disagreed on the significance of the works of Machiavelli, in particular concerning his thoughts on the topic of religion. Gino Capponi, the bard of the late Florentine Republic, considered Machiavelli to be a man without religious faith and, as such, a faithful reflection of Italy: “Nor was religion dead in his thoughts, nor in the thoughts of Italy: he revered it as an elevated thing, he loved it as something Italian; then he assailed it out of indignation at the misrule that he saw besmirching it; and with vices he cancelled it from his heart. Thus was Machiavelli and thus was Italy.”254 Pasquale Villari (1827–1917), who wrote his book The Life and Times of Niccolò Machiavelli) (Niccolò Machiavelli e i suoi tempi, 1877–82) with the ambition of understanding both the vices of Italy “against which we struggle today,” as well as the virtues of Italy, “which helped us to rise again,” claims that Machiavelli never thought about religious matters and “was a still greater Pagan, as is abundantly proved by every page of his works. It is proved by his boundless admiration for antiquity; his indifference to religion; his hatred towards the Papacy; by the way in which he spoke of Christianity, especially when comparing it with Paganism; and finally by the peculiar language he frequently employed and that demonstrated his mode of thought with singular lucidity”255 Machiavelli felt and showed with words and actions his “ardent and irresistible love for liberty, his country, and even for virtue,” but he had the great shortcoming of having sent Italy, directing it away from Savonarola, toward a political redemption without religious reform:

Italy had become incapable of a religious reformation similar to that accomplished in Germany. Instead of springing towards God, as Savonarola had predicted; instead of seeking strength in a new conception of faith, she aimed at a re-composition of the idea of the State and the motherland. She saw in the sacrifice of all to the universal good the only possible way of political and moral redemption. The unity of the regenerated country would have inevitably led to the re-establishment of morality, would have rekindled faith in public and private virtue, and discovered a method of sanctifying the purpose of life. This idea, vaguely and feebly felt by many, was the ruling thought of Machiavelli, the shrine upon which he offered up his entire existence.256


Oreste Tommasini (1844–1919), in his monumental work The Life and Writings of Niccolò Machiavelli in Their Connection with Machiavellism, (La vita e gli scritti di Niccolò Machiavelli nella loro relazione col Machiavellismo, 1883–1911), on the other hand, explicitly refutes the allegations of Machiavelli’s paganism and emphasizes the fact that the “the Risorgimento (‘resurrection’ or ‘rebirth’) of faith” through a “religious reformation, brought about and attained through any means possible,” was “among the most vibrant and personal of Machiavelli’s aspirations,” even if “his exhortation was not understood, was not taken into consideration, and had no echo in Italy.”257 In Tommasini’s view, Machiavelli was free of any theological prejudice and believed that divinity was honored through works, which was also an idea shared with men such as Cromwell, Robespierre, Garibaldi, or Mazzini, and is the way Americans see things. Machiavelli saw that the true problem afflicting Italy was a bad religious education:

These truly are the Gravamina italicae nationis [Lamentations of an Italian Nation] that only Machiavelli raised up and handed down to posterity, lacking confidence that his contemporaries would pay him heed, pained that they refused to understand. If the Italians only knew themselves as well as they know those with whom they have dealings, they would be the foremost men in the world: but their consciences allow themselves either to be deceived or corrupted. Christianity failed to enliven them sufficiently, theology perverted them; it even persuaded them that it was wicked to call wickedness wicked; so that their conscience lacked even a secure moral sentiment. Dante was not enough to revive and bring salvation to their conscience; and—“What are you getting me into, father?“—the anguished cry of Lucrezia in La Mandragola [The Mandrake] is the symbolic exclamation of the deceived conscience of an entire people, and the cry of all Italy is perpetual.258


Machiavelli “does not touch faith, but he wants it solid, simple, and practical, and he girds it, and penetrates it, with virile charity.” He recommended only one thing to the Italians:

to gather together all of Italy under the rule of a single chief; this cannot happen, until faith is reinvigorated and the priesthood loses every last shred of overlordship upon the peninsula. Unity brings internal and external emancipation. He wanted this thought, which contains the aspiration and the crucial pivot of the Italian resurgence, to become the sentiment of the people, because a deeply felt necessity, whatever the difficulties and obstacles placed along the path of its attainment, will persist in the souls becoming a faith, committing the people to battle, finding its martyrs, winning victories, and achieving its logical and ineluctable effects, all the more vast the less imminent they may prove to be. This truly became the solid faith of Italy.259


The long history of meditations on moral and religious reform had its high point in the 1920s and 1930s, when Italian fascism brought to vivid life the truth of Machiavelli’s prophecy that a people made morally weak by bad education and corrupt religion will become easy prey to wicked men. The awareness that fascism sank its roots into age-old moral ills, and that a liberation from that regime would only be possible through a moral renewal of the Italians, encouraged the birth of the concept of a “religion of liberty.” This concept, which more than any other encouraged the moral and political resistance to the Fascist regime, emerged in thoughts about Machiavelli or thoughts that took Machiavelli as a specific point of reference and departure.

The chief mastermind behind the development of Machiavelli’s God into the principle of a religion of liberty was Piero Gobetti (1901–26). Gobetti sensed a profound spiritual link to the Florentine Secretary. “We,” he wrote, speaking in the name of the founders of the journal Liberal Revolution, “feel that we are strangers to the spirit of the Gospels” and yet close “to the despair of the Old Testament”; our enthusiasm for actions, responsibility, and altruism spring not from faith but from despair; we are aware “everything is cruelly equal.” Gobetti sensed within himself the same passions and beliefs that he had perceived in Machiavelli:

We are no longer heroes, even if it were with the optimistic cunning of Don Quixote; but rather we are disinterested historians (artists) in the sense of Machiavelli who was able to find the same ethical nature (praxis) in Callimaco, in Castruccio Castracani, and in Duke Valentino, and to discuss with the same engagement and the same indifferent serenity the enterprise of La Mandragola [The Mandrake] and his own legations; his objective in both cases was to ensure that cunning and activity (serene, heroic, ethical) would prevail against the inert optimism of a Messer Nicia (have you never noticed that Fra Timoteo is a sympathetic character for M.?).260


Gobetti found Machiavelli’s God through the guidance of Alfieri, whom he had studied for his baccalaureate thesis at the University of Turin. Like Machiavelli, who wrote in favor of political action even though he was well aware that in his own lifetime no one would be able to put into practice his teachings and his exhortations, Alfieri wrote in favor of political action, prompted by a pessimistic serenity. He did not act out of faith, but because “he had no faith.” In Alfieri, “the ideal does not illuminate from without, hovering on high, unattainable, but rather springs from action, and lies in the very despair with which one accepts the ineluctable consciously, firmly renouncing all illusions and falseness, which are born only of weakness and selfishness, one rediscovers the austere criterion of virtue in the unselfishness of solitude.”261 Alfieri, in Gobetti’s view, was able to rediscover a “truer and more vigorous Machiavelli” than the Machiavelli who was a pure technician of government, and he was above all able to “rethink his intrinsic spiritual consistency.”262

Gobetti, following in Alfieri’s footsteps, emphasizes that the God that commands liberty as a moral principle is the necessary presupposition of political liberty. Alfieri sees no meaning in “a political liberty that is not based on an internal liberty—which would be understood as strong feelings,”263 Alfieri’s God is the same God that Machiavelli had opposed to the God who teaches us to be servile and who deprives peoples of the strength to achieve great things, who inspires the creators of religions, and who lives on, not in the Christ who teaches humility, but in the Christ who is a “creator of political liberty.”264 It is on this God that Gobetti founded the religion of liberty, as opposed to the religion of the servile, which must inspire a people of citizens capable of fighting on behalf of liberty because they must, and not because they are sure of victory. A religion that “is no longer a source of comfort for the weak, but of security for the strong, no longer a cult of a transcendent activity, but of our own activity, no longer faith but responsibility”; a religion “that excludes interests and calculations, that demands, as Alfieri writes so effectively, fanaticism in the founders, and in the founders enthusiasm of sincerity, and in one and all that complete ardor for which there is no break between thought and action.”265

Gobetti identifies this religion of liberty as the content of the Italian intellectual and moral reform. Harking back to the judgment of De Sanctis on which I commented at the beginning of this book, Gobetti wrote that “our Reformation was Machiavelli, an isolated individual, a political theorist. His concepts failed to find a social terrain upon which to establish a foundation, or men that could live them. Machiavelli is a modern man because he founded a conception of the State that rebelled against the transcendent, and conceived of an art of politics as the organization of the practical, as well as professing a religiosity of practical action understood as spontaneity of initiative and economy” In the first chapter of Rivoluzione liberale, Gobetti borrows the same concept with a significant modification: Machiavelli “professes a civil religiosity as spontaneity of initiatives and economy.” In order to attain its political reform, Italy needs this civil religion or religion of liberty, the religion that Machiavelli taught us with his refinement, as a “citizen who is an expert in historical contingencies,” not the “noisy program” of the peasant Luther.266

In the years in which fascism seemed to be invincible thanks to its powerful weapons and techniques of persuasion, the demand for moral reform resurfaced in the writings of a few who, like Carlo Rosselli (1899–1937), refused to resign themselves to lives of servitude. The Italian problem,” writes Rosselli in Socialismo liberale (1928–29),

is essentially the problem of liberty—but liberty understood in the fullest sense: as spiritual autonomy, as the setting free of the conscience for individuals, and as the organization of the social world (meaning the building of the state) and relations among groups and classes. Without free men, there is no possibility of a free state. If consciences are not set free, there is no possibility of social classes being set free. This is not to reason in a circle. Liberty begins with the formation of the individual and ends with the triumph of a state whose members are free individuals, equal in rights and duties, a state in which the liberty of each is the condition and limit of the liberty of all the rest.267


Rosselli takes from Mazzini the need for moral renewal as an indispensable requirement for liberty, but it is the same requirement that Machiavelli had set forth: “Now it is sad to have to admit this,” he writes, “but nonetheless it is true: in Italy the training of the individual, the formation of the individual as the basic moral unit, is still to a large extent unaccomplished. Because of poverty, indifference, and a long-standing resignation, most Italians still lack a sharp and deep-rooted sense of autonomy and responsibility. The state of subjection that lasted for centuries makes the average Italian vacillate between the habit of servility and anarchic revolt even today. The concept of life as a struggle and a mission, the notion of liberty as a moral duty, and the awareness of one’s limitations and those of others are all missing.”268

By a surprising but not inexplicable coincidence, Antonio Gramsci too explores, in his reflections on Machiavelli, the idea of moral and religious reform. Gramsci interprets The Prince as the work in which Machiavelli first explained how a political leader needed to be capable of guiding a scattered and dispersed people to founding a new state by encouraging and organizing its “collective will”; then he exhorts his readers to action with the impassioned cry of the Esortazione.269 Gramsci identifies in Machiavelli, “in nuce,” the constituent elements of an “intellectual and moral revolution” understood as a “religious question or a conception of the world.” From these elements he develops the belief that the modern prince, the Communist Party, “must and cannot fail to be the originator and organizer of a reform both intellectual and moral,” capable of developing “the collective national will toward the achievement of a superior form of modern civilization.”270

One of the factors in Machiavelli’s conception of the world, or religion, is the principle “to lose one’s soul in order to save the fatherland or state.” Gramsci describes this principle as “an element of absolute secularism” in the face of religion of the dominant conception, which in turn responded by depicting Machiavelli as “a diabolical apparition.” While for Gobetti moral reform was individual and internal, Gramsci’s was set forth by a collective political subject: “The Prince takes the place, in the consciences, of the divinity of the categorical imperative, he becomes the foundation of a modern secularism and a secularization of all life and the relationships of everyday existence.”271

In those same years Croce developed the idea of the religion of freedom in The History of Europe in the Nineteenth Century (Storia di Europa nel secolo XIX). He singled out the religion of freedom in the liberal, democratic, and national movements that flourished in Europe at the turn of the nineteenth century. That religion brought together and harmonized the long history of freedom and proved its power with the example of its “poets, theorists, orators, writers, propagandists, apostles, and martyrs.” It was capable of penetrating into the souls of the people, moving them to action and sacrifice: “The heroic figure, who spoke to the hearts, was that of the soldier-poet, the intellectual who is capable of fighting and dying for his ideas; a figure who did not linger in the raptures of imagination and the paradigms of education, but who appeared in flesh and blood on the battlefields and the barricades all over Europe. The ‘missionaries’ were accompanied by the ‘crusaders’ of liberty”

The liberal idea is a new religion because it is a conception of reality and it has a conformable ethic. It has no need of “personifications, myths, legends, dogmas, rites, propitiations, expiations, priestly classes, pontifical paludaments, and things of that sort”; it condemns the weak man of letters and the dreamy philosopher, servile mindsets and the adulation of the courtier; it refuses to “separate the man from the citizen, the individual from the society that shapes him and which he shapes”; it exhorts its audience to a love of the fatherland; it instills a sense of duty; it demands the pursuit of truth; it requires engagement in action or at least a desire for action; it teaches us to look on defeats as opportunities to show or reaffirm the power of liberty.272

The religion of liberty, Croce points out, brings together manifold religious and philosophical aspirations, and believes that it “represents the finest impulses,” that it is “purification, exploration, and reinforcement of the religious life of humanity.” Among the aspirations and requirements brought together by the religion of liberty, there is also that of those who sought to bring about a religious reform that would first and foremost teach a civil morality capable of upholding a faith in liberty. Croce does not consider Machiavelli to be a forerunner of the religion of liberty, but rather a man who displayed the “evident signs of a stern and sorrowful moral conscience,” and he mocks those who engage in “so much idle talk about Machiavelli’s immorality.”273

He also explains, better than anyone else could have aspired to do, that when discussing political action that founds and sustains states, Machiavelli quite frequently

speaks of the State in a religious tone, as when he recalls the saying that one must be prepared for the sake of the State to lose not only one’s reputation, but also the salvation of one’s own soul; or as when he looks back, with ill-hidden envy, at the pagan religion, which exalted, as the highest good, honor in this world, extolling human glory, and praising greatness of spirit, strength of body, and all the virtues which make man powerful; whereas the Christian religion, by showing the truth and the real way to the world beyond, despises this world, and praises abjection, setting contemplative men above the others, and endurance above action.274


Croce goes so far as to write, in his commentary on the passage that “the States must from time to time be called back to their principles,” that “Machiavelli becomes religious in dealing with the art of the State.”275 He considers Machiavelli’s religiosity not as a complete theory, but rather as a state of mind. The theory set forth by Machiavelli the political writer, whatever the state of mind of Machiavelli the man, was for Croce pure politics, that is, politics as the terrain of force and utility.276 Therefore Croce fails to perceive in the pages of Machiavelli the urgent need for a moral reform based on the idea of a God that is the principle of virtuous action.

When fascism, the true progeny of bad religious education, dragged Italy into the tragedy of the war, it was the religion of liberty that aided the country’s moral and civil rebirth: a religion without churches and without a Messiah, which instilled in the souls of the many the idea of a “human fatherland in which all free men recognized themselves and understood one another in the same language,” and gave them the strength to live and die for an ideal. Piero Calamandrei understood this clearly: “There are those who have spoken of a ‘collective soul,’ there are others who have spoken of ‘Providence’; perhaps we should speak of God: of an unknown God that is inside each and every one of us.” The Resistance had a “religious character” because religion is “seriousness of life, commitment to moral values, consistency between thought and action.” How and why a people, or at least the best among them, succeeded in being reborn from the moral death of living in servitude, appeared to Calamandrei and to others as a “miraculous and mysterious event.”277 Miraculous and mysterious, it certainly was, but if we set it within a broader historical span, we can also interpret it as a sign of the tenacious presence of the religious ideal of rebirth that spread throughout Italy along with republican liberty and experienced its highest moments during the Risorgimento.

Evidence of this can be seen in the fact that, once Italy began to be reborn, those who were protagonists and witnesses to that rebirth rediscovered the authentic significance of the ancient message of Machiavelli. Luigi Russo, who dedicated his book Machiavelli (1945) to Nello Rosselli and Leone Ginzburg, two anti-Fascist martyrs, captured in unrivaled fashion the leading role that the Florentine Secretary had played in the history of Italy’s moral and religious conscience. Russo explained that those who had truly kept alive the profound significance of Machiavelli’s lesson were men like Mazzini and Alfieri, who understood that the problem of liberty in Italy was first and foremost question of moral reform.278 Savonarola, the much mocked unarmed prophet, writes Russo, remains ideally vindicated by the anti-Machiavellian political thinkers who “struggled to establish the value, alongside the ‘effectual reality’ also the ‘ideal reality’ alongside the earth, heaven, how things ought to be alongside how things are, and finally to purify, brighten, and entirely humanize the savage nature of the Machiavellian centaur.” And above all Savonarola is vindicated by Italian history, “when the unity of the peninsula, an abstract prophecy at the turn of the sixteenth century, became a concrete standard at the end of the eighteenth century and in the nineteenth century, only because pure politics became also poetic and religious agitation, and the friar’s prophetic pathos returned in the words of an Alfieri and a Mazzini.”279

To Russo, political action that become prophecy and poetry in order to redeem peoples and found states was not the antithesis of the politics that Machiavelli had theorized, but rather a truer expression of that politics: “After a cold dissertation on the arts of the lion and the fox, at the end of the short treatise, he steals the colors, the images, and the style of his much satirized adversary [Savonarola], from the polemical idol of his fantasy, and speaks as a prophet, himself describing such biblical notions as a sea that has parted, a cloud that showed the path, a stone that has poured forth water, the heavens that have rained down manna, and he calls to his aid the voice of a poet; because he knew that ‘many times poets are filled with divine and prophetic spirit.’”280 Machiavelli becomes a prophet because he knows that “without prophetic pathos, without moral renewal, without civil conscience,” a principality, and to that much greater a degree, a republic, will remain a mere utopia, nothing more than yet another imaginary republic. It is the force of his political realism that obliges him to seek and imagine a God that commands civic virtue.281

The history of Italy’s aspiration to moral and religious reform is not over, and will never be concluded, except perhaps once it appears completely futile. There will be times, as there were in past, when that reform will appear possible and within reach; there will be other times in which it will seem to be distant or unattainable. One of the periods in Italian history in which the hopes for a moral reform were most widespread and powerful were the years that witnessed the foundation of the Italian Republic and the drafting of the Italian Constitution. In one of the debates that clearly expresses the hopes and fears of that time, the debate over the ratification of the peace treaty signed in Paris on 10 February 1947, the name of Machiavelli emerges once again. It was Luigi Einaudi—one of the leading figures, with Croce, of Italian liberalism—who cited Machiavelli’s name, in his speech to the council on 29 July 1947. Benedetto Croce, a few days earlier, had spoken strongly in favor of not signing that peace treaty; Einaudi responded with an argument entirely inspired by a faith in liberty that he, a secular speaker, couched in religious language:

the only course of action that appears before us is the preaching of the good word. We know what this good word is: it is the idea of liberty versus intolerance, cooperation versus brute force. The Europe that Italy hopes to see, and for whose creation Italy must fight, is not a Europe shut off to anyone, it is rather a Europe open to one and all, a Europe where people can freely uphold their differing ideals, in which the majorities respect minorities and themselves promote the aims of those minorities, to the very furthest point at which they remain compatible with the survival of the community as a whole. On behalf of the creation of this Europe, Italy must be ready to sacrifice a part of its sovereignty.282


We must learn from Machiavelli, Einaudi explains, and not from Machiavelli “meditating in solitude in the exile of his rustic Tuscan village on the theorems of pure political science,” but from Machiavelli “the man,” from Machiavelli “the citizen of Florence” who “had no fear, certainly, of turning to the people, whom he believed to be ‘capable of truth,’ capable that is of grasping the truth and moving away from false prophets when ‘some good man gets up who in orating demonstrates to them how they deceive themselves.’ Yes. Nowadays it is necessary that once again good men stand up, as Niccolò Machiavelli hoped, to show the peoples of Europe the path of salvation, persuading them to shatter the futile idols of the omnipotence of impotent states, of totalitarianism, ally of nationalism and fierce enemy of the liberty and independence of nations.”283


In the words that Einaudi, a man not given to flights of rhetoric, spoke at a very serious moment for Italy’s future, we see the return of the Machiavelli who had perceived the need of men endowed with that quality of moral integrity that gives their words the power to persuade and liberate; men who felt for the fatherland a religious sentiment of charity, much as he did. The idea of religious reform set forth by Machiavelli in his writings was directed toward the education of men of this kind, because he knew that only men who lived in accordance with a religion that commands its followers to love liberty and to be strong in order to win and defend that liberty would be able to redeem Italy from its moral servitude. Einaudi’s words on Machiavelli, entirely sustained by a faith in liberty that allowed him to glimpse, in the wake of the tragedies of war and Fascism, the possibility of a Europe of free peoples, can surprise, and should surprise us; but they allow us to understand, once again, that Machiavelli has been living in Italian history as the advocate of a moral and religious reform, and reminds us that in order to be free, we need the aid of that god of his.

12 In the dialogue, Luigi Guicciardini presents him as an adversary of the thesis that the heavens influence men’s bodies but not their souls: “Since heaven then instilled its power as you just prudently stated in human bodies and always infusing differently, by various lights and motions of its own, its influences in these and those human bodies, I cannot understand otherwise than that Justice, Chastity, Liberality, and many other admirable virtues, such as were found in many famous men (aside from those just mentioned by you), must derive from the heavens. And likewise for Pride, Cruelty, Lust, Greed, and other shameful sins. Since this is the case (as admitted by valiant philosophers and modern theologians) I cannot see or understand so easily (as you claim) the liberty of our actions,” See Felix Gilbert, “Machiavelli in an Unknown Contemporary Dialogue,” Journal of the Warburg and Courtauld Institutes 1 (1937), p. 164. Machiavelli had used the expression ‘libero arbitrio’ (free will) in his Exhortatio ad capessendam Italiam.

Caucci, Cantagalli, Siena 1978, 1, II, chapter 4, pp. 24–25. See Gennaro Maria Barbuto, Il principe e l’Anticristo: Gesuiti e ideologie politiche, Guida, Naples 1994, pp. 249–76. Another prestigious Jesuit, Adam Contzen, emphasized that in Machiavelli’s view, fate is nothing more than the ineluctable influence of the stars on human affairs (“fatum… astrorum influxum appellat et fatum ex astris inevitable”); Adam Contzen, Politicorum libri decem, in quibus de perfectae reipublicae forma, virtutibus et vitiis, institutione civium, legibus magistratu ecclesiastico, civili, potentia reipublicae, editio secunda auction sumpt. 149 As Luigi Russo made so abundantly clear, Alfieri was the herald “of that internal ethical liberty, a new and innovative world religion, which only was the yeast for all the enduring constructions and all the new orders that ensued.” He was anti-Christian in his criticism of that absolute and terrifying God that favored the tyrannies; he was most Christian indeed in his unyielding defense of the moral liberty of the individual and hence of peoples. It is important to emphasize that Delio Cantimori, Carlo Cantimori’s son, was one of the first to perceive the link between Machiavelli and the proponents of moral reform in Italy. In the foreword to Eretici italiani del Cinquecento, Delio Cantimori acknowledges the encouragement of his Mazzinian father to venture into a study of the Italian heretics. See Delio Cantimori, Eretici italiani del Cinquecento, edited by Adriano Prosperi, Einaudi, Turin 2002, p. 7.

234 Giuseppe Mazzini, I Sistemi e la Democrazia, in Scritti editi e inediti di Giuseppe Mazzini, Daelli Editore, Milan 1864, vol. 7, p. 334. In the letter he wrote to Francesco Bertioli in January 1833, Mazzini remarks: “I am not a Christian, in the sense of believing in the divinity of Christ, and other scenes of the sort: that I don’t accept any other revelation than that of Genius; that I believe that religion is a product, and an expression of Society, like literature, law, and politics, etc.:… that religion [is] subject to Progress like all things—that in this sense, I believe that Christianity is the first religion that is an expression of our civilization, of the modern world in opposition to antiquity—that Christianity has formulated for me the Dogma of Equality—and in this sense I am a Christian—that however I do not believe that Christianity preached anything other than Equality in the face of God, and individual improvement—that we are verging on the era in which we must preach Equality in the face of other men, and social improvement—that in this sense I am not Christian; but that proceeding progressively, society still under the domination of Catholicism, we cannot leap directly beyond Christianity, and preach pure Deism, which is my religion”; Scritti editi ed inediti di Giuseppe Mazzini, vol. 5, p. 216.
